# 0.0 Introduction: How Modern Mathematics Works

Before we write down a single definition, let us pause on the questions that every careful student eventually asks when meeting rigorous mathematics for the first time. What exactly *is* a mathematical truth? If every theorem is proved from axioms, and the axioms are simply assumed, are we not going around in a circle—calling something "true" because it follows from statements we *decided* to call "true"? And why on earth does a course about the real numbers begin with logic and sets instead of with numbers?

These are not naive questions. They are precisely the questions that forced mathematics to rebuild its own foundations between roughly 1820 and 1940, and the answers that came out of that upheaval shape how every working mathematician thinks today. This introduction tells that story—what mathematics used to be, why that picture broke, and what replaced it—so that when we start manipulating symbols in Section 0.1, you know exactly which game is being played and what the word "true" means inside it.

## From Self-Evident Truths to Foundational Crises

### Euclid and the Classical Picture of Axioms

For more than two thousand years, the gold standard of rigorous thought was Euclid's *Elements* (c. 300 BCE). Euclid started from a handful of definitions, "common notions", and five postulates—such as *"a straight line can be drawn between any two points"*—and deduced hundreds of geometric propositions from them by pure reasoning. The method was so successful that philosophers from Aristotle to Spinoza to Kant held it up as the very model of certain knowledge.

Notice what the word *axiom* meant in this classical picture. An axiom was a statement that was **self-evidently true about the real world**: physical space simply *is* like this, and anyone who understands the words must agree. Truth came first, built into the axioms by their obviousness, and proof merely carried that truth downhill to the theorems. Under this view, the circularity worry above would never even arise, because axioms were not *assumptions* at all—they were *insights* into how reality is.

### The First Crack: Non-Euclidean Geometry

To see what was at stake, here are Euclid's five postulates, in lightly modernized wording:

1. A straight line segment can be drawn joining any two points.
2. Any straight line segment can be extended indefinitely in a straight line.
3. Given any point and any distance, a circle can be drawn with that point as centre and that distance as radius.
4. All right angles are equal to one another.
5. If a straight line crossing two other straight lines makes the two interior angles on one side add up to less than two right angles, then those two lines, if extended indefinitely, meet on that side.

The first four are short, simple statements about drawing lines and circles and about right angles. The fifth, the **parallel postulate**, is long and complicated, and it talks about what happens when lines are extended "indefinitely", which no one can ever actually check. It never looked as obvious as the other four. In a common equivalent form (known as *Playfair's axiom*) it says: given a line $\ell$ and a point $P$ not on $\ell$, there is exactly one line through $P$ that never meets $\ell$. For centuries, mathematicians tried to prove it from the other four postulates, convinced that it was a theorem in disguise.

In the 1820s and 1830s, Nikolai Lobachevsky and János Bolyai (and, privately, Carl Friedrich Gauss) took the opposite route. They *replaced* the parallel postulate by its negation—through $P$ there pass *many* lines that miss $\ell$—and worked out the consequences at length. No contradiction ever appeared. Instead, a strange but perfectly coherent **hyperbolic geometry** emerged, in which the angles of every triangle sum to less than two right angles. In 1868 Eugenio Beltrami settled the matter by building concrete *models* of hyperbolic geometry out of ordinary Euclidean objects: reinterpret the words "point", "line", and "distance" suitably, and every hyperbolic axiom becomes a true statement about familiar Euclidean things. Consequently, if hyperbolic geometry ever produced a contradiction, Euclidean geometry would produce one too.

The philosophical fallout was enormous. There are at least two internally consistent geometries, and they flatly contradict each other on the parallel postulate. Pure reason alone cannot decide which one describes physical space—that turned out to be a question for physics, and Einstein's general relativity eventually described the geometry of our universe as curved and non-Euclidean. Axioms stopped being "obvious truths about the world" and began to be seen as **choices** that *define* which mathematical structure we are talking about.

### The Crisis in Calculus and the Arithmetization of Analysis

Meanwhile calculus—the direct ancestor of this course—had been growing for 150 years on a famously shaky footing. Newton and Leibniz (late 17th century) reasoned with **infinitesimals**: quantities smaller than every positive number yet not zero, which could be divided by when convenient and thrown away when convenient. In 1734 Bishop George Berkeley mocked them as *"the ghosts of departed quantities"*, and he had a point: nobody could say what an infinitesimal actually was. The results were spectacular, but the reasoning behind them could not be checked.

As analysis matured, intuition began to fail in earnest. Fourier's work on heat flow (1807–1822) produced infinite sums of perfectly smooth sine waves that converge to *discontinuous* functions, contradicting the widespread belief that a limit of continuous functions must be continuous—a belief that even appeared as a "theorem" in respected textbooks. Then, in 1872, Karl Weierstrass presented a function that is **continuous everywhere yet differentiable nowhere**: a curve with no tangent line at any of its points, demolishing the geometric intuition that a continuous curve is smooth except at a few isolated corners.

The response, led by Augustin-Louis Cauchy, Bernard Bolzano, Weierstrass, Richard Dedekind, and Georg Cantor, is known as the **arithmetization of analysis**. It had two parts:

1. **Banish infinitesimals and pictures from proofs.** Limits, continuity, and derivatives were redefined purely in terms of inequalities between ordinary numbers—the $\varepsilon$–$\delta$ definitions you will master in later chapters. A proof should be checkable line by line without drawing anything.
2. **Build the real numbers themselves out of simpler objects.** Even after step 1, analysis still rested on the "continuum" of real numbers, understood geometrically as the points of a line. In 1872 Dedekind (via *cuts*) and Cantor (via *Cauchy sequences*) independently showed how to construct the real numbers out of the rational numbers. The rationals are easily built from the integers, the integers from the natural numbers, and in 1889 Giuseppe Peano wrote down axioms characterizing the natural numbers.

In this way all of analysis was reduced to the natural numbers—and the natural numbers, Dedekind and Gottlob Frege hoped, could in turn be reduced to pure logic and the theory of sets. This chapter retraces exactly that chain, in the forward order of logic rather than the historical order of discovery: sets, then $\mathbb{N}$, then $\mathbb{Z}$, then $\mathbb{Q}$, then $\mathbb{R}$.

### Paradoxes at the Bottom: Sets, Russell, and the Need for Formal Foundations

A reduction is only as solid as its bottom layer. Cantor's set theory (1870s–1890s) offered a breathtakingly simple foundation—a set is *any* collection of objects sharing a describable property—and with it Cantor discovered that infinite sets come in different sizes. Frege spent decades building arithmetic out of logic on essentially this principle. In 1901 Bertrand Russell found that the principle contradicts itself: the "set of all sets that are not members of themselves" both is and is not a member of itself. Russell wrote to Frege in 1902, just as the second volume of Frege's *Grundgesetze der Arithmetik* was going to press, and Frege added an appendix admitting that the foundation of his life's work had collapsed. We will dissect this paradox precisely in Section 0.1.

The situation was now genuinely alarming. The geometric foundations of analysis had been replaced by arithmetic ones; arithmetic had been reduced to sets; and the intuitive notion of a set turned out to be *inconsistent*. Worse, a theory that proves even one contradiction proves *every* statement whatsoever (you will see why from the truth table of $\implies$ in Section 0.1), so in an inconsistent theory the word "proof" becomes worthless. Mathematicians were therefore forced to answer, with brand-new precision:

- Exactly **which statements count as axioms**, and which sets are guaranteed to exist?
- Exactly **what counts as a valid step** in a proof?
- What does it mean for a statement to be **true**, and how is truth related to being **provable**?
- Can we be sure that our axioms are **consistent**?

The work that answered these questions—by David Hilbert, Ernst Zermelo, Abraham Fraenkel, Thoralf Skolem, Kurt Gödel, Alfred Tarski, and many others between about 1900 and 1940—produced the picture of mathematics that is standard today. Let us look at that picture.

## The Modern Axiomatic Method

### Axioms as Specifications, Not Dogmas

In his *Foundations of Geometry* (1899), Hilbert rewrote Euclidean geometry with a complete and careful list of axioms, and he is reported to have remarked that one must always be able to say *"tables, chairs, and beer mugs"* in place of *"points, lines, and planes"*. What he meant is that a proof must never depend on what the undefined words *mean*—only on what the axioms *say about them*. If you replaced every occurrence of "point" with "beer mug" and the axioms still held for your beer mugs, every theorem would hold for them too.

This is the decisive shift in the meaning of the word *axiom*. A modern axiom system is not a list of claims about cosmic reality; it is a **specification**, much like the rules of chess or the interface of a piece of software. When we write down the axioms of a group, or of an ordered field, we are not asserting that they are "true". We are saying: *"Let us study every structure, whatever it happens to be made of, that satisfies these conditions."* A theorem is then a guarantee: **any** structure meeting the specification necessarily has the property stated in the theorem.

> [!note] Why Axioms Do Not Make Mathematics Circular
> The worry *"theorems are true because they follow from the axioms, and the axioms are true because we assume them"* dissolves once we notice that modern mathematics never claims that axioms are true in any absolute sense. Every theorem is, at bottom, a **conditional** statement:
> $$
> \text{If a structure satisfies the axioms } \mathcal{A}, \text{ then it satisfies the statement } \varphi.
> $$
> Nothing is assumed in order to prove itself. Whether some *particular* structure actually satisfies the axioms is a separate question—sometimes settled by constructing that structure, as we shall do for $\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$, and $\mathbb{R}$ in this chapter, and sometimes settled by experiment, when we apply mathematics to the physical world.

### Formal Systems: Language, Axioms, and Rules of Inference

To make *"what counts as a proof"* completely precise, modern logic packages a mathematical theory into a **formal system**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Formal System and Formal Proof
> A **formal system** (or **formal theory**) consists of three ingredients:
> 1. A **formal language**: a fixed alphabet of symbols—variables, logical symbols such as $\neg, \land, \implies, \forall, \exists, =$, and non-logical symbols such as $\in$ or $+$—together with precise grammatical rules determining which finite strings of symbols are **well-formed formulas**.
> 2. A collection of **axioms**: designated well-formed formulas that may be written down at any point of a proof.
> 3. A collection of **rules of inference**: purely mechanical rules for producing a new formula from formulas already obtained—for example **modus ponens**, which from $P$ and $P \implies Q$ produces $Q$.
>
> A **formal proof** of a formula $\varphi$ from a collection of axioms $\Gamma$ is a finite list of formulas ending in $\varphi$, in which every entry is either an axiom (a member of $\Gamma$ or a basic logical axiom) or follows from earlier entries by a rule of inference. If such a proof exists, we say that $\varphi$ is **provable** from $\Gamma$, or that $\varphi$ is a **theorem** of $\Gamma$, and we write
> $$
> \Gamma \vdash \varphi
> $$

The striking feature of this definition is that it uses **no notion of meaning or truth whatsoever**. Checking a formal proof is like checking that a chess game was played legally: you only verify that each move obeys the rules. A computer that has no idea what a "set" or a "limit" is can nevertheless verify, with complete reliability, that a list of strings is a valid proof. This is what makes mathematical proof *objective*: a disagreement about whether something has been proved can always, in principle, be settled mechanically.

The particular framework used throughout this course—and by the overwhelming majority of mathematicians—is **first-order predicate logic** whose only non-logical symbol is $\in$, together with the axioms of **Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory**, usually supplemented by the **Axiom of Choice** (the combination is called **ZFC**). Section 0.1 develops the logic and the ZF axioms, and Section 0.7 introduces the Axiom of Choice.

### Two Floors: Syntax and Semantics

If proofs are meaningless symbol games, where does *truth* come in? The answer of modern logic is that mathematics lives on two separate floors, and nearly all confusion between "true" and "provable" comes from mixing them up:

- **Syntax** is the floor of *proof*. It treats formulas as strings of symbols and asks what can be derived from what. Its central notion is provability, $\Gamma \vdash \varphi$.
- **Semantics** is the floor of *meaning*. It asks what formulas *say about* mathematical structures. Its central notion is truth in a structure.

On its own, a formula such as $\exists x \, (x + x = x)$ is neither true nor false—it is just a string of symbols. It acquires a truth value only once we **interpret** it: choose a universe of objects for the variable $x$ to range over, and decide which operation the symbol $+$ stands for. Such an interpretation is called a **structure**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Structure, Truth in a Structure, Model, and Logical Consequence
> 1. A **structure** $\mathcal{M}$ for a formal language consists of a non-empty universe $M$ of objects together with an interpretation of each non-logical symbol as a specific element, operation, or relation on $M$.
> 2. Following Alfred Tarski (1933), a sentence $\varphi$ is **true in $\mathcal{M}$**, written $\mathcal{M} \vDash \varphi$, when what $\varphi$ asserts actually holds of the objects of $M$. This is defined by recursion on the build-up of $\varphi$; for instance, $\mathcal{M} \vDash \neg \psi$ precisely when $\mathcal{M} \nvDash \psi$; $\mathcal{M} \vDash \psi \land \chi$ precisely when both $\mathcal{M} \vDash \psi$ and $\mathcal{M} \vDash \chi$; and $\mathcal{M} \vDash \exists x \, \psi(x)$ precisely when at least one object $a \in M$ satisfies $\psi$.
> 3. If $\mathcal{M} \vDash \varphi$ for every sentence $\varphi$ in a collection $\Gamma$, then $\mathcal{M}$ is called a **model** of $\Gamma$.
> 4. A sentence $\varphi$ is a **logical consequence** of $\Gamma$, written $\Gamma \vDash \varphi$, if $\varphi$ is true in **every** model of $\Gamma$.

Let us see the definition in action on $\exists x \, (x + x = x)$. Interpreted in the real numbers with ordinary addition, the sentence is **true**, because the object $0$ satisfies $0 + 0 = 0$. Interpreted in the strictly positive integers with ordinary addition, the same string is **false**, because $x + x$ is strictly larger than $x$ for every positive integer $x$. Nothing about the string changed; only the structure did.

In other words, Tarski's insight is that **truth is not something the axioms hand out**—truth is a *relationship* between a sentence and a structure. A sentence is true in a structure exactly when the structure really is the way the sentence says it is. You will notice that the recursive clauses for $\neg$ and $\land$ above are nothing but the truth tables you will meet at the start of Section 0.1: truth tables are simply the rules telling us how the truth of a compound sentence is computed from the truth of its parts.

### Soundness and Completeness: Where Proof Meets Truth

We now have two completely different notions: $\Gamma \vdash \varphi$ (there is a finite, mechanically checkable derivation) and $\Gamma \vDash \varphi$ (the sentence holds in every structure satisfying the axioms). The first is about symbol-pushing, the second about meaning. Is there any reason to expect them to agree? Remarkably, for first-order logic, there is.

> [!theorem] Soundness and Gödel's Completeness Theorem (1929)
> For first-order logic, for every collection of sentences $\Gamma$ and every sentence $\varphi$:
> 1. **Soundness:** If $\Gamma \vdash \varphi$, then $\Gamma \vDash \varphi$. That is, formal proofs never lead from axioms to a sentence that fails in some model of those axioms.
> 2. **Completeness:** If $\Gamma \vDash \varphi$, then $\Gamma \vdash \varphi$. That is, every sentence that is true in all models of $\Gamma$ has a formal proof from $\Gamma$.
>
> Together,
> $$
> \Gamma \vdash \varphi \iff \Gamma \vDash \varphi
> $$
> Equivalently, a collection of sentences $\Gamma$ is **consistent** (no contradiction is provable from it) if and only if $\Gamma$ has at least one model.

We will not prove these results—they belong to a course in mathematical logic—but their message is the key to the circularity puzzle. The tempting slogan *"true means being a logical consequence of the axioms"* is *almost* right, once it is phrased carefully: **"provable from the axioms" means exactly "true in every structure that satisfies the axioms."** It does *not* mean "true, period". The axioms carve out a class of structures, and the theorems are precisely the statements shared by all of them.

### Independence: Statements the Axioms Do Not Decide

Soundness has an immediate and illuminating consequence. Suppose a sentence $\varphi$ is true in one model of $\Gamma$ but false in another. Then $\varphi$ cannot be provable from $\Gamma$ (otherwise, by soundness, it would be true in every model), and by the same argument neither can $\neg \varphi$. Such a sentence is said to be **independent** of $\Gamma$.

Independence is not a defect—it is often the whole point. The axioms of a group describe a vast family of structures, and the commutative law $\forall x \, \forall y \, (x \cdot y = y \cdot x)$ holds in some of them (the integers under addition) and fails in others (the rearrangements of three objects under composition). So commutativity is independent of the group axioms, which is exactly why "abelian group" is a separate, useful notion. Likewise, Beltrami's models showed precisely that the parallel postulate is independent of Euclid's other axioms: Euclidean geometry is a model in which it holds, and hyperbolic geometry is a model in which it fails.

> [!warning] "Unprovable" Does Not Mean "False"
> If a statement $\varphi$ cannot be proved from $\Gamma$, it does not follow that $\varphi$ is false, nor that $\neg \varphi$ is provable. It may simply be that $\Gamma$ does not contain enough information to decide $\varphi$, in which case $\varphi$ is true in some models of $\Gamma$ and false in others.

### Gödel's Incompleteness Theorems: Truth Outruns Proof

Independence for groups is harmless, because nobody expects the group axioms to pin down a single group. But what about theories that are *meant* to describe one specific structure—like the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$? In the 1920s Hilbert proposed a grand program: formalize all of mathematics in a single formal system that (a) decides every statement, proving either it or its negation, and (b) can be proved consistent using only simple, unquestionable "finitary" reasoning. In 1931 the 25-year-old Kurt Gödel showed that both goals are unattainable.

> [!theorem] Gödel's Incompleteness Theorems (1931)
> Let $T$ be a consistent formal theory whose axioms can be listed by a mechanical procedure, and which is strong enough to express the basic arithmetic of the natural numbers (for instance Peano arithmetic, or ZFC).
> 1. **First Incompleteness Theorem:** There is a sentence $G$ such that neither $T \vdash G$ nor $T \vdash \neg G$. Moreover, $G$ can be taken to be a statement about natural numbers that is **true** in the standard natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$.
> 2. **Second Incompleteness Theorem:** $T$ cannot prove the sentence expressing its own consistency.

Does this contradict the Completeness Theorem? Not at all, and seeing why sharpens everything we have said. Completeness says that provable means *true in every model*. Gödel's sentence $G$ is true in the intended model $\mathbb{N}$, but—because it is unprovable—there must exist other, "nonstandard" models of $T$ in which $G$ is false. So no mechanically listable set of first-order axioms can capture *all* the truths about $\mathbb{N}$: **truth in the intended structure is strictly larger than provability**. Set theory has its own famous examples: the Continuum Hypothesis, which asks whether there is an infinite size strictly between that of $\mathbb{N}$ and that of $\mathbb{R}$, was shown by Gödel (1938) and Paul Cohen (1963) to be independent of ZFC.

> [!warning] What Gödel's Theorems Do *Not* Say
> Gödel's theorems do not say that mathematics is inconsistent, unreliable, or "merely subjective". Every theorem that has been proved remains proved, and none of the theorems of real analysis you will meet is affected in the slightest. What the incompleteness theorems say is that no single formal system can capture every mathematical truth, and that the consistency of a sufficiently strong foundation cannot be established from within that foundation itself.

## So What Does "True" Mean in Mathematics?

### Escaping the Circle: Object Language and Metalanguage

A sharp reader may now spot one last circle. To *define* formal systems we used words like "finite list", "symbol", "every model", and even counted the steps of a proof. Are we not using mathematics to lay the foundations of mathematics?

The resolution is to distinguish two levels of language. The **object language** is the formal system under study—the strings of symbols, axioms, and rules. The **metalanguage** is the language we use to *talk about* that formal system: here, ordinary English together with a small amount of very concrete reasoning about finite strings of symbols. The regress of justification has to stop somewhere, and in modern mathematics it stops at this minimal core: the ability to recognize symbols, write them one after another, and check that finitely many rule applications were carried out correctly—the same skill you use to confirm that a chess game was played legally. This core is not derived from anything; it is so concrete and so easily checked that no one seriously doubts it. That is not circularity—it is a *starting point*. Every system of knowledge has to start somewhere, and modern mathematics has made its starting point as small, explicit, and checkable as possible.

This distinction also explains a remark you will read at the very start of Section 0.1: *inside* the formal theory, no number systems exist yet, and we may not use arithmetic in our proofs—even though, in the metalanguage, we freely speak of "the first axiom" or "the third line of a proof". The numbers of the metalanguage are bookkeeping labels for finitely many symbols on a page; the number systems $\mathbb{N}, \mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{R}$ of the object theory are objects that must be *constructed* from the axioms.

### Consistency: The One Assumption We Cannot Remove

Where does all this leave real analysis? Every theorem in this course will, in principle, be a theorem of ZFC: a statement $\varphi$ with $\text{ZFC} \vdash \varphi$. In particular, we will *prove* inside ZF that a complete ordered field exists, rather than merely hoping that one does. The only remaining article of faith is that **ZFC is consistent**—and by the Second Incompleteness Theorem, this cannot be proved within ZFC itself (unless ZFC is in fact inconsistent).

Why, then, do mathematicians trust it? Partly because of more than a century of intensive use by thousands of mathematicians without a contradiction ever surfacing, and partly because the ZFC axioms are true descriptions of a clear intuitive picture—a universe of sets built up in stages, each stage collecting all subsets of what came before—in which no contradiction is in sight. Mathematicians therefore treat the consistency of ZFC much as physicists treat a theory that has survived every experiment: not as a certainty proved from nothing, but as an extraordinarily well-tested working hypothesis.

### What a Theorem of Real Analysis Really Says

Here is how to keep your philosophical footing while reading the rest of this course. The real line $\mathbb{R}$ is a specific mathematical structure, and its axioms—the field axioms, the order axioms, and the Least Upper Bound Property—form its "character sheet": they specify what any object deserving the name "real number system" must satisfy. When a theorem such as the Bolzano–Weierstrass Theorem asserts that *every bounded sequence of real numbers has a convergent subsequence*, it is really saying:

$$
\text{In every complete ordered field, every bounded sequence has a convergent subsequence.}
$$

In Section 0.5 we will show, within set theory, that complete ordered fields exist (by two different constructions) and that any two of them are isomorphic—they differ only in the names of their elements. That is what justifies speaking of *the* real numbers. So "true in real analysis" means: holding in every complete ordered field, as established by a proof from these axioms using the logical and set-theoretic tools that ZFC provides.

### Mathematics in Practice Today

None of this means that working mathematicians write formal proofs—a fully formal derivation of even a modest theorem would run to thousands of lines. Instead they write **rigorous informal proofs**: arguments in precise language and notation, detailed enough that any competent reader could, in principle, expand each step into a formal derivation. That expandability is the real standard behind the word "proof". Increasingly, the expansion is actually carried out: proof assistants such as Lean, Coq (now Rocq), and Isabelle check every step of a proof mechanically, and major results—such as Thomas Hales's proof of the Kepler conjecture on sphere packing, completed by the Flyspeck project in 2014—have been fully machine-verified.

For you, the practical consequences are a handful of habits that this course will drill relentlessly: define every term precisely; state every hypothesis explicitly; justify every step by a definition, an axiom, or a previously proved theorem; and never accept *"it is obvious from the picture"* as a proof. Pictures and intuition remain indispensable for *discovering* what might be true—but only proofs decide what *is* a theorem.

## Roadmap of This Chapter

With this picture in mind, the architecture of the chapter should now feel inevitable. We begin at the bottom of the foundational ladder and climb, constructing each number system from the one below it and verifying its axioms as theorems rather than assumptions:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    L["First-Order Logic"] --> S["ZF Set Theory"]
    S --> N["Natural Numbers N"]
    N --> Z["Integers Z"]
    Z --> Q["Rationals Q"]
    Q --> R["Real Numbers R"]
    R --> A["Real Analysis"]
```

- **Section 0.1** fixes the language and rules of inference (first-order logic) and the axioms of the universe of sets (ZF), and builds relations and functions out of sets.
- **Section 0.2** constructs the natural numbers inside set theory, justifies definition by recursion, and develops arithmetic and order on $\mathbb{N}$.
- **Section 0.3** introduces equivalence relations and quotients and uses them to build the integers $\mathbb{Z}$.
- **Section 0.4** builds the rationals $\mathbb{Q}$, abstracts the notion of an ordered field, and exposes the "gaps" in $\mathbb{Q}$.
- **Section 0.5** fills those gaps by constructing $\mathbb{R}$ in two ways and proves that the complete ordered field is unique up to isomorphism.
- **Section 0.6** develops the everyday toolkit of $\mathbb{R}$: supremum calculus, Archimedean consequences, roots, and the extended reals.
- **Section 0.7** studies sizes of infinite sets, proves that $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable, and introduces the Axiom of Choice.

> [!summary] Summary: The Modern Picture of Mathematics
> - Classical axioms were "self-evident truths about reality"; non-Euclidean geometry, the crisis in calculus, and the paradoxes of set theory destroyed that view.
> - Modern axioms are **specifications** of a class of structures; every theorem is a conditional guarantee about all structures meeting the specification.
> - **Provability** ($\Gamma \vdash \varphi$) is a purely mechanical, syntactic notion; **truth** ($\mathcal{M} \vDash \varphi$) is a semantic relationship between a sentence and a structure.
> - By **soundness and completeness**, being provable from $\Gamma$ is the same as being true in every model of $\Gamma$; a statement true in some models and false in others is **independent** of $\Gamma$.
> - By **Gödel's incompleteness theorems**, truth in an intended structure such as $\mathbb{N}$ outruns provability in any reasonable formal system, and such a system cannot prove its own consistency.
> - The apparent circularity is broken by separating the formal **object language** from the informal **metalanguage**, whose concrete symbol-checking core is our starting point; the consistency of ZFC is a well-tested working hypothesis.

---

# 0.1 Mathematical Logic, Proof Architecture, and Axiomatic Set Theory

In the introduction we saw that modern mathematics consists of a formal language, a list of axioms, and mechanical rules of inference, with "truth" understood as a relationship between sentences and the structures they describe. This section builds those ingredients concretely. Before we can speak meaningfully of sequences, limits, continuity, or even the real numbers themselves, we must establish the grammar of mathematical reasoning and the universe of objects within which all mathematical structures reside. Notice where we are standing: at the absolute beginning of mathematics. At this foundational stage, **no number systems—not even the natural numbers—exist yet** inside our theory. We cannot count elements, we cannot index steps by integers, and we cannot appeal to arithmetic intuition in our proofs. (As explained in the introduction, the informal metalanguage may still label "the first axiom" or "the third line of a proof"; those are bookkeeping labels, not objects of the theory.) Every concept we build in this section must stand purely on the pillars of formal logic and axiomatic set theory.

Why insist on such austere rigor? In elementary mathematics, one can often rely on geometric pictures or numerical experimentation to decide whether a claim seems plausible. Real analysis, however, routinely confronts us with phenomena where naive intuition collapses completely: functions that are continuous everywhere yet differentiable nowhere, sets that are uncountably large yet occupy zero length, and infinite processes whose behavior depends sensitively on the exact order in which limits are taken. To navigate this landscape without falling into contradiction or circularity, we need the two components of a formal system described in the introduction: an unambiguous symbolic language with precise rules of inference—**first-order predicate logic**—and a paradox-free list of axioms for collections of objects—**Zermelo–Fraenkel (ZF) set theory**.

When the definition below says that a proposition is "unambiguously true or false", read it in the semantic sense of the introduction: a truth value is something a statement has *relative to the situation or structure it describes*. The truth tables that follow do not tell us whether any particular atomic statement is true; they tell us how the truth value of a compound statement is determined by the truth values of its parts—which is exactly why logical laws such as tautologies hold no matter what the atomic statements mean.

## Propositional and First-Order Predicate Logic for Analysis

### Propositions, Logical Connectives, and Truth Tables

Mathematical discourse is built out of declarative statements whose truth or falsity is unambiguous.

> [!abstract] Definition: Proposition and Logical Connectives
> A **proposition** (or **statement**) is a declarative sentence $P$ that is unambiguously either **true** ($\text{T}$) or **false** ($\text{F}$), but never both. Given propositions $P$ and $Q$, we form compound propositions using five fundamental **logical connectives**:
> 1. **Negation ($\neg P$):** Read *"not $P$"*, which reverses the truth value of $P$.
> 2. **Conjunction ($P \land Q$):** Read *"$P$ and $Q$"*, which is true precisely when both $P$ and $Q$ are true.
> 3. **Disjunction ($P \lor Q$):** Read *"$P$ or $Q$"*, which is the **inclusive or**—true whenever at least one of $P$ or $Q$ is true.
> 4. **Material Implication ($P \implies Q$):** Read *"if $P$, then $Q$"* or *"$P$ implies $Q$"*, where $P$ is the **hypothesis** (or **antecedent**) and $Q$ is the **conclusion** (or **consequent**). It is false only when $P$ is true and $Q$ is false.
> 5. **Biconditional ($P \iff Q$):** Read *"$P$ if and only if $Q$"*, which is true precisely when $P$ and $Q$ have the same truth value.

The exact semantics of these five connectives are completely determined by their truth tables across all combinations of truth values of $P$ and $Q$:

| $P$ | $Q$ | $\neg P$ | $P \land Q$ | $P \lor Q$ | $P \implies Q$ | $P \iff Q$ |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ |
| $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ |
| $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ |
| $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ |

Two compound propositional formulas are said to be **logically equivalent** if they share the exact same truth value in every row of their joint truth table. When two statements $A$ and $B$ are logically equivalent, the biconditional $A \iff B$ is a **tautology**—a compound proposition that is true regardless of the truth values of its constituent atomic propositions. Conversely, a proposition that is false in every row of its truth table (such as $P \land \neg P$) is called a **contradiction**.

### Material Implication and Vacuous Truth

When you inspect the column for $P \implies Q$ in the truth table above, the first two rows match everyday intuition: if a promise $P \implies Q$ is made and the condition $P$ holds, the promise is kept if $Q$ holds ($\text{T} \implies \text{T}$ is $\text{T}$) and broken if $Q$ fails ($\text{T} \implies \text{F}$ is $\text{F}$). Why, however, do mathematicians insist that $P \implies Q$ is **true** whenever the hypothesis $P$ is false ($\text{F} \implies \text{T}$ and $\text{F} \implies \text{F}$ are both $\text{T}$)?

Think of $P \implies Q$ as a contract: *"If the trigger $P$ occurs, then the outcome $Q$ is guaranteed."* If the trigger $P$ never occurs, has the contract been violated? No! A contract can only be broken if its condition is met and its guarantee fails to materialize. Formally, this means $P \implies Q$ asserts solely that it is impossible to have $P$ true and $Q$ false simultaneously:
$$
(P \implies Q) \iff \neg(P \land \neg Q) \iff (\neg P \lor Q) \quad \quad \quad \quad \text{(Tautologies!)}
$$
Whenever an implication $P \implies Q$ holds because its antecedent $P$ is false, we say that $P \implies Q$ is **vacuously true**.

> [!note] Why Vacuous Truth is Indispensable in Analysis
> Vacuous truth is not an arbitrary convention; without it, universal theorems in mathematics would require endless awkward exceptions. For instance, once we introduce sets below, we will define a set $A$ to be a subset of $B$ ($A \subseteq B$) if for every object $x$, the implication $x \in A \implies x \in B$ holds. Because $x \in \emptyset$ is always false for the empty set $\emptyset$, the implication $x \in \emptyset \implies x \in B$ is vacuously true for every $x$. This immediately guarantees that $\emptyset \subseteq B$ for every set $B$ without needing a separate special case!

### Contrapositive, Converse, and Inverse

Given an implication $P \implies Q$, there are three related conditional statements obtained by permuting or negating the antecedent and consequent:

> [!abstract] Definition: Contrapositive, Converse, and Inverse
> Let $P$ and $Q$ be propositions. Relative to the implication $P \implies Q$:
> 1. The **contrapositive** is the implication $\neg Q \implies \neg P$.
> 2. The **converse** is the implication $Q \implies P$.
> 3. The **inverse** is the implication $\neg P \implies \neg Q$.

Let us compare their truth values directly against $P \implies Q$:

| $P$ | $Q$ | $\neg P$ | $\neg Q$ | $P \implies Q$ | Contrapositive ($\neg Q \implies \neg P$) | Converse ($Q \implies P$) | Inverse ($\neg P \implies \neg Q$) |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ |
| $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ |
| $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ |
| $\text{F}$ | $\text{F}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ | $\text{T}$ |

Observe that the columns for $P \implies Q$ and $\neg Q \implies \neg P$ are identical, whereas the columns for $Q \implies P$ and $\neg P \implies \neg Q$ match each other (since the inverse is the contrapositive of the converse) but differ from $P \implies Q$ whenever $P$ and $Q$ have opposite truth values.

> [!warning] Never Confuse the Converse with the Contrapositive
> Assuming that $Q \implies P$ follows from $P \implies Q$ is the logical fallacy of **affirming the consequent**, and assuming $\neg P \implies \neg Q$ follows from $P \implies Q$ is the fallacy of **denying the antecedent**. By contrast, the contrapositive $\neg Q \implies \neg P$ is logically identical to $P \implies Q$: proving one automatically proves the other. Meanwhile, the biconditional $P \iff Q$ is logically equivalent to the conjunction of the original implication and its converse:
> $$
> (P \iff Q) \iff ((P \implies Q) \land (Q \implies P))
> $$

### First-Order Predicates, Quantifiers, and Bound vs. Free Variables

Propositional logic treats whole sentences as indivisible atoms, which is too coarse for mathematics. Most mathematical statements depend on variables ranging over a domain of objects. A declarative sentence $P(x)$ containing a variable $x$ (or $P(x, y)$ containing several variables) is called a **predicate** (or **open sentence** or **propositional function**). By itself, a predicate $P(x)$ has no truth value until a specific object is substituted for $x$, or until $x$ is bound by a **quantifier**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Universal and Existential Quantifiers
> Let $P(x)$ be a predicate depending on a variable $x$, and let $D$ be a collection (or domain) of objects.
> 1. The **universal quantification** $\forall x \in D, P(x)$ (read *"for all $x$ in $D$, $P(x)$ holds"*) is an abbreviation for the unrestricted logical statement
>    $$
>    \forall x \, (x \in D \implies P(x))
>    $$
>    It is true if and only if $P(x)$ is true for every object $x$ belonging to $D$.
> 2. The **existential quantification** $\exists x \in D, P(x)$ (read *"there exists at least one $x$ in $D$ such that $P(x)$ holds"*) is an abbreviation for the unrestricted logical statement
>    $$
>    \exists x \, (x \in D \land P(x))
>    $$
>    It is true if and only if there is at least one object $x$ belonging to $D$ for which $P(x)$ is true.

Notice the crucial structural difference between how domain restriction works for $\forall$ versus $\exists$:
- Universal quantification over $D$ translates to an **implication** ($x \in D \implies P(x)$). Therefore, if $D = \emptyset$ (so $x \in \emptyset$ is false for every $x$), the implication $x \in \emptyset \implies P(x)$ is vacuously true for every $x$. Consequently, **$\forall x \in \emptyset, P(x)$ is always vacuously true**, regardless of the predicate $P(x)$!
- Existential quantification over $D$ translates to a **conjunction** ($x \in D \land P(x)$). If $D = \emptyset$, the first conjunct $x \in \emptyset$ is always false, making $x \in \emptyset \land P(x)$ false for every $x$. Consequently, **$\exists x \in \emptyset, P(x)$ is always false**, regardless of the predicate $P(x)$!

When a variable $x$ falls within the scope of a quantifier $\forall x$ or $\exists x$, we say that $x$ is a **bound variable** (or **dummy variable**); otherwise, $x$ is a **free variable**. A formula with no free variables is a closed **sentence** (a genuine proposition with a definite truth value), whereas a formula with one or more free variables remains an open predicate whose truth depends on the values assigned to those free variables. Because a bound variable is merely a placeholder ranging over its domain, renaming it consistently throughout its scope does not alter the meaning of the statement: $\forall x \in D, P(x)$ means the exact same thing as $\forall t \in D, P(t)$.

### The Order of Quantifiers

When a predicate $P(x, y)$ involves two or more variables, the order in which quantifiers are placed in front of $P(x, y)$ can drastically change the meaning of the statement. Suppose $A$ and $B$ are non-empty domains. If both quantifiers are of the same type, their order commutes effortlessly:
- $\forall x \in A \, \forall y \in B, P(x, y) \iff \forall y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y)$ (both assert that $P(x, y)$ holds for every choice of $x \in A$ and $y \in B$).
- $\exists x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y) \iff \exists y \in B \, \exists x \in A, P(x, y)$ (both assert that at least one pair of objects $x \in A$ and $y \in B$ makes $P(x, y)$ true).

When universal and existential quantifiers are **mixed**, however, commuting them changes who gets to depend on whom! Compare the two statements:
1. $\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y)$: First, an arbitrary $x \in A$ is handed to us; *after* seeing $x$, we are allowed to choose a witness $y \in B$ tailored specifically to that $x$ (so $y = y(x)$ may vary with $x$).
2. $\exists y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y)$: We must choose a **single, uniform** witness $y \in B$ *up front*, before $x$ is specified, and that one fixed $y$ must work simultaneously for every $x \in A$.

> [!note] Preview of Quantifier Order in Real Analysis
> While we have not yet constructed the real numbers, you can already see why this distinction is the heartbeat of real analysis. Later in the course, the difference between **pointwise convergence** and **uniform convergence**, or between **continuity** and **uniform continuity**, will boil down entirely to whether a tolerance witness $\delta$ is chosen *after* the point $x$ is fixed ($\forall \varepsilon \, \forall x \, \exists \delta$, allowing $\delta$ to depend on $x$) or *before* $x$ is chosen ($\forall \varepsilon \, \exists \delta \, \forall x$, forcing a single $\delta$ to work across all $x$ simultaneously).

The logical relationship between the two mixed quantifier orders is one-way: having a single uniform witness always guarantees a point-by-point witness, whereas the converse fails in general.

> [!theorem] Theorem: One-Way Implication for Mixed Quantifiers
> For any domains $A, B$ and any binary predicate $P(x, y)$, the uniform existential-universal statement implies the pointwise universal-existential statement:
> $$
> (\exists y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y)) \implies (\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y))
> $$
> However, the converse implication $(\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y)) \implies (\exists y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y))$ does not hold in general.

**Proof:**
To prove the forward implication, assume the hypothesis $\exists y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y)$ holds. By the meaning of the existential quantifier, we may fix a specific witness $y_0 \in B$ such that the statement $\forall x \in A, P(x, y_0)$ is true. We must show that $\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y)$ holds. To do so, let $x \in A$ be an arbitrary object in $A$. Since $\forall x \in A, P(x, y_0)$ holds, substituting our particular $x$ yields that $P(x, y_0)$ is true. Because $y_0 \in B$ and $P(x, y_0)$ holds, $y = y_0$ serves as a valid existential witness in $B$ for this $x$, meaning $\exists y \in B, P(x, y)$ is true. Since $x \in A$ was arbitrary, we conclude that $\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y)$.

To see why the converse fails in general, let $A = B$ be any domain containing at least two distinct objects $u$ and $v$ with $u \neq v$, and let $P(x, y)$ be the predicate $x = y$. Then for every $x \in A$, we can choose $y = x \in A$ so that $x = y$ holds; thus $\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in A, (x = y)$ is true. On the other hand, if $\exists y \in A \, \forall x \in A, (x = y)$ were true, there would exist a single element $y_0 \in A$ equal to every element of $A$. In particular, we would have $u = y_0$ and $v = y_0$, forcing $u = v$, which contradicts $u \neq v$. Hence $\exists y \in A \, \forall x \in A, (x = y)$ is false. $\blacksquare$

### Systematic Rules for Negating Quantified Statements

In analysis, showing that a property fails (for instance, proving that a sequence diverges or a function is discontinuous) requires negating a deeply nested quantified definition. Doing this informally in English is a notorious source of logical errors; doing it symbolically is completely mechanical.

First, recall the propositional negation rules (**De Morgan's Laws for Logic**, **Double Negation**, and **Negation of Implication**), all of which are immediately verified by checking truth tables:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\neg(\neg P) &\iff P \\
\neg(P \land Q) &\iff (\neg P) \lor (\neg Q) \\
\neg(P \lor Q) &\iff (\neg P) \land (\neg Q) \\
\neg(P \implies Q) &\iff P \land (\neg Q)
\end{aligned}
$$
Pay special attention to the fourth rule: **the negation of an implication $P \implies Q$ is NOT an implication—it is the conjunction $P \land \neg Q$!** Saying that $P \implies Q$ fails means that the hypothesis $P$ actually holds while the conclusion $Q$ simultaneously fails.

Next, what happens when we push a negation symbol $\neg$ past a quantifier over a domain $D$?
- To say that $\forall x \in D, P(x)$ is false means that $P(x)$ does not hold for every $x \in D$; hence there must be at least one counterexample $x \in D$ where $P(x)$ fails. Notice how our formal domain expansion confirms this via $\neg(A \implies B) \iff A \land \neg B$:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  \neg(\forall x \in D, P(x)) &\iff \neg(\forall x \, (x \in D \implies P(x))) \\
  &\iff \exists x \, \neg(x \in D \implies P(x)) \\
  &\iff \exists x \, (x \in D \land \neg P(x)) \\
  &\iff \exists x \in D, \neg P(x)
  \end{aligned}
  $$
- Dually, to say that $\exists x \in D, P(x)$ is false means that no element of $D$ satisfies $P(x)$, so every $x \in D$ satisfies $\neg P(x)$:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  \neg(\exists x \in D, P(x)) &\iff \neg(\exists x \, (x \in D \land P(x))) \\
  &\iff \forall x \, \neg(x \in D \land P(x)) \\
  &\iff \forall x \, (x \notin D \lor \neg P(x)) \\
  &\iff \forall x \, (x \in D \implies \neg P(x)) \\
  &\iff \forall x \in D, \neg P(x)
  \end{aligned}
  $$

> [!theorem] De Morgan's Laws for Quantifiers and Nested Negation Algorithm
> For any domain $D$ and predicate $P(x)$:
> $$
> \neg(\forall x \in D, P(x)) \iff \exists x \in D, \neg P(x) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \neg(\exists x \in D, P(x)) \iff \forall x \in D, \neg P(x)
> $$
> Notice that the domain membership $x \in D$ **never** gets negated to $x \notin D$ when flipping $\forall x \in D$ and $\exists x \in D$. Consequently, to negate any chain of quantifiers followed by an innermost predicate, we simply:
> 1. Flip every $\forall$ to $\exists$ and every $\exists$ to $\forall$ from left to right (keeping each variable's domain unchanged), and
> 2. Negate the innermost proposition using $\neg(P \implies Q) \iff P \land \neg Q$ and propositional De Morgan laws.

For example, consider a typical three-quantifier statement involving an implication:
$$
S \equiv \forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B \, \forall z \in C, (P(x, z) \implies Q(y, z))
$$
Pushing $\neg$ step-by-step from left to right yields:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\neg S &\iff \exists x \in A, \neg(\exists y \in B \, \forall z \in C, (P(x, z) \implies Q(y, z))) \\
&\iff \exists x \in A \, \forall y \in B, \neg(\forall z \in C, (P(x, z) \implies Q(y, z))) \\
&\iff \exists x \in A \, \forall y \in B \, \exists z \in C, \neg(P(x, z) \implies Q(y, z)) \\
&\iff \exists x \in A \, \forall y \in B \, \exists z \in C, (P(x, z) \land \neg Q(y, z))
\end{aligned}
$$

### The Uniqueness Quantifier and Standard Proof Architectures

Often in mathematics, we want to assert not merely that an object satisfying $P(x)$ exists, but that there is **one and only one** such object (for example, the uniqueness of a limit, an inverse, or the empty set).

> [!abstract] Definition: The Uniqueness Quantifier ($\exists!$)
> Given a domain $D$ and a predicate $P(x)$, the statement **$\exists! x \in D, P(x)$** (read *"there exists a unique $x \in D$ such that $P(x)$ holds"*) is defined as the conjunction of **existence** and **uniqueness**:
> $$
> \exists! x \in D, P(x) \quad :\Longleftrightarrow \quad (\exists x \in D, P(x)) \land (\forall u \in D \, \forall v \in D, (P(u) \land P(v) \implies u = v))
> $$
> Equivalently, in compressed form, it can be written as:
> $$
> \exists! x \in D, P(x) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \exists x \in D \, (P(x) \land \forall y \in D \, (P(y) \implies y = x))
> $$

Armed with these logical connectives and quantifiers, let us catalog the four primary proof architectures we will deploy throughout this text:
1. **Direct Proof of $P \implies Q$:** Assume the hypothesis $P$ is true, and construct a chain of valid logical deductions $P \implies R \implies S \implies \cdots \implies Q$ terminating at $Q$. (Notice that we never examine the case where $P$ is false, because $P \implies Q$ is already vacuously true in that case!)
2. **Proof by Contraposition of $P \implies Q$:** Exploiting the equivalence $(P \implies Q) \iff (\neg Q \implies \neg P)$, assume $\neg Q$ is true and deduce directly that $\neg P$ must be true. This is especially advantageous when $\neg Q$ gives you a concrete object or non-equality to manipulate (for instance, when proving that $x = y$ by showing that $x \neq y$ forces a violation of the hypothesis).
3. **Proof by Contradiction (*Reductio ad Absurdum*):**
   - To prove a standalone proposition $S$, assume its negation $\neg S$ and deduce a contradiction $C \land \neg C$ (a statement known to be false). Since $\neg S \implies (C \land \neg C)$ is true and its consequent $C \land \neg C$ is false, its antecedent $\neg S$ must be false, whence $S$ is true.
   - To prove an implication $P \implies Q$ by contradiction, assume the negation $\neg(P \implies Q)$, which is $P \land \neg Q$—that is, assume **both $P$ and $\neg Q$**—and derive a contradiction $C \land \neg C$.
4. **Existence and Uniqueness Proof of $\exists! x \in D, P(x)$:** Split the proof into two explicit parts following the definition of $\exists!$:
   - **Existence:** Construct (or deduce the existence of) a specific object $x \in D$ and verify that $P(x)$ holds.
   - **Uniqueness:** Let $u, v \in D$ be two arbitrary objects satisfying both $P(u)$ and $P(v)$, and prove using the properties of $P$ that $u = v$.

### Problems

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.9: Propositional Algebra Beyond the Truth Table
> Let $\bot$ denote any fixed contradiction (for instance $P \land \neg P$).
> 1. Prove that $\iff$ is commutative and **associative**: $((P \iff Q) \iff R) \iff (P \iff (Q \iff R))$ is a tautology. Then prove $\neg(P \iff Q) \iff (\neg P \iff Q)$.
> 2. Prove $(P \implies Q) \land (Q \implies R) \land (R \implies P) \iff (P \iff Q) \land (Q \iff R)$.
> 3. Prove **Peirce's law**, $((P \implies Q) \implies P) \implies P$, by a case split on the truth value of $P$ (not by a full table). Prove $P \lor Q \iff ((P \implies Q) \implies Q)$.
> 4. Show that $\neg$, $\land$, $\lor$ can each be expressed using only $\implies$ and $\bot$. Specifically, prove
> $$\neg P \iff (P \implies \bot), \quad P \lor Q \iff ((P \implies \bot) \implies Q), \quad P \land Q \iff ((P \implies (Q \implies \bot)) \implies \bot).$$

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.10: Distributing Quantifiers and Empty-Domain Traps
> Let $D$ be an arbitrary set (possibly empty), let $P(x), Q(x)$ be predicates, and let $R$ be a proposition in which $x$ does not occur.
> 1. Prove $\forall x \in D\,(P \land Q) \iff (\forall x \in D\, P) \land (\forall x \in D\, Q)$ and $\exists x \in D\,(P \lor Q) \iff (\exists x \in D\, P) \lor (\exists x \in D\, Q)$.
> 2. Prove the one-way implications $(\forall x \in D\,P) \lor (\forall x \in D\,Q) \implies \forall x \in D\,(P \lor Q)$ and $\exists x \in D\,(P \land Q) \implies (\exists x \in D\,P) \land (\exists x \in D\,Q)$. Give explicit counterexamples to both converses using only sets built from $\emptyset$.
> 3. Prove $\forall x \in D\,(P(x) \implies R) \iff \big((\exists x \in D\, P(x)) \implies R\big)$ for **every** $D$, including $D = \emptyset$.
> 4. Prove that $\exists x \in D\,(P(x) \implies R) \iff \big((\forall x \in D\, P(x)) \implies R\big)$ holds whenever $D \neq \emptyset$. For $D = \emptyset$, determine exactly for which truth values of $R$ the equivalence still holds.
> 5. (**Drinker's principle**) Prove that $\exists x \in D\,\big(P(x) \implies \forall y \in D\, P(y)\big)$ is true if and only if $D \neq \emptyset$.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.11: Exactly When Can Mixed Quantifiers Be Swapped?
> The text shows $\exists y \in B\,\forall x \in A\,P \implies \forall x \in A\,\exists y \in B\,P$ for all domains and predicates, and that the converse can fail. Let $C(A,B)$ be the assertion: *for every predicate $P(x,y)$ (parameters allowed), $\forall x \in A\,\exists y \in B\,P(x,y) \implies \exists y \in B\,\forall x \in A\,P(x,y)$.*
>
> Prove that $C(A,B)$ **fails** if and only if either
> - $A = \emptyset$ and $B = \emptyset$, or
> - $A$ contains two distinct elements and $B$ contains two distinct elements.
>
> (For the failure in the second case, build an explicit $P$ from two distinct elements of each set. For the other cases, argue directly from the meaning of the quantifiers, treating the cases $A = \emptyset$, $B = \emptyset$, "$A$ has exactly one element" and "$B$ has exactly one element" separately.)

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.12: The Uniqueness Quantifier, Its Negation, and Bijectivity
> 1. Prove that the two definitions of $\exists! x \in D,\,P(x)$ given in the text are logically equivalent.
> 2. Prove the negation rule
> $$\neg(\exists! x \in D,\,P(x)) \iff \big(\forall x \in D,\,\neg P(x)\big) \lor \big(\exists u \in D\,\exists v \in D,\,(P(u) \land P(v) \land u \neq v)\big).$$
> 3. Using parts 1–2 (and the contrapositive form of injectivity), prove directly from the definition $\forall b \in B\,\exists! a \in A,\,f(a)=b$ that $f$ is bijective if and only if it is injective and surjective. Then write out the fully expanded negation of "$f$ is bijective" with no $\neg$ in front of any quantifier, and use it to describe all ways a function can fail to be bijective.

## Why Naive Set Theory Fails and the Zermelo–Fraenkel (ZF) Axioms

### Naive Comprehension and Russell's Paradox

Having established the logical rules of inference, what are the mathematical objects that our variables $x, y, z$ actually range over? In modern mathematics, **every mathematical object is a set**. When Georg Cantor founded set theory in the late nineteenth century, a set was described intuitively as *"any collection into a whole of definite, distinguishable objects of our intuition or our thought."* Formally, this naive conception relies on a single sweeping principle known as **Unrestricted (or Naive) Comprehension**: for *any* well-formed predicate $\varphi(x)$ in first-order logic, there exists a set $\{x : \varphi(x)\}$ whose members are precisely the objects $x$ satisfying $\varphi(x)$:
$$
\forall y, (y \in \{x : \varphi(x)\} \iff \varphi(y))
$$

At first glance, Unrestricted Comprehension feels completely natural: if you can describe a property $\varphi(x)$, surely you can gather together all objects having that property into a set! In 1901, however, Bertrand Russell discovered that this innocent-looking principle destroys the consistency of mathematics in just three lines.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Russell's Paradox (Inconsistency of Unrestricted Comprehension)
> The Unrestricted Comprehension Principle leads directly to a logical contradiction. Specifically, if $\{x : \varphi(x)\}$ exists as a set for every predicate $\varphi(x)$, then defining $R := \{x : x \notin x\}$ yields the contradiction
> $$
> R \in R \iff R \notin R
> $$

**Proof:**
Consider the legitimate first-order predicate $\varphi(x) \equiv (x \notin x)$, which is an abbreviation for $\neg(x \in x)$. If Unrestricted Comprehension holds, we may form the set
$$
R := \{x : x \notin x\}
$$
whose defining property is that for every object $y$ in the universe of sets,
$$
y \in R \iff y \notin y
$$
Because $R$ is itself a set, we may instantiate the universally quantified variable $y$ with $y = R$ itself. Substituting $y = R$ into the equivalence above immediately produces
$$
R \in R \iff R \notin R
$$
which has the propositional form $P \iff \neg P$. However, a biconditional $P \iff \neg P$ is a contradiction: if $R \in R$ is true, the equivalence forces $R \notin R$ to be true, yielding $(R \in R) \land (R \notin R)$; conversely, if $R \notin R$ is true, the equivalence forces $R \in R$ to be true, again yielding $(R \in R) \land (R \notin R)$. Thus Unrestricted Comprehension is logically inconsistent. $\blacksquare$

What went wrong? Naive set theory permitted us to sweep over the entire universe of *all conceivable sets at once* to form arbitrarily massive collections—collections so large that treating them as single sets belonging to that same universe triggers self-referential collapse. To rescue mathematics, Ernst Zermelo (1908) and Abraham Fraenkel (1922) replaced Naive Comprehension with a carefully controlled list of axioms—the **Zermelo–Fraenkel (ZF) axioms**—that specify exactly which sets exist and how new sets may be constructed from already existing sets.

In ZF set theory, the universe of discourse consists **entirely of sets** (there are no "urelements" or non-set atoms: the elements of any set are themselves sets), and the binary relation $\in$ (read *"is an element of"* or *"belongs to"*) is an undefined primitive predicate. Let us build our set-theoretic toolkit axiom by axiom.

### Extensionality, the Empty Set, and Pairing

First, when are two sets $A$ and $B$ considered equal? A set is completely determined by its members—not by the order in which its members are listed, nor by how many times a member is mentioned, nor by the phrasing of the rule used to define it.

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom of Extensionality and Subsets
> **Axiom of Extensionality:** Two sets $A$ and $B$ are equal ($A = B$) if and only if they have the exact same elements:
> $$
> \forall A \, \forall B, (A = B \iff \forall x, (x \in A \iff x \in B))
> $$
> Furthermore, given sets $A$ and $B$, we say that $A$ is a **subset** of $B$, written $A \subseteq B$ (or $B \supseteq A$), if every element of $A$ is also an element of $B$:
> $$
> A \subseteq B \quad :\Longleftrightarrow \quad \forall x, (x \in A \implies x \in B)
> $$
> If $A \subseteq B$ and $A \neq B$, we say $A$ is a **proper subset** of $B$ and write $A \subsetneq B$.

Recalling our propositional equivalence $(P \iff Q) \iff ((P \implies Q) \land (Q \implies P))$, the Axiom of Extensionality translates into the single most common proof strategy in set theory—**double inclusion**:
$$
A = B \iff (A \subseteq B \land B \subseteq A)
$$
To prove that two sets $A$ and $B$ are equal, we either show directly that $x \in A \iff x \in B$ for an arbitrary $x$, or we split the argument into two steps: take an arbitrary $x \in A$ and deduce $x \in B$ (proving $A \subseteq B$), and then take an arbitrary $x \in B$ and deduce $x \in A$ (proving $B \subseteq A$).

Notice also that for any sets $A, B, C$, the subset relation is **reflexive** ($A \subseteq A$, since $x \in A \implies x \in A$ is a tautology), **antisymmetric** ($A \subseteq B \land B \subseteq A \implies A = B$ by Extensionality), and **transitive** ($A \subseteq B \land B \subseteq C \implies A \subseteq C$, since $(x \in A \implies x \in B) \land (x \in B \implies x \in C) \implies (x \in A \implies x \in C)$).

Extensionality tells us when two existing sets are equal, but it does not assert that any set actually exists! Our next axiom guarantees the existence of the simplest possible set: a set with no elements at all.

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom of the Empty Set
> **Axiom of the Empty Set:** There exists a set that contains no elements:
> $$
> \exists E \, \forall x, (x \notin E)
> $$

Before we are entitled to call such a set **the** empty set and assign it a dedicated symbol $\emptyset$, we must prove that there is only one such set!

> [!theorem] Proposition: Uniqueness of the Empty Set and Universal Subset Property
> There exists a unique set $\emptyset$ containing no elements. Moreover, for every set $A$, we have $\emptyset \subseteq A$.

**Proof:**
Existence is given directly by the Axiom of the Empty Set. To prove uniqueness, suppose $E$ and $E'$ are two sets satisfying $\forall x, (x \notin E)$ and $\forall x, (x \notin E')$. For any object $x$, the propositions $x \in E$ and $x \in E'$ are both false, so the biconditional $x \in E \iff x \in E'$ is true ($\text{F} \iff \text{F}$ is $\text{T}$). Hence $\forall x, (x \in E \iff x \in E')$, and the Axiom of Extensionality yields $E = E'$. We denote this unique empty set by $\emptyset$.

Finally, let $A$ be any set. For every $x$, the antecedent $x \in \emptyset$ is false, so the implication $x \in \emptyset \implies x \in A$ is vacuously true. Thus $\forall x, (x \in \emptyset \implies x \in A)$, which by definition means $\emptyset \subseteq A$. $\blacksquare$

Once we have at least one set ($\emptyset$), how do we build larger sets? The **Axiom of Pairing** allows us to take any two existing sets $a$ and $b$ and form a set whose only elements are $a$ and $b$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom of Pairing, Unordered Pairs, and Singletons
> **Axiom of Pairing:** For any two sets $a$ and $b$, there exists a set $P$ whose elements are precisely $a$ and $b$:
> $$
> \forall a \, \forall b \, \exists P \, \forall x, (x \in P \iff (x = a \lor x = b))
> $$
> By the Axiom of Extensionality, this set $P$ is unique; we denote it by $\{a, b\}$ and call it the **unordered pair** of $a$ and $b$. In the special case where $a = b$, the set $\{a, a\}$ satisfies $x \in \{a, a\} \iff (x = a \lor x = a) \iff x = a$; we denote $\{a, a\}$ simply by $\{a\}$ and call it the **singleton set** containing $a$.

> [!warning] Distinguish $\emptyset$ from $\{\emptyset\}$
> Because every object in ZF set theory is a set, sets can be elements of other sets! Applying the Axiom of Pairing to $a = b = \emptyset$ produces the singleton set $\{\emptyset\}$. Notice that $\emptyset \neq \{\emptyset\}$: the set $\emptyset$ has **no** elements, whereas $\{\emptyset\}$ is a non-empty set because $\emptyset \in \{\emptyset\}$. Think of $\emptyset$ as an empty box, and $\{\emptyset\}$ as a box containing an empty box inside it! By repeating this process, we can already manufacture distinct sets such as $\emptyset$, $\{\emptyset\}$, $\{\{\emptyset\}\}$, and $\{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$ without ever mentioning numbers.

### The Axiom Schema of Specification (Restricted Comprehension)

Now we come to Zermelo's masterstroke for resolving Russell's Paradox. Instead of allowing a property $\varphi(x)$ to carve a set out of thin air across the entire universe of sets, we are only permitted to use $\varphi(x)$ to filter elements from a **pre-existing set $A$**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom Schema of Specification (Restricted Comprehension / Separation)
> For every well-formed first-order formula $\varphi(x)$ (possibly depending on additional set parameters, but in which the variable $S$ does not occur free) and for every set $A$, there exists a set $S$ whose elements are precisely those elements $x \in A$ that satisfy $\varphi(x)$:
> $$
> \forall A \, \exists S \, \forall x, (x \in S \iff (x \in A \land \varphi(x)))
> $$
> By the Axiom of Extensionality, this set $S$ is uniquely determined by $A$ and $\varphi(x)$; we denote it by
> $$
> \{x \in A : \varphi(x)\}
> $$

Why is this called an **axiom schema** rather than a single axiom? Because in first-order logic we cannot quantify over predicates $\varphi$; instead, we have one distinct axiom for each first-order predicate $\varphi(x)$.

Let us immediately examine what happens when we try to run Russell's Paradox using the Axiom Schema of Specification. Instead of producing a logical contradiction, Russell's argument turns into a profound theorem proving that **there is no universal "set of all sets"**!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Resolution of Russell's Paradox and Non-Existence of a Universal Set
> 1. For every set $A$, the set $B := \{x \in A : x \notin x\}$ (which exists by the Axiom Schema of Specification) satisfies $B \notin A$.
> 2. Consequently, there does not exist a "universal set" $U$ containing all sets (that is, $\neg \exists U \, \forall x, (x \in U)$).

**Proof:**
1. Let $A$ be an arbitrary set. By the Axiom Schema of Specification applied to $A$ and the predicate $\varphi(x) \equiv (x \notin x)$, there exists a set $B = \{x \in A : x \notin x\}$ characterized by the equivalence
   $$
   \forall x, (x \in B \iff (x \in A \land x \notin x))
   $$
   Instantiating $x$ with $B$ itself gives
   $$
   B \in B \iff (B \in A \land B \notin B)
   $$
   We claim that $B \notin A$. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $B \in A$ is true. Then for the proposition $B \in A \land B \notin B$, its first conjunct $B \in A$ is true, so the conjunction simplifies to $B \notin B$. Substituting this into the biconditional above yields
   $$
   B \in B \iff B \notin B
   $$
   which is a contradiction! Therefore, our assumption $B \in A$ must be false, proving that $B \notin A$ (and consequently $B \notin B$ as well).
2. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a universal set $U$ such that $\forall x, (x \in U)$. Applying Part 1 to the set $A = U$ produces a legitimate set $B = \{x \in U : x \notin x\}$ satisfying $B \notin U$. However, since $B$ is a set, the universality property $\forall x, (x \in U)$ requires $B \in U$, yielding the contradiction $(B \in U) \land (B \notin U)$. Thus no universal set $U$ can exist. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Sets vs. Proper Classes
> Because no universal set exists, the totality of all sets cannot be gathered into a set inside ZF. Informally, mathematicians sometimes refer to a collection defined by an unrestricted predicate $\{x : \varphi(x)\}$ as a **class**. If a class coincides with an actual set in ZF, it is called a **set**; if it is "too large" to be a set (like the class of all sets $V = \{x : x = x\}$ or Russell's class $R = \{x : x \notin x\}$), it is called a **proper class**. In ZF set theory, talk of proper classes is purely a convenient linguistic shorthand for talking about the underlying predicates $\varphi(x)$ themselves.

### The Axiom of Union

Pairing lets us put two sets $a$ and $b$ into a container $\{a, b\}$, and Specification lets us carve smaller subsets out of a given set. How do we *merge* the elements of a collection of sets into a single combined set? That requires the **Axiom of Union**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom of Union, Pairwise Unions, and Indexed Family Unions
> **Axiom of Union:** For any set $\mathcal{F}$ (thought of as a family or collection of sets), there exists a set $U$ containing precisely the elements that belong to at least one member $A \in \mathcal{F}$:
> $$
> \forall \mathcal{F} \, \exists U \, \forall x, (x \in U \iff \exists A \in \mathcal{F}, (x \in A))
> $$
> By Extensionality, $U$ is unique and is denoted by $\bigcup \mathcal{F}$ (or $\bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$).
> 1. **Pairwise Union ($A \cup B$):** Given any two sets $A$ and $B$, the Axiom of Pairing guarantees the existence of the family $\mathcal{F} = \{A, B\}$. Applying the Axiom of Union to $\{A, B\}$ produces the **pairwise union** $A \cup B := \bigcup \{A, B\}$, characterized by:
>    $$
>    x \in A \cup B \iff (x \in A \lor x \in B)
>    $$
> 2. **Indexed Family Union ($\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$):** Given an **index set** $I$ and a collection of sets $\{A_i : i \in I\}$ assigning a set $A_i$ to each index $i \in I$, the union of the indexed family is the set
>    $$
>    \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i := \{x : \exists i \in I, (x \in A_i)\}
>    $$
>    so that $x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i \iff \exists i \in I, (x \in A_i)$.

Notice what happens when we take the union of an empty family ($\mathcal{F} = \emptyset$ or $I = \emptyset$): for any object $x$, the existential statement $\exists A \in \emptyset, (x \in A)$ (or $\exists i \in \emptyset, (x \in A_i)$) is false! Therefore, no $x$ belongs to $\bigcup \emptyset$, which means by Extensionality that
$$
\bigcup \emptyset = \emptyset \qquad \text{and} \qquad \bigcup_{i \in \emptyset} A_i = \emptyset
$$

### Intersections, Relative Complements, and Symmetric Differences

Do we need a separate "Axiom of Intersection" to define the intersection of sets? No! Because every element of an intersection must already belong to each of the sets being intersected, we can carve intersections directly out of one of the member sets using the **Axiom Schema of Specification**—provided the family of sets is **non-empty**!

> [!abstract] Definition: Pairwise and Arbitrary Intersections
> 1. **Pairwise Intersection ($A \cap B$):** Given any two sets $A$ and $B$, their **intersection** is defined via the Axiom Schema of Specification as:
>    $$
>    A \cap B := \{x \in A : x \in B\}
>    $$
>    so that $x \in A \cap B \iff (x \in A \land x \in B)$. If $A \cap B = \emptyset$, we say $A$ and $B$ are **disjoint**.
> 2. **Arbitrary Intersection ($\bigcap \mathcal{F}$ and $\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$):** Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a **non-empty** family of sets ($\mathcal{F} \neq \emptyset$). Choosing any fixed member $A_* \in \mathcal{F}$, we define the intersection $\bigcap \mathcal{F}$ via the Axiom Schema of Specification as:
>    $$
>    \bigcap \mathcal{F} := \{x \in A_* : \forall A \in \mathcal{F}, (x \in A)\}
>    $$
>    Notice that for any $x$, since $A_* \in \mathcal{F}$, the condition $(x \in A_*) \land (\forall A \in \mathcal{F}, x \in A)$ is logically equivalent simply to $\forall A \in \mathcal{F}, (x \in A)$ (and is therefore completely independent of which $A_* \in \mathcal{F}$ we chose!). Similarly, for a **non-empty** index set $I \neq \emptyset$ (fixing any $i_* \in I$), we define:
>    $$
>    \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i := \{x \in A_{i_*} : \forall i \in I, (x \in A_i)\}
>    $$
>    so that for $I \neq \emptyset$, we have $x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i \iff \forall i \in I, (x \in A_i)$.

> [!warning] Why $\bigcap \emptyset$ Cannot Exist as a Set in ZF
> Why did we insist that $\mathcal{F} \neq \emptyset$ (and $I \neq \emptyset$) when defining arbitrary intersections, even though $\bigcup \emptyset = \emptyset$ was completely harmless? Suppose we tried to define $\bigcap \emptyset$ by the membership rule
> $$
> x \in \bigcap \emptyset \iff \forall A \in \emptyset, (x \in A)
> $$
> Because the universal quantification is over the empty set $\emptyset$, the statement $\forall A \in \emptyset, (x \in A)$ is **vacuously true for every set $x$ in the universe**! Consequently, if $\bigcap \emptyset$ existed as a set, every set $x$ would satisfy $x \in \bigcap \emptyset$, making $\bigcap \emptyset$ the **universal set of all sets**. As we proved in our resolution of Russell's Paradox, no universal set exists in ZF! Thus $\bigcap \emptyset$ is not a set in ZF, and whenever you take an intersection $\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$, you must verify that the index set $I$ is non-empty (unless all sets are restricted to be subsets of a fixed ambient set $X$, in which case the relative empty intersection inside $X$ is $\{x \in X : \forall i \in \emptyset, x \in A_i\} = X$).

For the exact same reason—because there is no universal set $U$—there is no such thing as an "absolute complement" $A^c = \{x : x \notin A\}$ of a set $A$ in ZF (if $\emptyset^c$ were a set, it would be the universal set!). Instead, complements in ZF are always **relative complements** taken inside a specified ambient set.

> [!abstract] Definition: Relative Complement and Symmetric Difference
> Given any two sets $A$ and $B$:
> 1. The **relative complement** of $B$ in $A$ (or **set difference** $A$ minus $B$), denoted $A \setminus B$, is defined via the Axiom Schema of Specification as:
>    $$
>    A \setminus B := \{x \in A : x \notin B\}
>    $$
>    so that $x \in A \setminus B \iff (x \in A \land x \notin B)$. (When all sets under discussion are subsets of a fixed ambient space $X$, one often writes $B^c := X \setminus B$ for the complement of $B$ relative to $X$.)
> 2. The **symmetric difference** of $A$ and $B$, denoted $A \triangle B$, is the set of elements belonging to $A$ or $B$, but not to both:
>    $$
>    A \triangle B := (A \setminus B) \cup (B \setminus A)
>    $$
>    Equivalently, $A \triangle B = (A \cup B) \setminus (A \cap B)$.

### The Axiom of Power Set

Given a set $A$, we know how to test whether another set $S$ satisfies $S \subseteq A$. Does there exist a single set that collects **all** subsets of $A$ together under one roof? Specification and Union alone cannot build this set; we need a dedicated axiom.

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom of Power Set
> **Axiom of Power Set:** For every set $A$, there exists a set $\mathcal{P}(A)$, called the **power set** of $A$, whose elements are precisely all the subsets of $A$:
> $$
> \forall A \, \exists \mathcal{P}(A) \, \forall S, (S \in \mathcal{P}(A) \iff S \subseteq A)
> $$
> By the Axiom of Extensionality, $\mathcal{P}(A)$ is uniquely determined by $A$ and may be written symbolically as $\mathcal{P}(A) = \{S : S \subseteq A\}$.

Notice that for every set $A$, since $\emptyset \subseteq A$ and $A \subseteq A$, we always have $\emptyset \in \mathcal{P}(A)$ and $A \in \mathcal{P}(A)$. In particular, even for the empty set $A = \emptyset$, its power set is non-empty:
$$
\mathcal{P}(\emptyset) = \{\emptyset\}
$$
because the only subset of $\emptyset$ is $\emptyset$ itself! Applying the power set operation once more to $\{\emptyset\}$ yields $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\emptyset)) = \mathcal{P}(\{\emptyset\}) = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$.

### Distributive Laws and De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Families of Sets

Because membership in unions, intersections, and relative complements translates directly into existential quantification ($\exists$), universal quantification ($\forall$), and conjunction with negation ($\land \neg$), the laws of first-order predicate logic lift immediately to exact identities for arbitrary indexed families of sets. Let us state and prove both the **Infinite Distributive Laws** and **De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Families** by rigorous element-chasing.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Distributive Laws for Arbitrary Unions and Intersections
> Let $B$ be a set and let $\{A_i : i \in I\}$ be an indexed family of sets.
> 1. **Intersection Distributes over Arbitrary Unions:** For any index set $I$ (even if $I = \emptyset$),
>    $$
>    B \cap \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \cap A_i)
>    $$
> 2. **Union Distributes over Arbitrary Non-Empty Intersections:** Provided $I \neq \emptyset$,
>    $$
>    B \cup \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \cup A_i)
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. Let $x$ be an arbitrary object. Unpacking the definitions of pairwise intersection and indexed union step by step:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \in B \cap \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) &\iff (x \in B) \land \left(x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) \\
   &\iff (x \in B) \land (\exists i \in I, x \in A_i) \\
   &\iff \exists i \in I, (x \in B \land x \in A_i) \\
   &\iff \exists i \in I, (x \in B \cap A_i) \\
   &\iff x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \cap A_i)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   where the third equivalence holds because the proposition $x \in B$ does not depend on the bound index variable $i$. By the Axiom of Extensionality, the two sets are equal.
2. Assume $I \neq \emptyset$, so that both intersections are well-defined sets in ZF. For an arbitrary object $x$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \in B \cup \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) &\iff (x \in B) \lor \left(x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) \\
   &\iff (x \in B) \lor (\forall i \in I, x \in A_i) \\
   &\iff \forall i \in I, (x \in B \lor x \in A_i) \\
   &\iff \forall i \in I, (x \in B \cup A_i) \\
   &\iff x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \cup A_i)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   where the third equivalence uses the fact that $I \neq \emptyset$ and $x \in B$ is independent of $i$. By the Axiom of Extensionality, equality holds. $\blacksquare$

Now let us prove **De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Families of Sets**, which show that taking the complement relative to a set $B$ converts arbitrary unions into intersections and arbitrary intersections into unions—mirroring the quantifier negation rules $\neg \exists \iff \forall \neg$ and $\neg \forall \iff \exists \neg$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Families of Sets
> Let $B$ be a set and let $\{A_i : i \in I\}$ be an indexed family of sets with **non-empty** index set $I \neq \emptyset$. Then:
> 1. **Complement of an Arbitrary Union:**
>    $$
>    B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)
>    $$
> 2. **Complement of an Arbitrary Intersection:**
>    $$
>    B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)
>    $$

**Proof:**
Since $I \neq \emptyset$, we may fix an index $i_* \in I$, ensuring that the intersections in both identities are well-defined sets.
