1. To prove $B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$, we establish both inclusions (or direct logical equivalence) by element-chasing. Let $x$ be an arbitrary object.
   - First, suppose $x \in B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right)$. By the definition of relative complement, we have $x \in B$ and $x \notin \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$. Since $x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i \iff \exists i \in I, (x \in A_i)$, De Morgan's law for quantifiers yields:
     $$
     x \notin \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i \iff \neg(\exists i \in I, x \in A_i) \iff \forall i \in I, (x \notin A_i)
     $$
     Thus $x \in B$ and for every $i \in I$, we have $x \notin A_i$. It follows that for every $i \in I$, both $x \in B$ and $x \notin A_i$ hold, which means $x \in B \setminus A_i$ for every $i \in I$. By definition of arbitrary intersection (with $I \neq \emptyset$), we conclude $x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$. This proves $B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$.
   - Conversely, suppose $x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$. Then $\forall i \in I, (x \in B \setminus A_i)$, meaning $\forall i \in I, (x \in B \land x \notin A_i)$. Because $I \neq \emptyset$, picking our fixed index $i_* \in I$ gives $x \in B \setminus A_{i_*}$, which ensures in particular that $x \in B$. Furthermore, since $\forall i \in I, (x \notin A_i)$ holds, it is impossible that $\exists i \in I, (x \in A_i)$; hence $x \notin \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$. Combining $x \in B$ and $x \notin \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$ gives $x \in B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right)$, proving $\bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i) \subseteq B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right)$.
   By the Axiom of Extensionality, $B \setminus \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$.

2. To prove $B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$, let $x$ be an arbitrary object.
   - Suppose first that $x \in B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right)$. Then $x \in B$ and $x \notin \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$. Since $I \neq \emptyset$, membership in the intersection means $\forall i \in I, (x \in A_i)$. Negating this universal quantifier via De Morgan's law for quantifiers gives:
     $$
     x \notin \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i \iff \neg(\forall i \in I, x \in A_i) \iff \exists i \in I, (x \notin A_i)
     $$
     Choose a witness index $j \in I$ such that $x \notin A_j$. Since we also know $x \in B$, we have $x \in B \land x \notin A_j$, so $x \in B \setminus A_j$. Because $j \in I$, this implies $\exists i \in I, (x \in B \setminus A_i)$, which means $x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$. Thus $B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$.
   - Conversely, suppose $x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$. By definition of indexed union, there exists at least one index $j \in I$ such that $x \in B \setminus A_j$, which means $x \in B$ and $x \notin A_j$. Because $j \in I$ and $x \notin A_j$, the universal statement $\forall i \in I, (x \in A_i)$ is false, so $x \notin \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$. Combining $x \in B$ with $x \notin \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$ shows $x \in B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right)$, establishing the reverse inclusion $\bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i) \subseteq B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right)$.
   By the Axiom of Extensionality, $B \setminus \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} (B \setminus A_i)$. $\blacksquare$

### Problems

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.13: A Family of "Too Large" Collections
> Use the Axiom of Union, the Axiom of Power Set, and the non-existence of a universal set to prove that **none** of the following sets exists:
> 1. A set $S$ such that $\{x\} \in S$ for every set $x$ (a "set of all singletons").
> 2. A set $S$ such that $\mathcal{P}(x) \in S$ for every set $x$.
> 3. A set $S$ containing every ordered pair $(x, x)$ (for all sets $x$). *(Hint: apply $\bigcup$ twice and track where $x$ appears.)*
> 4. A set $S$ such that for every set $x$ there is $y \in S$ with $x \subseteq y$. *(Hint: consider $\mathcal{P}(\bigcup S)$.)*
>
> Then state and prove a single general principle that explains parts 1 and 2.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.14: The Power Set Operation Under the Microscope
> For all sets $A, B$, prove:
> 1. $\bigcup \mathcal{P}(A) = A$, and $\bigcap \mathcal{P}(A) = \emptyset$.
> 2. $\mathcal{P}(A) = \mathcal{P}(B) \implies A = B$. Also prove $A \subseteq B \iff \mathcal{P}(A) \subseteq \mathcal{P}(B)$.
> 3. $\mathcal{P}(A) \cap \mathcal{P}(B) = \mathcal{P}(A \cap B)$.
> 4. $\mathcal{P}(A) \cup \mathcal{P}(B) \subseteq \mathcal{P}(A \cup B)$, with equality **if and only if** $A \subseteq B$ or $B \subseteq A$.
> 5. Write out $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\emptyset)))$ explicitly using only $\emptyset$, braces and commas. Verify that its four listed elements are pairwise distinct, and compute $\bigcup$ and $\bigcap$ of this set.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.15: Solving Equations in the Algebra of Sets
> 1. (**Cancellation**) Prove that if $A \cap X = A \cap Y$ and $A \cup X = A \cup Y$, then $X = Y$. Show by example that neither hypothesis alone suffices.
> 2. Given sets $A, B, C$, consider the simultaneous system in the unknown set $X$:
> $$A \cup X = B, \qquad A \cap X = C.$$
> Prove that the system has a solution if and only if $C \subseteq A \subseteq B$, and that in that case the solution is **unique**, namely $X = (B \setminus A) \cup C$.
> 3. Prove $A \setminus (B \setminus C) = (A \setminus B) \cup (A \cap C)$ and $(A \setminus B) \setminus C = A \setminus (B \cup C)$. Prove also that $A \setminus B = A \setminus C \iff A \cap B = A \cap C$.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.16: De Morgan's Laws Without the Non-Empty Hypothesis
> The De Morgan theorem in the text requires $I \neq \emptyset$ because $\bigcap \emptyset$ is not a set. Repair this inside a fixed ambient set $X$. For any set $\mathcal{F}$ of sets (possibly empty), define
> $$\bigcap\nolimits_X \mathcal{F} := \{x \in X : \forall A \in \mathcal{F},\, x \in A\}.$$
> 1. Prove that for **every** family $\mathcal{F}$, including $\mathcal{F} = \emptyset$,
> $$X \setminus \bigcup \mathcal{F} = \bigcap\nolimits_X \{X \setminus A : A \in \mathcal{F}\}, \qquad X \setminus \bigcap\nolimits_X \mathcal{F} = \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{F}} (X \setminus A).$$
> 2. Compute both sides of each identity explicitly when $\mathcal{F} = \emptyset$.
> 3. Prove that if $\mathcal{F} \neq \emptyset$ and every member of $\mathcal{F}$ is a subset of $X$, then $\bigcap_X \mathcal{F} = \bigcap \mathcal{F}$. Also prove $\bigcap_X(\mathcal{F} \cup \mathcal{G}) = \bigcap_X \mathcal{F} \cap \bigcap_X \mathcal{G}$ for **all** families $\mathcal{F}, \mathcal{G}$, and explain why the unrestricted version with $\bigcap$ fails when $\mathcal{F} = \emptyset$.

## Ordered Pairs, Cartesian Products, Relations, and Functions

### Kuratowski's Ordered Pair

By the Axiom of Extensionality, an unordered pair $\{a, b\}$ is completely blind to the order in which $a$ and $b$ are written: since $(x = a \lor x = b) \iff (x = b \lor x = a)$, we always have $\{a, b\} = \{b, a\}$. In analysis and geometry, however, order is indispensable: the point $(a, b)$ must be distinguishable from $(b, a)$ whenever $a \neq b$, and an input-output pair $(x, f(x))$ of a function must distinguish which coordinate is the input and which is the output.

Can we define an **ordered pair** $(a, b)$ purely as a set using only the axioms we already have, without introducing $(a, b)$ as a new undefined primitive? In 1921, Kazimierz Kuratowski gave a remarkably elegant construction using just two applications of the Axiom of Pairing.

> [!abstract] Definition: Kuratowski's Ordered Pair
> Given any two sets $a$ and $b$, the **ordered pair** $(a, b)$ is defined to be the set
> $$
> (a, b) := \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}
> $$
> where $a$ is called the **first coordinate** (or **first projection**) and $b$ is called the **second coordinate** (or **second projection**) of $(a, b)$.

Why does this definition work? Notice the asymmetry: $a$ belongs to *both* member sets $\{a\}$ and $\{a, b\}$, whereas $b$ belongs only to $\{a, b\}$ (unless $a = b$, in which case $\{a, b\} = \{a, a\} = \{a\}$ and $(a, a) = \{\{a\}, \{a\}\} = \{\{a\}\}$). To verify that Kuratowski's definition faithfully captures the behavior of an ordered pair, we must prove the **Fundamental Theorem of Ordered Pairs**: two ordered pairs $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ are equal as sets if and only if $a = c$ and $b = d$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Characteristic Property of Kuratowski's Ordered Pair
> For any sets $a, b, c, d$, we have
> $$
> (a, b) = (c, d) \iff (a = c \land b = d)
> $$

**Proof:**
($\Longleftarrow$) If $a = c$ and $b = d$, then $\{a\} = \{c\}$ and $\{a, b\} = \{c, d\}$, so $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$, which is $(a, b) = (c, d)$.

($\Longrightarrow$) Conversely, suppose $(a, b) = (c, d)$, meaning that
$$
\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}
$$
We divide the argument into two exhaustive logical cases depending on whether $a = b$ or $a \neq b$, using only the Axiom of Extensionality and the definition of unordered pairs.

- **Case 1: $a = b$.**
  If $a = b$, then $\{a, b\} = \{a, a\} = \{a\}$, so the left-hand side simplifies to
  $$
  (a, b) = \{\{a\}, \{a\}\} = \{\{a\}\}
  $$
  Thus $\{\{a\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$. Since $\{c\} \in \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} = \{\{a\}\}$ and $\{c, d\} \in \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} = \{\{a\}\}$, the definition of the singleton $\{\{a\}\}$ forces both
  $$
  \{c\} = \{a\} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \{c, d\} = \{a\}
  $$
  From $\{c\} = \{a\}$, since $c \in \{c\}$, Extensionality implies $c \in \{a\}$, so $c = a$. Similarly, from $\{c, d\} = \{a\}$, since $d \in \{c, d\}$, Extensionality implies $d \in \{a\}$, so $d = a$. Since $a = b$, we conclude that $a = c$ and $b = a = d$, as required.

- **Case 2: $a \neq b$.**
  First, we claim that $\{a\} \neq \{a, b\}$. Indeed, since $b \in \{a, b\}$, if $\{a\} = \{a, b\}$ held, Extensionality would force $b \in \{a\}$, yielding $b = a$, which contradicts $a \neq b$.
  Now, since $\{a\} \in \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$, the definition of unordered pair gives two possibilities:
  $$
  \{a\} = \{c\} \qquad \text{or} \qquad \{a\} = \{c, d\}
  $$
  Suppose momentarily that $\{a\} = \{c, d\}$. Since $c \in \{c, d\} = \{a\}$ and $d \in \{c, d\} = \{a\}$, we would have $c = a$ and $d = a$, so $c = d = a$. Then $\{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} = \{\{a\}\}$, which would force $\{a, b\} \in \{\{a\}\}$, hence $\{a, b\} = \{a\}$, contradicting $a \neq b$!
  Therefore, $\{a\} = \{c, d\}$ is impossible, so we must have
  $$
  \{a\} = \{c\}
  $$
  Since $a \in \{a\} = \{c\}$, this immediately implies **$a = c$**.
  Next, consider the element $\{a, b\} \in \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$. By the definition of unordered pair, we have either
  $$
  \{a, b\} = \{c\} \qquad \text{or} \qquad \{a, b\} = \{c, d\}
  $$
  Since we already know $\{c\} = \{a\}$ and $\{a, b\} \neq \{a\}$, the first option $\{a, b\} = \{c\}$ is impossible! Hence we must have
  $$
  \{a, b\} = \{c, d\}
  $$
  Substituting $c = a$ gives $\{a, b\} = \{a, d\}$. Since $b \in \{a, b\} = \{a, d\}$, we have $b = a$ or $b = d$. Because $a \neq b$, we cannot have $b = a$; thus we must have **$b = d$**.

In both cases, $(a, b) = (c, d)$ implies $a = c$ and $b = d$. $\blacksquare$

### The Cartesian Product $A \times B$

Given two sets $A$ and $B$, we want to form the **Cartesian product** $A \times B$ consisting of all ordered pairs $(a, b)$ with $a \in A$ and $b \in B$. Remember, however, that in ZF set theory we are not allowed to write $\{z : \exists a \in A \, \exists b \in B, z = (a, b)\}$ unless we first identify a **bounding ambient set** guaranteed to exist by our axioms! Let us verify that the Power Set and Union axioms provide the exact bounding set we need.

> [!theorem] Proposition: Existence of the Cartesian Product in ZF
> For any two sets $A$ and $B$, every ordered pair $(a, b)$ with $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ is an element of $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B))$. Consequently, the **Cartesian product**
> $$
> A \times B := \{z \in \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B)) : \exists a \in A \, \exists b \in B, (z = (a, b))\}
> $$
> exists as a legitimate set in ZF by the Axioms of Pairing, Union, Power Set, and Specification, and satisfies
> $$
> z \in A \times B \iff \exists a \in A \, \exists b \in B, (z = (a, b))
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ be arbitrary. By the definition of pairwise union, both $a$ and $b$ belong to $A \cup B$. Therefore, for every $x \in \{a\}$, we have $x = a \in A \cup B$, which means $\{a\} \subseteq A \cup B$, and hence $\{a\} \in \mathcal{P}(A \cup B)$. Similarly, for every $x \in \{a, b\}$, we have $x = a \in A \cup B$ or $x = b \in A \cup B$, which means $\{a, b\} \subseteq A \cup B$, and hence $\{a, b\} \in \mathcal{P}(A \cup B)$.

Now consider the Kuratowski ordered pair $(a, b) = \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$. Both of its elements $\{a\}$ and $\{a, b\}$ belong to $\mathcal{P}(A \cup B)$. Therefore, $(a, b) \subseteq \mathcal{P}(A \cup B)$, which by definition of the power set means
$$
(a, b) \in \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B))
$$
Since $A \cup B$ exists by Pairing and Union, $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B))$ exists by two applications of the Axiom of Power Set. Applying the Axiom Schema of Specification to the ambient set $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B))$ with the predicate $\varphi(z) \equiv (\exists a \in A \, \exists b \in B, z = (a, b))$ proves that $A \times B$ exists as a set in ZF. $\blacksquare$

Notice that $A \times B = \emptyset$ if and only if $A = \emptyset$ or $B = \emptyset$ (for if $a \in A$ and $b \in B$, then $(a, b) \in A \times B \neq \emptyset$). Moreover, when $A \neq B$ and both are non-empty, $A \times B \neq B \times A$, so the Cartesian product is not commutative.

### Formal Definitions of Relations and Functions

In casual calculus courses, a function is often described vaguely as a "rule" or "machine" that transforms an input $x$ into an output $f(x)$. What, however, is a "rule" in pure mathematics? If we want our foundation to rest entirely on sets, we cannot leave "rule" as a mystical psychological concept: we must define relations and functions purely as sets constructed from Cartesian products!

> [!abstract] Definition: Binary Relation
> Let $A$ and $B$ be sets. A **binary relation** from $A$ to $B$ is any subset $R \subseteq A \times B$ (that is, any element $R \in \mathcal{P}(A \times B)$). When $(a, b) \in R$, we often write $a \mathrel{R} b$ and say that $a$ is related to $b$ by $R$. If $A = B$, we say that $R \subseteq A \times A$ is a **binary relation on $A$**.

What distinguishes a **function** from an arbitrary relation $R \subseteq A \times B$? Two things:
1. For *every* input $a \in A$, there is a **unique** output $b \in B$ paired with $a$ in the relation (often called the **graph** $G_f \subseteq A \times B$).
2. We also keep explicit track of the declared **domain** $A$ and **codomain** $B$ as part of the function's identity (so that we can speak unambiguously about whether a function is onto its codomain $B$).

> [!abstract] Definition: Function, Domain, Codomain, Graph, and Range
> A **function** (or **map** or **mapping**) from a set $A$ to a set $B$, written $f: A \to B$, is an ordered triple $f = (A, B, G_f)$—defined set-theoretically via nested ordered pairs as $((A, B), G_f)$—consisting of:
> - A set $A$, called the **domain** of $f$ (denoted $\operatorname{dom}(f)$);
> - A set $B$, called the **codomain** (or **target**) of $f$ (denoted $\operatorname{cod}(f)$); and
> - A relation $G_f \subseteq A \times B$, called the **graph** of $f$, satisfying the **functional condition**:
>   $$
>   \forall a \in A, \exists! b \in B, ((a, b) \in G_f)
>   $$
> For each $a \in A$, the unique element $b \in B$ satisfying $(a, b) \in G_f$ is denoted by **$f(a)$** and is called the **value** of $f$ at $a$ (or the **image** of $a$ under $f$). Thus, for any $a \in A$ and $b \in B$,
> $$
> b = f(a) \iff (a, b) \in G_f
> $$
> The **range** (or **image**) of the function $f: A \to B$ is the subset of $B$ consisting of all actual outputs of $f$:
> $$
> \operatorname{ran}(f) = f(A) := \{b \in B : \exists a \in A, (f(a) = b)\}
> $$

Let us unpack two immediate consequences of this rigorous set-theoretic definition:
1. **Equality of Functions:** Two functions $f: A \to B$ and $g: C \to D$ are equal ($f = g$) if and only if they have the same domain ($A = C$), the same codomain ($B = D$), and the same graph ($G_f = G_g$). Assuming $A = C$ and $B = D$, when does $G_f = G_g$ hold? For any $(a, b) \in A \times B$, we have $(a, b) \in G_f \iff b = f(a)$ and $(a, b) \in G_g \iff b = g(a)$. Thus, two functions $f, g: A \to B$ sharing the same domain and codomain are equal if and only if they agree pointwise at every element of their domain:
   $$
   (f = g) \iff (\forall a \in A, f(a) = g(a))
   $$
2. **The Empty Function:** What if the domain is empty ($A = \emptyset$)? For any codomain $B$, consider the empty graph $G_\emptyset = \emptyset \subseteq \emptyset \times B = \emptyset$. Does $G_\emptyset$ satisfy the functional condition $\forall a \in \emptyset, \exists! b \in B, ((a, b) \in \emptyset)$? Yes—vacuously, because the universal quantifier $\forall a \in \emptyset$ ranges over the empty set! Thus, for every set $B$, there is a unique **empty function** $\emptyset_B : \emptyset \to B$ with graph $\emptyset$. Conversely, if $A \neq \emptyset$ and $B = \emptyset$, then $A \times \emptyset = \emptyset$, so picking any $a_0 \in A$ makes it impossible to find $b \in \emptyset$ with $(a_0, b) \in G_f$; hence there are **no** functions from a non-empty set $A$ to $\emptyset$.

For any set $A$, the **identity function** on $A$, denoted $\operatorname{id}_A : A \to A$, is the function with graph $\{(a, a) \in A \times A : a \in A\}$, so that $\operatorname{id}_A(a) = a$ for all $a \in A$. Furthermore, if $f: A \to B$ is a function and $E \subseteq A$, the **restriction** of $f$ to $E$, denoted $f|_E : E \to B$, is the function with domain $E$, codomain $B$, and graph $G_{f|_E} := G_f \cap (E \times B)$, so that $f|_E(x) = f(x)$ for all $x \in E$.

### Injections, Surjections, Bijections, Composition, and Inverses

Given a function $f: A \to B$, the functional condition guarantees that for every $a \in A$, the equation $f(x) = b$ has a unique output $b$ for a given $x = a$. However, if we reverse perspective and ask how many inputs $x \in A$ map to a given target element $b \in B$, three fundamental structural properties emerge:

> [!abstract] Definition: Injective, Surjective, and Bijective Functions
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function.
> 1. We say $f$ is **injective** (or **one-to-one**, or an **injection**) if distinct elements of $A$ always map to distinct elements of $B$:
>    $$
>    \forall x_1 \in A \, \forall x_2 \in A, (x_1 \neq x_2 \implies f(x_1) \neq f(x_2))
>    $$
>    Equivalently, replacing the inner implication by its contrapositive, $f$ is injective if and only if
>    $$
>    \forall x_1 \in A \, \forall x_2 \in A, (f(x_1) = f(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2)
>    $$
>    (That is, each $b \in B$ has **at most one** preimage $x \in A$ with $f(x) = b$.)
> 2. We say $f$ is **surjective** (or **onto**, or a **surjection**) if every element of the codomain $B$ is achieved as an output of $f$ (so that $f(A) = B$):
>    $$
>    \forall b \in B, \exists a \in A, (f(a) = b)
>    $$
>    (That is, each $b \in B$ has **at least one** preimage $a \in A$ with $f(a) = b$.)
> 3. We say $f$ is **bijective** (or a **one-to-one correspondence**, or a **bijection**) if $f$ is both injective and surjective. Equivalently, $f: A \to B$ is bijective if and only if every element of $B$ has **exactly one** preimage in $A$:
>    $$
>    \forall b \in B, \exists! a \in A, (f(a) = b)
>    $$

Next, let us define the **composition** of functions and establish its fundamental algebraic and structural properties.

> [!abstract] Definition: Composition of Functions
> Let $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to C$ be functions such that the codomain of $f$ coincides with the domain of $g$. The **composition** of $g$ and $f$, denoted $g \circ f : A \to C$ (read *"$g$ composed with $f$"* or *"$g$ after $f$"*), is the function with domain $A$, codomain $C$, and graph
> $$
> G_{g \circ f} := \{(a, c) \in A \times C : \exists b \in B, ((a, b) \in G_f \land (b, c) \in G_g)\}
> $$
> Because for each $a \in A$ there is a unique $b = f(a) \in B$ with $(a, b) \in G_f$, and for that $b \in B$ there is a unique $c = g(b) = g(f(a)) \in C$ with $(b, c) \in G_g$, the relation $G_{g \circ f}$ satisfies the functional condition, and for every $a \in A$:
> $$
> (g \circ f)(a) = g(f(a))
> $$

Although function composition is not commutative in general (indeed, $f \circ g$ is not even defined unless $C = A$, and even when $A = B = C$, $g(f(a))$ need not equal $f(g(a))$), composition is always **associative**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Associativity of Function Composition and Interaction with Injectivity / Surjectivity
> Let $f: A \to B$, $g: B \to C$, and $h: C \to D$ be functions.
> 1. **Associativity:** $h \circ (g \circ f) = (h \circ g) \circ f$ as functions from $A$ to $D$.
> 2. **Preservation under Composition:**
>    - If $f$ and $g$ are both injective, then $g \circ f$ is injective.
>    - If $f$ and $g$ are both surjective, then $g \circ f$ is surjective.
>    - Consequently, if $f$ and $g$ are both bijective, then $g \circ f$ is bijective.
> 3. **Cancellation / Inheritance from a Composite:**
>    - If $g \circ f$ is injective, then the **first** function $f$ must be injective (though $g$ need not be injective).
>    - If $g \circ f$ is surjective, then the **second** function $g$ must be surjective (though $f$ need not be surjective).

**Proof:**
1. Both $h \circ (g \circ f)$ and $(h \circ g) \circ f$ have domain $A$ and codomain $D$. To prove they are equal, let $a \in A$ be arbitrary. Applying the definition of composition twice in each grouping gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (h \circ (g \circ f))(a) &= h((g \circ f)(a)) \\
   &= h(g(f(a))) \\
   &= (h \circ g)(f(a)) \\
   &= ((h \circ g) \circ f)(a)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Since they agree at every $a \in A$, we have $h \circ (g \circ f) = (h \circ g) \circ f$.

2. - Suppose $f$ and $g$ are injective. Let $x_1, x_2 \in A$ satisfy $(g \circ f)(x_1) = (g \circ f)(x_2)$, so that $g(f(x_1)) = g(f(x_2))$. Since $g$ is injective and $f(x_1), f(x_2) \in B$, this implies $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$. Since $f$ is injective, $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ implies $x_1 = x_2$. Thus $g \circ f$ is injective.
   - Suppose $f$ and $g$ are surjective. Let $c \in C$ be arbitrary. Since $g: B \to C$ is surjective, there exists $b \in B$ such that $g(b) = c$. Since $f: A \to B$ is surjective, for this $b \in B$ there exists $a \in A$ such that $f(a) = b$. Then $(g \circ f)(a) = g(f(a)) = g(b) = c$. Thus $g \circ f$ is surjective.
   - Combining the two previous assertions, if $f$ and $g$ are bijective, then $g \circ f$ is both injective and surjective, hence bijective.

3. - Suppose $g \circ f$ is injective. To see that $f$ is injective, let $x_1, x_2 \in A$ satisfy $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$. Applying the function $g$ to both sides yields $g(f(x_1)) = g(f(x_2))$, or $(g \circ f)(x_1) = (g \circ f)(x_2)$. Since $g \circ f$ is injective, this forces $x_1 = x_2$, proving that $f$ is injective.
   - Suppose $g \circ f$ is surjective. To see that $g$ is surjective, let $c \in C$ be arbitrary. Since $g \circ f : A \to C$ is surjective, there exists $a \in A$ such that $(g \circ f)(a) = c$, which means $g(f(a)) = c$. Setting $b := f(a) \in B$, we have found an element $b \in B$ such that $g(b) = c$, proving that $g$ is surjective. $\blacksquare$

Now we are ready to characterize invertible functions. Given $f: A \to B$, a function $g: B \to A$ is called a **left inverse** (or **retraction**) of $f$ if $g \circ f = \operatorname{id}_A$ (meaning $g(f(a)) = a$ for all $a \in A$), and a **right inverse** (or **section**) of $f$ if $f \circ g = \operatorname{id}_B$ (meaning $f(g(b)) = b$ for all $b \in B$). A function $g: B \to A$ that is simultaneously a left inverse and a right inverse of $f$ is called a **two-sided inverse** (or simply an **inverse function**) of $f$.

Notice that by Part 3 of the theorem above, since $\operatorname{id}_A$ is injective, if $f$ has a left inverse $g$ ($g \circ f = \operatorname{id}_A$), then $f$ must be injective; and since $\operatorname{id}_B$ is surjective, if $f$ has a right inverse $g$ ($f \circ g = \operatorname{id}_B$), then $f$ must be surjective. This leads directly to the fundamental theorem of invertible functions.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Characterization of Bijections via Two-Sided Inverse Functions
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function. Then $f$ is a bijection if and only if there exists a two-sided inverse function $g: B \to A$ satisfying
> $$
> g \circ f = \operatorname{id}_A \qquad \text{and} \qquad f \circ g = \operatorname{id}_B
> $$
> Moreover, when such an inverse function $g$ exists, it is **unique**; we denote it by $f^{-1}: B \to A$, and its graph is the converse relation $G_{f^{-1}} = \{(b, a) \in B \times A : (a, b) \in G_f\}$, so that for all $a \in A$ and $b \in B$:
> $$
> f^{-1}(b) = a \iff f(a) = b
> $$

**Proof:**
($\Longleftarrow$) Suppose there exists a function $g: B \to A$ such that $g \circ f = \operatorname{id}_A$ and $f \circ g = \operatorname{id}_B$. Because $\operatorname{id}_A : A \to A$ is injective (if $\operatorname{id}_A(x_1) = \operatorname{id}_A(x_2)$, then $x_1 = x_2$), $g \circ f$ is injective, so by Part 3 of our previous theorem, $f$ is injective. Similarly, because $\operatorname{id}_B : B \to B$ is surjective (for any $b \in B$, $\operatorname{id}_B(b) = b$), $f \circ g$ is surjective, so by Part 3 of our previous theorem, $f$ is surjective. Hence $f$ is a bijection.

($\Longrightarrow$) Conversely, suppose $f: A \to B$ is a bijection. Using the Axiom Schema of Specification on the Cartesian product $B \times A$, define the subset
$$
G_g := \{(b, a) \in B \times A : (a, b) \in G_f\}
$$
We claim that $g := (B, A, G_g)$ is a well-defined function from $B$ to $A$. Let $b \in B$ be arbitrary. Because $f$ is a bijection, there exists a unique $a \in A$ such that $f(a) = b$, which is equivalent to $(a, b) \in G_f$, and hence equivalent to $(b, a) \in G_g$. Thus $\forall b \in B, \exists! a \in A, ((b, a) \in G_g)$, confirming that $g: B \to A$ is a valid function satisfying $g(b) = a \iff f(a) = b$.
Let us verify both composition identities:
- For any $a \in A$, let $b := f(a) \in B$. By the characterization $g(b) = a \iff f(a) = b$, we have $g(f(a)) = g(b) = a = \operatorname{id}_A(a)$. Thus $g \circ f = \operatorname{id}_A$.
- For any $b \in B$, let $a := g(b) \in A$. By the characterization $g(b) = a \iff f(a) = b$, we have $f(g(b)) = f(a) = b = \operatorname{id}_B(b)$. Thus $f \circ g = \operatorname{id}_B$.

**Uniqueness of the Inverse:** Suppose $g_1: B \to A$ and $g_2: B \to A$ are both two-sided inverses of $f$, so that $g_1 \circ f = \operatorname{id}_A$ and $f \circ g_2 = \operatorname{id}_B$. Using the associativity of composition, we compute:
$$
\begin{aligned}
g_1 &= g_1 \circ \operatorname{id}_B \\
&= g_1 \circ (f \circ g_2) \\
&= (g_1 \circ f) \circ g_2 \\
&= \operatorname{id}_A \circ g_2 \\
&= g_2
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus the inverse function $f^{-1} := g$ is unique. $\blacksquare$

### Direct Images and Preimages (Inverse Images) of Sets

Every function $f: A \to B$ between sets $A$ and $B$ automatically induces two companion operations at the level of power sets: one pushing subsets of $A$ forward into $\mathcal{P}(B)$, and one pulling subsets of $B$ backward into $\mathcal{P}(A)$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Direct Image and Preimage (Inverse Image) of Subsets
> Let $f: A \to B$ be any function.
> 1. **Direct Image:** For any subset $E \subseteq A$, the **image** (or **direct image**) of $E$ under $f$, denoted $f(E)$, is the subset of $B$ consisting of all outputs attained on $E$:
>    $$
>    f(E) := \{b \in B : \exists x \in E, (f(x) = b)\} = \{f(x) : x \in E\}
>    $$
>    so that for any $b \in B$, we have $b \in f(E) \iff \exists x \in E, (f(x) = b)$.
> 2. **Preimage (Inverse Image):** For any subset $F \subseteq B$, the **preimage** (or **inverse image**) of $F$ under $f$, denoted $f^{-1}(F)$, is the subset of $A$ consisting of all inputs in $A$ whose outputs land inside $F$:
>    $$
>    f^{-1}(F) := \{x \in A : f(x) \in F\}
>    $$
>    so that for any $x \in A$, we have $x \in f^{-1}(F) \iff f(x) \in F$.

> [!warning] Do Not Confuse the Preimage Map $f^{-1}(F)$ with an Inverse Function!
> The preimage $f^{-1}(F) \subseteq A$ is defined for **every** function $f: A \to B$ and **every** subset $F \subseteq B$—even when $f$ is neither injective nor surjective and no inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$ exists! Why? Because $f^{-1}$ on subsets is actually a function between power sets, $\mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$, sending each subset $F \in \mathcal{P}(B)$ to the subset $\{x \in A : f(x) \in F\} \in \mathcal{P}(A)$. If no elements of $A$ map into $F$, then $f^{-1}(F)$ is simply the empty set $\emptyset \in \mathcal{P}(A)$; if many elements of $A$ map into $F$, they are all collected together inside the set $f^{-1}(F)$. Never assume $f$ is bijective merely because the symbol $f^{-1}(F)$ appears applied to a subset $F \subseteq B$!

Notice the striking contrast between the membership criteria for $f(E)$ and $f^{-1}(F)$:
- Testing whether $b \in f(E)$ introduces an **existential quantifier** ($\exists x \in E, f(x) = b$).
- Testing whether $x \in f^{-1}(F)$ introduces **no quantifier at all**—it simply translates directly into $f(x) \in F$!

Because $x \in f^{-1}(F) \iff f(x) \in F$ has no quantifier to get in the way, **preimages preserve all set-theoretic operations (arbitrary unions, arbitrary intersections, and complements) with exact equality**! Direct images, by contrast, must push an existential quantifier $\exists x \in E$ across the set operation: since $\exists$ commutes with $\lor$ ($\exists$ commutes with $\exists$), direct images preserve arbitrary unions; however, since $(\exists x, P(x) \land Q(x)) \implies (\exists x, P(x)) \land (\exists x, Q(x))$ is only a one-way implication (recall our mixed-quantifier theorem!), direct images in general only satisfy an **inclusion** for intersections! Let us prove all of these fundamental properties in detail.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Algebraic Properties of Preimages (Inverse Images)
> Let $f: A \to B$ be any function.
> 1. **Preservation of Arbitrary Unions:** For any indexed family $\{F_i : i \in I\}$ of subsets of $B$,
>    $$
>    f^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} F_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} f^{-1}(F_i)
>    $$
> 2. **Preservation of Arbitrary Non-Empty Intersections:** For any indexed family $\{F_i : i \in I\}$ of subsets of $B$ with $I \neq \emptyset$,
>    $$
>    f^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} F_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} f^{-1}(F_i)
>    $$
> 3. **Preservation of Relative Complements:** For any subsets $F_1, F_2 \subseteq B$ and any subset $F \subseteq B$,
>    $$
>    f^{-1}(F_1 \setminus F_2) = f^{-1}(F_1) \setminus f^{-1}(F_2) \qquad \text{and} \qquad f^{-1}(B \setminus F) = A \setminus f^{-1}(F)
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. Let $x \in A$ be arbitrary (note that both sets are subsets of $A$). Unpacking the definitions directly:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \in f^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} F_i\right) &\iff f(x) \in \bigcup_{i \in I} F_i \\
   &\iff \exists i \in I, (f(x) \in F_i) \\
   &\iff \exists i \in I, (x \in f^{-1}(F_i)) \\
   &\iff x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} f^{-1}(F_i)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   By the Axiom of Extensionality, $f^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} F_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} f^{-1}(F_i)$.

2. Assume $I \neq \emptyset$. For any $x \in A$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \in f^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} F_i\right) &\iff f(x) \in \bigcap_{i \in I} F_i \\
   &\iff \forall i \in I, (f(x) \in F_i) \\
   &\iff \forall i \in I, (x \in f^{-1}(F_i)) \\
   &\iff x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} f^{-1}(F_i)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   By the Axiom of Extensionality, $f^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} F_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} f^{-1}(F_i)$.

3. Let $x \in A$ be arbitrary. Then:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \in f^{-1}(F_1 \setminus F_2) &\iff f(x) \in F_1 \setminus F_2 \\
   &\iff (f(x) \in F_1) \land (f(x) \notin F_2) \\
   &\iff (x \in f^{-1}(F_1)) \land (x \notin f^{-1}(F_2)) \\
   &\iff x \in f^{-1}(F_1) \setminus f^{-1}(F_2)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   giving $f^{-1}(F_1 \setminus F_2) = f^{-1}(F_1) \setminus f^{-1}(F_2)$. Taking $F_1 = B$ and $F_2 = F$, and observing that $f^{-1}(B) = \{x \in A : f(x) \in B\} = A$ (since $f(x) \in B$ for every $x \in A$), we immediately obtain $f^{-1}(B \setminus F) = A \setminus f^{-1}(F)$. $\blacksquare$

Now let us examine the behavior of **direct images** under unions and intersections, and prove why equality for intersections is equivalent to injectivity of $f$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Algebraic Properties of Direct Images
> Let $f: A \to B$ be any function.
> 1. **Preservation of Arbitrary Unions:** For any indexed family $\{E_i : i \in I\}$ of subsets of $A$,
>    $$
>    f\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} E_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} f(E_i)
>    $$
> 2. **Sub-Distributivity over Intersections:** For any indexed family $\{E_i : i \in I\}$ of subsets of $A$ with $I \neq \emptyset$,
>    $$
>    f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)
>    $$
>    Moreover, equality $f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$ holds for all non-empty families $\{E_i : i \in I\}$ of subsets of $A$ (or even for all pairs of subsets $E_1, E_2 \subseteq A$) if and only if $f$ is **injective**.

**Proof:**
1. Let $b \in B$ be arbitrary. Because two existential quantifiers $\exists x \in A$ and $\exists i \in I$ commute freely, we have:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   b \in f\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} E_i\right) &\iff \exists x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} E_i, (f(x) = b) \\
   &\iff \exists x \in A, (\exists i \in I, x \in E_i \land f(x) = b) \\
   &\iff \exists i \in I, (\exists x \in E_i, f(x) = b) \\
   &\iff \exists i \in I, (b \in f(E_i)) \\
   &\iff b \in \bigcup_{i \in I} f(E_i)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   By the Axiom of Extensionality, $f\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} E_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} f(E_i)$.

2. Assume $I \neq \emptyset$, and let $b \in f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right)$. By definition of direct image, there exists a single element $x_0 \in \bigcap_{i \in I} E_i$ such that $f(x_0) = b$. Because $x_0 \in \bigcap_{i \in I} E_i$, we have $x_0 \in E_i$ for every $i \in I$. Thus, for each $i \in I$, the element $x_0 \in E_i$ satisfies $f(x_0) = b$, which implies $b \in f(E_i)$ for every $i \in I$. Hence $b \in \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$, proving the inclusion $f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$.

   Now let us prove that equality holds for all families of subsets of $A$ if and only if $f$ is injective:
   - ($\Longleftarrow$) Suppose $f$ is injective, and let $b \in \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$ with $I \neq \emptyset$. Fix an index $i_0 \in I$. Since $b \in f(E_{i_0})$, there exists an element $x_0 \in E_{i_0}$ such that $f(x_0) = b$. We claim that $x_0 \in E_i$ for every $i \in I$. Indeed, let $i \in I$ be arbitrary. Since $b \in \bigcap_{j \in I} f(E_j) \subseteq f(E_i)$, there exists some $x_i \in E_i$ such that $f(x_i) = b$. Then $f(x_i) = b = f(x_0)$, and since $f$ is injective, this forces $x_i = x_0$! Because $x_i \in E_i$ and $x_0 = x_i$, we have $x_0 \in E_i$. Since $i \in I$ was arbitrary, $x_0 \in \bigcap_{i \in I} E_i$, and therefore $b = f(x_0) \in f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right)$. Thus $\bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i) \subseteq f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right)$, and equality holds.
   - ($\Longrightarrow$) Conversely, suppose $f(E_1 \cap E_2) = f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$ holds for all subsets $E_1, E_2 \subseteq A$. To prove that $f$ is injective, let $x_1, x_2 \in A$ satisfy $f(x_1) = f(x_2) =: b$. Apply our hypothesis to the singleton subsets $E_1 := \{x_1\}$ and $E_2 := \{x_2\}$. On the right-hand side, $f(\{x_1\}) = \{b\}$ and $f(\{x_2\}) = \{b\}$, so
     $$
     f(\{x_1\} \cap \{x_2\}) = f(\{x_1\}) \cap f(\{x_2\}) = \{b\} \cap \{b\} = \{b\} \neq \emptyset
     $$
     Because the direct image of the empty set is always empty ($f(\emptyset) = \emptyset$), having $f(\{x_1\} \cap \{x_2\}) \neq \emptyset$ forces $\{x_1\} \cap \{x_2\} \neq \emptyset$. Choosing an element $z \in \{x_1\} \cap \{x_2\}$ gives $z = x_1$ and $z = x_2$, so $x_1 = x_2$. Thus $f$ is injective. $\blacksquare$

Finally, what happens when we compose the direct image and preimage operations on subsets—applying $f^{-1}$ after $f$ to a subset $E \subseteq A$, or applying $f$ after $f^{-1}$ to a subset $F \subseteq B$? In general, neither round-trip returns the original set unchanged; instead, we obtain two fundamental round-trip inclusions whose equalities characterize injectivity and surjectivity!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Round-Trip Inclusions for Direct Images and Preimages
> Let $f: A \to B$ be any function.
> 1. **Pullback of a Pushforward ($E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$):** For every subset $E \subseteq A$, we have
>    $$
>    E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))
>    $$
>    Moreover, equality $f^{-1}(f(E)) = E$ holds for all subsets $E \subseteq A$ if and only if $f$ is **injective**.
> 2. **Pushforward of a Pullback ($f(f^{-1}(F)) \subseteq F$):** For every subset $F \subseteq B$, we have
>    $$
>    f(f^{-1}(F)) \subseteq F
>    $$
>    Moreover, equality $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F$ holds for all subsets $F \subseteq B$ if and only if $f$ is **surjective** (and in general, $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F \cap f(A)$ for every $F \subseteq B$).

**Proof:**
1. Let $E \subseteq A$ be any subset, and let $x \in E$ be arbitrary. By definition of the direct image $f(E)$, since $x \in E$, its output $f(x)$ belongs to $f(E)$: that is, $f(x) \in f(E)$. By definition of the preimage of the set $f(E)$, the statement $f(x) \in f(E)$ means precisely that $x \in f^{-1}(f(E))$. Thus $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$.
   - Suppose further that $f$ is injective, and let $x \in f^{-1}(f(E))$. Then $f(x) \in f(E)$, which means there exists $x_0 \in E$ such that $f(x) = f(x_0)$. Because $f$ is injective, $f(x) = f(x_0)$ forces $x = x_0 \in E$. Thus $f^{-1}(f(E)) \subseteq E$, giving $f^{-1}(f(E)) = E$.
   - Conversely, suppose $f^{-1}(f(E)) = E$ for every $E \subseteq A$. If $x_1, x_2 \in A$ satisfy $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$, let $E := \{x_1\}$. Then $f(x_2) = f(x_1) \in f(\{x_1\})$, so $x_2 \in f^{-1}(f(\{x_1\})) = \{x_1\}$, which implies $x_2 = x_1$. Hence $f$ is injective.

2. Let $F \subseteq B$ be any subset, and let $b \in f(f^{-1}(F))$ be arbitrary. By definition of direct image, there exists $x \in f^{-1}(F)$ such that $f(x) = b$. By definition of preimage, $x \in f^{-1}(F)$ means $f(x) \in F$. Since $b = f(x)$, we immediately have $b \in F$. Thus $f(f^{-1}(F)) \subseteq F$. (Notice further that since $b = f(x) \in f(A)$, we have $f(f^{-1}(F)) \subseteq F \cap f(A)$; and conversely, if $b \in F \cap f(A)$, then $b \in F$ and $b = f(x)$ for some $x \in A$, so $f(x) = b \in F \implies x \in f^{-1}(F) \implies b = f(x) \in f(f^{-1}(F))$, establishing the general identity $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F \cap f(A)$.)
   - If $f$ is surjective, then $f(A) = B$, so for every $F \subseteq B$, we have $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F \cap f(A) = F \cap B = F$.
   - Conversely, if $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F$ for every $F \subseteq B$, taking $F = B$ gives $f(A) = f(f^{-1}(B)) = B$, which means $f$ is surjective. $\blacksquare$

> [!summary] Summary of Section 0.1
> We have constructed the logical and set-theoretic bedrock of real analysis without appealing to any pre-existing number system:
> 1. **Logic & Quantifiers:** Material implication $P \implies Q$ is equivalent to $\neg Q \implies \neg P$ and is vacuously true when $P$ is false; universal quantifiers over $\emptyset$ hold vacuously while existential quantifiers over $\emptyset$ fail; and $\exists y \, \forall x, P(x, y) \implies \forall x \, \exists y, P(x, y)$, with negation flipping $\forall \leftrightarrow \exists$.
> 2. **ZF Axioms:** Restricting comprehension via the Axiom Schema of Specification $\{x \in A : \varphi(x)\}$ resolves Russell's Paradox and proves that no universal set exists; consequently, arbitrary intersections $\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$ require $I \neq \emptyset$ and complements $B \setminus A$ are always relative.
> 3. **Functions & Set Images:** Kuratowski's ordered pair $(a, b) = \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$ allows us to build $A \times B \subseteq \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B))$ and define functions rigorously as triples $(A, B, G_f)$. Two-sided inverses exist if and only if $f$ is bijective, while the induced preimage map $f^{-1}: \mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ preserves all set operations unconditionally.

### Problems

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.17: Reading Coordinates Out of a Kuratowski Pair
> 1. For all sets $a, b$, prove $\bigcap (a,b) = \{a\}$ and $\bigcup (a,b) = \{a, b\}$.
> 2. Define predicates $\pi_1(p, x) :\equiv \bigcap p = \{x\}$ and
> $$\pi_2(p, y) :\equiv y \in \bigcup p \,\land\, \big(\bigcup p \neq \bigcap p \implies y \notin \bigcap p\big).$$
> Prove that $\pi_1((a,b), x) \iff x = a$ and $\pi_2((a,b), y) \iff y = b$, treating $a = b$ and $a \neq b$ separately.
> 3. Use part 2 to give a second proof that $(a,b) = (c,d) \implies a = c \land b = d$.
> 4. Define the **swap** $\sigma : A \times B \to B \times A$ by $G_\sigma := \{(z, w) \in (A \times B) \times (B \times A) : \exists a\,\exists b\,(z = (a,b) \land w = (b,a))\}$. Prove that $\sigma$ is a well-defined function, that it is a bijection, and that its inverse is the swap map of $B \times A$.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.18: The Algebra of Cartesian Products
> For all sets $A, B, C, D$, prove:
> 1. $(A \times B) \cap (C \times D) = (A \cap C) \times (B \cap D)$.
> 2. $A \times B \subseteq C \times D \iff \big(A = \emptyset \lor B = \emptyset \lor (A \subseteq C \land B \subseteq D)\big)$.
> 3. $(A \times B) \setminus (C \times D) = \big((A \setminus C) \times B\big) \cup \big(A \times (B \setminus D)\big)$.
> 4. If $A \times B = C \times D \neq \emptyset$, then $A = C$ and $B = D$. Show by example that the hypothesis "$\neq \emptyset$" cannot be dropped. Deduce that if $A \times B = B \times A \neq \emptyset$, then $A = B$.
> 5. For a **non-empty** index set $I$: $A \times \bigcap_{i \in I} B_i = \bigcap_{i \in I}(A \times B_i)$, and for any $I$: $A \times \bigcup_{i \in I} B_i = \bigcup_{i \in I}(A \times B_i)$.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.19: Left Inverses, Edge Cases, and Uniqueness
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function.
> 1. Prove that $f$ has a left inverse if and only if $f$ is injective **and** ($A \neq \emptyset$ or $B = \emptyset$). (Construct the graph of the left inverse inside $B \times A$ using Specification; the empty-function discussion in the text handles the degenerate cases.)
> 2. Prove that if $f$ has a left inverse $g$ and a right inverse $h$, then $g = h$ and $f$ is bijective.
> 3. Suppose $g$ is a left inverse of $f$. Prove that $g$ is surjective, and that $g$ is injective if and only if $f$ is surjective.
> 4. Let $f$ be injective with a left inverse. Prove that $f$ has **more than one** left inverse if and only if $B \setminus f(A) \neq \emptyset$ **and** $A$ contains two distinct elements. Deduce that a bijection has exactly one left inverse, while a non-surjective injection from a one-element set has exactly one as well.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.20: Images, Preimages, and an Adjunction
> Let $f: A \to B$, $E \subseteq A$, $F \subseteq B$.
> 1. (**Frobenius identity**) Prove $f\big(E \cap f^{-1}(F)\big) = f(E) \cap F$. Deduce that $f(E) \cap F = \emptyset \iff E \cap f^{-1}(F) = \emptyset$.
> 2. Prove that $f(A \setminus E) = B \setminus f(E)$ holds for **every** $E \subseteq A$ if and only if $f$ is bijective.
> 3. (**Adjunction**) Prove $f(E) \subseteq F \iff E \subseteq f^{-1}(F)$.
> 4. Using part 3, prove
> $$f(E) = \bigcap \{F \in \mathcal{P}(B) : E \subseteq f^{-1}(F)\}, \qquad f^{-1}(F) = \bigcup \{E \in \mathcal{P}(A) : f(E) \subseteq F\}.$$
> Verify that the families involved are non-empty so the intersection is a legitimate set, and explain in words why the first formula says that $f(E)$ is the *smallest* subset of $B$ whose preimage contains $E$.

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.21: The Set of All Functions and Characteristic Functions
> Let $2 := \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$, and for sets $A, B$ let $B^A$ denote the collection of all functions $A \to B$ (identified with their graphs).
> 1. Prove that $B^A$ is a **set**, by exhibiting it as a subset of $\mathcal{P}(A \times B)$ cut out by Specification. Prove that $B^\emptyset$ has exactly one element, and that $\emptyset^A = \emptyset$ whenever $A \neq \emptyset$.
> 2. For $E \subseteq A$ define $\chi_E : A \to 2$ by $\chi_E(x) = \{\emptyset\}$ if $x \in E$ and $\chi_E(x) = \emptyset$ if $x \notin E$ (write down its graph with Specification and verify the functional condition). Prove that $\chi : \mathcal{P}(A) \to 2^A$, $E \mapsto \chi_E$, is a bijection. Check that the case $A = \emptyset$ is consistent.
> 3. For a function $f : A \to B$ and $F \subseteq B$, prove $\chi_{f^{-1}(F)} = \chi_F \circ f$. Conclude that, under the identification $\chi$, the preimage map $f^* : \mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ becomes "precomposition with $f$".

> [!abstract] Problem 0.1.22: Cantor's Theorem and Its Consequences
> (You may use Problems 0.1.19 and 0.1.21.)
> 1. Prove that for every set $A$, **no** function $f: A \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ is surjective. (*Hint: consider $D := \{x \in A : x \notin f(x)\}$ and imitate the proof of Russell's Paradox.*)
> 2. Prove that the map $A \to \mathcal{P}(A)$, $a \mapsto \{a\}$, is injective but never surjective, including for $A = \emptyset$.
> 3. Prove that there is no injective function $\mathcal{P}(A) \to A$.
> 4. Deduce that there is no surjection $A \to 2^A$ and no injection $2^A \to A$.
> 5. Give a **second proof that no universal set exists**: assuming a universal set $U$, show that $\mathcal{P}(U) \subseteq U$, then show that $x \mapsto x \cap U$ is a surjection $U \to \mathcal{P}(U)$, contradicting part 1.

## 0.1 Exercises

### Problem 0.1.1: Wiener's Alternative Ordered Pair Definition

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Before Kuratowski introduced $(a, b) := \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$ in 1921, Norbert Wiener (1914) gave the first purely set-theoretic definition of an ordered pair by setting
> $$
> \langle a, b \rangle_W := \{\{\{a\}, \emptyset\}, \{\{b\}\}\}
> $$
> Using only the Axiom of Extensionality, the Axiom of the Empty Set, and the Axiom of Pairing, prove that Wiener's ordered pair satisfies the characteristic property of ordered pairs: for all sets $a, b, c, d$,
> $$
> \langle a, b \rangle_W = \langle c, d \rangle_W \iff (a = c \land b = d)
> $$

### Problem 0.1.2: Algebraic Laws of Symmetric Difference

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Recall that for any two sets $A$ and $B$, their symmetric difference is defined by $A \triangle B := (A \setminus B) \cup (B \setminus A)$. Prove the following algebraic identities for all sets $A, B, C$:
> 1. $A \triangle B = (A \cup B) \setminus (A \cap B)$.
> 2. **Commutativity and Identity / Self-Inverse Laws:** $A \triangle B = B \triangle A$, $A \triangle \emptyset = A$, and $A \triangle A = \emptyset$.
> 3. **Associativity:** $(A \triangle B) \triangle C = A \triangle (B \triangle C)$.
> 4. **Distributivity of Intersection over Symmetric Difference:** $A \cap (B \triangle C) = (A \cap B) \triangle (A \cap C)$.
> 5. **Cancellation Law:** If $A \triangle C = B \triangle C$, then $A = B$.

### Problem 0.1.3: Characterizations of Injectivity and Surjectivity via Set Operations

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function.
> 1. Prove that the following four conditions are logically equivalent:
>    - (a) $f$ is injective.
>    - (b) For every subset $E \subseteq A$, $f^{-1}(f(E)) = E$.
>    - (c) For all subsets $X, Y \subseteq A$, $f(X \cap Y) = f(X) \cap f(Y)$.
>    - (d) For all subsets $X, Y \subseteq A$, $f(X \setminus Y) = f(X) \setminus f(Y)$.
> 2. Prove that the following three conditions are logically equivalent:
>    - (a) $f$ is surjective.
>    - (b) For every subset $F \subseteq B$, $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F$.
>    - (c) For every subset $F \subseteq B$, $B \setminus f(f^{-1}(F)) = f(A \setminus f^{-1}(F))$.

### Problem 0.1.4: Saturated Subsets and Induced Power Set Maps

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function. A subset $E \subseteq A$ is said to be **$f$-saturated** if $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$. Let $\mathcal{S}_f := \{E \in \mathcal{P}(A) : E = f^{-1}(f(E))\}$ denote the set of all $f$-saturated subsets of $A$.
> 1. Prove that for every subset $F \subseteq B$, its preimage $f^{-1}(F)$ is $f$-saturated (that is, $f^{-1}(f(f^{-1}(F))) = f^{-1}(F)$), and therefore $E \subseteq A$ is $f$-saturated if and only if there exists $F \subseteq B$ such that $E = f^{-1}(F)$.
> 2. Let $f_* : \mathcal{P}(A) \to \mathcal{P}(B)$ be the direct image map $f_*(E) := f(E)$, and let $f^* : \mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ be the preimage map $f^*(F) := f^{-1}(F)$. Prove that:
>    - $f_*$ is injective if and only if $f$ is injective, and $f_*$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is surjective;
>    - $f^*$ is injective if and only if $f$ is surjective, and $f^*$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is injective.
> 3. Prove that restricting the domain of $f_*$ to $\mathcal{S}_f$ and its codomain to $\mathcal{P}(f(A))$ yields a bijection $\widetilde{f}_* : \mathcal{S}_f \to \mathcal{P}(f(A))$.

### Problem 0.1.5: The Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem on $\mathcal{P}(X)$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $X$ be a set, and let $\Phi : \mathcal{P}(X) \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ be an **inclusion-preserving** (or **monotone**) map on the power set of $X$, meaning that for all $A, B \in \mathcal{P}(X)$,
> $$
> A \subseteq B \implies \Phi(A) \subseteq \Phi(B)
> $$
> A subset $S \subseteq X$ is called a **fixed point** of $\Phi$ if $\Phi(S) = S$. Define the collections of **pre-fixed points** and **post-fixed points** of $\Phi$ by
> $$
> \mathcal{A} := \{A \in \mathcal{P}(X) : \Phi(A) \subseteq A\} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \mathcal{B} := \{B \in \mathcal{P}(X) : B \subseteq \Phi(B)\}
> $$
> 1. Verify that $\mathcal{A} \neq \emptyset$ and $\mathcal{B} \neq \emptyset$ by checking $X \in \mathcal{A}$ and $\emptyset \in \mathcal{B}$, so that $S_* := \bigcap \mathcal{A}$ and $S^* := \bigcup \mathcal{B}$ exist as subsets of $X$.
> 2. Prove that $\Phi(S_*) \subseteq S_*$, and deduce by applying $\Phi$ to this inclusion that $S_* \subseteq \Phi(S_*)$, thereby establishing that $\Phi(S_*) = S_*$. Conclude that $S_*$ is the **smallest fixed point** of $\Phi$ (meaning $\Phi(S_*) = S_*$, and for every $S \subseteq X$ with $\Phi(S) = S$, we have $S_* \subseteq S$).
> 3. By a dual argument, prove that $\Phi(S^*) = S^*$ and that $S^*$ is the **largest fixed point** of $\Phi$ (meaning $\Phi(S^*) = S^*$, and for every $S \subseteq X$ with $\Phi(S) = S$, we have $S \subseteq S^*$).

### Problem 0.1.6: Minimax Inclusion for Doubly-Indexed Set Families

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $I$ and $J$ be non-empty index sets, and for each ordered pair $(i, j) \in I \times J$, let $A_{i, j}$ be a set.
> 1. Prove the general set-theoretic minimax inclusion:
>    $$
>    \bigcup_{i \in I} \left(\bigcap_{j \in J} A_{i, j}\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{j \in J} \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_{i, j}\right)
>    $$
> 2. Construct an explicit purely set-theoretic example (using only sets built from $\emptyset$, such as $I = J = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$) showing that this inclusion can be strict:
>    $$
>    \bigcup_{i \in I} \left(\bigcap_{j \in J} A_{i, j}\right) \subsetneq \bigcap_{j \in J} \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_{i, j}\right)
>    $$

### Problem 0.1.7: Categorical Monomorphisms and Epimorphisms in $\mathbf{Set}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function between sets $A$ and $B$.
> 1. We say $f$ is **left-cancellative** (or a **monomorphism**) if for every set $Z$ and every pair of functions $g_1, g_2 : Z \to A$,
>    $$
>    f \circ g_1 = f \circ g_2 \implies g_1 = g_2
>    $$
>    Prove that $f: A \to B$ is injective if and only if $f$ is left-cancellative. (For the reverse implication, test left-cancellation using the singleton set $Z = \{\emptyset\}$.)
> 2. We say $f$ is **right-cancellative** (or an **epimorphism**) if for every set $W$ and every pair of functions $h_1, h_2 : B \to W$,
>    $$
>    h_1 \circ f = h_2 \circ f \implies h_1 = h_2
>    $$
>    Prove that $f: A \to B$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is right-cancellative. (For the reverse implication, test right-cancellation using the two-element set $W = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$.)

### Problem 0.1.8: Set-Theoretic Sandwich Bijection (Schröder–Bernstein Core)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Suppose $A, B, C$ are sets satisfying the nested inclusion $A \subseteq B \subseteq C$, and suppose there exists a bijection $f: C \to A$.
> 1. Define a map $\Phi : \mathcal{P}(C) \to \mathcal{P}(C)$ by setting, for each subset $E \subseteq C$,
>    $$
>    \Phi(E) := (C \setminus B) \cup f(E)
>    $$
>    Prove that $\Phi$ is inclusion-preserving ($E_1 \subseteq E_2 \implies \Phi(E_1) \subseteq \Phi(E_2)$), and apply Problem 0.1.5 (the Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem) to conclude that there exists a subset $S \subseteq C$ such that $\Phi(S) = S$, i.e., $S = (C \setminus B) \cup f(S)$.
> 2. Prove that for this fixed point $S$, the sets $C \setminus B$ and $f(S)$ are disjoint, that $C \setminus S = B \setminus f(S)$, and that $B$ is the disjoint union $B = f(S) \cup (C \setminus S)$.
> 3. Define a function $g: C \to B$ piecewise on the partition $C = S \cup (C \setminus S)$ by setting $g(x) := f(x)$ for $x \in S$ and $g(x) := x$ for $x \in C \setminus S$ (formally, $G_g := (G_f \cap (S \times B)) \cup (\operatorname{id}_C \cap ((C \setminus S) \times B))$). Prove that $g: C \to B$ is a bijection.

---

# 0.2 The Natural Numbers ($\mathbb{N}$) — Peano Axioms, Recursion, and Arithmetic

In Section 0.1, we assembled the foundational grammar of mathematics: first-order logic, the Zermelo-Fraenkel (ZF) axioms of set theory, ordered pairs, relations, and functions. Yet notice what is conspicuously missing from our universe so far: **numbers**. We cannot yet count the elements of a set, index a sequence $x_0, x_1, x_2, \dots$, or perform even the simplest addition $1 + 1 = 2$. 

In elementary mathematics, we take the natural numbers $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ and their familiar algebraic laws—such as $a + b = b + a$ or $a \cdot (b + c) = a \cdot b + a \cdot c$—as self-evident truths of nature. Why can we not simply rely on intuition in Real Analysis? Because intuition is a treacherous guide when dealing with infinity. If we do not specify *precisely* what the natural numbers are and what rules govern the act of "counting to the next number," we have no rigorous foundation for mathematical induction, recursive definitions, or the infinite limiting processes that define the real line $\mathbb{R}$.

Our mission in this section is to construct the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$, together with their arithmetic operations ($+$ and $\cdot$) and order structure ($\le$), completely from scratch. We will assume *only* the set-theoretic and logical machinery of Section 0.1. Every single algebraic and order-theoretic law you have used since childhood will be proved here as a rigorous theorem in strict logical sequence, without a trace of circularity.

## The Peano Axioms and the Von Neumann Set-Theoretic Model

### The Essence of Counting and the Peano Postulates

If we strip away all cultural notation (such as Hindu-Arabic numerals or Roman numerals), what is the bare structural essence of the counting numbers? Counting requires just two ingredients: a **starting point** (which we call $0$), and a deterministic **step operation** that takes any number $n$ we have already reached and produces the "very next" number, called its **successor** $S(n)$.

However, not just any set with a starting element and a stepping function behaves like the natural numbers. In 1889, Giuseppe Peano distilled the exact structural laws required to ensure that repeated stepping from $0$ generates an unending, non-looping, single-file chain of elements.

> [!abstract] Definition: Peano System (The Peano Axioms)
> A **Peano system** (or **model of the natural numbers**) is a triple $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ consisting of a set $\mathbb{N}$, a distinguished element $0 \in \mathbb{N}$ (called **zero**), and a function $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ (called the **successor function**), satisfying the following five axioms:
> 1. **(P1 — Existence of Zero):** $0 \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. **(P2 — Closure under Successor):** $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ is a total function; that is, for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, there is a unique element $S(n) \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 3. **(P3 — Zero is Not a Successor):** For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, $S(n) \neq 0$. In set-theoretic terms, $0 \notin S(\mathbb{N})$.
> 4. **(P4 — Injectivity of Successor):** The function $S$ is injective; that is, for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    S(n) = S(m) \implies n = m
>    $$
> 5. **(P5 — Principle of Mathematical Induction):** If $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is any subset of $\mathbb{N}$ satisfying both:
>    - **(Base Case):** $0 \in P$, and
>    - **(Inductive Step):** $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \, (n \in P \implies S(n) \in P)$,
>    
>    then $P = \mathbb{N}$.

Once a Peano system $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ is given, we introduce standard human-readable abbreviations for the first few successors of $0$:
$$
1 := S(0), \quad 2 := S(1) = S(S(0)), \quad 3 := S(2) = S(S(S(0))), \quad 4 := S(3)
$$
Notice that P3 immediately guarantees $1 = S(0) \neq 0$, $2 = S(1) \neq 0$, and so on. Moreover, applying the contrapositive of P4 ($n \neq m \implies S(n) \neq S(m)$) to $1 \neq 0$ yields $S(1) \neq S(0)$, which means $2 \neq 1$. Every step produces a genuinely new element!

> [!note] First-Order Predicates vs. Subset Induction
> In Axiom P5, induction is stated in terms of *subsets* $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$. In practice, we frequently want to prove that a first-order logical property $\varphi(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. How do these two perspectives connect? By the **Axiom Schema of Specification** from Section 0.1! Given any well-formed formula $\varphi(n)$, we form the subset
> $$
> P := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : \varphi(n)\} \subseteq \mathbb{N}
> $$
> Proving the base case $\varphi(0)$ shows $0 \in P$. Proving the inductive step $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \, (\varphi(n) \implies \varphi(S(n)))$ shows that $n \in P \implies S(n) \in P$. Axiom P5 then concludes $P = \mathbb{N}$, meaning $\varphi(n)$ is true for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

### Pathological Toy Models: Why Every Peano Axiom is Indispensable

To appreciate the surgical precision of Peano's axioms, let us ask: *What happens if we drop just one of P3, P4, or P5?* Can a structure satisfy the remaining four axioms while failing utterly to look like the natural numbers? By constructing explicit "broken" models, we see why each axiom is logically independent and indispensable.

1. **Dropping P3 (The Cyclic Clock Model):**
   Suppose we satisfy P1, P2, P4, and P5, but allow $0$ to be a successor. Let $X = \{0, a\}$ be a two-element set with $0 \neq a$, and define $S: X \to X$ by $S(0) = a$ and $S(a) = 0$.
   - P1 and P2 clearly hold.
   - P4 holds because $S$ is a bijection on $\{0, a\}$, hence injective.
   - P5 holds because any subset $P \subseteq X$ containing $0$ and closed under $S$ must contain $S(0) = a$, so $P = \{0, a\} = X$.
   - Yet $X$ is finite and loops back to $0$! Axiom P3 is the anchor that prevents the chain of numbers from wrapping around into a finite cycle containing $0$.

2. **Dropping P4 (The Lasso / Collapsing Tail Model):**
   Suppose we satisfy P1, P2, P3, and P5, but drop the injectivity of $S$. Let $X = \{0, 1\}$ with $0 \neq 1$, and define $S: X \to X$ by $S(0) = 1$ and $S(1) = 1$.
   - P1 and P2 hold.
   - P3 holds because $S(0) = 1 \neq 0$ and $S(1) = 1 \neq 0$, so $0$ is never reached by $S$.
   - P5 holds because any subset $P \subseteq X$ containing $0$ and closed under $S$ must contain $S(0) = 1$, forcing $P = X$.
   - Yet P4 fails because $S(0) = 1 = S(1)$ while $0 \neq 1$. Without P4, the chain of successors can collide with an earlier nonzero element and get trapped in a loop forever.

3. **Dropping P5 (The Unreachable Rogue Copy Model):**
   Suppose we satisfy P1, P2, P3, and P4, but omit the Induction Axiom P5. Imagine taking a true copy of the natural numbers $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$ together with a completely separate, disjoint chain of "rogue elements" $Z' = \{\dots, \omega_{-2}, \omega_{-1}, \omega_0, \omega_1, \omega_2, \dots\}$ (or even a second disconnected ray $\{\omega_0, \omega_1, \omega_2, \dots\}$ with $\omega_k \mapsto \omega_{k+1}$, where $\omega_0$ is has a predecessor $\omega_{-1}$ so that P3 isn't even threatened, or simply a bi-infinite chain where every $\omega_k$ has successor $\omega_{k+1}$). On the union $X = \mathbb{N} \cup Z'$, define $S(n) = n+1$ on $\mathbb{N}$ and $S(\omega_k) = \omega_{k+1}$ on $Z'$.
   - P1 and P2 hold ($0 \in X$ and $S: X \to X$ is a function).
   - P3 holds ($0$ has no predecessor in $\mathbb{N}$ or in $Z'$).
   - P4 holds ($S$ is injective on $\mathbb{N}$, injective on $Z'$, and their images are disjoint).
   - However, $X$ contains "junk" elements in $Z'$ that can never be reached from $0$ by applying $S$ finitely many times! The subset $P = \mathbb{N} \subsetneq X$ contains $0$ and is closed under $S$, yet $P \neq X$.
   - Thus, **Axiom P5 (Induction) is a minimality axiom**: it guarantees that $\mathbb{N}$ contains *nothing else* besides $0$ and the elements reachable from $0$ by repeated application of $S$.

### The Fundamental Predecessor Lemma

As our very first demonstration of the power of Axiom P5, let us prove something that feels completely obvious intuitively, yet cannot be deduced from P1–P4 alone: *every nonzero natural number is the successor of some natural number*. In our third pathological model above, if we attached an isolated starting element $\omega_0 \neq 0$ with $S(\omega_k) = \omega_{k+1}$, then $\omega_0 \neq 0$ would have no predecessor! Induction rules out such orphan elements.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Fundamental Predecessor Lemma
> Let $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ be a Peano system. For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $n \neq 0$, there exists a **unique** $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = n$. Equivalently, $\mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\} = S(\mathbb{N})$, and $S$ restricts to a bijection from $\mathbb{N}$ onto $\mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$.

**Proof:**
We must prove both the *existence* and the *uniqueness* of the predecessor $m$ for any $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $n \neq 0$.

1. **Existence (via Peano Induction):**
   Define the subset $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ consisting of $0$ together with the image of $S$:
   $$
   P := \{0\} \cup S(\mathbb{N}) = \{n \in \mathbb{N} : n = 0 \lor \exists m \in \mathbb{N}, \, S(m) = n\}
   $$
   We apply Axiom P5 to show that $P = \mathbb{N}$:
   - **Base Case:** By definition of $P$, $0 \in \{0\} \subseteq P$, so $0 \in P$.
   - **Inductive Step:** Let $n \in \mathbb{N}$ be arbitrary and suppose $n \in P$. (Notice that we do not even need to use the inductive hypothesis $n \in P$ here, though it is harmless!) Since $n \in \mathbb{N}$, the element $S(n)$ belongs to $S(\mathbb{N})$ by definition of the image of $S$ (simply taking $m = n$). Therefore, $S(n) \in \{0\} \cup S(\mathbb{N}) = P$.
   
   By Axiom P5 (Mathematical Induction), we conclude that $P = \mathbb{N}$. Consequently, for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, if $n \neq 0$, then $n \notin \{0\}$, which forces $n \in S(\mathbb{N})$. Thus there exists at least one $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = n$.

2. **Uniqueness (via Injectivity of $S$):**
   Suppose $m_1, m_2 \in \mathbb{N}$ both satisfy $S(m_1) = n$ and $S(m_2) = n$. Then $S(m_1) = S(m_2)$. By Axiom P4 (injectivity of $S$), it follows immediately that $m_1 = m_2$.

Finally, since $0 \notin S(\mathbb{N})$ by Axiom P3 and every $n \neq 0$ lies in $S(\mathbb{N})$, we have $S(\mathbb{N}) = \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, so $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ is both injective and surjective, hence a bijection. $\blacksquare$

### Existence in ZF Set Theory: The Axiom of Infinity and Von Neumann Ordinals

Wait a moment: we have stated the Peano Axioms for a triple $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$, and we have begun proving theorems *assuming* such a triple exists. But **does a Peano system actually exist inside ZF set theory?** If no set satisfies P1–P5, then every theorem we prove about $\mathbb{N}$ is vacuously true about the empty universe!

Using the ZF axioms of Empty Set, Pairing, and Union from Section 0.1, we can build individual finite sets one by one. In 1923, John von Neumann proposed a brilliant set-theoretic definition of $0$ and the successor operation $S$ in which each natural number $n$ is literally the set of all natural numbers preceding it:

> [!abstract] Definition: Von Neumann Successor and Finite Ordinals
> For any set $x$, its **Von Neumann successor** is defined via Pairing and Union as
> $$
> S(x) := x \cup \{x\}
> $$
> Starting from the empty set $0 := \emptyset$, the first few **Von Neumann natural numbers** are constructed explicitly as:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> 0 &:= \emptyset \\
> 1 &:= S(0) = 0 \cup \{0\} = \emptyset \cup \{\emptyset\} = \{0\} = \{\emptyset\} \\
> 2 &:= S(1) = 1 \cup \{1\} = \{0\} \cup \{1\} = \{0, 1\} = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\} \\
> 3 &:= S(2) = 2 \cup \{2\} = \{0, 1\} \cup \{2\} = \{0, 1, 2\} = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}, \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}\}
> \end{aligned}
> $$

Notice the remarkable elegance of Von Neumann's construction: the set $0$ has $0$ elements, $1 = \{0\}$ has $1$ element, $2 = \{0, 1\}$ has $2$ elements, and $3 = \{0, 1, 2\}$ has $3$ elements! Furthermore, membership $\in$ directly mirrors the "less than" relation: $0 \in 1 \in 2 \in 3$.

However, the axioms of Empty Set, Pairing, Union, Power Set, and Specification only allow us to form new sets from *already existing* sets in finitely many steps. None of those axioms can gather all infinitely many sets $0, 1, 2, 3, \dots$ into a single completed set $\mathbb{N}$. To cross the bridge from the finite to the infinite, ZF set theory invokes a dedicated existence postulate: the **Axiom of Infinity**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Inductive Set and the Axiom of Infinity
> A set $I$ is called **inductive** (or **successor-closed containing $\emptyset$**) if it satisfies two conditions:
> 1. $\emptyset \in I$, and
> 2. $\forall x \, (x \in I \implies x \cup \{x\} \in I)$.
> 
> **The Axiom of Infinity (ZF):** There exists at least one inductive set $I_0$.

An inductive set $I_0$ guaranteed by the Axiom of Infinity certainly contains $0, 1, 2, \dots$, but it might also contain extraneous "junk" elements (just like our third pathological model earlier). How do we pare $I_0$ down to the minimal set containing *only* the natural numbers? We take the intersection of **all** inductive subsets of $I_0$!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Set-Theoretic Construction of $\mathbb{N}$
> Let $I_0$ be an inductive set provided by the Axiom of Infinity, and let $\mathcal{F}$ be the collection of all inductive subsets of $I_0$:
> $$
> \mathcal{F} := \{A \in \mathcal{P}(I_0) : \emptyset \in A \land \forall x \in A \, (x \cup \{x\} \in A)\}
> $$
> Define the set $\mathbb{N}$ as the intersection of the family $\mathcal{F}$:
> $$
> \mathbb{N} := \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A = \{x \in I_0 : \forall A \in \mathcal{F} \, (x \in A)\}
> $$
> Then $\mathbb{N}$ is well-defined, independent of the choice of $I_0$, and the triple $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ with $0 := \emptyset$ and $S(n) := n \cup \{n\}$ satisfies all five Peano Axioms P1–P5.

**Proof:**
First, note that $I_0 \in \mathcal{F}$, so $\mathcal{F}$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathcal{P}(I_0)$ formed via the Axiom of Specification, and its intersection $\mathbb{N}$ is a well-defined subset of $I_0$. (If $I_1$ is any other inductive set, then $\mathbb{N} \cap I_1 \in \mathcal{F}$ is an inductive subset of $I_0$, so $\mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{N} \cap I_1 \subseteq I_1$, which shows by symmetry that $\mathbb{N}$ is a subset of *every* inductive set and thus completely independent of $I_0$.) Let us verify P1–P5:

1. **Verification of P1 ($0 \in \mathbb{N}$):** Since every $A \in \mathcal{F}$ is inductive, $0 = \emptyset \in A$ for all $A \in \mathcal{F}$. By definition of intersection, $0 \in \mathbb{N}$.
2. **Verification of P2 ($S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$):** Let $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Then for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$, we have $n \in A$. Because each $A \in \mathcal{F}$ is inductive, $S(n) = n \cup \{n\} \in A$ for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$. Hence $S(n) \in \mathbb{N}$, making $S$ a well-defined function from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{N}$.
3. **Verification of P3 ($S(n) \neq 0$):** For any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we have $n \in \{n\} \subseteq n \cup \{n\} = S(n)$. Since $S(n)$ contains the element $n$, whereas $0 = \emptyset$ has no elements, the Axiom of Extensionality ensures $S(n) \neq 0$.
4. **Verification of P5 (Mathematical Induction):** We verify P5 before P4 because the minimality of $\mathbb{N}$ gives P5 immediately, and we can use P5 (or Problem 0.2.4) to establish P4! Suppose $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ satisfies $0 \in P$ and $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \, (n \in P \implies S(n) \in P)$. Since $\mathbb{N} \subseteq I_0$, we have $P \subseteq I_0$, so $P \in \mathcal{P}(I_0)$ is itself an inductive subset of $I_0$. Thus $P \in \mathcal{F}$. By definition of $\mathbb{N} = \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$, we have $\mathbb{N} \subseteq P$. Combining $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ and $\mathbb{N} \subseteq P$ gives $P = \mathbb{N}$ by Extensionality.
5. **Verification of P4 (Injectivity of $S$):** To see why $S(n) = S(m) \implies n = m$ for $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, one first shows by induction (P5) that every $x \in \mathbb{N}$ is a *transitive set*—meaning that every element of $x$ is also a subset of $x$ ($\forall y \in x, \, y \subseteq x$)—and consequently $x \notin x$ (either by induction or the Axiom of Foundation; you will write out this lovely inductive proof in full in **Problem 0.2.4**). Assuming this property, suppose $S(n) = S(m)$, i.e., $n \cup \{n\} = m \cup \{m\}$. If $n \neq m$, then since $n \in n \cup \{n\} = m \cup \{m\}$, we must have $n \in m$, and symmetrically $m \in n \cup \{n\}$ forces $m \in n$. Because $m$ is a transitive set, $n \in m \implies n \subseteq m$, so $m \in n \subseteq m$ implies $m \in m$, contradicting $m \notin m$! Therefore $n = m$. $\blacksquare$

From this point forward, we work purely from the Peano Axioms P1–P5 (without needing to peer inside the set-theoretic internals $n = \{0, 1, \dots, n-1\}$ unless specifically studying the Von Neumann model). In **Problem 0.2.1**, you will prove that any two Peano systems are uniquely isomorphic, meaning P1–P5 completely determine the mathematical structure of $\mathbb{N}$!

## Dedekind's Recursion Theorem and the Construction of Addition

### Why Recursive Definitions Require a Theorem

Now that we have the set $\mathbb{N}$ and the successor function $S$, how do we define addition $n + m$? Intuitively, adding $m$ to $n$ means starting at $n$ and applying the successor function $S$ a total of $m$ times:
$$
n + 0 = n, \quad n + 1 = S(n), \quad n + 2 = S(S(n)), \quad \dots, \quad n + S(m) = S(n + m)
$$
This is a **recursive definition**: we specify the value of a function at $0$, and we give a rule for computing its value at $S(m)$ in terms of its value at $m$.

> [!warning] Recursion is Not Automatic!
> A very common beginner mistake is to confuse **Mathematical Induction (Axiom P5)** with **Recursive Definition**.
> - **Induction** proves that an *already existing* subset $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ equals $\mathbb{N}$.
> - **Recursion** claims that there *exists a function* $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ satisfying $f(0) = a$ and $f(S(m)) = g(m, f(m))$.
> 
> In ZF set theory, a function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ is a subset of $\mathbb{N} \times X$ satisfying the vertical line test ($\forall m \in \mathbb{N}, \, \exists! x \in X, \, (m, x) \in f$). Writing down two equations $f(0) = a$ and $f(S(m)) = g(m, f(m))$ does not magically conjure such a subset of $\mathbb{N} \times X$ into existence! For example, on our cyclic clock model $X = \{0, a\}$ where P3 failed and $S(0) = a, S(a) = 0$, if we tried to define $f(0) = 0$ and $f(S(m)) = S(f(m))$ on $X \to \mathbb{N}$, we would get $f(a) = f(S(0)) = 1$ and $0 = f(0) = f(S(a)) = S(f(a)) = 2$, a contradiction!

It was Richard Dedekind (in his landmark 1888 monograph *Was sind und was sollen die Zahlen?*) who realized that the legitimacy of recursive definitions is a non-trivial theorem that requires all five Peano axioms—especially P3, P4, and P5—to prove.

### Dedekind's Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$

How do we construct a function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ inside $\mathbb{N} \times X$ using only set theory and induction? We use the exact dual of the trick we used to build $\mathbb{N}$: we consider all "candidate graphs" $R \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ that contain the initial point $(0, a)$ and are closed under the recursive stepping rule, and we take their intersection!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Dedekind's Recursion Theorem
> Let $X$ be any non-empty set, let $a \in X$ be a fixed initial element, and let $g: \mathbb{N} \times X \to X$ be any function (called the **transition function**). Then there exists a **unique** function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ satisfying both of the following conditions:
> 1. **(Initial Condition):**
>    $$
>    f(0) = a
>    $$
> 2. **(Recurrence Relation):** For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    f(S(n)) = g(n, f(n))
>    $$

**Proof:**
We divide the proof into two parts: **Uniqueness** (which is quick and uses Induction directly) and **Existence** (which constructs the graph of $f$ as a subset of $\mathbb{N} \times X$).

1. **Proof of Uniqueness:**
   Suppose $f_1: \mathbb{N} \to X$ and $f_2: \mathbb{N} \to X$ are two functions both satisfying conditions (1) and (2). Define the set of agreement:
   $$
   P := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : f_1(n) = f_2(n)\} \subseteq \mathbb{N}
   $$
   - **Base Case:** By condition (1), $f_1(0) = a = f_2(0)$, so $0 \in P$.
   - **Inductive Step:** Suppose $n \in P$, so that $f_1(n) = f_2(n)$. Using condition (2) and the fact that $g$ is a function, we obtain:
     $$
     f_1(S(n)) = g(n, f_1(n)) = g(n, f_2(n)) = f_2(S(n))
     $$
     Hence $S(n) \in P$.
   
   By Axiom P5 (Induction), $P = \mathbb{N}$, so $f_1(n) = f_2(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, meaning $f_1 = f_2$.

2. **Proof of Existence:**
   Recall from Section 0.1 that a function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ is a relation $f \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ such that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, there exists a unique $x \in X$ with $(n, x) \in f$.
   Let us call a subset $R \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ **acceptable** if it satisfies:
   - **(A1):** $(0, a) \in R$, and
   - **(A2):** $\forall n \in \mathbb{N}, \, \forall x \in X, \, \big((n, x) \in R \implies (S(n), g(n, x)) \in R\big)$.
   
   Let $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N} \times X)$ be the collection of all acceptable subsets of $\mathbb{N} \times X$. Notice that $\mathcal{A}$ is non-empty because the entire Cartesian product $R_{\text{full}} := \mathbb{N} \times X$ trivially satisfies (A1) and (A2). Define $f \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ to be the intersection of all acceptable subsets:
   $$
   f := \bigcap_{R \in \mathcal{A}} R = \{(n, x) \in \mathbb{N} \times X : \forall R \in \mathcal{A}, \, (n, x) \in R\}
   $$
   First, observe that $f$ itself is acceptable ($f \in \mathcal{A}$): since $(0, a) \in R$ for every $R \in \mathcal{A}$, we have $(0, a) \in f$; and if $(n, x) \in f$, then $(n, x) \in R$ for every $R \in \mathcal{A}$, so $(S(n), g(n, x)) \in R$ for every $R \in \mathcal{A}$, whence $(S(n), g(n, x)) \in f$. Furthermore, because $f$ is the intersection of $\mathcal{A}$, **$f$ is the minimal acceptable subset**: for any acceptable $R \in \mathcal{A}$, we have $f \subseteq R$.

   It remains only to prove that $f$ is a valid function from $\mathbb{N}$ to $X$. Define the subset $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ of inputs at which $f$ has a unique output:
   $$
   P := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : \exists! x \in X, \, (n, x) \in f\}
   $$
   We prove $P = \mathbb{N}$ via Peano Induction (Axiom P5):
   - **Base Case ($0 \in P$):** Since $f$ is acceptable, we already know $(0, a) \in f$ (existence). To prove uniqueness at $0$, suppose toward a contradiction that there exists some $b \in X$ with $b \neq a$ such that $(0, b) \in f$. Consider the punctured relation obtained by deleting $(0, b)$ from $f$:
     $$
     R' := f \setminus \{(0, b)\}
     $$
     We claim $R'$ is still acceptable! First, since $b \neq a$, $(0, a) \neq (0, b)$, so $(0, a) \in R'$ (satisfying A1). Second, suppose $(n, x) \in R'$. Since $R' \subseteq f$ and $f$ is acceptable, we have $(S(n), g(n, x)) \in f$. Could $(S(n), g(n, x))$ equal the deleted pair $(0, b)$? If $(S(n), g(n, x)) = (0, b)$, then by equality of ordered pairs we would have $S(n) = 0$, which **directly violates Axiom P3** ($S(n) \neq 0$)! Therefore $(S(n), g(n, x)) \neq (0, b)$, which means $(S(n), g(n, x)) \in f \setminus \{(0, b)\} = R'$ (satisfying A2).
     Thus $R'$ is acceptable ($R' \in \mathcal{A}$). By the minimality of $f$, we must have $f \subseteq R' = f \setminus \{(0, b)\}$, contradicting $(0, b) \in f$! Hence $a$ is the unique element of $X$ with $(0, a) \in f$, so $0 \in P$.

   - **Inductive Step ($n \in P \implies S(n) \in P$):** Let $n \in P$. Then there exists a unique $x \in X$ such that $(n, x) \in f$. Since $f$ is acceptable, $(S(n), g(n, x)) \in f$, which establishes the *existence* of an output for $S(n)$.
     To prove *uniqueness* at $S(n)$, suppose toward a contradiction that there exists some $y \in X$ with $y \neq g(n, x)$ such that $(S(n), y) \in f$. Consider the punctured relation obtained by deleting $(S(n), y)$ from $f$:
     $$
     R'' := f \setminus \{(S(n), y)\}
     $$
     We claim $R''$ is acceptable!
     - For (A1): Since $S(n) \neq 0$ by **Axiom P3**, the pair $(0, a)$ cannot equal $(S(n), y)$, so $(0, a) \in R''$.
     - For (A2): Let $(m, z) \in R'' \subseteq f$. Since $f$ is acceptable, $(S(m), g(m, z)) \in f$. We must check that $(S(m), g(m, z)) \neq (S(n), y)$. Suppose toward a contradiction that $(S(m), g(m, z)) = (S(n), y)$. By equality of ordered pairs, $S(m) = S(n)$ and $g(m, z) = y$. By **Axiom P4 (injectivity of $S$)**, $S(m) = S(n)$ implies $m = n$! Thus $(n, z) = (m, z) \in R'' \subseteq f$. But since $n \in P$, there is a *unique* element $x \in X$ with $(n, x) \in f$, which forces $z = x$. Consequently, $y = g(m, z) = g(n, x)$, contradicting our assumption that $y \neq g(n, x)$!
     
     Therefore $(S(m), g(m, z)) \neq (S(n), y)$, so $(S(m), g(m, z)) \in R''$. This proves $R'' \in \mathcal{A}$ is acceptable. By the minimality of $f$, $f \subseteq R'' = f \setminus \{(S(n), y)\}$, contradicting $(S(n), y) \in f$. Thus $g(n, x)$ is the unique output for $S(n)$, meaning $S(n) \in P$.

   By Axiom P5 (Induction), $P = \mathbb{N}$. Therefore $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ is a well-defined function.
