Since $(0, a) \in f$, we have $f(0) = a$; and for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, since $(n, f(n)) \in f$ and $f$ is acceptable, $(S(n), g(n, f(n))) \in f$, which means $f(S(n)) = g(n, f(n))$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] How Each Peano Axiom Powered the Proof
> Pause to admire how the proof of Dedekind's Recursion Theorem used every single Peano axiom:
> - **Axiom P3 ($S(n) \neq 0$)** guaranteed that the recursive step never overwrote the initial value $f(0) = a$.
> - **Axiom P4 ($S(m) = S(n) \implies m = n$)** guaranteed that two distinct predecessors $m \neq n$ could never collide at the same successor $S(n)$ and assign conflicting values to $f(S(n))$.
> - **Axiom P5 (Induction)** drove both the uniqueness proof and the verification that $f$ is single-valued and defined on all of $\mathbb{N}$.

### Recursive Definition of Addition on $\mathbb{N}$

With Dedekind's Recursion Theorem in hand, we can rigorously define addition on $\mathbb{N}$. Fix an arbitrary natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Applying Dedekind's Recursion Theorem with $X = \mathbb{N}$, initial element $a = n$, and transition function $g(m, x) = S(x)$, we obtain a unique function $A_n: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying $A_n(0) = n$ and $A_n(S(m)) = S(A_n(m))$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Writing $n + m$ for $A_n(m)$ gives our formal definition of addition.

> [!abstract] Definition: Addition on $\mathbb{N}$
> **Addition** on $\mathbb{N}$ is the binary operation $+: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ defined recursively for each fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$ by the two rules:
> 1. **(Right Additive Identity):**
>    $$
>    n + 0 := n
>    $$
> 2. **(Right Successor Shift):** For every $m \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    n + S(m) := S(n + m)
>    $$

Before proving general theorems, let us check how this definition interacts with $1 := S(0)$. For any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, applying rule (2) with $m = 0$ and then rule (1) yields:
$$
n + 1 = n + S(0) = S(n + 0) = S(n)
$$
Thus, adding $1$ on the right is literally the successor operation $S(n)$! In particular, $1 + 1 = 1 + S(0) = S(1) = 2$.

### The Algebraic Laws of Addition (Strict Non-Circular Sequence)

Look carefully at the definition of addition above: it is **completely asymmetric**! We know by definition that $n + 0 = n$ and $n + S(m) = S(n + m)$, where $0$ and $S(m)$ sit on the *right*. We do **not** yet know that $0 + n = n$, nor do we know that $S(m) + n = S(m + n)$ when $0$ or $S(m)$ sits on the *left*! 

If we try to prove commutativity $n + m = m + n$ directly by induction on $m$, the base case requires $n + 0 = 0 + n$, and the inductive step requires simplifying $S(m) + n$. Therefore, we must first prove the left-hand versions of the two recursive rules as standalone lemmas via induction.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Left Additive Identity
> For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> 0 + n = n
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $P := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : 0 + n = n\}$. We proceed by Mathematical Induction (Axiom P5) on $n$:
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** By the first defining rule of addition ($m + 0 = m$ applied with $m = 0$), we have $0 + 0 = 0$. Thus $0 \in P$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $n \in P$, so that $0 + n = n$ (our inductive hypothesis). We compute $0 + S(n)$ using the second defining rule of addition ($m + S(n) = S(m + n)$ with $m = 0$) followed by the inductive hypothesis:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  0 + S(n) &= S(0 + n) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+ \text{ on the right)} \\
           &= S(n)     \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis } 0 + n = n\text{)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $S(n) \in P$.

By Axiom P5, $P = \mathbb{N}$, so $0 + n = n$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!theorem] Lemma: Left Successor Shift
> For all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> S(m) + n = S(m + n)
> $$

**Proof:**
Fix an arbitrary $m \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_m := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : S(m) + n = S(m + n)\}$. We induct on $n$:
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** Using the first defining rule of addition ($k + 0 = k$) twice, we have:
  $$
  S(m) + 0 = S(m) = S(m + 0)
  $$
  Thus $0 \in P_m$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $n \in P_m$, so that $S(m) + n = S(m + n)$. We compute $S(m) + S(n)$ using the second defining rule of addition and the inductive hypothesis:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  S(m) + S(n) &= S\big(S(m) + n\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+ \text{ on the right)} \\
              &= S\big(S(m + n)\big) \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
              &= S\big(m + S(n)\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+ \text{ on the right: } m + S(n) = S(m + n)\text{)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $S(n) \in P_m$.

By Axiom P5, $P_m = \mathbb{N}$ for every $m \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

With the two left-hand lemmas established, commutativity of addition falls into place effortlessly.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Commutativity of Addition
> For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> n + m = m + n
> $$

**Proof:**
Fix an arbitrary $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_n := \{m \in \mathbb{N} : n + m = m + n\}$. We induct on $m$:
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** By the definition of addition, $n + 0 = n$. By the Left Additive Identity Lemma, $0 + n = n$. Therefore $n + 0 = 0 + n$, so $0 \in P_n$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $m \in P_n$, so that $n + m = m + n$. Using the definition of addition on the left-hand side and the Left Successor Shift Lemma on the right-hand side, we compute:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  n + S(m) &= S(n + m) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+\text{)} \\
           &= S(m + n) \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis } n + m = m + n\text{)} \\
           &= S(m) + n \quad &&\text{(by the Left Successor Shift Lemma)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $S(m) \in P_n$.

By Axiom P5, $P_n = \mathbb{N}$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

Next, we prove **Associativity of Addition**. When proving an identity involving three variables $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, which variable should we induct on? Look at the definition of addition: the recursive rule $x + S(y) = S(x + y)$ operates on the **rightmost** summand. Therefore, inducting on the rightmost variable $c$ makes the proof remarkably clean (in fact, associativity does not even require commutativity!).

> [!theorem] Theorem: Associativity of Addition
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> (a + b) + c = a + (b + c)
> $$

**Proof:**
Fix arbitrary $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_{a,b} := \{c \in \mathbb{N} : (a + b) + c = a + (b + c)\}$. We induct on $c$:
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** Using the definition of addition ($x + 0 = x$) on both sides, we have:
  $$
  (a + b) + 0 = a + b = a + (b + 0)
  $$
  Thus $0 \in P_{a,b}$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $c \in P_{a,b}$, so that $(a + b) + c = a + (b + c)$. Applying the second defining rule of addition ($x + S(y) = S(x + y)$) repeatedly, we obtain:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (a + b) + S(c) &= S\big((a + b) + c\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+\text{)} \\
                 &= S\big(a + (b + c)\big) \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                 &= a + S(b + c)           \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+\text{)} \\
                 &= a + \big(b + S(c)\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+\text{)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $S(c) \in P_{a,b}$.

By Axiom P5, $P_{a,b} = \mathbb{N}$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

In $\mathbb{N}$, we do not have negative numbers or additive inverses (so we cannot "add $-c$ to both sides" of an equation $a + c = b + c$). Nevertheless, we *can* cancel a common summand $c$ from both sides of an equation! How? Because adding $c$ corresponds to applying the successor function $S$ a total of $c$ times, and **Axiom P4 guarantees that $S$ is injective**!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Additive Cancellation Law
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> a + c = b + c \implies a = b
> $$
> By Commutativity of Addition, left cancellation also holds: $c + a = c + b \implies a = b$.

**Proof:**
Fix arbitrary $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_{a,b} := \{c \in \mathbb{N} : a + c = b + c \implies a = b\}$. We induct on $c$:
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** Suppose $a + 0 = b + 0$. Since $a + 0 = a$ and $b + 0 = b$ by definition of addition, we immediately obtain $a = b$. Thus $0 \in P_{a,b}$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $c \in P_{a,b}$, so that $a + c = b + c \implies a = b$. Now suppose $a + S(c) = b + S(c)$. By the definition of addition, this equation is equivalent to:
  $$
  S(a + c) = S(b + c)
  $$
  By **Axiom P4 (injectivity of $S$)**, $S(a + c) = S(b + c)$ implies:
  $$
  a + c = b + c
  $$
  Applying our inductive hypothesis ($c \in P_{a,b}$) to $a + c = b + c$, we conclude $a = b$. Thus $S(c) \in P_{a,b}$.

By Axiom P5, $P_{a,b} = \mathbb{N}$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$. Finally, if $c + a = c + b$, then Commutativity of Addition gives $a + c = b + c$, so $a = b$. $\blacksquare$

We close our investigation of addition with a crucial structural property that distinguishes $\mathbb{N}$ from $\mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{Q},$ and $\mathbb{R}$: the only way two natural numbers can sum to $0$ is if *both* summands are $0$. Notice how the proof relies directly on the Fundamental Predecessor Lemma and Axiom P3!

> [!theorem] Proposition: Sum Equal to Zero (Absence of Non-Trivial Additive Inverses)
> For all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> a + b = 0 \iff (a = 0 \land b = 0)
> $$

**Proof:**
If $a = 0$ and $b = 0$, then $a + b = 0 + 0 = 0$ by definition of addition.
Conversely, suppose $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfy $a + b = 0$. Suppose toward a contradiction that $b \neq 0$. By the **Fundamental Predecessor Lemma**, there exists $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $b = S(m)$. Substituting $b = S(m)$ into $a + b = 0$ and using the definition of addition gives:
$$
0 = a + b = a + S(m) = S(a + m)
$$
Thus $S(a + m) = 0$, where $a + m \in \mathbb{N}$. This **directly contradicts Axiom P3**, which states that $S(n) \neq 0$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$! Therefore our assumption $b \neq 0$ is false, forcing $b = 0$. Substituting $b = 0$ back into $a + b = 0$ yields $a = a + 0 = 0$. Thus $a = 0$ and $b = 0$. $\blacksquare$

## Recursive Construction of Multiplication and Semiring Structure

### Recursive Definition of Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$

Having constructed addition and established its fundamental properties, we now define **multiplication** on $\mathbb{N}$. What does $n \cdot m$ mean intuitively? It means adding $n$ to itself $m$ times, starting from $0$:
$$
n \cdot 0 = 0, \quad n \cdot 1 = n, \quad n \cdot 2 = n + n, \quad \dots, \quad n \cdot S(m) = (n \cdot m) + n
$$
For any fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we apply **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** with $X = \mathbb{N}$, initial value $a = 0$, and transition function $g(m, x) = x + n$ (which is a well-defined function now that addition has been constructed!). This guarantees a unique function $M_n: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$, written $M_n(m) = n \cdot m$, satisfying the following recursive laws:

> [!abstract] Definition: Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$
> **Multiplication** on $\mathbb{N}$ is the binary operation $\cdot : \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ defined recursively for each fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$ by the two rules:
> 1. **(Right Zero Annihilation):**
>    $$
>    n \cdot 0 := 0
>    $$
> 2. **(Right Successor Expansion):** For every $m \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    n \cdot S(m) := (n \cdot m) + n
>    $$

Just as with addition, we frequently write $nm$ instead of $n \cdot m$ when there is no risk of ambiguity, and we assign multiplication higher precedence than addition (so $a \cdot b + c$ means $(a \cdot b) + c$).

### The Algebraic Laws of Multiplication (Strict Non-Circular Sequence)

Once again, notice the asymmetry of our recursive definition: $0$ and $S(m)$ appear on the *right* in $n \cdot 0 = 0$ and $n \cdot S(m) = (n \cdot m) + n$. To prove that multiplication is commutative ($n \cdot m = m \cdot n$), we must first establish the corresponding left-hand rules via induction on the right-hand factor. Watch closely how the proof of the Left Successor Rule for multiplication crucially uses both **Associativity** and **Commutativity** of addition!

> [!theorem] Lemma: Left Zero Annihilation and Left Successor Rule for Multiplication
> For all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **(Left Zero Annihilation):**
>    $$
>    0 \cdot n = 0
>    $$
> 2. **(Left Successor Rule):**
>    $$
>    S(m) \cdot n = (m \cdot n) + n
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. **Proof of $0 \cdot n = 0$:**
   Define $P := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : 0 \cdot n = 0\}$. We induct on $n$:
   - **Base Case ($n = 0$):** By the first defining rule of multiplication ($k \cdot 0 = 0$ with $k = 0$), we have $0 \cdot 0 = 0$. Thus $0 \in P$.
   - **Inductive Step:** Suppose $n \in P$, so that $0 \cdot n = 0$. Using the second defining rule of multiplication, the inductive hypothesis, and the definition of addition, we compute:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     0 \cdot S(n) &= (0 \cdot n) + 0 \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)} \\
                  &= 0 + 0           \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis } 0 \cdot n = 0\text{)} \\
                  &= 0               \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+\text{)}
     \end{aligned}
     $$
     Thus $S(n) \in P$. By Axiom P5, $P = \mathbb{N}$.

2. **Proof of $S(m) \cdot n = (m \cdot n) + n$:**
   Fix an arbitrary $m \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_m := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : S(m) \cdot n = (m \cdot n) + n\}$. We induct on $n$:
   - **Base Case ($n = 0$):** By the definition of multiplication and addition,
     $$
     S(m) \cdot 0 = 0 = 0 + 0 = (m \cdot 0) + 0
     $$
     Thus $0 \in P_m$.
   - **Inductive Step:** Suppose $n \in P_m$, so that $S(m) \cdot n = (m \cdot n) + n$. We expand $S(m) \cdot S(n)$ using the definition of multiplication, the inductive hypothesis, and the algebraic laws of addition proved earlier:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     S(m) \cdot S(n) &= \big(S(m) \cdot n\big) + S(m) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)} \\
                     &= \big((m \cdot n) + n\big) + S(m) \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                     &= S\Big(\big((m \cdot n) + n\big) + m\Big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+: x + S(y) = S(x + y)\text{)} \\
                     &= S\Big((m \cdot n) + (n + m)\Big) \quad &&\text{(by Associativity of Addition)} \\
                     &= S\Big((m \cdot n) + (m + n)\Big) \quad &&\text{(by Commutativity of Addition: } n + m = m + n\text{)} \\
                     &= S\Big(\big((m \cdot n) + m\big) + n\Big) \quad &&\text{(by Associativity of Addition)} \\
                     &= S\big((m \cdot S(n)) + n\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot: (m \cdot n) + m = m \cdot S(n)\text{)} \\
                     &= \big(m \cdot S(n)\big) + S(n) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+: S(x + y) = x + S(y)\text{)}
     \end{aligned}
     $$
     Thus $S(n) \in P_m$. By Axiom P5, $P_m = \mathbb{N}$ for every $m \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!theorem] Theorem: Commutativity of Multiplication
> For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> n \cdot m = m \cdot n
> $$

**Proof:**
Fix an arbitrary $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_n := \{m \in \mathbb{N} : n \cdot m = m \cdot n\}$. We induct on $m$:
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** By definition of multiplication, $n \cdot 0 = 0$. By Left Zero Annihilation, $0 \cdot n = 0$. Thus $n \cdot 0 = 0 \cdot n$, so $0 \in P_n$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $m \in P_n$, so that $n \cdot m = m \cdot n$. Using the definition of multiplication, the inductive hypothesis, and the Left Successor Rule for multiplication, we obtain:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  n \cdot S(m) &= (n \cdot m) + n \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)} \\
               &= (m \cdot n) + n \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis } n \cdot m = m \cdot n\text{)} \\
               &= S(m) \cdot n    \quad &&\text{(by the Left Successor Rule for }\cdot\text{)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $S(m) \in P_n$. By Axiom P5, $P_n = \mathbb{N}$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!theorem] Proposition: Multiplicative Identity
> Let $1 := S(0)$. For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> n \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot n = n
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Using $1 = S(0)$, the recursive definition of multiplication, the Left Additive Identity Lemma ($0 + n = n$), and Commutativity of Multiplication, we compute:
$$
\begin{aligned}
n \cdot 1 &= n \cdot S(0) \\
          &= (n \cdot 0) + n \\
          &= 0 + n \\
          &= n
\end{aligned}
$$
By Commutativity of Multiplication, $1 \cdot n = n \cdot 1 = n$ as well. $\blacksquare$

To prove that multiplication is associative, we first need the **Distributive Law**, which links addition and multiplication. Why must distributivity come *before* associativity of multiplication? Because in the inductive step for $(a \cdot b) \cdot S(c)$, the definition of multiplication produces $(a \cdot b) \cdot c + a \cdot b$, whereas on the right-hand side $a \cdot (b \cdot S(c)) = a \cdot (b \cdot c + b)$, which cannot be expanded without the Distributive Law!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Distributive Law of Multiplication over Addition
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **(Left Distributivity):**
>    $$
>    a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)
>    $$
> 2. **(Right Distributivity):**
>    $$
>    (b + c) \cdot a = (b \cdot a) + (c \cdot a)
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. **Proof of Left Distributivity:**
   Fix arbitrary $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_{a,b} := \{c \in \mathbb{N} : a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)\}$. We induct on $c$:
   - **Base Case ($c = 0$):** Using $b + 0 = b$, $a \cdot 0 = 0$, and $(a \cdot b) + 0 = a \cdot b$, we have:
     $$
     a \cdot (b + 0) = a \cdot b = (a \cdot b) + 0 = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot 0)
     $$
     Thus $0 \in P_{a,b}$.
   - **Inductive Step:** Suppose $c \in P_{a,b}$, so that $a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)$. Using the recursive definitions of addition and multiplication together with Associativity of Addition, we compute:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     a \cdot \big(b + S(c)\big) &= a \cdot S(b + c) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }+\text{)} \\
                                &= a \cdot (b + c) + a \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)} \\
                                &= \big((a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)\big) + a \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                                &= (a \cdot b) + \big((a \cdot c) + a\big) \quad &&\text{(by Associativity of Addition)} \\
                                &= (a \cdot b) + \big(a \cdot S(c)\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)}
     \end{aligned}
     $$
     Thus $S(c) \in P_{a,b}$. By Axiom P5, $P_{a,b} = \mathbb{N}$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.

2. **Proof of Right Distributivity:**
   By Commutativity of Multiplication and Left Distributivity, for any $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$ we have:
   $$
   (b + c) \cdot a = a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c) = (b \cdot a) + (c \cdot a)
   $$
   This completes the proof. $\blacksquare$

> [!theorem] Theorem: Associativity of Multiplication
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> (a \cdot b) \cdot c = a \cdot (b \cdot c)
> $$

**Proof:**
Fix arbitrary $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, and define $P_{a,b} := \{c \in \mathbb{N} : (a \cdot b) \cdot c = a \cdot (b \cdot c)\}$. We induct on $c$:
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** Using the first defining rule of multiplication ($x \cdot 0 = 0$) repeatedly, we have:
  $$
  (a \cdot b) \cdot 0 = 0 = a \cdot 0 = a \cdot (b \cdot 0)
  $$
  Thus $0 \in P_{a,b}$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $c \in P_{a,b}$, so that $(a \cdot b) \cdot c = a \cdot (b \cdot c)$. Using the definition of multiplication, the inductive hypothesis, and Left Distributivity, we compute:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (a \cdot b) \cdot S(c) &= \big((a \cdot b) \cdot c\big) + (a \cdot b) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)} \\
                         &= \big(a \cdot (b \cdot c)\big) + (a \cdot b) \quad &&\text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                         &= a \cdot \big((b \cdot c) + b\big) \quad &&\text{(by Left Distributivity)} \\
                         &= a \cdot \big(b \cdot S(c)\big) \quad &&\text{(by definition of }\cdot\text{)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $S(c) \in P_{a,b}$. By Axiom P5, $P_{a,b} = \mathbb{N}$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Algebraic Summary: $(\mathbb{N}, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ is a Commutative Semiring
> Together, the theorems proved in the last two subsections show that $(\mathbb{N}, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ forms a **commutative semiring** (sometimes called a *rig*—a **r**ing without **n**egatives):
> - $(\mathbb{N}, +, 0)$ is a commutative monoid with additive cancellation;
> - $(\mathbb{N}, \cdot, 1)$ is a commutative monoid;
> - Multiplication distributes over addition from both sides, and $0$ annihilates all elements under multiplication.

Finally, we prove that $\mathbb{N}$ has **no zero divisors**: a product of two natural numbers is zero if and only if at least one of the factors is zero.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Absence of Zero Divisors in $\mathbb{N}$
> For all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> a \cdot b = 0 \iff (a = 0 \lor b = 0)
> $$
> Equivalently, if $a \neq 0$ and $b \neq 0$, then $a \cdot b \neq 0$.

**Proof:**
If $a = 0$ or $b = 0$, then $a \cdot b = 0$ by Left or Right Zero Annihilation.
Conversely, suppose $a \cdot b = 0$. If $b = 0$, the claim $(a = 0 \lor b = 0)$ holds immediately. Suppose instead that $b \neq 0$. By the **Fundamental Predecessor Lemma**, there exists $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $b = S(m)$. Substituting $b = S(m)$ into $a \cdot b = 0$ and using the recursive definition of multiplication gives:
$$
0 = a \cdot b = a \cdot S(m) = (a \cdot m) + a
$$
Thus $(a \cdot m) + a = 0$. By our earlier Proposition (**Sum Equal to Zero**), a sum of two natural numbers equals $0$ if and only if both summands equal $0$. Therefore $a \cdot m = 0$ and $a = 0$. In either case, $a = 0$ or $b = 0$. $\blacksquare$

## Order on $\mathbb{N}$, Trichotomy, Strong Induction, and Well-Ordering

### Defining the Order Relation via Addition

How should we define what it means for one natural number $a$ to be less than or equal to another natural number $b$ ($a \le b$)? Rather than introducing $\le$ as a new primitive relation, we can define $\le$ *algebraically* in terms of addition: $a \le b$ means that we can get from $a$ to $b$ by adding some natural number $d \in \mathbb{N}$!

> [!abstract] Definition: Order Relations $\le$ and $<$ on $\mathbb{N}$
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 1. We say that $a$ is **less than or equal to** $b$, written $a \le b$ (or equivalently $b \ge a$), if there exists $d \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
>    $$
>    a + d = b
>    $$
> 2. We say that $a$ is **strictly less than** $b$, written $a < b$ (or equivalently $b > a$), if
>    $$
>    a \le b \quad \text{and} \quad a \neq b
>    $$

Let us immediately establish a concrete algebraic characterization of the strict inequality $a < b$: whereas $a \le b$ means $b$ is obtained from $a$ by adding *some* $d \in \mathbb{N}$, the strict inequality $a < b$ means $b$ is obtained from $a$ by adding a **nonzero** natural number $S(k)$.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Algebraic Characterization of Strict Inequality
> For all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. $0 \le a$ for every $a \in \mathbb{N}$, and there is no $a \in \mathbb{N}$ with $a < 0$.
> 2. $a < b \iff \exists k \in \mathbb{N}, \, a + S(k) = b \iff S(a) \le b$.

**Proof:**
1. For any $a \in \mathbb{N}$, we have $0 + a = a$ by the Left Additive Identity Lemma, so $0 \le a$ (taking $d = a$). If $a \le 0$, then $a + d = 0$ for some $d \in \mathbb{N}$, which implies $a = 0$ by the Sum Equal to Zero Proposition; hence $a < 0$ ($a \le 0 \land a \neq 0$) is impossible.
2. Suppose $a < b$, so $a \le b$ and $a \neq b$. By definition of $a \le b$, there exists $d \in \mathbb{N}$ with $a + d = b$. If $d = 0$, then $b = a + 0 = a$, contradicting $a \neq b$. Thus $d \neq 0$. By the **Fundamental Predecessor Lemma**, there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $d = S(k)$, yielding $a + S(k) = b$.
   Conversely, suppose $a + S(k) = b$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Then $a \le b$ (taking $d = S(k)$). Could $a = b$? If $a = b$, then $a + S(k) = a = a + 0$. By the **Additive Cancellation Law** ($c + x = c + y \implies x = y$), cancelling $a$ gives $S(k) = 0$, which **violates Axiom P3**! Thus $a \neq b$, so $a < b$.
   Finally, notice that by the Left Successor Shift Lemma and definition of addition,
   $$
   a + S(k) = S(a + k) = S(a) + k
   $$
   Therefore $\exists k \in \mathbb{N}, \, a + S(k) = b \iff \exists k \in \mathbb{N}, \, S(a) + k = b \iff S(a) \le b$. $\blacksquare$

### Partial Order Properties, Trichotomy, and Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$

Next, we prove that $\le$ is a **total order** on $\mathbb{N}$. Notice how Antisymmetry relies directly on Additive Cancellation and the Sum Equal to Zero Proposition!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Partial Order Properties of $(\mathbb{N}, \le)$
> The relation $\le$ is a partial order on $\mathbb{N}$; that is, for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **(Reflexivity):** $a \le a$.
> 2. **(Antisymmetry):** $(a \le b \land b \le a) \implies a = b$.
> 3. **(Transitivity):** $(a \le b \land b \le c) \implies a \le c$. (Moreover, if $a < b$ and $b \le c$, or if $a \le b$ and $b < c$, then $a < c$.)

**Proof:**
1. **Reflexivity:** For any $a \in \mathbb{N}$, $a + 0 = a$ by definition of addition. Taking $d = 0 \in \mathbb{N}$ shows $a \le a$.
2. **Antisymmetry:** Suppose $a \le b$ and $b \le a$. Then there exist $d_1, d_2 \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $a + d_1 = b$ and $b + d_2 = a$. Substituting the first equation into the second and using Associativity of Addition gives:
   $$
   a + 0 = a = (a + d_1) + d_2 = a + (d_1 + d_2)
   $$
   By the **Additive Cancellation Law**, cancelling $a$ from $a + (d_1 + d_2) = a + 0$ yields:
   $$
   d_1 + d_2 = 0
   $$
   By the **Sum Equal to Zero Proposition**, $d_1 + d_2 = 0$ implies $d_1 = 0$ and $d_2 = 0$. Therefore $b = a + d_1 = a + 0 = a$.
3. **Transitivity:** Suppose $a \le b$ and $b \le c$. Then there exist $d_1, d_2 \in \mathbb{N}$ with $a + d_1 = b$ and $b + d_2 = c$. By Associativity of Addition,
   $$
   c = b + d_2 = (a + d_1) + d_2 = a + (d_1 + d_2)
   $$
   Since $d_1 + d_2 \in \mathbb{N}$, this proves $a \le c$. Furthermore, if $a \neq b$ or $b \neq c$, then $d_1 \neq 0$ or $d_2 \neq 0$, so $d_1 + d_2 \neq 0$ by the Sum Equal to Zero Proposition; hence $a \neq c$ (by Additive Cancellation), giving $a < c$. $\blacksquare$

To upgrade our partial order $\le$ to a *linear* (or *total*) order, we must prove that any two natural numbers $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ are comparable—and more specifically, that the **Trichotomy Law** holds.

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Trichotomy Law for $\mathbb{N}$
> For any two natural numbers $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, **exactly one** of the following three relations holds:
> $$
> a < b, \qquad a = b, \qquad \text{or} \qquad b < a
> $$

**Proof:**
We must prove two things: **Mutual Exclusivity** (at most one of the three holds) and **Exhaustiveness** (at least one of the three holds).

1. **Mutual Exclusivity (At Most One Holds):**
   By definition of strict inequality ($x < y \iff x \le y \land x \neq y$), neither $a < b$ nor $b < a$ can hold simultaneously with $a = b$. And if both $a < b$ and $b < a$ held simultaneously, then in particular $a \le b$ and $b \le a$, which by **Antisymmetry** forces $a = b$, contradicting $a \neq b$. Thus at most one of the three cases can occur.

2. **Exhaustiveness (At Least One Holds — via Induction on $b$):**
   Fix an arbitrary $a \in \mathbb{N}$, and define the subset
   $$
   P_a := \{b \in \mathbb{N} : a < b \lor a = b \lor b < a\}
   $$
   We prove $P_a = \mathbb{N}$ by induction on $b$:
   - **Base Case ($b = 0$):** By our earlier Lemma, $0 \le a$ holds for every $a \in \mathbb{N}$. If $a = 0$, then $a = b$ holds. If $a \neq 0$, then $0 \le a \land 0 \neq a$ gives $0 < a$, i.e., $b < a$. In either case, $0 \in P_a$.
   - **Inductive Step:** Suppose $b \in P_a$. Then one of three cases holds for $a$ and $b$:
     - **Case 1 ($a < b$):** Since $b + 1 = S(b)$ with $1 = S(0)$, we have $b < S(b)$. By Transitivity of $<$, $a < b$ and $b < S(b)$ imply $a < S(b)$.
     - **Case 2 ($a = b$):** Since $b < S(b)$, substituting $a = b$ yields $a < S(b)$.
     - **Case 3 ($b < a$):** By the Algebraic Characterization of Strict Inequality ($b < a \iff \exists k \in \mathbb{N}, \, b + S(k) = a$), there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
       $$
       a = b + S(k) = S(b + k) = S(b) + k
       $$
       Now examine $k \in \mathbb{N}$:
       - If $k = 0$, then $a = S(b) + 0 = S(b)$, so the relation $a = S(b)$ holds!
       - If $k \neq 0$, then by the Fundamental Predecessor Lemma, $k = S(m)$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$, so $a = S(b) + S(m)$, which means $S(b) < a$!
     
     In all three cases, at least one of $a < S(b)$, $a = S(b)$, or $S(b) < a$ holds. Therefore $S(b) \in P_a$.

   By Axiom P5 (Induction), $P_a = \mathbb{N}$ for every $a \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

Notice the key insight uncovered inside Case 3 of the Trichotomy proof above: if $b < a$, then $S(b) \le a$. This immediately yields one of the most fundamental structural properties of the natural numbers—their **discreteness**: there is no natural number strictly between $n$ and its successor $S(n)$!

> [!theorem] Corollary: Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$
> For all $n, k \in \mathbb{N}$, there exists no $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $n < k < S(n)$. Equivalently, for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$:
> $$
> a < S(b) \iff a \le b, \qquad \text{and} \qquad b < a \iff S(b) \le a
> $$

**Proof:**
We already proved in the Algebraic Characterization of Strict Inequality that $b < a \iff S(b) \le a$.
Suppose toward a contradiction that there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $n < k$ and $k < S(n)$. Since $n < k$, the equivalence $n < k \iff S(n) \le k$ gives $S(n) \le k$. Combining $S(n) \le k$ with $k < S(n)$ (which implies $k \le S(n)$ and $k \neq S(n)$) gives $k = S(n)$ by Antisymmetry, contradicting $k \neq S(n)$!
Finally, to see that $a < S(b) \iff a \le b$: if $a \le b$, then since $b < S(b)$, Transitivity gives $a < S(b)$; conversely, if $a < S(b)$, then we cannot have $b < a$ (for $b < a \implies S(b) \le a$, contradicting $a < S(b)$), so by Trichotomy we must have $a < b$ or $a = b$, i.e., $a \le b$. $\blacksquare$

### Order Compatibility with Arithmetic and Multiplicative Cancellation

Next, we verify that the order relations $\le$ and $<$ behave harmonically with addition and multiplication, and we use Trichotomy together with order preservation to prove the **Multiplicative Cancellation Law**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Compatibility of Order with Addition and Multiplication
> Let $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 1. **(Additive Order Preservation and Cancellation):**
>    $$
>    a \le b \iff a + c \le b + c, \qquad \text{and} \qquad a < b \iff a + c < b + c
>    $$
> 2. **(Multiplicative Order Preservation):**
>    $$
>    a \le b \implies a \cdot c \le b \cdot c
>    $$
>    Moreover, if $c \neq 0$, then
>    $$
>    a < b \iff a \cdot c < b \cdot c, \qquad \text{and} \qquad a \le b \iff a \cdot c \le b \cdot c
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. **Additive Compatibility:**
   Suppose $a \le b$, so $a + d = b$ for some $d \in \mathbb{N}$. Using Commutativity and Associativity of Addition, we have:
   $$
   (a + c) + d = (a + d) + c = b + c
   $$
   Thus $a + c \le b + c$. Conversely, if $a + c \le b + c$, then $(a + c) + d = b + c$ for some $d \in \mathbb{N}$, so $(a + d) + c = b + c$. By the **Additive Cancellation Law**, cancelling $c$ yields $a + d = b$, so $a \le b$.
   Furthermore, by the Additive Cancellation Law, $a = b \iff a + c = b + c$ (hence $a \neq b \iff a + c \neq b + c$). Combining $a \le b \iff a + c \le b + c$ with $a \neq b \iff a + c \neq b + c$ gives $a < b \iff a + c < b + c$.

2. **Multiplicative Compatibility:**
   Suppose $a \le b$, so $a + d = b$ for some $d \in \mathbb{N}$. Multiplying by $c$ and applying Right Distributivity yields:
   $$
   (a \cdot c) + (d \cdot c) = (a + d) \cdot c = b \cdot c
   $$
   Since $d \cdot c \in \mathbb{N}$, this shows $a \cdot c \le b \cdot c$.
   Now assume $c \neq 0$. If $a < b$, then $d \neq 0$. Because $d \neq 0$ and $c \neq 0$, the **Absence of Zero Divisors Theorem** guarantees that $d \cdot c \neq 0$! Therefore $(a \cdot c) + (d \cdot c) = b \cdot c$ with $d \cdot c \neq 0$ implies $a \cdot c < b \cdot c$.
   Conversely, for $c \neq 0$, if $a \cdot c < b \cdot c$, then we cannot have $b \le a$ (as that would imply $b \cdot c \le a \cdot c$, contradicting $a \cdot c < b \cdot c$). By the **Trichotomy Law**, since $b \le a$ is false, we must have $a < b$. The equivalence $a \le b \iff a \cdot c \le b \cdot c$ for $c \neq 0$ follows similarly by Trichotomy. $\blacksquare$

> [!theorem] Corollary: Multiplicative Cancellation Law
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$ with $c \neq 0$,
> $$
> a \cdot c = b \cdot c \implies a = b
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$ with $c \neq 0$, and suppose $a \cdot c = b \cdot c$. By the **Trichotomy Law**, either $a < b$, $a = b$, or $b < a$.
- If $a < b$, then since $c \neq 0$, Multiplicative Order Preservation gives $a \cdot c < b \cdot c$, contradicting $a \cdot c = b \cdot c$.
- If $b < a$, then since $c \neq 0$, Multiplicative Order Preservation gives $b \cdot c < a \cdot c$, again contradicting $a \cdot c = b \cdot c$.

By elimination, the only remaining possibility is $a = b$. $\blacksquare$

### Strong Induction and the Well-Ordering Principle (WOP)

In standard Peano Induction (Axiom P5), to prove that a property holds for $S(n)$, we are allowed to assume only that it holds for the single immediate predecessor $n$. In many mathematical arguments—such as factoring a number into primes or proving the Euclidean division algorithm—knowing the statement for $n$ alone is not enough; we need to know that the statement holds for **all** natural numbers $k \le n$ (or all $k < m$). This more powerful-looking principle is called **Strong Induction** (or **Course-of-Values Induction**).

Closely related to Strong Induction is a geometric property of the ordering $(\mathbb{N}, \le)$ called the **Well-Ordering Principle (WOP)**: every non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$ has a smallest element. (Notice that $\mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{Q},$ and $\mathbb{R}$ fail this property—for instance, the set of negative integers has no smallest element, and the open interval $(0, 1) \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ has no smallest element!)

Let us state both principles formally and then prove a landmark theorem: **Peano Induction, Strong Induction, and the Well-Ordering Principle are all logically equivalent!**

> [!abstract] Definition: Principle of Strong Induction and the Well-Ordering Principle
> - **Principle of Strong Induction:** Let $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ be a subset such that for every $m \in \mathbb{N}$,
>   $$
>   \big(\forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, k < m \implies k \in P\big) \implies m \in P
>   $$
>   Then $P = \mathbb{N}$.
> - **Well-Ordering Principle (WOP):** Every non-empty subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ ($A \neq \emptyset$) possesses a **unique minimum (least) element** $m_0 \in A$; that is, there exists a unique $m_0 \in A$ such that $m_0 \le a$ for all $a \in A$.

> [!note] Where Did the Base Case Go in Strong Induction?
> Look at the statement of Strong Induction when $m = 0$: what does the premise $\big(\forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, k < 0 \implies k \in P\big)$ say? Since there are **no** natural numbers $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $k < 0$, the implication $k < 0 \implies k \in P$ is **vacuously true** for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$! Therefore, the condition of Strong Induction at $m = 0$ automatically requires $\text{True} \implies 0 \in P$, which means $0 \in P$ must be verified directly. In practice, when writing a proof by Strong Induction, we always explicitly check the base case $0 \in P$, and then for $m \in \mathbb{N}$ we assume $\{0, 1, \dots, m\} \subseteq P$ to prove $S(m) \in P$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Equivalence of Peano Induction, Strong Induction, and Well-Ordering
> Given Axioms P1–P4 and the arithmetic/order properties derived above, the following three statements are logically equivalent:
> $$
> \text{(1) Peano Induction (Axiom P5)} \iff \text{(2) Strong Induction} \iff \text{(3) Well-Ordering Principle (WOP)}
> $$

**Proof:**
We prove the cycle of implications $(1) \implies (2) \implies (3) \implies (1)$.

1. **Proof that $(\text{Peano Induction}) \implies (\text{Strong Induction})$:**
   Assume Peano Induction (Axiom P5) holds, and let $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ satisfy the Strong Induction hypothesis:
   $$
   \forall m \in \mathbb{N}, \quad \big(\forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, k < m \implies k \in P\big) \implies m \in P
   $$
   Why can we not apply Axiom P5 directly to $P$? Because in Axiom P5, the inductive hypothesis is merely $n \in P$, which is too weak to trigger the premise $\forall k < S(n), \, k \in P$. The classic trick is to **strengthen the inductive hypothesis** by defining an auxiliary set $Q \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ that bundles together all predecessors up to $n$:
   $$
   Q := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : \forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, (k < n \implies k \in P)\}
   $$
   Let us apply Peano Induction (Axiom P5) to the set $Q$:
   - **Base Case ($0 \in Q$):** Since there exists no $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $k < 0$, the statement $\forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, (k < 0 \implies k \in P)$ holds vacuously. Thus $0 \in Q$.
   - **Inductive Step ($n \in Q \implies S(n) \in Q$):** Suppose $n \in Q$. By definition of $Q$, we have $\forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, (k < n \implies k \in P)$.
     First, applying the Strong Induction hypothesis of $P$ with $m = n$, this exact premise implies $n \in P$!
     Now, to show $S(n) \in Q$, let $k \in \mathbb{N}$ be any element satisfying $k < S(n)$. By the **Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$ Corollary** ($k < S(n) \iff k \le n$), we have $k \le n$, which means either $k < n$ or $k = n$.
     - If $k < n$, then $k \in P$ because $n \in Q$.
     - If $k = n$, then $k \in P$ because we just proved $n \in P$!
     
     Thus $\forall k \in \mathbb{N}, \, (k < S(n) \implies k \in P)$, which means $S(n) \in Q$.

   By Axiom P5 (Peano Induction), we conclude that $Q = \mathbb{N}$. Now let $m \in \mathbb{N}$ be arbitrary. Since $Q = \mathbb{N}$, we know $S(m) \in Q$. Since $m < S(m)$, the definition of $S(m) \in Q$ immediately yields $m \in P$. Hence $P = \mathbb{N}$!

2. **Proof that $(\text{Strong Induction}) \implies (\text{Well-Ordering Principle})$:**
   Assume Strong Induction holds. First, note that if a subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a minimum element, that minimum element is **unique**: if $m_1, m_2 \in A$ both satisfy $m_1 \le a$ and $m_2 \le a$ for all $a \in A$, then $m_1 \le m_2$ and $m_2 \le m_1$, so $m_1 = m_2$ by **Antisymmetry**.
   To prove existence, we argue by contraposition (or contradiction). Suppose $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is a subset that has **no minimum element**. We will prove using Strong Induction that $A = \emptyset$!
   Consider the complement of $A$ in $\mathbb{N}$:
   $$
   P := \mathbb{N} \setminus A = \{n \in \mathbb{N} : n \notin A\}
   $$
   Let $m \in \mathbb{N}$ be arbitrary, and suppose that for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $k < m$, we have $k \in P$ (meaning $k \notin A$ for every $k < m$). Could $m$ belong to $A$? Suppose toward a contradiction that $m \in A$. Then for every $a \in A$, we cannot have $a < m$ (since no element strictly less than $m$ belongs to $A$). By the **Trichotomy Law**, for every $a \in A$ we must have $m \le a$! But this means $m \in A$ is a minimum element of $A$, contradicting our assumption that $A$ has no minimum element!
   Therefore $m \notin A$, which means $m \in P$.
   We have shown that for every $m \in \mathbb{N}$, $(\forall k < m, \, k \in P) \implies m \in P$. By **Strong Induction**, $P = \mathbb{N}$. Since $\mathbb{N} \setminus A = \mathbb{N}$, it follows that $A = \emptyset$. Thus every non-empty subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ must possess a minimum element.

3. **Proof that $(\text{Well-Ordering Principle}) \implies (\text{Peano Induction})$:**
   Assume the Well-Ordering Principle holds, and let $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ satisfy the two hypotheses of Peano Induction: $0 \in P$, and $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \, (n \in P \implies S(n) \in P)$.
   Suppose toward a contradiction that $P \neq \mathbb{N}$. Then the set of "counterexamples"
   $$
   A := \mathbb{N} \setminus P = \{n \in \mathbb{N} : n \notin P\}
   $$
   is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$ ($A \neq \emptyset$). By the **Well-Ordering Principle**, $A$ has a minimum element $m_0 \in A$.
   Since $0 \in P$ and $m_0 \in A = \mathbb{N} \setminus P$, we must have $m_0 \neq 0$. By the **Fundamental Predecessor Lemma**, there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(k) = m_0$.
   Because $k + 1 = S(k) = m_0$, we have $k < m_0$. Since $m_0$ is the *minimum* element of $A$, and $k < m_0$, the element $k$ cannot belong to $A$! Thus $k \notin A$, which means $k \in P$.
   Now apply the inductive step hypothesis of $P$ to $k \in P$: since $k \in P$, we must have $S(k) \in P$, i.e., $m_0 \in P$. This contradicts $m_0 \in A = \mathbb{N} \setminus P$!
   Therefore $A = \emptyset$, so $P = \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

### The Euclidean Division Algorithm on $\mathbb{N}$

To conclude our theoretical development of $\mathbb{N}$ and showcase the Well-Ordering Principle in action, let us prove the foundational theorem of elementary number theory: **Euclidean Division with Remainder**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Euclidean Division Algorithm on $\mathbb{N}$
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ with $b \neq 0$. Then there exist **unique** natural numbers $q, r \in \mathbb{N}$ (called the **quotient** and **remainder**, respectively) such that
> $$
> a = q \cdot b + r \quad \text{and} \quad 0 \le r < b
> $$

**Proof:**
1. **Existence (via the Well-Ordering Principle):**
   Consider the set $R \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ of all natural numbers $x \in \mathbb{N}$ that can be left over after subtracting a multiple of $b$ from $a$:
   $$
   R := \{x \in \mathbb{N} : \exists k \in \mathbb{N}, \, a = k \cdot b + x\}
   $$
   First, notice that $R$ is **non-empty**: taking $k = 0 \in \mathbb{N}$ gives $a = 0 \cdot b + a = 0 + a = a$, so $a \in R$.
   Since $R \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is non-empty, the **Well-Ordering Principle** guarantees that $R$ has a minimum element $r := \min(R) \in R$. Because $r \in R$, there exists $q \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
   $$
   a = q \cdot b + r
   $$
   Clearly $0 \le r$ since $r \in \mathbb{N}$. It remains to prove that $r < b$.
   Suppose toward a contradiction that $r < b$ is false. By the **Trichotomy Law**, we must have $b \le r$. By definition of $b \le r$, there exists $r' \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $b + r' = r$. Substituting $r = b + r'$ into $a = q \cdot b + r$ and using Associativity of Addition and the Left Successor Rule for Multiplication yields:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   a &= q \cdot b + (b + r') \\
     &= (q \cdot b + b) + r' \\
     &= \big(S(q) \cdot b\big) + r'
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Since $S(q) \in \mathbb{N}$, this equation shows that $r' \in R$! However, because $b \neq 0$ and $r' + b = r$, the Algebraic Characterization of Strict Inequality guarantees that $r' < r$. Thus $r' \in R$ with $r' < r$, which contradicts the fact that $r$ is the **minimum element** of $R$!
   Therefore $b \le r$ is impossible, so $0 \le r < b$.

2. **Uniqueness (via Trichotomy and Order Compatibility):**
   Suppose $(q_1, r_1)$ and $(q_2, r_2)$ are two pairs in $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ satisfying
   $$
   a = q_1 \cdot b + r_1 = q_2 \cdot b + r_2 \quad \text{with} \quad 0 \le r_1 < b \text{ and } 0 \le r_2 < b
   $$
   We first prove $q_1 = q_2$ using the **Trichotomy Law**. Suppose toward a contradiction that $q_1 \neq q_2$. Without loss of generality (by symmetry of indices), assume $q_1 < q_2$.
   By the Algebraic Characterization of Strict Inequality, there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $q_1 + S(k) = q_2$. Expanding $q_2 \cdot b + r_2$ via Right Distributivity gives:
   $$
   q_1 \cdot b + r_1 = q_2 \cdot b + r_2 = \big(q_1 + S(k)\big) \cdot b + r_2 = q_1 \cdot b + \big(S(k) \cdot b + r_2\big)
   $$
   By the **Additive Cancellation Law**, cancelling $q_1 \cdot b$ from both sides yields:
   $$
   r_1 = S(k) \cdot b + r_2 = (k \cdot b + b) + r_2 = b + (k \cdot b + r_2)
   $$
   Since $k \cdot b + r_2 \in \mathbb{N}$, the equation $b + (k \cdot b + r_2) = r_1$ implies $b \le r_1$ by definition of $\le$. This contradicts our hypothesis that $r_1 < b$!
   Thus $q_1 < q_2$ (and symmetrically $q_2 < q_1$) is impossible, forcing $q_1 = q_2$.
   Finally, substituting $q_1 = q_2$ into $q_1 \cdot b + r_1 = q_2 \cdot b + r_2$ gives $q_1 \cdot b + r_1 = q_1 \cdot b + r_2$, and applying the **Additive Cancellation Law** once more yields $r_1 = r_2$. $\blacksquare$

## 0.2 Exercises

### Problem 0.2.1: Categoricity of the Peano Axioms

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(N_1, 0_1, S_1)$ and $(N_2, 0_2, S_2)$ be any two Peano systems satisfying Axioms P1–P5.
> 1. Using **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** and **Mathematical Induction**, prove that there exists a **unique bijection** $\Phi: N_1 \to N_2$ such that $\Phi(0_1) = 0_2$ and $\Phi(S_1(n)) = S_2(\Phi(n))$ for all $n \in N_1$ (that is, $\Phi \circ S_1 = S_2 \circ \Phi$).
> 2. Let $+_1, \cdot_1, \le_1$ and $+_2, \cdot_2, \le_2$ denote the addition, multiplication, and order relations recursively constructed on $N_1$ and $N_2$, respectively. Prove by induction that $\Phi$ is an **ordered semiring isomorphism**: for all $a, b \in N_1$,
>    $$
>    \Phi(a +_1 b) = \Phi(a) +_2 \Phi(b), \quad \Phi(a \cdot_1 b) = \Phi(a) \cdot_2 \Phi(b), \quad \text{and} \quad a \le_1 b \iff \Phi(a) \le_2 \Phi(b)
>    $$

### Problem 0.2.2: Recursive Construction and Laws of Exponentiation on $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For each fixed base $a \in \mathbb{N}$, use Dedekind's Recursion Theorem to define the **exponentiation function** $b \mapsto a^b$ from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{N}$ by the recursive rules:
> $$
> a^0 := 1 \quad (\text{where } 1 := S(0)), \qquad \text{and} \qquad a^{S(b)} := a^b \cdot a \quad \text{for all } b \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> (Note that under this recursive convention, $0^0 = 1$, while $0^{S(b)} = 0$ for all $b \in \mathbb{N}$.)
> Prove the following three **Laws of Exponents** strictly by Mathematical Induction on $\mathbb{N}$, stating clearly which previously proved laws of addition and multiplication are invoked at each step:
> 1. **Product of Powers Law:** For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$.
> 2. **Power of a Power Law:** For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$.
> 3. **Power of a Product Law:** For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$.

### Problem 0.2.3: Fermat's Principle of Infinite Descent

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> **Fermat's Principle of Infinite Descent** states that there does **not** exist a function $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ that is strictly decreasing at every step; that is, there is no function $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying
> $$
> f(S(n)) < f(n) \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> Prove inside ZF set theory (assuming Axioms P1–P4 and the basic properties of order on $\mathbb{N}$, together with the Axiom of Dependent Choice or by constructing an explicit recursive descent function on a non-empty subset without a minimum) that **Fermat's Principle of Infinite Descent is logically equivalent to the Well-Ordering Principle (WOP)** on $\mathbb{N}$. Specifically:
> 1. Assuming WOP, prove that no function $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfies $f(S(n)) < f(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ by examining the image $f(\mathbb{N}) \subseteq \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. Conversely, assuming the Principle of Infinite Descent (or using Peano/Strong Induction to show that any strictly decreasing sequence must terminate), prove that every non-empty subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ possesses a minimum element.

### Problem 0.2.4: Transitive Sets in the Von Neumann Construction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Recall from Section 0.1 and the Von Neumann model of $\mathbb{N}$ that $0 := \emptyset$ and $S(x) := x \cup \{x\}$. A set $T$ is called a **transitive set** if every element of $T$ is also a subset of $T$:
> $$
> \forall x \in T, \quad x \subseteq T \qquad (\text{equivalently, } \forall x, y, \, (y \in x \land x \in T) \implies y \in T)
> $$
> Using only ZF set theory and Mathematical Induction (Axiom P5) on the Von Neumann natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$ (without appealing to the Axiom of Foundation):
> 1. Prove by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ that every $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is a transitive set.
> 2. Using part (1), prove by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ that $n \notin n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and deduce directly that $S(n) = S(m) \implies n = m$ (Axiom P4).
> 3. Prove by induction that for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$, the algebraic order relations $<$ and $\le$ (defined via addition) coincide with set membership and set inclusion:
>    $$
>    m < n \iff m \in n, \qquad \text{and} \qquad m \le n \iff m \subseteq n
>    $$

### Problem 0.2.5: The Finite Pigeonhole Principle via Peano Induction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For each $n \in \mathbb{N}$, define the **initial segment** $I_n := \{k \in \mathbb{N} : k < n\}$ (so $I_0 = \emptyset$, $I_1 = \{0\}$, $I_2 = \{0, 1\}$, and $I_{S(n)} = I_n \cup \{n\}$ by the Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$).
> 1. **(Swap Lemma):** Let $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and let $f: I_{S(n)} \to I_{S(m)}$ be an injective function. Prove that there exists an injective function $g: I_{S(n)} \to I_{S(m)}$ such that $g(n) = m$, and deduce by restricting $g$ to $I_n$ that there exists an injective function $\tilde{g}: I_n \to I_m$.
> 2. **(Finite Pigeonhole Principle):** Prove by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ that for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$ with $m < n$, there does **not** exist an injective function $f: I_n \to I_m$.
> 3. **(Dedekind Finiteness of $I_n$):** Deduce that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, a function $h: I_n \to I_n$ is injective if and only if $h$ is surjective (and hence bijective). Contrast this with the successor map $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ on the infinite set $\mathbb{N}$.

### Problem 0.2.6: Mutual Recursion and Simultaneous Induction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In many applications, two sequences or functions $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ and $g: \mathbb{N} \to Y$ are defined via **mutual recursion**, where each function's value at $S(n)$ depends on *both* $f(n)$ and $g(n)$.
> Let $X$ and $Y$ be non-empty sets, let $x_0 \in X$ and $y_0 \in Y$ be fixed initial elements, and let
> $$
> H_1: \mathbb{N} \times X \times Y \to X \quad \text{and} \quad H_2: \mathbb{N} \times X \times Y \to Y
> $$
> be given transition functions.
> 1. **(Simultaneous Recursion Theorem):** Prove that there exists a **unique** pair of functions $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ and $g: \mathbb{N} \to Y$ satisfying $f(0) = x_0$, $g(0) = y_0$, and for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    $$
>    f(S(n)) = H_1\big(n, f(n), g(n)\big) \quad \text{and} \quad g(S(n)) = H_2\big(n, f(n), g(n)\big)
>    $$
>    by reducing the problem to a single application of **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** on the Cartesian product $Z := X \times Y$ together with the canonical projection maps $\pi_1: X \times Y \to X$ and $\pi_2: X \times Y \to Y$.
> 2. **(Two-Step / Fibonacci-Style Recursion):** Let $X$ be a non-empty set, let $a_0, a_1 \in X$, and let $G: \mathbb{N} \times X \times X \to X$ be a function. Using part (1) with $Y = X$, prove that there exists a unique function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ such that $f(0) = a_0$, $f(1) = a_1$, and $f(S(S(n))) = G(n, f(n), f(S(n)))$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

### Problem 0.2.7: Partial Subtraction on $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Although $\mathbb{N}$ is not closed under subtraction, we can define a rigorous **partial subtraction** operation whenever $b \le a$.
> 1. Let $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ with $b \le a$. Using the definition of $\le$ and the **Additive Cancellation Law**, prove that there exists a **unique** natural number $d \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $b + d = a$. We denote this unique element $d$ by $a \ominus b$.
> 2. Prove that for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    - If $b + c \le a$, then $b \le a$, $c \le a \ominus b$, and
>      $$
>      (a \ominus b) \ominus c = a \ominus (b + c)
>      $$
>    - If $b \le a$, then $c \cdot b \le c \cdot a$ and **Multiplication Distributes over Partial Subtraction**:
>      $$
>      c \cdot (a \ominus b) = (c \cdot a) \ominus (c \cdot b)
>      $$

### Problem 0.2.8: Ackermann's Function via Higher-Order Recursion

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In 1928, Wilhelm Ackermann constructed a total computable function on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ that grows faster than any function definable by a single primitive recursion (such as addition, multiplication, exponentiation, or iterated towers of exponents).
> Consider the **Ackermann-Péter recurrence relations** for a function $\operatorname{Ack}: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$:
> - **(A1):** $\operatorname{Ack}(0, n) = S(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$;
> - **(A2):** $\operatorname{Ack}(S(m), 0) = \operatorname{Ack}(m, 1)$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$;
> - **(A3):** $\operatorname{Ack}(S(m), S(n)) = \operatorname{Ack}\big(m, \operatorname{Ack}(S(m), n)\big)$ for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 
> 1. By applying **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** (or Peano Induction on $m$ to construct a sequence of functions $A_m: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ where $A_0(n) = S(n)$ and $A_{S(m)}$ is defined from $A_m$ via Dedekind's Recursion Theorem with $A_{S(m)}(0) = A_m(1)$ and $A_{S(m)}(S(n)) = A_m(A_{S(m)}(n))$), prove that there exists a **unique** function $\operatorname{Ack}: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying (A1)–(A3).
> 2. Using **nested Peano Induction** (an outer induction on $m \in \mathbb{N}$ and an inner induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$), prove that for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    $$
>    n < \operatorname{Ack}(m, n) \quad \text{and} \quad \operatorname{Ack}(m, n) < \operatorname{Ack}(m, S(n))
>    $$
>    and deduce that $\operatorname{Ack}(m, n) < \operatorname{Ack}(S(m), n)$ for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$.

---

# 0.3 Equivalence Relations, Quotients, and the Construction of the Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$)

In Section 0.2, we constructed the semiring of natural numbers $(\mathbb{N}, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ from the Peano axioms and established its foundational arithmetic and order properties, including mathematical induction, the trichotomy of order, the Well-Ordering Principle, and the additive and multiplicative cancellation laws. Yet as rich as $\mathbb{N}$ is for counting and induction, it suffers from a glaring algebraic deficiency: while we can add any two natural numbers, we cannot in general solve linear equations of the form $a + x = b$. Whenever $b < a$, no natural number $x \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfies this equation. In algebraic language, $(\mathbb{N}, +, 0)$ is a commutative monoid with cancellation, but it fails to be a group because nonzero elements lack additive inverses.

How do we rigorously manufacture "negative numbers" and subtraction when only $\mathbb{N}$ and the axioms of Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory are available to us? We cannot simply postulate negative symbols $-n$ out of thin air without proving that a set obeying the laws of integer arithmetic actually exists within set theory. Instead, we must construct each integer as an explicit set built out of natural numbers. The fundamental set-theoretic machinery that allows us to build new mathematical objects by gluing together different representations of the same underlying quantity is the theory of **equivalence relations** and **quotient sets**. This same quotient machinery will recur throughout analysis—first here to construct the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ from $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, next in Section 0.4 to construct the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ from $\mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$, later to construct the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ via equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences, and eventually in measure theory to construct the $L^p$ spaces by identifying functions that agree almost everywhere.

## Equivalence Relations, Partitions, and Quotient Sets

### Binary Relations and the Equivalence Axioms

Recall from Section 0.1 that a binary relation $R$ on a set $X$ is formally a subset $R \subseteq X \times X$, and we write $x \mathrel{R} y$ as shorthand for $(x, y) \in R$. In mathematics, we frequently encounter situations where two distinct elements $x, y \in X$ carry the exact same information for the purpose at hand and ought to be treated as interchangeable. What structural properties must a relation $\sim$ on $X$ satisfy in order to behave like a genuine notion of "sameness" or "equivalence"?

First, every object must be equivalent to itself. Second, equivalence cannot be directional: if $x$ is equivalent to $y$, then $y$ must be equivalent to $x$. Third, equivalence must chain together: if $x$ is equivalent to $y$ and $y$ is equivalent to $z$, then $x$ must be equivalent to $z$. Abstracting these three requirements yields the definition of an equivalence relation.

> [!abstract] Definition: Reflexivity, Symmetry, Transitivity, and Equivalence Relations
> Let $X$ be a set and let $\sim$ be a binary relation on $X$ (that is, ${\sim} \subseteq X \times X$). We say that $\sim$ is:
> 1. **Reflexive** if for every $x \in X$,
>    $$
>    x \sim x
>    $$
> 2. **Symmetric** if for all $x, y \in X$,
>    $$
>    x \sim y \implies y \sim x
>    $$
> 3. **Transitive** if for all $x, y, z \in X$,
>    $$
>    (x \sim y \land y \sim z) \implies x \sim z
>    $$
>
> A binary relation $\sim$ on $X$ is called an **equivalence relation** if it is simultaneously reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.

> [!warning] Caution: Why Reflexivity Cannot Be Derived from Symmetry and Transitivity Alone
> A classic logical trap is to argue that symmetry and transitivity automatically imply reflexivity via the following syllogism: *"Take $x \in X$. Pick $y \in X$ such that $x \sim y$. By symmetry, $y \sim x$. By transitivity applied to $x \sim y$ and $y \sim x$, we obtain $x \sim x$."*
>
> Where does this argument fail? It implicitly assumes that for a given $x \in X$, there exists at least one $y \in X$ such that $x \sim y$! If an element $x \in X$ is related to *nothing at all* in $X$, the implication $(x \sim y \land y \sim x) \implies x \sim x$ is vacuously true, yet $x \sim x$ is false. For instance, the empty relation $\emptyset \subseteq X \times X$ on any non-empty set $X$ is vacuously symmetric and transitive, but it fails reflexivity at every element of $X$. Thus, reflexivity is an indispensable independent axiom.

### Equivalence Classes, Quotient Sets, and the Canonical Projection

Once we have an equivalence relation $\sim$ on a set $X$, how do we turn "elements that are equivalent under $\sim$" into a *single* mathematical object? The brilliant insight of set theory is to bundle all elements equivalent to a given $x \in X$ into a single subset $[x]_\sim \subseteq X$. Because two equivalent elements $x \sim y$ will generate the exact same bundle of equivalent elements, the bundles themselves become the new individual points of a compressed space!

> [!abstract] Definition: Equivalence Class, Quotient Set, and Canonical Projection
> Let $\sim$ be an equivalence relation on a set $X$.
> 1. For each element $x \in X$, the **equivalence class** of $x$ modulo $\sim$, denoted $[x]_\sim$ (or simply $[x]$ when $\sim$ is clear from context), is the subset of $X$ consisting of all elements related to $x$:
>    $$
>    [x]_\sim := \{y \in X : y \sim x\} \subseteq X
>    $$
>    Any element $y \in [x]_\sim$ is called a **representative** of the equivalence class $[x]_\sim$.
> 2. The **quotient set** of $X$ modulo $\sim$, denoted $X / {\sim}$ (read "$X$ modulo $\sim$" or "$X$ mod $\sim$"), is the set of all equivalence classes of elements of $X$:
>    $$
>    X / {\sim} := \{[x]_\sim : x \in X\} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(X)
>    $$
> 3. The **canonical projection** (or **natural quotient map**) is the surjective function $\pi : X \to X / {\sim}$ defined by sending each element to its own equivalence class:
>    $$
>    \pi(x) := [x]_\sim \quad \text{for all } x \in X
>    $$

Notice how the ZF axioms from Section 0.1 guarantee that these objects are legitimate sets: for each $x \in X$, the equivalence class $[x]_\sim$ is a well-defined subset of $X$ by the Axiom Schema of Specification, so $[x]_\sim \in \mathcal{P}(X)$. Consequently, the quotient set $X / {\sim} = \{C \in \mathcal{P}(X) : \exists x \in X, C = [x]_\sim\}$ is a well-defined subset of the power set $\mathcal{P}(X)$ by the Axiom of Power Set and Specification.

### Partitions and the Fundamental Correspondence

Geometrically, passing from $X$ to $X / {\sim}$ carves the set $X$ into non-overlapping blocks that completely cover $X$. Let us make this geometric notion of a "carving" precise.

> [!abstract] Definition: Partition of a Set
> Let $X$ be a set. A collection of subsets $\mathcal{P} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(X)$ is called a **partition** of $X$ if it satisfies the following three conditions:
> 1. **Non-Emptiness of Blocks:** Every block $A \in \mathcal{P}$ is non-empty:
>    $$
>    \forall A \in \mathcal{P}, \quad A \neq \emptyset
>    $$
> 2. **Pairwise Disjointness:** Distinct blocks in $\mathcal{P}$ have no elements in common:
>    $$
>    \forall A, B \in \mathcal{P}, \quad A \cap B \neq \emptyset \implies A = B
>    $$
> 3. **Exhaustive Coverage:** The union of all blocks in $\mathcal{P}$ equals $X$:
>    $$
>    \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{P}} A = X
>    $$

We now establish the fundamental duality between algebra and geometry in set theory: equivalence relations on $X$ and partitions of $X$ are two ways of describing the exact same structure.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations and Partitions
> Let $X$ be a set.
> 1. **From Equivalence Relation to Partition:** If $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $X$, then its equivalence classes satisfy the following properties for all $x, y \in X$:
>    - (i) $x \in [x]_\sim$, and in particular $[x]_\sim \neq \emptyset$.
>    - (ii) $[x]_\sim = [y]_\sim \iff x \sim y$.
>    - (iii) $[x]_\sim \cap [y]_\sim \neq \emptyset \implies [x]_\sim = [y]_\sim$.
>    Consequently, the quotient set $X / {\sim}$ is a partition of $X$.
> 2. **From Partition to Equivalence Relation:** Conversely, if $\mathcal{P}$ is any partition of $X$, then the binary relation $\sim_{\mathcal{P}}$ on $X$ defined by
>    $$
>    x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} y \iff \exists A \in \mathcal{P} \text{ such that } x \in A \text{ and } y \in A
>    $$
>    is an equivalence relation on $X$ whose quotient set is precisely $X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}} = \mathcal{P}$. Furthermore, the assignments ${\sim} \mapsto X / {\sim}$ and $\mathcal{P} \mapsto {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}}$ are mutually inverse bijections between the set of all equivalence relations on $X$ and the set of all partitions of $X$.

**Proof:**
We first prove Part 1. Let $\sim$ be an equivalence relation on $X$, and let $x, y \in X$.
- **Proof of (i):** By the reflexivity of $\sim$, we have $x \sim x$. By the definition of the equivalence class $[x]_\sim = \{z \in X : z \sim x\}$, it follows immediately that $x \in [x]_\sim$. In particular, $[x]_\sim \neq \emptyset$.
- **Proof of (ii):** For the forward implication ($\implies$), suppose $[x]_\sim = [y]_\sim$. By (i), we know $x \in [x]_\sim$. Since $[x]_\sim = [y]_\sim$, we have $x \in [y]_\sim$, which by definition means $x \sim y$.
  For the reverse implication ($\Longleftarrow$), suppose $x \sim y$. We show $[x]_\sim = [y]_\sim$ by mutual inclusion. Let $z \in [x]_\sim$; then $z \sim x$. Since $z \sim x$ and $x \sim y$, transitivity of $\sim$ yields $z \sim y$, so $z \in [y]_\sim$. Thus $[x]_\sim \subseteq [y]_\sim$. Conversely, let $z \in [y]_\sim$; then $z \sim y$. Since $x \sim y$, symmetry of $\sim$ gives $y \sim x$. Applying transitivity to $z \sim y$ and $y \sim x$ yields $z \sim x$, so $z \in [x]_\sim$. Thus $[y]_\sim \subseteq [x]_\sim$, and by set extensionality $[x]_\sim = [y]_\sim$.
- **Proof of (iii):** Suppose $[x]_\sim \cap [y]_\sim \neq \emptyset$. Then there exists an element $z \in [x]_\sim \cap [y]_\sim$. By definition of intersection and equivalence classes, $z \in [x]_\sim$ means $z \sim x$, and $z \in [y]_\sim$ means $z \sim y$. By symmetry applied to $z \sim x$, we obtain $x \sim z$. By transitivity applied to $x \sim z$ and $z \sim y$, we obtain $x \sim y$. Finally, by part (ii), $x \sim y$ implies $[x]_\sim = [y]_\sim$.
- **Verification that $X / {\sim}$ is a partition:** By (i), every class $[x]_\sim \in X / {\sim}$ is non-empty. By (iii), any two classes in $X / {\sim}$ with non-empty intersection are identical, establishing pairwise disjointness. Finally, since $[x]_\sim \subseteq X$ for all $x \in X$, we have $\bigcup_{x \in X} [x]_\sim \subseteq X$; conversely, for any $x \in X$, property (i) gives $x \in [x]_\sim \subseteq \bigcup_{z \in X} [z]_\sim$, so $X \subseteq \bigcup_{z \in X} [z]_\sim$. Thus $\bigcup_{C \in X / {\sim}} C = X$, and $X / {\sim}$ is a partition of $X$.

Next, we prove Part 2. Let $\mathcal{P}$ be a partition of $X$, and define $x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} y \iff \exists A \in \mathcal{P} \text{ such that } x \in A \text{ and } y \in A$.
- **Reflexivity:** Let $x \in X$. Since $\bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{P}} A = X$, there exists $A \in \mathcal{P}$ with $x \in A$. Then $x \in A$ and $x \in A$, so $x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} x$.
- **Symmetry:** Let $x, y \in X$ with $x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} y$. Then there exists $A \in \mathcal{P}$ such that $x \in A$ and $y \in A$. By the commutativity of logical conjunction, $y \in A$ and $x \in A$, so $y \sim_{\mathcal{P}} x$.
- **Transitivity:** Let $x, y, z \in X$ with $x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} y$ and $y \sim_{\mathcal{P}} z$. Then there exist blocks $A, B \in \mathcal{P}$ such that $x, y \in A$ and $y, z \in B$. Notice that $y \in A \cap B$, so $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$. By the pairwise disjointness of the partition $\mathcal{P}$, we must have $A = B$. Therefore $x \in A$ and $z \in B = A$, which means $x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} z$. Thus $\sim_{\mathcal{P}}$ is an equivalence relation on $X$.
- **Equality $X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}} = \mathcal{P}$ and Mutual Inversion:** Let $C \in X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}}$, so $C = [x]_{\sim_{\mathcal{P}}}$ for some $x \in X$. Since $\mathcal{P}$ covers $X$ by pairwise disjoint blocks, there is a unique block $A \in \mathcal{P}$ containing $x$. An element $y \in X$ satisfies $y \sim_{\mathcal{P}} x$ if and only if $y$ and $x$ belong to a common block of $\mathcal{P}$, which by uniqueness of the block containing $x$ holds if and only if $y \in A$. Thus $[x]_{\sim_{\mathcal{P}}} = A \in \mathcal{P}$, showing $X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}} \subseteq \mathcal{P}$. Conversely, let $A \in \mathcal{P}$. Since every block in a partition is non-empty, there exists $x \in A$, and the exact same argument shows $A = [x]_{\sim_{\mathcal{P}}} \in X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}}$. Hence $X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}} = \mathcal{P}$. Finally, if we start with an equivalence relation $\sim$ and form the partition $\mathcal{P} = X / {\sim}$, then for any $x, y \in X$, we have $x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} y \iff \exists z \in X \text{ with } x, y \in [z]_\sim \iff x \sim y$ (by symmetry and transitivity of $\sim$), so $\sim_{X / {\sim}}$ recovers $\sim$. $\blacksquare$

### The Principle of Well-Definedness on Quotient Sets

When we work with a quotient set $X / {\sim}$, its elements are equivalence classes $[x]_\sim$—subsets of $X$ that generally contain infinitely many different representatives. In practice, however, we almost always want to define functions or operations on $X / {\sim}$ by picking a single representative $x \in [x]_\sim$, performing a calculation $\text{expr}(x)$ using $x$, and declaring the result to be the value of our function on the entire class $[x]_\sim$.

Why is this potentially dangerous? Suppose $[x]_\sim = [x']_\sim$, so $x$ and $x'$ are two *different* elements of $X$ that represent the *exact same* equivalence class in $X / {\sim}$. Remember from Section 0.1 that a function $F : X / {\sim} \to Y$ must assign a **unique** output in $Y$ to each input in its domain $X / {\sim}$. If our formula gives one output $\text{expr}(x)$ when we feed in the representative $x$, and a *different* output $\text{expr}(x') \neq \text{expr}(x)$ when we feed in the equivalent representative $x'$, then the prescription "$F([x]_\sim) := \text{expr}(x)$" is not a function at all—it is an ambiguous, self-contradictory rule!

For example, suppose we define an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ by $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c$, and someone attempts to define a function $g : (\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}) / {\sim} \to \mathbb{N}$ by writing "$g([(a, b)]) := a$." Observe that $(2, 1) \sim (5, 4)$ because $2 + 4 = 6 = 1 + 5$, so the equivalence classes $[(2, 1)]$ and $[(5, 4)]$ are literally the *same set*: $[(2, 1)] = [(5, 4)]$. Yet the proposed formula yields $g([(2, 1)]) = 2$ and $g([(5, 4)]) = 5$, forcing $2 = 5$, a catastrophic contradiction! We say that such a formula is **ill-defined** (or **not well-defined**).

To guarantee that a prescription on equivalence classes genuinely defines a function, we must prove **invariance under change of representative**. This fundamental requirement is formalized by the Universal Property of Quotient Sets.

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Universal Property of Quotient Sets (Principle of Well-Definedness)
> Let $\sim$ be an equivalence relation on a set $X$ with canonical projection $\pi : X \to X / {\sim}$ given by $\pi(x) = [x]_\sim$, and let $Y$ be any set. Let $f : X \to Y$ be a function. Then there exists a function $\tilde{f} : X / {\sim} \to Y$ satisfying
> $$
> \tilde{f}([x]_\sim) = f(x) \quad \text{for all } x \in X \qquad (\text{that is, } \tilde{f} \circ \pi = f)
> $$
> if and only if $f$ respects the equivalence relation $\sim$ (is **invariant on equivalence classes**), meaning:
> $$
> \forall x, x' \in X, \quad x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')
> $$
> Moreover, when this invariance condition holds, the induced function $\tilde{f} : X / {\sim} \to Y$ is **unique**.

**Proof:**
First, suppose that such a function $\tilde{f} : X / {\sim} \to Y$ exists with $\tilde{f}([x]_\sim) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$. Let $x, x' \in X$ satisfy $x \sim x'$. By the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations, $x \sim x'$ implies $[x]_\sim = [x']_\sim$ as elements of $X / {\sim}$. Since $\tilde{f}$ is a function on $X / {\sim}$, applying $\tilde{f}$ to the equal inputs $[x]_\sim$ and $[x']_\sim$ yields equal outputs:
$$
f(x) = \tilde{f}([x]_\sim) = \tilde{f}([x']_\sim) = f(x')
$$
Thus the invariance condition is necessary.

Conversely, suppose that $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$ holds for all $x, x' \in X$. Following our set-theoretic definition of a function from Section 0.1, we define $\tilde{f}$ as the subset of $(X / {\sim}) \times Y$ given by:
$$
\tilde{f} := \{([x]_\sim, f(x)) : x \in X\} \subseteq (X / {\sim}) \times Y
$$
We must verify existence and uniqueness of outputs for every element of the domain $X / {\sim}$:
1. **Existence:** Let $C \in X / {\sim}$. By the definition of the quotient set, there exists at least one $x \in X$ such that $C = [x]_\sim$. Then $(C, f(x)) = ([x]_\sim, f(x)) \in \tilde{f}$.
2. **Uniqueness (Well-Definedness):** Suppose $(C, y_1) \in \tilde{f}$ and $(C, y_2) \in \tilde{f}$. By definition of $\tilde{f}$, there exist elements $x, x' \in X$ such that $(C, y_1) = ([x]_\sim, f(x))$ and $(C, y_2) = ([x']_\sim, f(x'))$. Comparing first coordinates gives $[x]_\sim = C = [x']_\sim$. By the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations, $[x]_\sim = [x']_\sim \implies x \sim x'$. By our invariance hypothesis on $f$, $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$. Therefore $y_1 = f(x) = f(x') = y_2$.

Thus $\tilde{f} : X / {\sim} \to Y$ is a well-defined function satisfying $\tilde{f}([x]_\sim) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$. Finally, to see uniqueness of $\tilde{f}$, suppose $g : X / {\sim} \to Y$ is any function satisfying $g([x]_\sim) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$. Since every element $C \in X / {\sim}$ is of the form $C = [x]_\sim$ for some $x \in X$, we have $g(C) = g([x]_\sim) = f(x) = \tilde{f}([x]_\sim) = \tilde{f}(C)$ for all $C \in X / {\sim}$, whence $g = \tilde{f}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Well-Definedness of Binary Operations on Quotient Sets
> In exactly the same way, if we wish to define a binary operation $\star : (X / {\sim}) \times (X / {\sim}) \to X / {\sim}$ by choosing representatives and writing $[x]_\sim \star [y]_\sim := [x * y]_\sim$ for some binary operation $*$ on $X$, we must prove that whenever $x \sim x'$ and $y \sim y'$ in $X$, the outputs are equivalent: $(x * y) \sim (x' * y')$, so that $[x * y]_\sim = [x' * y']_\sim$. Proving this invariance will be our very first duty as soon as we define addition and multiplication of integers.

## Set-Theoretic Construction of the Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$)

### Motivation: Formal Differences of Natural Numbers

Let us return to our motivating algebraic problem: in the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$, the equation $a + x = b$ has no solution whenever $b < a$. Intuitively, we would like to represent every integer as the difference "$a - b$" of two natural numbers $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$—for instance, representing $+3$ as "$3 - 0$" (or "$5 - 2$") and representing $-3$ as "$0 - 3$" (or "$2 - 5$").

However, we must be ruthlessly careful to avoid circularity: **subtraction and negative numbers do not exist yet!** We cannot write "$a - b$" in $\mathbb{N}$ when $a < b$. How can we capture the pair of numbers $a$ and $b$ in their exact order without subtracting them? By Section 0.1, set theory provides the exact tool we need: the **ordered pair** $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$!

Think of the ordered pair $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ as a *ledger entry* recording "$a$ credits and $b$ debits." Now, different ledger entries represent the exact same net balance: $(5, 2)$ and $(3, 0)$ both represent a net credit of $3$, while $(2, 5)$ and $(0, 3)$ both represent a net debit of $3$. In general, if subtraction already existed, two pairs $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ would represent the same net difference when "$a - b = c - d$." Although that equation is illegal in $\mathbb{N}$ because of the minus signs, we can clear the formal minus signs by adding $b + d$ to both sides, transforming the condition into an equation that uses **only natural number addition**:
$$
a + d = b + c
$$
Notice the miracle here: for any $a, b, c, d \in \mathbb{N}$, both $a + d$ and $b + c$ are always well-defined elements of $\mathbb{N}$! We can therefore use the equation $a + d = b + c$ as our completely rigorous, subtraction-free definition of when two ordered pairs in $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ are equivalent.

### The Equivalence Relation on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Definition: The Integer Equivalence Relation on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$
> We define a binary relation $\sim$ on the Cartesian product $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ by stipulating that for all $(a, b), (c, d) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$,
> $$
> (a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c
> $$
> where $+$ denotes addition of natural numbers as constructed in Section 0.2.

Before we can take the quotient of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ by $\sim$, we must prove that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation. As you read the proof of transitivity below, pay close attention to how the **Additive Cancellation Law on $\mathbb{N}$** (Theorem in Section 0.2: $x + k = y + k \implies x = y$) plays a decisive, irreplaceable role!

> [!theorem] Theorem: $\sim$ is an Equivalence Relation on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$
> The binary relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ defined by $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c$ is an equivalence relation.

**Proof:**
We verify reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity using only the commutativity, associativity, and cancellation laws of addition on $\mathbb{N}$ established in Section 0.2.
1. **Reflexivity:** Let $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$. By the commutativity of addition on $\mathbb{N}$, we have
   $$
   a + b = b + a
   $$
   By the definition of $\sim$, this means $(a, b) \sim (a, b)$. Thus $\sim$ is reflexive.
2. **Symmetry:** Let $(a, b), (c, d) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ and suppose $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$. By definition, this means $a + d = b + c$. Using the symmetry of equality and the commutativity of addition on $\mathbb{N}$ on both sides, we obtain:
   $$
   c + b = b + c = a + d = d + a
   $$
   Since $c + b = d + a$, the definition of $\sim$ yields $(c, d) \sim (a, b)$. Thus $\sim$ is symmetric.
3. **Transitivity:** Let $(a, b), (c, d), (e, f) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ and suppose that $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$ and $(c, d) \sim (e, f)$. By definition of $\sim$, we have two equations in $\mathbb{N}$:
   $$
   a + d = b + c \quad \text{and} \quad c + f = d + e
   $$
   We wish to prove that $(a, b) \sim (e, f)$, which requires showing $a + f = b + e$. Since we cannot subtract $c$ or $d$ in $\mathbb{N}$, we instead add the two known equations together and regroup terms using the associativity and commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (a + f) + (c + d) &= (a + d) + (c + f) \\
                     &= (b + c) + (d + e) \\
                     &= (b + e) + (c + d)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Observe that the natural number $k := c + d \in \mathbb{N}$ appears as a common summand on both the far left and far right:
   $$
   (a + f) + (c + d) = (b + e) + (c + d)
   $$
   Applying the **Additive Cancellation Law on $\mathbb{N}$** (Section 0.2) to cancel the summand $(c + d)$ from both sides yields:
   $$
   a + f = b + e
   $$
   By definition of $\sim$, this means $(a, b) \sim (e, f)$. Thus $\sim$ is transitive, completing the proof that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Why Additive Cancellation on $\mathbb{N}$ is Indispensable
> Notice that in proving transitivity, we arrived at $(a + f) + (c + d) = (b + e) + (c + d)$ and needed to deduce $a + f = b + e$. If $(\mathbb{N}, +)$ did not satisfy the additive cancellation law, the relation $\sim$ would fail to be transitive! (You will explore an explicit counterexample in Problem 0.3.1). Thus, the inductive proof of additive cancellation in Section 0.2 is the exact structural pillar that makes the construction of $\mathbb{Z}$ possible.

With this theorem in hand, we can formally define the set of integers.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Set of Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$)
> The **set of integers**, denoted $\mathbb{Z}$ (from the German *Zahlen*, meaning "numbers"), is the quotient set of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ modulo the equivalence relation $\sim$:
> $$
> \mathbb{Z} := (\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}) / {\sim} = \{[(a, b)] : (a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}\}
> $$
> where $[(a, b)] := \{(c, d) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} : a + d = b + c\}$ denotes the equivalence class of $(a, b)$ under $\sim$.

## Ring Structure and Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$

### Defining Integer Addition, Negation, and Multiplication

Now that $\mathbb{Z}$ exists as a set of equivalence classes, how should we define addition, negation (additive inverse), and multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$?
