We let our heuristic interpretation—where $[(a, b)]$ represents the formal difference "$a - b$"—guide our definitions, while making sure that the actual definitions are written purely in terms of natural number addition and multiplication:
- **Addition:** Intuitively, "$(a - b) + (c - d) = (a + c) - (b + d)$." Thus, the sum of $[(a, b)]$ and $[(c, d)]$ should be $[(a + c, b + d)]$.
- **Negation:** Intuitively, "$-(a - b) = b - a$." Reversing credits and debits simply swaps the coordinates of the ordered pair! Thus, the additive inverse of $[(a, b)]$ should be $[(b, a)]$.
- **Multiplication:** Intuitively, expanding the product "$(a - b)(c - d) = ac - ad - bc + bd = (ac + bd) - (ad + bc)$" groups the positive terms $ac + bd$ into the first coordinate and the subtracted terms $ad + bc$ into the second coordinate. Thus, the product of $[(a, b)]$ and $[(c, d)]$ should be $[(ac + bd, ad + bc)]$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Integer Addition, Negation, and Multiplication
> We define the operations of **integer addition** $+_\mathbb{Z} : \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$, **negation (additive inverse)** $- : \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$, and **integer multiplication** $\cdot_\mathbb{Z} : \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$ on equivalence classes $[(a, b)], [(c, d)] \in \mathbb{Z}$ by:
> 1. **Addition:**
>    $$
>    [(a, b)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(c, d)] := [(a + c, b + d)]
>    $$
> 2. **Negation:**
>    $$
>    -[(a, b)] := [(b, a)]
>    $$
> 3. **Multiplication:**
>    $$
>    [(a, b)] \cdot_\mathbb{Z} [(c, d)] := [(ac + bd, ad + bc)]
>    $$
> where $a + c$, $ac$, etc., on the right-hand sides denote addition and multiplication in $\mathbb{N}$. Furthermore, we define the special elements **zero** and **one** in $\mathbb{Z}$ by:
> $$
> 0_\mathbb{Z} := [(0, 0)] \quad \text{and} \quad 1_\mathbb{Z} := [(1, 0)]
> $$

As emphasized in our discussion of quotient sets, writing down formulas in terms of representatives $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ does *not* automatically guarantee that $+_\mathbb{Z}$, $-$, and $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$ are valid functions! We must immediately prove that these three operations are **well-defined** (independent of the choice of representatives).

> [!theorem] Theorem: Well-Definedness of Integer Arithmetic
> Let $(a, b), (a', b'), (c, d), (c', d') \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ satisfy $[(a, b)] = [(a', b')]$ and $[(c, d)] = [(c', d')]$. Then:
> 1. $[(a + c, b + d)] = [(a' + c', b' + d')]$ (so $+_\mathbb{Z}$ is well-defined).
> 2. $[(b, a)] = [(b', a')]$ (so negation $-$ is well-defined).
> 3. $[(ac + bd, ad + bc)] = [(a'c' + b'd', a'd' + b'c')]$ (so $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$ is well-defined).

**Proof:**
By the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations and the definition of $\sim$, our hypotheses $[(a, b)] = [(a', b')]$ and $[(c, d)] = [(c', d')]$ mean $(a, b) \sim (a', b')$ and $(c, d) \sim (c', d')$, which translate into the two natural number equations:
$$
a + b' = b + a' \quad \text{and} \quad c + d' = d + c'
$$
1. **Well-Definedness of Addition ($+_\mathbb{Z}$):** We must show that $(a + c, b + d) \sim (a' + c', b' + d')$, which means showing $(a + c) + (b' + d') = (b + d) + (a' + c')$. Using the associativity and commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$ together with our two hypothesis equations, we compute:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (a + c) + (b' + d') &= (a + b') + (c + d') \\
                       &= (b + a') + (d + c') \\
                       &= (b + d) + (a' + c')
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Thus $(a + c, b + d) \sim (a' + c', b' + d')$, proving that $+_\mathbb{Z}$ is well-defined.
2. **Well-Definedness of Negation ($-$):** We must show that $(b, a) \sim (b', a')$, which means showing $b + a' = a + b'$. This is literally the symmetry of our hypothesis equation $a + b' = b + a'$. Thus negation is well-defined.
3. **Well-Definedness of Multiplication ($\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$):** Rather than changing both pairs at once in a massive eight-term manipulation, we can proceed cleanly in two steps: first we show that replacing $(a, b)$ with $(a', b')$ while keeping $(c, d)$ fixed preserves the equivalence class:
   $$
   (ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c)
   $$
   and then we replace $(c, d)$ with $(c', d')$ while keeping $(a', b')$ fixed. To verify the first equivalence, we must show that
   $$
   (ac + bd) + (a'd + b'c) = (ad + bc) + (a'c + b'd)
   $$
   Using the commutativity, associativity, and distributivity of arithmetic in $\mathbb{N}$, together with our hypothesis $a + b' = b + a'$, we regroup terms by factoring out $c$ and $d$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (ac + bd) + (a'd + b'c) &= (ac + b'c) + (bd + a'd) \\
                           &= (a + b')c + (b + a')d \\
                           &= (b + a')c + (a + b')d \\
                           &= (bc + a'c) + (ad + b'd) \\
                           &= (ad + bc) + (a'c + b'd)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Therefore, $[(ac + bd, ad + bc)] = [(a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c)]$. By an identical calculation (grouping by $a'$ and $b'$ and using $c + d' = d + c'$), we similarly obtain:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (a'c + b'd) + (a'd' + b'c') &= a'(c + d') + b'(d + c') \\
                               &= a'(d + c') + b'(c + d') \\
                               &= (a'd + b'c) + (a'c' + b'd')
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   which means $[(a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c)] = [(a'c' + b'd', a'd' + b'c')]$. By the transitivity of equality, $[(ac + bd, ad + bc)] = [(a'c' + b'd', a'd' + b'c')]$, proving that $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$ is well-defined. $\blacksquare$

### The Commutative Ring Axioms for $\mathbb{Z}$

Having verified that $+_\mathbb{Z}$, $-$, and $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$ are well-defined operations on $\mathbb{Z}$, we now prove that they endow $\mathbb{Z}$ with the structure of a **commutative ring with unity**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: $(\mathbb{Z}, +_\mathbb{Z}, \cdot_\mathbb{Z}, 0_\mathbb{Z}, 1_\mathbb{Z})$ is a Commutative Ring with Unity
> The set of integers $\mathbb{Z}$ equipped with addition $+_\mathbb{Z}$, multiplication $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$, additive identity $0_\mathbb{Z} = [(0, 0)]$, and multiplicative identity $1_\mathbb{Z} = [(1, 0)]$ satisfies all axioms of a commutative ring with unity (with $1_\mathbb{Z} \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$):
> 1. **Associativity of Addition:** For all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$, $(x +_\mathbb{Z} y) +_\mathbb{Z} z = x +_\mathbb{Z} (y +_\mathbb{Z} z)$.
> 2. **Commutativity of Addition:** For all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x +_\mathbb{Z} y = y +_\mathbb{Z} x$.
> 3. **Additive Identity:** For all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x +_\mathbb{Z} 0_\mathbb{Z} = x = 0_\mathbb{Z} +_\mathbb{Z} x$.
> 4. **Existence of Additive Inverses:** For every $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, its negation $-x$ satisfies $x +_\mathbb{Z} (-x) = 0_\mathbb{Z} = (-x) +_\mathbb{Z} x$.
> 5. **Associativity of Multiplication:** For all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$, $(x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y) \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z = x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} (y \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)$.
> 6. **Commutativity of Multiplication:** For all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y = y \cdot_\mathbb{Z} x$.
> 7. **Multiplicative Identity:** For all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} 1_\mathbb{Z} = x = 1_\mathbb{Z} \cdot_\mathbb{Z} x$, and $1_\mathbb{Z} \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
> 8. **Distributivity:** For all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} (y +_\mathbb{Z} z) = (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y) +_\mathbb{Z} (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)$ and $(x +_\mathbb{Z} y) \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z = (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z) +_\mathbb{Z} (y \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)$.

**Proof:**
Let $x = [(a, b)]$, $y = [(c, d)]$, and $z = [(e, f)]$ be arbitrary elements of $\mathbb{Z}$, where $a, b, c, d, e, f \in \mathbb{N}$. Each axiom reduces directly to the corresponding semiring properties of $\mathbb{N}$ proved in Section 0.2:
1. **Associativity of $+_\mathbb{Z}$:** Using associativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$,
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (x +_\mathbb{Z} y) +_\mathbb{Z} z &= [(a + c, b + d)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(e, f)] \\
                                     &= [((a + c) + e, (b + d) + f)] \\
                                     &= [(a + (c + e), b + (d + f))] \\
                                     &= [(a, b)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(c + e, d + f)] = x +_\mathbb{Z} (y +_\mathbb{Z} z)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
2. **Commutativity of $+_\mathbb{Z}$:** Using commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$,
   $$
   x +_\mathbb{Z} y = [(a + c, b + d)] = [(c + a, d + b)] = y +_\mathbb{Z} x
   $$
3. **Additive Identity:** Since $a + 0 = a$ and $b + 0 = b$ in $\mathbb{N}$,
   $$
   x +_\mathbb{Z} 0_\mathbb{Z} = [(a, b)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(0, 0)] = [(a + 0, b + 0)] = [(a, b)] = x
   $$
   and $0_\mathbb{Z} +_\mathbb{Z} x = x$ follows by commutativity of $+_\mathbb{Z}$.
4. **Existence of Additive Inverses:** For $x = [(a, b)]$, we defined $-x = [(b, a)]$. Then
   $$
   x +_\mathbb{Z} (-x) = [(a, b)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(b, a)] = [(a + b, b + a)]
   $$
   Notice that $(a + b, b + a) \sim (0, 0)$ because $(a + b) + 0 = a + b = b + a = (b + a) + 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$. Therefore $[(a + b, b + a)] = [(0, 0)] = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, and $(-x) +_\mathbb{Z} x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ follows by commutativity.
5. **Associativity of $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$:** Using the associativity, commutativity, and distributivity of arithmetic in $\mathbb{N}$, we expand both triple products:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y) \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z &= [(ac + bd, ad + bc)] \cdot_\mathbb{Z} [(e, f)] \\
   &= [((ac + bd)e + (ad + bc)f, \, (ac + bd)f + (ad + bc)e)] \\
   &= [(ace + bde + adf + bcf, \, acf + bdf + ade + bce)] \\
   &= [(a(ce + df) + b(cf + de), \, a(cf + de) + b(ce + df))] \\
   &= [(a, b)] \cdot_\mathbb{Z} [(ce + df, cf + de)] = x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} (y \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
6. **Commutativity of $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$:** Using commutativity of addition and multiplication in $\mathbb{N}$,
   $$
   x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y = [(ac + bd, ad + bc)] = [(ca + db, cb + da)] = [(ca + db, da + cb)] = y \cdot_\mathbb{Z} x
   $$
7. **Multiplicative Identity and Non-Triviality:** Since $a \cdot 1 = a$ and $a \cdot 0 = 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, we have
   $$
   x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} 1_\mathbb{Z} = [(a, b)] \cdot_\mathbb{Z} [(1, 0)] = [(a \cdot 1 + b \cdot 0, a \cdot 0 + b \cdot 1)] = [(a + 0, 0 + b)] = [(a, b)] = x
   $$
   and $1_\mathbb{Z} \cdot_\mathbb{Z} x = x$ by commutativity. Moreover, $1_\mathbb{Z} = [(1, 0)] \neq [(0, 0)] = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ because $(1, 0) \sim (0, 0)$ would require $1 + 0 = 0 + 0$, i.e., $1 = 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, contradicting the Peano axiom that $0$ is not the successor of any natural number.
8. **Distributivity:** We compute $x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} (y +_\mathbb{Z} z)$ and $(x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y) +_\mathbb{Z} (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)$ using distributivity and commutativity in $\mathbb{N}$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} (y +_\mathbb{Z} z) &= [(a, b)] \cdot_\mathbb{Z} [(c + e, d + f)] \\
   &= [(a(c + e) + b(d + f), \, a(d + f) + b(c + e))] \\
   &= [((ac + bd) + (ae + bf), \, (ad + bc) + (af + be))] \\
   &= [(ac + bd, ad + bc)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(ae + bf, af + be)] \\
   &= (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} y) +_\mathbb{Z} (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Right distributivity $(x +_\mathbb{Z} y) \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z = (x \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z) +_\mathbb{Z} (y \cdot_\mathbb{Z} z)$ follows immediately from left distributivity and the commutativity of $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$. $\blacksquare$

Now that $\mathbb{Z}$ is a commutative ring, we can formally define **subtraction** on $\mathbb{Z}$ and derive all the standard algebraic identities governing zero and minus signs purely from the ring axioms!

> [!abstract] Definition: Subtraction on $\mathbb{Z}$
> For any two integers $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, the **difference** $x -_\mathbb{Z} y$ (usually written simply $x - y$) is defined as the sum of $x$ and the additive inverse of $y$:
> $$
> x - y := x +_\mathbb{Z} (-y)
> $$
> In terms of representatives $x = [(a, b)]$ and $y = [(c, d)]$, we have $x - y = [(a, b)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(d, c)] = [(a + d, b + c)]$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Basic Ring Identities and Additive Cancellation in $\mathbb{Z}$
> Let $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$. The following identities hold in $\mathbb{Z}$ (where we henceforth omit the subscript $\mathbb{Z}$ on $+$ and $\cdot$ when no ambiguity arises):
> 1. **Additive Cancellation Law:** If $x + z = y + z$, then $x = y$.
> 2. **Uniqueness of Additive Inverses and Double Negation:** If $x + w = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $w = -x$. Consequently, $-(-x) = x$, $-0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, and $-(x + y) = (-x) + (-y)$.
> 3. **Unique Solvability of Linear Equations:** For any $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, there exists a unique $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $a + x = b$, namely $x = b - a$.
> 4. **Multiplication by Zero:** $0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x = 0_\mathbb{Z} = x \cdot 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
> 5. **Sign Rules for Multiplication:** $(-1_\mathbb{Z}) \cdot x = -x$, $(-x) \cdot y = -(x \cdot y) = x \cdot (-y)$, and $(-x) \cdot (-y) = x \cdot y$.

**Proof:**
Every statement follows directly from the commutative ring axioms:
1. **Additive Cancellation:** Suppose $x + z = y + z$. Adding $(-z)$ to both sides and using associativity and the additive identity/inverse axioms gives:
   $$
   x = x + 0_\mathbb{Z} = x + (z + (-z)) = (x + z) + (-z) = (y + z) + (-z) = y + (z + (-z)) = y + 0_\mathbb{Z} = y
   $$
2. **Uniqueness of Additive Inverses:** Suppose $x + w = 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Since $x + (-x) = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ as well, we have $w + x = x + w = 0_\mathbb{Z} = x + (-x) = (-x) + x$. By Additive Cancellation (Part 1), $w = -x$. Next, since $(-x) + x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, $x$ is an additive inverse of $-x$, so uniqueness gives $x = -(-x)$. Similarly, $0_\mathbb{Z} + 0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ implies $-0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, and $(x + y) + ((-x) + (-y)) = (x + (-x)) + (y + (-y)) = 0_\mathbb{Z} + 0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ implies $-(x + y) = (-x) + (-y)$.
3. **Unique Solvability of $a + x = b$:** Substituting $x = b - a = b + (-a)$ gives $a + (b + (-a)) = b + (a + (-a)) = b + 0_\mathbb{Z} = b$, proving existence. If $a + x = b$ and $a + x' = b$, then $x + a = x' + a$, so $x = x'$ by Additive Cancellation.
4. **Multiplication by Zero:** By the additive identity property $0_\mathbb{Z} + 0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and distributivity, we have:
   $$
   0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x + 0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x = (0_\mathbb{Z} + 0_\mathbb{Z}) \cdot x = 0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x + 0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x
   $$
   Applying Additive Cancellation to cancel $0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x$ from both sides yields $0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot x$. Commutativity gives $x \cdot 0_\mathbb{Z} = 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
5. **Sign Rules for Multiplication:** To show $(-x) \cdot y = -(x \cdot y)$, we compute the sum $(x \cdot y) + ((-x) \cdot y)$ using distributivity and Part 4:
   $$
   (x \cdot y) + ((-x) \cdot y) = (x + (-x)) \cdot y = 0_\mathbb{Z} \cdot y = 0_\mathbb{Z}
   $$
   By the uniqueness of additive inverses (Part 2), $(-x) \cdot y = -(x \cdot y)$. By commutativity, $x \cdot (-y) = -(x \cdot y)$ as well. Setting $x = 1_\mathbb{Z}$ gives $(-1_\mathbb{Z}) \cdot y = -(1_\mathbb{Z} \cdot y) = -y$. Finally, applying the identity twice together with double negation yields $(-x) \cdot (-y) = -(x \cdot (-y)) = -(-(x \cdot y)) = x \cdot y$. $\blacksquare$

### The Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$ and Sign Trichotomy

Strictly speaking, a natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and an integer $[(n, 0)] \in \mathbb{Z}$ are different set-theoretic objects: $n$ is a von Neumann ordinal (such as $2 = \{0, 1\}$), whereas $[(n, 0)]$ is an infinite equivalence class of ordered pairs of natural numbers, $[(2, 0)] = \{(2, 0), (3, 1), (4, 2), \dots\}$. How, then, are we justified in viewing $\mathbb{N}$ as a subset of $\mathbb{Z}$?

We bridge this gap by exhibiting a **canonical embedding** $\iota : \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$—an injective map that preserves $0$, $1$, addition, and multiplication, thereby embedding an exact isomorphic copy of the semiring $\mathbb{N}$ inside $\mathbb{Z}$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$
> Define the **canonical embedding** $\iota : \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$ by
> $$
> \iota(n) := [(n, 0)] \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> Then $\iota$ satisfies the following properties:
> 1. **Injectivity:** For all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$, $\iota(m) = \iota(n) \implies m = n$.
> 2. **Preservation of Algebraic Structure (Semiring Homomorphism):** $\iota(0) = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, $\iota(1) = 1_\mathbb{Z}$, and for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    \iota(m + n) = \iota(m) +_\mathbb{Z} \iota(n) \quad \text{and} \quad \iota(m \cdot n) = \iota(m) \cdot_\mathbb{Z} \iota(n)
>    $$
> 3. **Difference Representation:** Every integer $[(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Z}$ can be written as the difference of two embedded natural numbers:
>    $$
>    [(a, b)] = \iota(a) - \iota(b)
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. **Injectivity:** Suppose $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfy $\iota(m) = \iota(n)$, so $[(m, 0)] = [(n, 0)]$. By definition of $\sim$, $(m, 0) \sim (n, 0)$ means $m + 0 = 0 + n$ in $\mathbb{N}$, which simplifies to $m = n$. Thus $\iota$ is injective.
2. **Semiring Homomorphism:** By definition, $\iota(0) = [(0, 0)] = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and $\iota(1) = [(1, 0)] = 1_\mathbb{Z}$. For any $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$,
   $$
   \iota(m) +_\mathbb{Z} \iota(n) = [(m, 0)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(n, 0)] = [(m + n, 0 + 0)] = [(m + n, 0)] = \iota(m + n)
   $$
   and similarly for multiplication,
   $$
   \iota(m) \cdot_\mathbb{Z} \iota(n) = [(m, 0)] \cdot_\mathbb{Z} [(n, 0)] = [(m \cdot n + 0 \cdot 0, m \cdot 0 + 0 \cdot n)] = [(m \cdot n, 0)] = \iota(m \cdot n)
   $$
3. **Difference Representation:** For any $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, we compute:
   $$
   \iota(a) - \iota(b) = [(a, 0)] +_\mathbb{Z} (-[(b, 0)]) = [(a, 0)] +_\mathbb{Z} [(0, b)] = [(a + 0, 0 + b)] = [(a, b)]
   $$
   Thus our heuristic intuition that $[(a, b)]$ represents "$a - b$" is now a completely rigorous theorem inside $\mathbb{Z}$! $\blacksquare$

What do the elements of $\mathbb{Z}$ outside $\iota(\mathbb{N})$ look like? Using the Trichotomy Law on $\mathbb{N}$ proved in Section 0.2, we now show that every equivalence class $[(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Z}$ has a **unique canonical representative** in which at least one coordinate is $0$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Canonical Representatives and Sign Trichotomy of $\mathbb{Z}$
> For every integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, **exactly one** of the following three conditions holds:
> 1. **Positive Integer:** There exists a unique nonzero natural number $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that $x = [(n, 0)] = \iota(n)$.
> 2. **Zero:** $x = [(0, 0)] = 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
> 3. **Negative Integer:** There exists a unique nonzero natural number $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that $x = [(0, n)] = -\iota(n)$.
>
> Consequently, setting $\mathbb{Z}^+ := \{[(n, 0)] : n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}\}$ (the **positive integers**) and $\mathbb{Z}^- := \{[(0, n)] : n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}\}$ (the **negative integers**), the set $\mathbb{Z}$ decomposes into the pairwise disjoint union:
> $$
> \mathbb{Z} = \mathbb{Z}^+ \sqcup \{0_\mathbb{Z}\} \sqcup \mathbb{Z}^-
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $x = [(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Z}$, where $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Existence of a Canonical Form:** By the Trichotomy Law for the total order $\le$ on $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2), exactly one of $b < a$, $a = b$, or $a < b$ holds in $\mathbb{N}$:
  - *Case 1 ($b < a$):* By the definition of strict order on $\mathbb{N}$ in Section 0.2, there exists a unique $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that $b + n = a$. Then $a + 0 = a = b + n$, which means $(a, b) \sim (n, 0)$. Hence $x = [(a, b)] = [(n, 0)] = \iota(n) \in \mathbb{Z}^+$.
  - *Case 2 ($a = b$):* Then $a + 0 = a = b = b + 0$, which means $(a, b) \sim (0, 0)$. Hence $x = [(a, b)] = [(0, 0)] = 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
  - *Case 3 ($a < b$):* By definition of strict order on $\mathbb{N}$, there exists a unique $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that $a + n = b$. Then $a + n = b = b + 0$, which means $(a, b) \sim (0, n)$. Hence $x = [(a, b)] = [(0, n)] = -[(n, 0)] = -\iota(n) \in \mathbb{Z}^-$.
- **Mutual Exclusivity and Uniqueness:** We verify that the three cases are mutually exclusive and that $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ is unique whenever Cases 1 or 3 hold:
  - Uniqueness in Case 1 follows from the injectivity of $\iota$: if $[(n, 0)] = [(m, 0)]$, then $n = m$. Similarly, in Case 3, if $[(0, n)] = [(0, m)]$, then $0 + m = n + 0$, so $n = m$.
  - If $x \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \cap \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$, then $[(n, 0)] = [(0, 0)]$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, so $n + 0 = 0 + 0 \implies n = 0$, a contradiction.
  - If $x \in \mathbb{Z}^- \cap \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$, then $[(0, n)] = [(0, 0)]$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, so $0 + 0 = n + 0 \implies n = 0$, a contradiction.
  - If $x \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \cap \mathbb{Z}^-$, then $[(n, 0)] = [(0, m)]$ for some $n, m \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. By definition of $\sim$, this means $n + m = 0 + 0 = 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$. However, since $m \neq 0$, $m = S(k)$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $n + m = n + S(k) = S(n + k) \neq 0$ by the Peano axioms, a contradiction!
Thus $\mathbb{Z}^+$, $\{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$, and $\mathbb{Z}^-$ are pairwise disjoint and exhaust $\mathbb{Z}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Identifying $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $\iota(n) \in \mathbb{Z}$
> Because $\iota : \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ is an injective homomorphism of addition and multiplication, it is standard mathematical practice to identify each natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with its image $\iota(n) = [(n, 0)] \in \mathbb{Z}$, writing $n$ for $[(n, 0)]$, $0$ for $0_\mathbb{Z}$, $1$ for $1_\mathbb{Z}$, and $-n$ for $[(0, n)] = -\iota(n)$. Under this identification, $\mathbb{N} = \mathbb{Z}^+ \cup \{0\} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z} = \{\dots, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, \dots\}$. Whenever we prove foundational theorems about $\mathbb{Z}$ in this section, however, we will remain explicit about equivalence classes and $\iota$ whenever precision demands it.

## Order Structure, Integral Domain Properties, and Absolute Value on $\mathbb{Z}$

### The Total Order on $\mathbb{Z}$

How should we extend the natural order $\le_\mathbb{N}$ from $\mathbb{N}$ to all of $\mathbb{Z}$? In $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2), we defined $a \le_\mathbb{N} b \iff \exists k \in \mathbb{N}, a + k = b$. Once subtraction is available in $\mathbb{Z}$, the equation $x + k = y$ with $k \in \iota(\mathbb{N})$ simply says that the difference $y - x$ belongs to $\iota(\mathbb{N}) = \mathbb{Z}^+ \cup \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$, while the strict inequality $x < y$ says that $y - x$ belongs to $\mathbb{Z}^+$. Let us check how this intrinsic algebraic condition translates into coordinates for $x = [(a, b)]$ and $y = [(c, d)]$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Order Relation on $\mathbb{Z}$
> For any two integers $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, we define the **order relation** $\le_\mathbb{Z}$ (written simply $\le$) and the **strict order relation** $<_\mathbb{Z}$ (written simply $<$) by:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> x \le y &\iff y - x \in \iota(\mathbb{N}) = \mathbb{Z}^+ \cup \{0_\mathbb{Z}\} \\
> x < y &\iff y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \quad (\text{equivalently, } x \le y \text{ and } x \neq y)
> \end{aligned}
> $$
> We also write $y \ge x$ for $x \le y$, and $y > x$ for $x < y$. In particular, $x > 0_\mathbb{Z} \iff x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $x < 0_\mathbb{Z} \iff x \in \mathbb{Z}^-$.

 Notice immediately that because $y - x$ is defined directly in terms of the elements $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$ rather than particular representatives, the relations $\le$ and $<$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ are **automatically well-defined**! Even better, we can characterize $x \le y$ directly in terms of any representatives $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ using the natural number order $\le_\mathbb{N}$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Coordinate Characterization and Total Order Properties of $(\mathbb{Z}, \le)$
> Let $x = [(a, b)]$ and $y = [(c, d)]$ be integers in $\mathbb{Z}$.
> 1. **Coordinate Characterization:** $x \le_\mathbb{Z} y \iff a + d \le_\mathbb{N} b + c$, and $x <_\mathbb{Z} y \iff a + d <_\mathbb{N} b + c$. In particular, for $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$, $\iota(m) \le_\mathbb{Z} \iota(n) \iff m \le_\mathbb{N} n$, so $\iota$ is an order-embedding.
> 2. **Closure of $\mathbb{Z}^+$ under $+_\mathbb{Z}$ and $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$:** For any $u, v \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, both $u +_\mathbb{Z} v \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $u \cdot_\mathbb{Z} v \in \mathbb{Z}^+$.
> 3. **Total Order and Trichotomy:** The relation $\le$ is a **total order** on $\mathbb{Z}$ (reflexive, antisymmetric, transitive, and total), and for any $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, **exactly one** of $x < y$, $x = y$, or $x > y$ holds.
> 4. **Compatibility with Addition and Multiplication (Ordered Ring Axioms):** For all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$:
>    - **Translation Invariance:** $x \le y \iff x + z \le y + z$, and $x < y \iff x + z < y + z$.
>    - **Order Reversal under Negation:** $x \le y \iff -y \le -x$, and $x < y \iff -y < -x$.
>    - **Compatibility with Multiplication:** If $x < y$ and $z > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $x \cdot z < y \cdot z$ (and if $x \le y$ and $z \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $x \cdot z \le y \cdot z$). Conversely, if $x < y$ and $z < 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $x \cdot z > y \cdot z$.

**Proof:**
1. **Coordinate Characterization:** By definition, $y - x = [(c, d)] - [(a, b)] = [(c + b, d + a)]$. Thus $x \le_\mathbb{Z} y$ holds if and only if there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $[(c + b, d + a)] = [(k, 0)]$, which by definition of $\sim$ means $(c + b) + 0 = (d + a) + k$, or equivalently $(a + d) + k = b + c$ in $\mathbb{N}$. By definition of $\le_\mathbb{N}$ in Section 0.2, the existence of $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $(a + d) + k = b + c$ is equivalent to $a + d \le_\mathbb{N} b + c$. Similarly, $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ means there exists $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ with $(a + d) + k = b + c$, which is equivalent to $a + d <_\mathbb{N} b + c$. Setting $x = [(m, 0)]$ and $y = [(n, 0)]$ gives $\iota(m) \le_\mathbb{Z} \iota(n) \iff m + 0 \le_\mathbb{N} 0 + n \iff m \le_\mathbb{N} n$.
2. **Closure of $\mathbb{Z}^+$:** Let $u = \iota(m)$ and $v = \iota(n)$ with $m, n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. Since $\iota$ is a homomorphism, $u + v = \iota(m + n)$ and $u \cdot v = \iota(m \cdot n)$. In $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2), the sum and product of two nonzero natural numbers are nonzero ($m \ge 1$ and $n \ge 1$ imply $m + n \ge 2 > 0$, and $m \cdot n = 0 \implies m = 0 \lor n = 0$ by multiplicative cancellation/zero-divisor freedom on $\mathbb{N}$). Thus $m + n, m \cdot n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, so $u + v \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $u \cdot v \in \mathbb{Z}^+$.
3. **Total Order and Trichotomy:**
   - *Reflexivity:* For any $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x - x = 0_\mathbb{Z} = \iota(0) \in \iota(\mathbb{N})$, so $x \le x$.
   - *Antisymmetry:* Suppose $x \le y$ and $y \le x$. Then $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \cup \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$ and $-(y - x) = x - y \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \cup \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$. If $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, then $x - y = -(y - x) \in \mathbb{Z}^-$, contradicting $x - y \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \cup \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$ by Sign Trichotomy. Thus $y - x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, which means $x = y$.
   - *Transitivity:* Suppose $x \le y$ and $y \le z$. Then $y - x = \iota(m)$ and $z - y = \iota(n)$ for some $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$. Adding these gives $z - x = (z - y) + (y - x) = \iota(n) + \iota(m) = \iota(n + m) \in \iota(\mathbb{N})$, so $x \le z$.
   - *Trichotomy and Totality:* For any $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, apply Sign Trichotomy to the integer $d := y - x$. Exactly one of the following holds: $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ (meaning $x < y$), $y - x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ (meaning $x = y$), or $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^-$, which is equivalent to $x - y = -(y - x) \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ (meaning $y < x$). In all cases, either $x \le y$ or $y \le x$, so $\le$ is a total order.
4. **Compatibility with Ring Operations:**
   - *Translation Invariance:* Notice that $(y + z) - (x + z) = y + z + (-x) + (-z) = y - x$. Therefore $(y + z) - (x + z) \in \iota(\mathbb{N}) \iff y - x \in \iota(\mathbb{N})$, so $x + z \le y + z \iff x \le y$ (and similarly for $<$).
   - *Negation:* $(-x) - (-y) = -x + y = y - x$, so $-y \le -x \iff x \le y$ and $-y < -x \iff x < y$.
   - *Multiplication:* Suppose $x < y$ and $z > 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Then $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $z \in \mathbb{Z}^+$. By closure of $\mathbb{Z}^+$ under multiplication (Part 2) and distributivity, we have:
     $$
     (y \cdot z) - (x \cdot z) = (y - x) \cdot z \in \mathbb{Z}^+
     $$
     which means $x \cdot z < y \cdot z$. Finally, if $x < y$ and $z < 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $-z \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, so $x \cdot (-z) < y \cdot (-z)$, which gives $-(x \cdot z) < -(y \cdot z)$, and hence $x \cdot z > y \cdot z$. $\blacksquare$

### $\mathbb{Z}$ as an Ordered Integral Domain

In Section 0.4, we will construct the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ as fractions $p / q$ with $p, q \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Just as constructing $\mathbb{Z}$ from $\mathbb{N}$ required the **Additive Cancellation Law** on $\mathbb{N}$, constructing $\mathbb{Q}$ from $\mathbb{Z}$ will critically require the **Multiplicative Cancellation Law** on $\mathbb{Z}$ (or equivalently, the absence of nonzero **zero divisors** in $\mathbb{Z}$). A commutative ring with unity $1 \neq 0$ in which the product of any two nonzero elements is nonzero is called an **integral domain**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: $\mathbb{Z}$ is an Ordered Integral Domain (Zero-Divisor Freedom and Multiplicative Cancellation)
> The ring of integers $(\mathbb{Z}, +, \cdot, 0_\mathbb{Z}, 1_\mathbb{Z})$ is an integral domain:
> 1. **No Zero Divisors:** For all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$,
>    $$
>    x \cdot y = 0_\mathbb{Z} \implies (x = 0_\mathbb{Z} \lor y = 0_\mathbb{Z})
>    $$
> 2. **Multiplicative Cancellation Law:** For all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $z \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$,
>    $$
>    x \cdot z = y \cdot z \implies x = y
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. **No Zero Divisors:** We prove the contrapositive: suppose $x \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and $y \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$; we will show that $x \cdot y \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$. By Sign Trichotomy, since $x \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$, either $x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ or $x \in \mathbb{Z}^-$ (in which case $-x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$). Similarly, either $y \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ or $-y \in \mathbb{Z}^+$. In all four sign combinations, we can express $x \cdot y$ in terms of a product of two elements of $\mathbb{Z}^+$:
   - If $x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $y \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, then $x \cdot y \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ by the closure of $\mathbb{Z}^+$ under multiplication, so $x \cdot y > 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and hence $x \cdot y \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
   - If $x \in \mathbb{Z}^-$ and $y \in \mathbb{Z}^-$, then $-x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $-y \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, so $x \cdot y = (-x) \cdot (-y) \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, whence $x \cdot y \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
   - If one of $x, y$ is in $\mathbb{Z}^+$ and the other is in $\mathbb{Z}^-$ (say $x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$ and $y \in \mathbb{Z}^-$), then $-y \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, so $-(x \cdot y) = x \cdot (-y) \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, which means $x \cdot y \in \mathbb{Z}^-$ and hence $x \cdot y \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
   In every case, $x \cdot y \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
2. **Multiplicative Cancellation Law:** Suppose $x \cdot z = y \cdot z$ with $z \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Subtracting $y \cdot z$ from both sides and using distributivity yields:
   $$
   (x - y) \cdot z = x \cdot z - y \cdot z = 0_\mathbb{Z}
   $$
   By Part 1 (No Zero Divisors), either $x - y = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ or $z = 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Since $z \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$ by hypothesis, we must have $x - y = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, and therefore $x = y$. $\blacksquare$

### Absolute Value on $\mathbb{Z}$ and the Triangle Inequality

In analysis, measuring the magnitude of a number or the distance between two numbers requires stripping away its sign. Sign Trichotomy allows us to define the **absolute value** on $\mathbb{Z}$ and prove the foundational **Triangle Inequality**, which will govern every convergence and continuity argument in real analysis.

> [!abstract] Definition: Absolute Value on $\mathbb{Z}$
> For any integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, the **absolute value** of $x$, denoted $|x| \in \mathbb{Z}$, is defined via Sign Trichotomy by:
> $$
> |x| := \begin{cases} x & \text{if } x \ge 0_\mathbb{Z} \\ -x & \text{if } x < 0_\mathbb{Z} \end{cases}
> $$

> [!theorem] Theorem: Fundamental Properties of the Absolute Value on $\mathbb{Z}$
> For all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$, the absolute value satisfies:
> 1. **Non-Negativity and Positive Definiteness:** $|x| \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$ (that is, $|x| \in \iota(\mathbb{N})$), with $|x| = 0_\mathbb{Z} \iff x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Moreover, $|-x| = |x|$ and $-|x| \le x \le |x|$.
> 2. **Multiplicativity:** $|x \cdot y| = |x| \cdot |y|$.
> 3. **Order Bound Equivalence:** For any $M \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$, $|x| \le M \iff -M \le x \le M$.
> 4. **The Triangle Inequality and Reverse Triangle Inequality:**
>    $$
>    |x + y| \le |x| + |y| \quad \text{and} \quad \bigl||x| - |y|\bigr| \le |x - y|
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. **Non-Negativity and Basic Bounds:** If $x \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $|x| = x \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$, with $|x| = 0_\mathbb{Z} \iff x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$. If $x < 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $x \in \mathbb{Z}^-$, so $|x| = -x \in \mathbb{Z}^+$, which means $|x| > 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Thus $|x| \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, and $|x| = 0_\mathbb{Z} \iff x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Checking $|-x| = |x|$ is immediate from the three cases $x > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, $x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, and $x < 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Furthermore, since $|x| \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$, we have $-|x| \le 0_\mathbb{Z} \le |x|$; because $x$ equals either $|x|$ or $-|x|$, it follows in both cases that $-|x| \le x \le |x|$.
2. **Multiplicativity:** We check the cases from Sign Trichotomy:
   - If $x = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ or $y = 0_\mathbb{Z}$, both $|x \cdot y|$ and $|x| \cdot |y|$ equal $0_\mathbb{Z}$.
   - If $x > 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and $y > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $x \cdot y > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, so $|x \cdot y| = x \cdot y = |x| \cdot |y|$.
   - If $x < 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and $y < 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $|x| = -x > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, $|y| = -y > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, and $x \cdot y = (-x)(-y) > 0_\mathbb{Z}$, so $|x \cdot y| = x \cdot y = (-x) \cdot (-y) = |x| \cdot |y|$.
   - If $x > 0_\mathbb{Z}$ and $y < 0_\mathbb{Z}$ (or vice versa), then $x \cdot y < 0_\mathbb{Z}$, so $|x \cdot y| = -(x \cdot y) = x \cdot (-y) = |x| \cdot |y|$.
3. **Order Bound Equivalence:** Let $M \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$.
   - ($\implies$) Suppose $|x| \le M$. Multiplying by $-1_\mathbb{Z}$ reverses the inequality to give $-M \le -|x|$. Combining this with $-|x| \le x \le |x|$ from Part 1 yields $-M \le -|x| \le x \le |x| \le M$.
   - ($\Longleftarrow$) Suppose $-M \le x \le M$. Note that $-M \le x$ implies $-x \le M$. If $x \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $|x| = x \le M$; if $x < 0_\mathbb{Z}$, then $|x| = -x \le M$. Thus $|x| \le M$ in both cases.
4. **Triangle Inequality and Reverse Triangle Inequality:** By Part 1, we have the two pairs of inequalities:
   $$
   -|x| \le x \le |x| \quad \text{and} \quad -|y| \le y \le |y|
   $$
   Adding these inequalities using the compatibility of $\le$ with addition yields:
   $$
   -(|x| + |y|) = (-|x|) + (-|y|) \le x + y \le |x| + |y|
   $$
   Applying the Order Bound Equivalence (Part 3) with $M := |x| + |y| \ge 0_\mathbb{Z}$ immediately gives the **Triangle Inequality**:
   $$
   |x + y| \le |x| + |y|
   $$
   Finally, to prove the **Reverse Triangle Inequality**, write $x = (x - y) + y$ and apply the Triangle Inequality:
   $$
   |x| = |(x - y) + y| \le |x - y| + |y| \implies |x| - |y| \le |x - y|
   $$
   Swapping the roles of $x$ and $y$ gives $|y| - |x| \le |y - x| = |-(x - y)| = |x - y|$, which multiplies by $-1_\mathbb{Z}$ to become $-|x - y| \le |x| - |y|$. Combining $-|x - y| \le |x| - |y| \le |x - y|$ and applying Part 3 yields $\bigl||x| - |y|\bigr| \le |x - y|$. $\blacksquare$

## 0.3 Exercises

### Problem 0.3.1: Failure of Transitivity Without Additive Cancellation

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(M, +, 0)$ be a commutative monoid (that is, $+$ is an associative and commutative binary operation on $M$ with identity element $0 \in M$) in which the additive cancellation law fails.
> 1. Let $X$ be a non-empty set, and consider the power set $M := \mathcal{P}(X)$ equipped with the binary operation $A + B := A \cup B$ and identity $0 := \emptyset$. Verify that $(\mathcal{P}(X), \cup, \emptyset)$ is a commutative monoid, and prove explicitly that the relation $\sim$ on $M \times M$ defined by
>    $$
>    (A, B) \sim (C, D) \iff A + D = B + C
>    $$
>    is reflexive and symmetric, but **fails to be transitive**.
> 2. Now let $(M, +, 0)$ be *any* commutative monoid, and define a modified binary relation $\approx$ on $M \times M$ (called the **Grothendieck equivalence relation**) by
>    $$
>    (a, b) \approx (c, d) \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } a + d + k = b + c + k
>    $$
>    Prove that $\approx$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$ even when cancellation fails in $M$, and show that on $(\mathcal{P}(X), \cup, \emptyset)$ the quotient $(M \times M) / {\approx}$ collapses to a single one-element set!

### Problem 0.3.2: The Universal Property of the Grothendieck Group $\mathbb{Z}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(G, +_G, 0_G)$ be any abelian group (that is, a set with an associative, commutative binary operation $+_G$, identity $0_G$, and additive inverses $-g$ for all $g \in G$), and let $f : \mathbb{N} \to G$ be a monoid homomorphism, meaning that $f(0) = 0_G$ and
> $$
> f(m + n) = f(m) +_G f(n) \quad \text{for all } m, n \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> Prove that there exists a **unique** group homomorphism $\tilde{f} : \mathbb{Z} \to G$ (satisfying $\tilde{f}(x +_\mathbb{Z} y) = \tilde{f}(x) +_G \tilde{f}(y)$ for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$) such that $\tilde{f} \circ \iota = f$, where $\iota : \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ is the canonical embedding $\iota(n) = [(n, 0)]$.

### Problem 0.3.3: Discreteness and Units of the Ring $\mathbb{Z}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. Prove directly from the construction of $\mathbb{Z}$ and the properties of $\mathbb{N}$ established in Section 0.2 that the order on $\mathbb{Z}$ is **discrete**: there exists no integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $0_\mathbb{Z} < x < 1_\mathbb{Z}$, and more generally, for any $n \in \mathbb{Z}$, there exists no integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $n < x < n + 1_\mathbb{Z}$.
> 2. An element $u \in \mathbb{Z}$ is called a **multiplicative unit** (or **invertible element**) of the ring $\mathbb{Z}$ if there exists $v \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $u \cdot v = 1_\mathbb{Z}$. Using Part 1 and the properties of the absolute value or order on $\mathbb{Z}$, prove that the only multiplicative units in $\mathbb{Z}$ are $1_\mathbb{Z}$ and $-1_\mathbb{Z}$.

### Problem 0.3.4: Bounded Well-Ordering and Two-Sided Induction on $\mathbb{Z}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Although $\mathbb{Z}$ is not well-ordered (since $\mathbb{Z}$ itself has no minimum element), it inherits a powerful bounded well-ordering structure from $\mathbb{N}$:
> 1. Let $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ be a non-empty subset that is **bounded below**, meaning there exists $B \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $B \le s$ for all $s \in S$. Prove using the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2) that $S$ has a unique **minimum element** $s_{\min} \in S$ (satisfying $s_{\min} \le s$ for all $s \in S$).
> 2. Deduce by a symmetry argument that every non-empty subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ that is **bounded above** ($\exists U \in \mathbb{Z}, \forall t \in T, t \le U$) has a unique **maximum element** $t_{\max} \in T$.
> 3. **Bidirectional Mathematical Induction:** Let $P(x)$ be a predicate defined for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$. Prove that if $P(0_\mathbb{Z})$ holds, and for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ both $P(x) \implies P(x + 1_\mathbb{Z})$ and $P(x) \implies P(x - 1_\mathbb{Z})$ hold, then $P(x)$ is true for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$.

### Problem 0.3.5: The Euclidean Division Algorithm Extended to All of $\mathbb{Z}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ be arbitrary integers with $b \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Prove that there exist **unique** integers $q, r \in \mathbb{Z}$ (called the **quotient** and **remainder**, respectively) such that
> $$
> a = q \cdot b + r \quad \text{and} \quad 0_\mathbb{Z} \le r < |b|
> $$
> *(Be sure to prove both existence and uniqueness for all four sign combinations of $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$!)*

### Problem 0.3.6: Order-Preserving Ring Automorphisms of $\mathbb{Z}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\phi : \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$ be a **ring homomorphism**, meaning that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$,
> $$
> \phi(x + y) = \phi(x) + \phi(y) \quad \text{and} \quad \phi(x \cdot y) = \phi(x) \cdot \phi(y)
> $$
> 1. Prove that $\phi(1_\mathbb{Z})$ must satisfy $(\phi(1_\mathbb{Z}))^2 = \phi(1_\mathbb{Z})$ (an **idempotent** in $\mathbb{Z}$), and use the Integral Domain property of $\mathbb{Z}$ to deduce that either $\phi(1_\mathbb{Z}) = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ or $\phi(1_\mathbb{Z}) = 1_\mathbb{Z}$.
> 2. Prove that if $\phi$ is not the identically zero map ($\phi(x) = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$), then $\phi$ is necessarily the identity map $\text{id}_\mathbb{Z}$ ($\phi(x) = x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$). In particular, $\text{id}_\mathbb{Z}$ is the unique ring automorphism of $\mathbb{Z}$, and $\phi$ automatically preserves the order relation $\le$ on $\mathbb{Z}$.

---

# 0.4 The Rational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}$), Ordered Fields, and the Incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$

In Section 0.3, we constructed the ring of integers $\mathbb{Z}$ from the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$ in order to make subtraction universally solvable: for every $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, the linear equation $x + b = a$ possesses a unique solution $x = a - b \in \mathbb{Z}$. Yet the moment we turn from addition to multiplication, we encounter a second algebraic barrier. Given two integers $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $b \neq 0$, when does the multiplicative equation
$$
b \cdot x = a
$$
admit a solution $x \in \mathbb{Z}$? By definition of divisibility, a solution exists in $\mathbb{Z}$ if and only if $b$ divides $a$ ($b \mid a$). For example, the equation $2 \cdot x = 1$ has no solution in $\mathbb{Z}$; indeed, since $0 < 1 < 2$, multiplying by any positive integer $x \ge 1$ yields $2 \cdot x \ge 2 > 1$, while multiplying by any non-positive integer $x \le 0$ yields $2 \cdot x \le 0 < 1$. Because no integer other than $1$ and $-1$ possesses a multiplicative inverse in $\mathbb{Z}$, the ordered integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ fails to be closed under division by nonzero elements.

To remedy this defect without circularity—that is, without assuming that fractions or real numbers already exist—we mirror the equivalence-class strategy of Section 0.3. Just as an integer $a - b$ was encoded as an equivalence class of ordered pairs $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ under the cross-addition relation $a + d = b + c$, a rational number $a / b$ will be encoded as an equivalence class of ordered pairs $(a, b) \in \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$ under the cross-multiplication relation $a \cdot d = b \cdot c$. This general algebraic procedure constructs the **field of fractions** of an integral domain.

## Construction of the Rational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}$) as the Field of Fractions of $\mathbb{Z}$

### The Relation of Cross-Multiplication on Formal Fraction Pairs

Why must we restrict the second coordinate of our ordered pairs to be nonzero? In any ring $R$, multiplying any element $x$ by $0$ yields $0 \cdot x = 0$. Consequently, an equation of the form $0 \cdot x = a$ has *no* solution if $a \neq 0$, and *every* element as a solution if $a = 0$. Division by zero cannot be made consistent with the field axioms in any nontrivial ring. We therefore restrict our denominators to the set of nonzero integers $\mathbb{Z}^* := \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Formal Fraction Pairs and the Rational Equivalence Relation
> Let $\mathbb{Z}^* := \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$ denote the set of nonzero integers, and define the Cartesian product of **formal fraction pairs** by
> $$
> S := \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^* = \{(a, b) : a \in \mathbb{Z} \text{ and } b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}\}.
> $$
> We define a binary relation $\sim$ on $S$ by declaring, for any $(a, b), (c, d) \in S$,
> $$
> (a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a \cdot_{\mathbb{Z}} d = b \cdot_{\mathbb{Z}} c.
> $$

For readability, we henceforth write $a d$ or $a \cdot d$ for integer multiplication $a \cdot_{\mathbb{Z}} d$ and $a + b$ for integer addition $a +_{\mathbb{Z}} b$ whenever the context is clear. Intuitively, the pair $(a, b) \in S$ represents the intended quotient $a / b$, and the condition $a d = b c$ expresses the familiar equality of fractions $a / b = c / d$ solely in terms of integer multiplication, which has already been rigorously established in Section 0.3.

Before we can form the quotient set $S / {\sim}$, we must verify that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $S$. Pay special attention to the proof of transitivity: this is the exact moment where both the restriction $d \neq 0$ and the fact that $\mathbb{Z}$ has **no zero divisors** (equivalently, satisfies the **multiplicative cancellation law** proved in Section 0.3) become indispensable.

> [!theorem] Theorem: $\sim$ is an Equivalence Relation on $\mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$
> The binary relation $\sim$ on $S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$ defined by $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a d = b c$ is an equivalence relation on $S$.

**Proof:**
We verify reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity in turn using the commutative ring properties and cancellation law of $\mathbb{Z}$ established in Section 0.3.

1. **Reflexivity:** Let $(a, b) \in S$. By the commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$, we have $a b = b a$. By definition of $\sim$, this means $(a, b) \sim (a, b)$.
2. **Symmetry:** Let $(a, b), (c, d) \in S$ and suppose $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$, so that $a d = b c$. Using the commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$ on both sides and the symmetry of equality, we obtain
   $$
   c b = b c = a d = d a.
   $$
   Since $c b = d a$, we have $(c, d) \sim (a, b)$.
3. **Transitivity:** Let $(a, b), (c, d), (e, f) \in S$ and suppose both $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$ and $(c, d) \sim (e, f)$. By definition of $\sim$, we are given the two integer equations
   $$
   a d = b c \quad \text{and} \quad c f = d e.
   $$
   Multiplying both sides of the first equation by $f \in \mathbb{Z}$ yields $(a d) f = (b c) f$. By the associativity and commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$, we can rearrange this as
   $$
   (a f) d = b (c f).
   $$
   Substituting the second hypothesis $c f = d e$ into the right-hand side and applying associativity and commutativity again gives
   $$
   (a f) d = b (d e) = (b e) d.
   $$
   Subtracting $(b e) d$ from both sides and using the distributive law in $\mathbb{Z}$, we arrive at
   $$
   (a f - b e) \cdot d = 0.
   $$
   Here is the crucial step: because $(c, d) \in S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$, we know that $d \neq 0$. Because $\mathbb{Z}$ is an integral domain (having no zero divisors, as proved in Section 0.3), the product of two integers is zero if and only if at least one factor is zero. Since $d \neq 0$, we are forced to conclude that $a f - b e = 0$, or equivalently
   $$
   a f = b e.
   $$
   By definition of $\sim$, this means $(a, b) \sim (e, f)$, completing the proof that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Why Zero Denominators and Zero Divisors Would Destroy Transitivity
> Notice what would happen if we had allowed $(0, 0) \in S$: we would have $(1, 2) \sim (0, 0)$ (since $1 \cdot 0 = 2 \cdot 0$) and $(0, 0) \sim (3, 4)$ (since $0 \cdot 4 = 0 \cdot 3$), which by transitivity would force $(1, 2) \sim (3, 4)$, collapsing every fraction into a single equivalence class! Likewise, in a ring that possesses nonzero zero divisors, the deduction $(a f - b e) \cdot d = 0 \implies a f = b e$ fails unless $d$ is explicitly restricted to be a non-zero-divisor. Thus the integral domain structure of $\mathbb{Z}$ is the bedrock upon which $\mathbb{Q}$ rests.

### The Quotient Set $\mathbb{Q}$ and Its Algebraic Operations

With $\sim$ certified as an equivalence relation on $S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$, the fundamental theory of quotient sets from Section 0.3 guarantees that the collection of all equivalence classes partitions $S$ into pairwise disjoint subsets.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Set of Rational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}$)
> The set of **rational numbers**, denoted $\mathbb{Q}$, is the quotient set of $S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$ modulo the equivalence relation $\sim$:
> $$
> \mathbb{Q} := \big(\mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})\big) / {\sim} = \{[(a, b)] : a \in \mathbb{Z}, \, b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}\},
> $$
> where the equivalence class of $(a, b) \in S$ is
> $$
> [(a, b)] := \{(c, d) \in \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}) : a d = b c\}.
> $$
> We frequently write $\frac{a}{b}$ or $a / b$ as a convenient synonym for the equivalence class $[(a, b)]$.

How should we define addition, negation, multiplication, and reciprocals on $\mathbb{Q}$? Guided by the rules of elementary arithmetic—where $\frac{a}{b} + \frac{c}{d} = \frac{a d + b c}{b d}$ and $\frac{a}{b} \cdot \frac{c}{d} = \frac{a c}{b d}$—we formulate these operations directly in terms of equivalence class representatives.

> [!abstract] Definition: Arithmetic Operations on $\mathbb{Q}$
> For any rational numbers $x = [(a, b)]$ and $y = [(c, d)]$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ (where $a, c \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b, d \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$), we define:
> 1. **Rational Addition ($+_{\mathbb{Q}}$):**
>    $$
>    [(a, b)] +_{\mathbb{Q}} [(c, d)] := [(a d + b c, \, b d)].
>    $$
> 2. **Rational Negation ($-_{\mathbb{Q}}$):**
>    $$
>    -_{\mathbb{Q}} [(a, b)] := [(-a, \, b)].
>    $$
> 3. **Rational Multiplication ($\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$):**
>    $$
>    [(a, b)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(c, d)] := [(a c, \, b d)].
>    $$
> 4. **Distinguished Constants ($0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ and $1_{\mathbb{Q}}$):**
>    $$
>    0_{\mathbb{Q}} := [(0, 1)], \qquad 1_{\mathbb{Q}} := [(1, 1)].
>    $$
> 5. **Multiplicative Inverse / Reciprocal ($x^{-1}$):** For any $[(a, b)] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ (which holds if and only if $a \neq 0$),
>    $$
>    [(a, b)]^{-1} := [(b, a)].
>    $$

Before we can use these formulas, two foundational questions demand proof:
1. **Closure of Denominators:** Since $(a, b), (c, d) \in S$ requires $b \neq 0$ and $d \neq 0$, is the new denominator $b d$ guaranteed to be in $\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$? And when $[(a, b)] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, is $a$ guaranteed to be in $\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$ so that $(b, a) \in S$?
2. **Independence of Representatives (Well-Definedness):** Since an equivalence class $[(a, b)]$ has infinitely many representatives (such as $(a, b)$ and $(2a, 2b)$), does replacing $(a, b)$ by an equivalent pair $(a', b') \sim (a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ by $(c', d') \sim (c, d)$ produce the *exact same* resulting equivalence class?

We answer both questions and establish the complete field structure of $\mathbb{Q}$ in the following comprehensive theorem.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Well-Definedness and Field Structure of $\mathbb{Q}$
> The operations $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$, $-_{\mathbb{Q}}$, $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$, and $(\cdot)^{-1}$ are well-defined on $\mathbb{Q}$ (independent of the choice of representatives, with all denominators lying in $\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$). Furthermore, $(\mathbb{Q}, +_{\mathbb{Q}}, \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}, 0_{\mathbb{Q}}, 1_{\mathbb{Q}})$ is a **field**—that is, a commutative ring with unity $1_{\mathbb{Q}} \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ in which every nonzero element possesses a multiplicative inverse. Finally, the canonical map $j: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$ given by
> $$
> j(a) := [(a, 1)]
> $$
> is an injective ring homomorphism that embeds $\mathbb{Z}$ as a subring of $\mathbb{Q}$.

**Proof:**
We divide the proof into four logical stages: denominator validity, well-definedness of all operations, verification of the field axioms, and verification of the canonical embedding $j$.

**Stage 1: Validity of Denominators and Characterization of $0_{\mathbb{Q}}$.**
Let $(a, b), (c, d) \in S$, so $b \neq 0$ and $d \neq 0$. Because $\mathbb{Z}$ is an integral domain (Section 0.3), the product of two nonzero integers is nonzero; hence $b d \neq 0$. Thus $(a d + b c, b d) \in S$ and $(a c, b d) \in S$, and obviously $(-a, b) \in S$ since $b \neq 0$.

Next, let us characterize when $[(a, b)] = 0_{\mathbb{Q}} = [(0, 1)]$. By definition of $\sim$, we have
$$
(a, b) \sim (0, 1) \iff a \cdot 1 = b \cdot 0 \iff a = 0.
$$
Consequently, $[(a, b)] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ if and only if $a \neq 0$. Whenever $[(a, b)] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, its numerator satisfies $a \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$, which ensures that the inverted pair $(b, a)$ belongs to $S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$. Note also that for any $b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$, we have $(0, b) \sim (0, 1)$ since $0 \cdot 1 = b \cdot 0 = 0$, and $(b, b) \sim (1, 1)$ since $b \cdot 1 = b \cdot 1$.

**Stage 2: Well-Definedness of $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$, $-_{\mathbb{Q}}$, $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$, and $(\cdot)^{-1}$.**
Suppose $(a, b) \sim (a', b')$ and $(c, d) \sim (c', d')$ in $S$. By definition of $\sim$, this means
$$
a b' = b a' \quad \text{and} \quad c d' = d c'.
$$
1. *Well-definedness of $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* We must show that $(a d + b c, b d) \sim (a' d' + b' c', b' d')$, which requires proving that $(a d + b c)(b' d') = (b d)(a' d' + b' c')$. Expanding the left-hand side using the distributive, associative, and commutative laws of $\mathbb{Z}$ and substituting $a b' = b a'$ and $c d' = d c'$, we compute:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (a d + b c)(b' d') &= (a d)(b' d') + (b c)(b' d') \\
                      &= (a b')(d d') + (b b')(c d') \\
                      &= (b a')(d d') + (b b')(d c') \\
                      &= (b d)(a' d') + (b d)(b' c') \\
                      &= (b d)(a' d' + b' c').
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Hence $(a d + b c, b d) \sim (a' d' + b' c', b' d')$, so $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$ is well-defined.
2. *Well-definedness of $-_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* Since $a b' = b a'$, multiplying both sides by $-1$ in $\mathbb{Z}$ gives $(-a) b' = b (-a')$, so $(-a, b) \sim (-a', b')$. Thus $-_{\mathbb{Q}}$ is well-defined.
3. *Well-definedness of $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* We must show that $(a c, b d) \sim (a' c', b' d')$, i.e., $(a c)(b' d') = (b d)(a' c')$. Using commutativity and associativity in $\mathbb{Z}$ along with $a b' = b a'$ and $c d' = d c'$, we find
   $$
   (a c)(b' d') = (a b')(c d') = (b a')(d c') = (b d)(a' c'),
   $$
   so $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$ is well-defined.
4. *Well-definedness of $(\cdot)^{-1}$:* Suppose $[(a, b)] = [(a', b')] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, so $a \neq 0$, $a' \neq 0$, and $a b' = b a'$. By commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$, we can rewrite $a b' = b a'$ as $b a' = a b'$, which is precisely the condition $(b, a) \sim (b', a')$. Thus $[(a, b)]^{-1} = [(b, a)]$ is well-defined.

**Stage 3: Verification of the Field Axioms.**
Let $x = [(a, b)]$, $y = [(c, d)]$, and $z = [(e, f)]$ be arbitrary elements of $\mathbb{Q}$.
- *Commutativity of $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* Using the commutativity of addition and multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$,
  $$
  x +_{\mathbb{Q}} y = [(a d + b c, b d)] = [(c b + d a, d b)] = y +_{\mathbb{Q}} x.
  $$
- *Associativity of $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* We compute both groupings directly:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (x +_{\mathbb{Q}} y) +_{\mathbb{Q}} z &= [(a d + b c, b d)] +_{\mathbb{Q}} [(e, f)] \\
                                       &= [((a d + b c) f + (b d) e, \, (b d) f)] \\
                                       &= [(a d f + b c f + b d e, \, b d f)] \\
                                       &= [(a (d f) + b (c f + d e), \, b (d f))] \\
                                       &= [(a, b)] +_{\mathbb{Q}} [(c f + d e, d f)] = x +_{\mathbb{Q}} (y +_{\mathbb{Q}} z).
  \end{aligned}
  $$
- *Additive Identity ($0_{\mathbb{Q}}$):* For $0_{\mathbb{Q}} = [(0, 1)]$, we have
  $$
  x +_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}} = [(a, b)] +_{\mathbb{Q}} [(0, 1)] = [(a \cdot 1 + b \cdot 0, \, b \cdot 1)] = [(a, b)] = x.
  $$
- *Additive Inverses:* Adding $x = [(a, b)]$ and $-_{\mathbb{Q}} x = [(-a, b)]$ yields
  $$
  x +_{\mathbb{Q}} (-_{\mathbb{Q}} x) = [(a b + b(-a), \, b^2)] = [(0, b^2)] = [(0, 1)] = 0_{\mathbb{Q}},
  $$
  since $(0, b^2) \sim (0, 1)$.
- *Commutativity and Associativity of $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* Using commutativity and associativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$,
  $$
  x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} y = [(a c, b d)] = [(c a, d b)] = y \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} x,
  $$
  and
  $$
  (x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} y) \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} z = [((a c) e, (b d) f)] = [(a (c e), b (d f))] = x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} (y \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} z).
  $$
- *Multiplicative Identity ($1_{\mathbb{Q}} \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$):* Since $1 \cdot 1 = 1 \neq 0 = 1 \cdot 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, we have $(1, 1) \nsim (0, 1)$, so $1_{\mathbb{Q}} \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$. Moreover,
  $$
  x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} 1_{\mathbb{Q}} = [(a, b)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(1, 1)] = [(a \cdot 1, b \cdot 1)] = [(a, b)] = x.
  $$
- *Multiplicative Inverses:* If $x = [(a, b)] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, then $a \neq 0$ and $b \neq 0$, so $a b \neq 0$. Multiplying $x$ by $x^{-1} = [(b, a)]$ gives
  $$
  x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} x^{-1} = [(a, b)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(b, a)] = [(a b, b a)] = [(a b, a b)] = [(1, 1)] = 1_{\mathbb{Q}},
  $$
  because $(a b) \cdot 1 = (a b) \cdot 1$.
- *Distributivity of $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$ over $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$:* On the one hand,
  $$
  x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} (y +_{\mathbb{Q}} z) = [(a, b)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(c f + d e, d f)] = [(a c f + a d e, \, b d f)].
  $$
  On the other hand,
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} y) +_{\mathbb{Q}} (x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} z) &= [(a c, b d)] +_{\mathbb{Q}} [(a e, b f)] \\
                                                                   &= [(a c b f + b d a e, \, b^2 d f)] \\
                                                                   &= [(b(a c f + a d e), \, b(b d f))].
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Since $(b(a c f + a d e)) \cdot (b d f) = (b^2 d f) \cdot (a c f + a d e)$, we have $[(b(a c f + a d e), b(b d f))] = [(a c f + a d e, b d f)]$, establishing $x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} (y +_{\mathbb{Q}} z) = (x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} y) +_{\mathbb{Q}} (x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} z)$.

**Stage 4: The Canonical Ring Embedding $j: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$.**
Define $j(a) := [(a, 1)]$ for $a \in \mathbb{Z}$. For any $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, we check:
- *Preservation of Addition:* $j(a) +_{\mathbb{Q}} j(b) = [(a, 1)] +_{\mathbb{Q}} [(b, 1)] = [(a \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot b, \, 1 \cdot 1)] = [(a + b, 1)] = j(a + b)$.
- *Preservation of Multiplication and Unity:* $j(a) \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} j(b) = [(a, 1)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(b, 1)] = [(a b, 1)] = j(a b)$, and $j(1) = [(1, 1)] = 1_{\mathbb{Q}}$.
- *Injectivity:* If $j(a) = j(b)$, then $(a, 1) \sim (b, 1)$, so $a \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot b$, which yields $a = b$.

Finally, observe that for any rational number $[(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Q}$, we can write
$$
[(a, b)] = [(a, 1)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(1, b)] = [(a, 1)] \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} [(b, 1)]^{-1} = j(a) \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} j(b)^{-1}.
$$
Thus every element of $\mathbb{Q}$ is the quotient of two embedded integers. Identifying each $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ with its image $j(a) = [(a, 1)]$, we regard $\mathbb{Z}$ as a subring of $\mathbb{Q}$ and write $[(a, b)] = a / b = a b^{-1}$. $\blacksquare$

## Abstract Ordered Fields and Their Fundamental Algebraic Consequences

### The Axioms of an Abstract Field and an Ordered Field

Having constructed $\mathbb{Q}$ as a concrete field, we now step back and axiomatize the structure we have just discovered. Why formulate the axioms of an **abstract field** and an **abstract ordered field** instead of proving inequalities exclusively for $\mathbb{Q}$? There is a profound strategic reason: in Section 0.5, we will construct a brand-new number system—the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$—which will also satisfy the axioms of an ordered field. Every algebraic identity and inequality that we prove today for an *arbitrary* ordered field $F$ will hold simultaneously in $\mathbb{Q}$ right now *and* in $\mathbb{R}$ the instant $\mathbb{R}$ is born, saving us from ever having to reprove a single algebraic rule or absolute-value inequality!

> [!abstract] Definition: Abstract Field
> A **field** is a quintuple $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ consisting of a set $F$, two binary operations $+ : F \times F \to F$ (addition) and $\cdot : F \times F \to F$ (multiplication), and two distinguished elements $0, 1 \in F$, satisfying the following axioms for all $x, y, z \in F$:
>
> **Field Addition Axioms (A1–A5):**
> - **(A1) Closure of Addition:** $x + y \in F$.
> - **(A2) Commutativity of Addition:** $x + y = y + x$.
> - **(A3) Associativity of Addition:** $(x + y) + z = x + (y + z)$.
> - **(A4) Additive Identity:** $x + 0 = x$.
> - **(A5) Additive Inverse:** For each $x \in F$, there exists an element $-x \in F$ such that $x + (-x) = 0$.
>
> **Field Multiplication Axioms (M1–M5):**
> - **(M1) Closure of Multiplication:** $x \cdot y \in F$ (usually written $x y$).
> - **(M2) Commutativity of Multiplication:** $x y = y x$.
> - **(M3) Associativity of Multiplication:** $(x y) z = x (y z)$.
> - **(M4) Multiplicative Identity (Nontriviality):** $1 \neq 0$, and $x \cdot 1 = x$ for all $x \in F$.
> - **(M5) Multiplicative Inverse:** For each $x \in F$ with $x \neq 0$, there exists an element $x^{-1} \in F$ (also written $1 / x$) such that $x \cdot x^{-1} = 1$.
>
> **Distributive Axiom (D):**
> - **(D) Distributivity:** $x (y + z) = x y + x z$.

In any field $F$, we define subtraction by $x - y := x + (-y)$ and, for $y \neq 0$, division by $x / y := x \cdot y^{-1}$. Because every field $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ is in particular a commutative ring with unity, all general ring properties proved in Section 0.3—such as uniqueness of $0$, $1$, and $-x$, the cancellation law $x + z = y + z \implies x = y$, the zero-product rule $x \cdot 0 = 0$, and the sign rules $(-1) x = -x$, $-(-x) = x$, $(-x) y = -(x y)$, and $(-x)(-y) = x y$—hold automatically in $F$. Furthermore, because every nonzero $x \in F$ has an inverse $x^{-1}$, every field is an integral domain: if $x y = 0$ and $x \neq 0$, multiplying both sides by $x^{-1}$ yields $y = 1 \cdot y = (x^{-1} x) y = x^{-1}(x y) = x^{-1} \cdot 0 = 0$. Notice also that the multiplicative inverse $x^{-1}$ of $x \neq 0$ is unique (if $x y = 1$ and $x z = 1$, then $y = y(x z) = (y x) z = 1 \cdot z = z$), and satisfies $(x^{-1})^{-1} = x$ and $(x y)^{-1} = x^{-1} y^{-1}$ for $x, y \neq 0$.

Now let us equip a field $F$ with a strict order relation $<$ that interacts harmoniously with both addition and multiplication.

> [!abstract] Definition: Ordered Field
> An **ordered field** is a sextuple $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ where $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ is a field and $<$ is a binary relation on $F$ satisfying the following four **Order Axioms** for all $x, y, z \in F$:
> - **(O1) Trichotomy:** For every pair $x, y \in F$, *exactly one* of the following three relations holds:
>   $$
>   x < y, \qquad x = y, \qquad \text{or} \qquad y < x.
>   $$
> - **(O2) Transitivity:** If $x < y$ and $y < z$, then $x < z$.
> - **(O3) Additive Compatibility (Translation Invariance):** If $x < y$, then for every $z \in F$,
>   $$
>   x + z < y + z.
>   $$
> - **(O4) Multiplicative Positivity:** If $0 < x$ and $0 < y$, then $0 < x y$.

As is standard, we write $y > x$ to mean $x < y$, $x \le y$ to mean $(x < y \text{ or } x = y)$, and $x \ge y$ to mean $y \le x$. An element $x \in F$ is called **positive** if $x > 0$, **negative** if $x < 0$, **non-negative** if $x \ge 0$, and **non-positive** if $x \le 0$. Notice that by Axiom (O3), adding $-x$ to both sides of $x < y$ yields $0 = x + (-x) < y + (-x) = y - x$, and conversely adding $x$ to both sides of $0 < y - x$ yields $x < y$. Thus in any ordered field,
$$
x < y \iff y - x > 0.
$$
In other words, the entire order relation $<$ on $F$ is completely determined by the **positive cone** $P := \{x \in F : x > 0\}$, which satisfies three defining properties: (i) for every $x \in F$, exactly one of $x \in P$, $x = 0$, or $-x \in P$ holds; (ii) if $x, y \in P$, then $x + y \in P$ (since $x > 0 \implies x + y > y > 0$ by (O3) and (O2)); and (iii) if $x, y \in P$, then $x y \in P$.

### Making $\mathbb{Q}$ an Ordered Field

Let us immediately verify that the field of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ constructed above can be endowed with a canonical order $<_{\mathbb{Q}}$ extending the order $<_{\mathbb{Z}}$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ from Section 0.3. When should a fraction $[(a, b)] = a / b$ be positive? In elementary arithmetic, $a / b > 0$ if and only if the numerator $a$ and denominator $b$ have the *same sign* in $\mathbb{Z}$. How can we express "having the same sign in $\mathbb{Z}$" using a single algebraic condition that never requires choosing a representative with $b > 0$? Two nonzero integers $a$ and $b$ have the same sign in the ordered integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ if and only if their integer product is strictly positive: $a \cdot_{\mathbb{Z}} b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$!

> [!abstract] Definition: Positivity and Order on $\mathbb{Q}$
> A rational number $x = [(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Q}$ is defined to be **positive**, written $x >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, if and only if
> $$
> a \cdot_{\mathbb{Z}} b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0_{\mathbb{Z}}.
> $$
> For arbitrary $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$, we define the strict order relation $<_{\mathbb{Q}}$ by
> $$
> x <_{\mathbb{Q}} y \iff y -_{\mathbb{Q}} x >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}}.
> $$
> Equivalently, for $x = [(a, b)]$ and $y = [(c, d)]$, since $y -_{\mathbb{Q}} x = [(c b - d a, \, b d)]$, we have
> $$
> [(a, b)] <_{\mathbb{Q}} [(c, d)] \iff (c b - d a)(b d) >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0_{\mathbb{Z}}.
> $$

> [!theorem] Theorem: $(\mathbb{Q}, +_{\mathbb{Q}}, \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}, 0_{\mathbb{Q}}, 1_{\mathbb{Q}}, <_{\mathbb{Q}})$ is an Ordered Field
> The positivity condition $[(a, b)] >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}} \iff a b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ is well-defined (independent of the choice of representative $(a, b) \in S$), and the resulting relation $<_{\mathbb{Q}}$ satisfies Order Axioms (O1)–(O4), making $\mathbb{Q}$ an ordered field. Moreover, the canonical embedding $j: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$ via $j(a) = [(a, 1)]$ is strictly order-preserving: $a <_{\mathbb{Z}} b \iff j(a) <_{\mathbb{Q}} j(b)$.

**Proof:**
**Step 1: Well-Definedness of Positivity on $\mathbb{Q}$.**
Suppose $(a, b) \sim (a', b')$ in $S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$, so that $a b' = b a'$ and $b, b' \neq 0$. We must prove that $a b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ if and only if $a' b' >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$. Multiplying the equation $a b' = b a'$ by $b b'$ and using commutativity and associativity in $\mathbb{Z}$, we obtain
$$
(a b)(b')^2 = (a b')(b b') = (b a')(b b') = (a' b') b^2.
$$
In the ordered integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ (Section 0.3), the square of any nonzero integer is strictly positive; since $b \neq 0$ and $b' \neq 0$, we have $b^2 >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ and $(b')^2 >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$.
- If $a b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, then $(a' b') b^2 = (a b)(b')^2 >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ as the product of two positive integers. Since $b^2 >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, trichotomy in $\mathbb{Z}$ forces $a' b' >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ (if $a' b' \le_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, multiplying by $b^2 >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ would yield $(a' b') b^2 \le_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, a contradiction).
- By complete symmetry, if $a' b' >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, then $a b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$.
Thus positivity on $\mathbb{Q}$ is independent of the chosen representative. Note also that every rational class $[(a, b)]$ has a **positive-denominator representative**: if $b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, $(a, b)$ already has positive denominator; if $b <_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, then $-b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ and $(-a, -b) \sim (a, b)$ since $a(-b) = b(-a)$. When $b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ and $d >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, we have $b d >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, so $[(a, b)] <_{\mathbb{Q}} [(c, d)] \iff a d <_{\mathbb{Z}} b c$.

**Step 2: Verification of the Order Axioms (O1)–(O4).**
Let $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Q}$. By choosing positive-denominator representatives, we may write $x = [(a, b)]$, $y = [(c, d)]$, and $z = [(e, f)]$ with $b, d, f >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$.
- **(O1) Trichotomy:** By definition, $x <_{\mathbb{Q}} y \iff (b c - a d)(b d) >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0 \iff a d <_{\mathbb{Z}} b c$ (since $b d >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$), while $x = y \iff a d = b c$, and $y <_{\mathbb{Q}} x \iff b c <_{\mathbb{Z}} a d$. Because the integers $a d$ and $b c$ satisfy trichotomy in $\mathbb{Z}$ (Section 0.3), *exactly one* of $a d <_{\mathbb{Z}} b c$, $a d = b c$, or $b c <_{\mathbb{Z}} a d$ holds. Thus *exactly one* of $x <_{\mathbb{Q}} y$, $x = y$, or $y <_{\mathbb{Q}} x$ holds.
- **(O2) Transitivity:** Suppose $x <_{\mathbb{Q}} y$ and $y <_{\mathbb{Q}} z$. With positive denominators $b, d, f >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, this means $a d <_{\mathbb{Z}} b c$ and $c f <_{\mathbb{Z}} d e$. Multiplying the first inequality by $f >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ and the second by $b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ in the ordered ring $\mathbb{Z}$ preserves the strict inequalities:
  $$
  a d f <_{\mathbb{Z}} b c f \quad \text{and} \quad b c f <_{\mathbb{Z}} b d e.
  $$
  By transitivity of $<_{\mathbb{Z}}$, we obtain $(a f) d <_{\mathbb{Z}} (b e) d$. Since $d >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$, cancelling $d$ in $\mathbb{Z}$ yields $a f <_{\mathbb{Z}} b e$, which means $x = [(a, b)] <_{\mathbb{Q}} [(e, f)] = z$.
- **(O3) Additive Compatibility:** Suppose $x <_{\mathbb{Q}} y$, which by definition means $y -_{\mathbb{Q}} x >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$. For any $z \in \mathbb{Q}$, the field axioms in $\mathbb{Q}$ give
  $$
  (y +_{\mathbb{Q}} z) -_{\mathbb{Q}} (x +_{\mathbb{Q}} z) = y -_{\mathbb{Q}} x >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}},
  $$
  which by definition of $<_{\mathbb{Q}}$ means $x +_{\mathbb{Q}} z <_{\mathbb{Q}} y +_{\mathbb{Q}} z$.
- **(O4) Multiplicative Positivity:** Suppose $0_{\mathbb{Q}} <_{\mathbb{Q}} x = [(a, b)]$ and $0_{\mathbb{Q}} <_{\mathbb{Q}} y = [(c, d)]$. By definition of positivity, $a b >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$ and $c d >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0$. Since $x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} y = [(a c, b d)]$, we test the sign of $(a c)(b d)$ in $\mathbb{Z}$:
  $$
  (a c)(b d) = (a b)(c d) >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0
  $$
  as the product of two positive integers in $\mathbb{Z}$. Hence $x \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}} y >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$.

**Step 3: Order-Preservation of $j: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$.**
For any $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, we have $j(b) -_{\mathbb{Q}} j(a) = [(b - a, 1)]$. By definition, $[(b - a, 1)] >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}} \iff (b - a) \cdot 1 >_{\mathbb{Z}} 0 \iff a <_{\mathbb{Z}} b$. Thus $a <_{\mathbb{Z}} b \iff j(a) <_{\mathbb{Q}} j(b)$. $\blacksquare$

### Fundamental Algebraic and Order Theorems in Any Ordered Field $F$

Now let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be an **arbitrary ordered field**. Every theorem we prove in this subsection uses *only* the Field Axioms (A1)–(A5), (M1)–(M5), (D) and the Order Axioms (O1)–(O4).

> [!theorem] Theorem: Fundamental Sign, Square, and Reciprocal Laws in an Ordered Field
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be any ordered field, and let $x, y, z, w \in F$. Then the following properties hold:
> 1. **Sign Reversal under Negation:** $x > 0 \iff -x < 0$, and more generally,
>    $$
>    x < y \iff -y < -x.
>    $$
> 2. **Addition of Inequalities:** If $x < y$ and $z < w$, then $x + z < y + w$.
> 3. **Multiplication by Positive and Negative Elements:**
>    - If $x < y$ and $z > 0$, then $x z < y z$.
>    - If $x < y$ and $z < 0$, then $x z > y z$.
> 4. **Strict Positivity of Nonzero Squares ($x^2 > 0$):** For every $x \in F$ with $x \neq 0$,
>    $$
>    x^2 > 0.
>    $$
>    Consequently, for every $x \in F$, $x^2 \ge 0$, with $x^2 = 0 \iff x = 0$. In particular, the multiplicative identity is strictly positive:
>    $$
>    1 = 1^2 > 0 \quad \text{and} \quad -1 < 0.
>    $$
> 5. **Positivity and Order Reversal of Reciprocals:**
>    - If $x > 0$, then $x^{-1} > 0$; if $x < 0$, then $x^{-1} < 0$.
>    - If $0 < x < y$, then $0 < y^{-1} < x^{-1}$.

**Proof:**
1. *Sign Reversal:* Suppose $x < y$. By Axiom (O3), adding $(-x) + (-y)$ to both sides of $x < y$ yields
   $$
   x + \big((-x) + (-y)\big) < y + \big((-x) + (-y)\big).
   $$
   By associativity, commutativity, and the additive inverse axiom, the left-hand side simplifies to $(x + (-x)) + (-y) = 0 + (-y) = -y$, and the right-hand side simplifies to $(y + (-y)) + (-x) = 0 + (-x) = -x$. Thus $-y < -x$. Conversely, if $-y < -x$, applying the same implication and using $-(-x) = x$ and $-(-y) = y$ gives $x < y$. Setting $x = 0$ (or $y = 0$) and noting $-0 = 0$ gives $y > 0 \iff -y < 0$.
2. *Addition of Inequalities:* Suppose $x < y$ and $z < w$. Adding $z$ to both sides of $x < y$ via (O3) gives $x + z < y + z$. Adding $y$ to both sides of $z < w$ via (O3) and (A2) gives $y + z < y + w$. By transitivity (O2), $x + z < y + w$.
3. *Multiplication by $z > 0$ or $z < 0$:* Suppose $x < y$. Adding $-x$ to both sides via (O3) gives $0 < y - x$.
   - If $z > 0$, Axiom (O4) applied to the positive elements $y - x > 0$ and $z > 0$ yields $0 < (y - x) z = y z - x z$. Adding $x z$ to both sides via (O3) gives $x z < y z$.
   - If $z < 0$, then by Property 1 we have $-z > 0$. Applying Axiom (O4) to $y - x > 0$ and $-z > 0$ gives $0 < (y - x)(-z) = x z - y z$. Adding $y z$ to both sides via (O3) gives $y z < x z$, i.e., $x z > y z$.
4. *Strict Positivity of Nonzero Squares:* Let $x \in F$ with $x \neq 0$. By Trichotomy (O1), either $x > 0$ or $x < 0$.
   - **Case 1 ($x > 0$):** By Axiom (O4) with $y = x > 0$, we have $x^2 = x \cdot x > 0$.
   - **Case 2 ($x < 0$):** By Property 1, $-x > 0$. Applying Axiom (O4) to $-x > 0$ and $-x > 0$, and using the ring identity $(-x)(-x) = x^2$, we obtain
     $$
     x^2 = (-x)(-x) > 0.
     $$
   In both cases, $x^2 > 0$. Since $0^2 = 0$, we have $x^2 \ge 0$ for all $x \in F$, with equality iff $x = 0$. Finally, by Field Axiom (M4), $1 \neq 0$, and since $1 = 1 \cdot 1 = 1^2$, it follows immediately that $1 > 0$, whence $-1 < 0$ by Property 1.
5. *Positivity and Order Reversal of Reciprocals:* Suppose $x > 0$. Then $x \neq 0$ by Trichotomy (O1), so $x^{-1} \in F$ exists and satisfies $x \cdot x^{-1} = 1$. Could $x^{-1} \le 0$? If $x^{-1} = 0$, then $1 = x \cdot 0 = 0$, contradicting $1 \neq 0$. If $x^{-1} < 0$, then multiplying $x^{-1} < 0$ by $x > 0$ via Property 3 yields $1 = x^{-1} x < 0 \cdot x = 0$, contradicting $1 > 0$ from Property 4! By Trichotomy (O1), we conclude $x^{-1} > 0$. (Similarly, if $x < 0$, then $-x > 0$, so $-x^{-1} = (-x)^{-1} > 0$, giving $x^{-1} < 0$.)
   Now suppose $0 < x < y$. By transitivity, $y > 0$, so both $x^{-1} > 0$ and $y^{-1} > 0$, and their product satisfies $x^{-1} y^{-1} > 0$ by (O4). Multiplying both sides of $x < y$ by $x^{-1} y^{-1} > 0$ via Property 3 gives
   $$
   y^{-1} = (x \cdot x^{-1}) y^{-1} = x (x^{-1} y^{-1}) < y (x^{-1} y^{-1}) = (y \cdot y^{-1}) x^{-1} = x^{-1}.
   $$
   Combining $0 < y^{-1}$ and $y^{-1} < x^{-1}$ yields $0 < y^{-1} < x^{-1}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Remark: Every Ordered Field Has Characteristic Zero and Contains $\mathbb{Q}$
> Look closely at the consequence $1 > 0$ in Property 4 above. By inductively adding $1 > 0$ to itself using Property 2, we find that in *any* ordered field $F$, the natural multiples of the multiplicative identity $1_F$ satisfy
> $$
> 0_F < 1_F < 1_F + 1_F < 1_F + 1_F + 1_F < \cdots.
> $$
> Consequently, $n \cdot 1_F > 0_F$ (and hence $n \cdot 1_F \neq 0_F$) for every nonzero natural number $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. In algebraic terminology, every ordered field has **characteristic zero** (and therefore must be an infinite set). Furthermore, because $b \cdot 1_F \neq 0_F$ for every $b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$, every ordered field $F$ contains a canonical isomorphic copy of the rational field $\mathbb{Q}$ via the map $[(a, b)] \mapsto (a \cdot 1_F)(b \cdot 1_F)^{-1}$ (see **Problem 0.4.1**). In this precise sense, $\mathbb{Q}$ is the *smallest* ordered field: every ordered field is an extension of $\mathbb{Q}$!

Another indispensable tool across all of real analysis is **Bernoulli's Inequality**, which provides a sharp linear lower bound for powers $(1 + x)^n$. Because its proof relies only on mathematical induction (Section 0.2) and the ordered field axioms, we establish it now in complete generality for any ordered field $F$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Bernoulli's Inequality in an Ordered Field
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be any ordered field. For every $x \in F$ satisfying $x \ge -1$ and every natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we have
> $$
> (1 + x)^n \ge 1 + n x,
> $$
> where $(1 + x)^0 := 1$ and $n x := n \cdot 1_F \cdot x$. Moreover, if $n \ge 2$ and $x > -1$ with $x \neq 0$, the inequality is strict: $(1 + x)^n > 1 + n x$.

**Proof:**
Fix $x \in F$ with $x \ge -1$, and observe that adding $1$ to both sides of $x \ge -1$ yields the crucial non-negativity condition
$$
1 + x \ge 0.
$$
We proceed by mathematical induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2). Let $P(n)$ be the statement $(1 + x)^n \ge 1 + n x$.
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** When $n = 0$, the left-hand side is $(1 + x)^0 = 1$ and the right-hand side is $1 + 0 \cdot x = 1$. Since $1 \ge 1$, $P(0)$ holds (and for $n = 1$, $(1 + x)^1 = 1 + 1 \cdot x$ holds with equality as well).
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $P(k)$ holds for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so that
  $$
  (1 + x)^k \ge 1 + k x.
  $$
  Since $1 + x \ge 0$, multiplying both sides of the inductive hypothesis by the non-negative element $1 + x$ preserves the inequality $\ge$ (if $1 + x > 0$, it preserves strict inequality by Property 3 above; if $1 + x = 0$, both sides become $0$). Expanding the right-hand side via the distributive law in $F$, we obtain
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (1 + x)^{k+1} &= (1 + x)^k (1 + x) \\
                &\ge (1 + k x)(1 + x) \\
                &= 1 + x + k x + k x^2 \\
                &= 1 + (k + 1) x + k x^2.
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Now examine the quadratic remainder term $k x^2$. By Property 4 of an ordered field, $x^2 \ge 0$ for every $x \in F$, and since $k \in \mathbb{N}$, we have $k \ge 0$ in $F$, so $k x^2 \ge 0$. Adding $1 + (k + 1) x$ to both sides of $k x^2 \ge 0$ yields
  $$
  1 + (k + 1) x + k x^2 \ge 1 + (k + 1) x.
  $$
  By transitivity of $\ge$, we conclude that
  $$
  (1 + x)^{k+1} \ge 1 + (k + 1) x,
  $$
  completing the inductive step. By the Principle of Mathematical Induction, $(1 + x)^n \ge 1 + n x$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Finally, if $x > -1$ ($1 + x > 0$) and $x \neq 0$ ($x^2 > 0$), then for $n = 2$ we have $(1 + x)^2 = 1 + 2 x + x^2 > 1 + 2 x$, and induction for $n \ge 2$ preserves the strict inequality because $1 + x > 0$ and $k x^2 > 0$. $\blacksquare$

### Absolute Value and Distance in an Ordered Field

To measure the "size" of an element $x \in F$ or the "closeness" between two elements $x, y \in F$ in analysis, we introduce the absolute value and distance function on any ordered field.

> [!abstract] Definition: Absolute Value and Distance in an Ordered Field
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be an ordered field. For any $x \in F$, the **absolute value** of $x$, denoted $|x|$, is defined piecewise by
> $$
> |x| := \begin{cases} x & \text{if } x > 0, \\ 0 & \text{if } x = 0, \\ -x & \text{if } x < 0. \end{cases}
> $$
> (Notice that by Trichotomy (O1), these three cases are mutually exclusive and exhaustive, and since $-0 = 0$, we can compactly write $|x| = x$ if $x \ge 0$ and $|x| = -x$ if $x < 0$.)
