> For any two elements $x, y \in F$, the **distance** between $x$ and $y$ is defined by
> $$
> d(x, y) := |x - y|.
> $$

Let us establish the basic algebraic properties of $|x|$ and then prove the most important inequality in all of mathematical analysis: the **Triangle Inequality**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Fundamental Properties of Absolute Value and the Triangle Inequality
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be an ordered field. For all $x, y, c \in F$, the following properties hold:
> 1. **Non-Negativity and Definiteness:** $|x| \ge 0$, with $|x| = 0 \iff x = 0$.
> 2. **Symmetry and Square Identity:** $|-x| = |x|$ and $|x|^2 = x^2$.
> 3. **Multiplicativity:** $|x y| = |x| \cdot |y|$, and if $y \neq 0$, then $|x / y| = |x| / |y|$.
> 4. **Fundamental Sandwich Bounds:** $-|x| \le x \le |x|$.
> 5. **Symmetric Interval Characterization:** For any $c \in F$,
>    $$
>    |x| \le c \iff -c \le x \le c,
>    $$
>    and for strict inequalities, $|x| < c \iff -c < x < c$.
> 6. **The Triangle Inequality:**
>    $$
>    |x + y| \le |x| + |y|.
>    $$
> 7. **The Reverse Triangle Inequality:**
>    $$
>    \big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - y| \quad \text{and} \quad \big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x + y|.
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. *Non-Negativity and Definiteness:* By Trichotomy (O1), if $x > 0$, then $|x| = x > 0$; if $x = 0$, then $|x| = 0$; if $x < 0$, then $|x| = -x > 0$. Thus $|x| > 0$ whenever $x \neq 0$, and $|x| = 0 \iff x = 0$.
2. *Symmetry and Square Identity:* If $x > 0$, then $-x < 0$, so $|-x| = -(-x) = x = |x|$. If $x = 0$, $|-0| = |0| = 0$. If $x < 0$, then $-x > 0$, so $|-x| = -x = |x|$. Moreover, since $|x| = \pm x$, we have $|x|^2 = (\pm x)^2 = x^2$.
3. *Multiplicativity:* We check cases using Trichotomy:
   - If $x = 0$ or $y = 0$, then $x y = 0$, so $|x y| = 0 = |x| \cdot |y|$.
   - If $x > 0$ and $y > 0$, then $x y > 0$ by (O4), so $|x y| = x y = |x| \cdot |y|$.
   - If $x < 0$ and $y < 0$, then $-x > 0$ and $-y > 0$, so $x y = (-x)(-y) > 0$, giving $|x y| = x y = (-x)(-y) = |x| \cdot |y|$.
   - If $x > 0$ and $y < 0$ (or vice versa), then $x y < 0$, so $|x y| = -(x y) = x (-y) = |x| \cdot |y|$.
   For $y \neq 0$, applying multiplicativity to $x = (x / y) \cdot y$ gives $|x| = |x / y| \cdot |y|$, and dividing by $|y| > 0$ yields $|x / y| = |x| / |y|$.
4. *Fundamental Sandwich Bounds:* If $x \ge 0$, then $|x| = x$, so $x \le |x|$ holds with equality, and since $|x| \ge 0 \ge -|x|$, we have $-|x| \le x \le |x|$. If $x < 0$, then $|x| = -x > 0$, so $-|x| = x$ (hence $-|x| \le x$ holds with equality) and $x < 0 < |x|$, again giving $-|x| \le x \le |x|$.
5. *Symmetric Interval Characterization:*
   - ($\Longrightarrow$) Suppose $|x| \le c$. Multiplying by $-1$ reverses the inequality to $-c \le -|x|$. Combining this with Property 4 ($-|x| \le x \le |x|$) via transitivity yields
     $$
     -c \le -|x| \le x \le |x| \le c,
     $$
     so $-c \le x \le c$.
   - ($\Longleftarrow$) Suppose $-c \le x \le c$. Multiplying the left inequality $-c \le x$ by $-1$ gives $-x \le c$. Thus *both* $x \le c$ and $-x \le c$ hold. Since $|x|$ is by definition equal to either $x$ or $-x$, it follows in either case that $|x| \le c$. (The exact same argument with $<$ in place of $\le$ proves $|x| < c \iff -c < x < c$.)
6. *The Triangle Inequality:* By Property 4 applied to $x$ and to $y$, we have the two pairs of inequalities
   $$
   -|x| \le x \le |x| \quad \text{and} \quad -|y| \le y \le |y|.
   $$
   Adding these inequalities term-by-term (using $a \le b \text{ and } c \le d \implies a + c \le b + d$) gives
   $$
   -(|x| + |y|) = -|x| - |y| \le x + y \le |x| + |y|.
   $$
   Applying Property 5 with $x + y$ in place of $x$ and $c = |x| + |y|$ immediately yields
   $$
   |x + y| \le |x| + |y|.
   $$
7. *The Reverse Triangle Inequality:* Write $x = (x - y) + y$ and apply the Triangle Inequality (Property 6) to obtain
   $$
   |x| = |(x - y) + y| \le |x - y| + |y|.
   $$
   Subtracting $|y|$ from both sides gives
   $$
   |x| - |y| \le |x - y|.
   $$
   Interchanging the roles of $x$ and $y$ and using $|y - x| = |-(x - y)| = |x - y|$ (by Property 2) gives
   $$
   |y| - |x| \le |y - x| = |x - y|, \quad \text{so} \quad -|x - y| \le |x| - |y|.
   $$
   Combining $-|x - y| \le |x| - |y| \le |x - y|$ with Property 5 yields $\big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - y|$. Finally, replacing $y$ by $-y$ and using $|-y| = |y|$ yields $\big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - (-y)| = |x + y|$. $\blacksquare$

From the properties of $|x|$, we see immediately that the distance function $d(x, y) = |x - y|$ on any ordered field $F$ satisfies the three axioms of a metric:
1. **Positivity and Definiteness:** $d(x, y) \ge 0$, with $d(x, y) = 0 \iff x = y$.
2. **Symmetry:** $d(x, y) = |x - y| = |-(y - x)| = |y - x| = d(y, x)$.
3. **Triangle Inequality:** For all $x, y, z \in F$, writing $x - z = (x - y) + (y - z)$ gives
   $$
   d(x, z) = |x - z| = |(x - y) + (y - z)| \le |x - y| + |y - z| = d(x, y) + d(y, z).
   $$

### The $\varepsilon$-Principle in an Ordered Field

In analysis, we constantly prove that two numbers $a$ and $b$ satisfy $a \le b$ (or $a = b$) not by comparing them directly, but by showing that $a$ cannot exceed $b$ by any positive tolerance $\varepsilon > 0$. Why does $a \le b + \varepsilon$ for *every* $\varepsilon > 0$ force $a \le b$? Because in any ordered field $F$, there is no "smallest positive element": if $a > b$, the positive gap $\delta := a - b > 0$ can be cut in half to produce $\varepsilon := \delta / 2 > 0$, which satisfies $0 < \varepsilon < \delta$! Let us formalize this pivotal principle.

> [!theorem] Theorem: The $\varepsilon$-Principle for Inequalities and Equality in an Ordered Field
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be any ordered field, and let $a, b \in F$.
> 1. **Inequality Form:** If $a \le b + \varepsilon$ (or $a < b + \varepsilon$) holds for *every* $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, then $a \le b$.
> 2. **Equality / Metric Form:** If $|a - b| \le \varepsilon$ (or $|a - b| < \varepsilon$) holds for *every* $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, then $a = b$.

**Proof:**
1. *Inequality Form:* Suppose that $a \le b + \varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$. Seeking a contradiction, assume that $a \le b$ fails; by Trichotomy (O1), we must have $a > b$, or equivalently $a - b > 0$. In the ordered field $F$, let $2 := 1 + 1$. Since $1 > 0$, we have $2 = 1 + 1 > 1 > 0$, so $2^{-1} > 0$ exists in $F$. Define the test tolerance
   $$
   \varepsilon_0 := \frac{a - b}{2} = (a - b) \cdot 2^{-1}.
   $$
   Because $a - b > 0$ and $2^{-1} > 0$, Axiom (O4) guarantees that $\varepsilon_0 > 0$. Moreover, since $1 < 2$, multiplying by $(a - b) \cdot 2^{-1} > 0$ gives
   $$
   \varepsilon_0 = 1 \cdot (a - b) \cdot 2^{-1} < 2 \cdot (a - b) \cdot 2^{-1} = a - b.
   $$
   Adding $b$ to both sides of $\varepsilon_0 < a - b$ via Axiom (O3) yields
   $$
   b + \varepsilon_0 < a.
   $$
   By Trichotomy (O1), $b + \varepsilon_0 < a$ directly contradicts our hypothesis that $a \le b + \varepsilon$ holds for all $\varepsilon > 0$ (applied to $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0 > 0$). Therefore, the assumption $a > b$ is impossible, and Trichotomy forces $a \le b$.
2. *Equality Form:* Suppose $|a - b| \le \varepsilon$ (or $|a - b| < \varepsilon$) for every $\varepsilon > 0$ in $F$. Applying Part 1 with $|a - b|$ in place of $a$ and $0$ in place of $b$ yields $|a - b| \le 0$. On the other hand, we already know that $|a - b| \ge 0$ in any ordered field. By Trichotomy, $|a - b| \le 0$ and $|a - b| \ge 0$ together force $|a - b| = 0$, which by definiteness of the absolute value implies $a - b = 0$, i.e., $a = b$. $\blacksquare$

## The Archimedean Property, Density, and the Incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$

### The Archimedean Property and Density of $\mathbb{Q}$

Not every ordered field behaves like our geometric intuition of a number line: as you will prove in **Problem 0.4.4**, the field of rational functions $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ is an ordered field that contains "infinitely large" elements $t > n \cdot 1$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and "infinitesimally small" positive elements $1 / t$ satisfying $0 < 1 / t < 1 / n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. What prevents such infinite and infinitesimal elements from existing in $\mathbb{Q}$? It is the **Archimedean Property**, which expresses the geometric intuition that no matter how small a positive step $x > 0$ you take, and no matter how distant a target $y$ you choose, adding $x$ to itself enough times ($n x$) will eventually surpass $y$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{Q}$
> The ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ is **Archimedean**: for any two rational numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $x > 0$, there exists a positive natural number $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that
> $$
> n x > y.
> $$
> Consequently, we obtain three equivalent formulations in $\mathbb{Q}$:
> 1. **Unboundedness of $\mathbb{N}$ in $\mathbb{Q}$:** For every $y \in \mathbb{Q}$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that $n > y$.
> 2. **Arbitrarily Small Reciprocals ($1 / n < \varepsilon$):** For every $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that
>    $$
>    0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon.
>    $$
> 3. **Absence of Positive Infinitesimals:** If $z \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $0 \le z < \frac{1}{n}$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, then $z = 0$.

**Proof:**
Let $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $x > 0$. By choosing positive-denominator representatives, we may write $x = a / b$ and $y = c / d$ with $a, b, c, d \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b > 0$, $d > 0$. Since $x = a / b > 0$ and $b > 0$, we have $a > 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, which means $a \ge 1$ (since $a \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0} = \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ from Section 0.3).
- **Case 1 ($y \le 0$):** Choose $n = 1 \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. Since $x > 0 \ge y$, we immediately have $1 \cdot x = x > y$.
- **Case 2 ($y > 0$):** Here $c > 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, so $b c \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0} = \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. Let us choose the positive natural number
  $$
  n := b c + 1 \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}.
  $$
  Since $a \ge 1$ and $d \ge 1$ are positive integers, their product satisfies $a d \ge 1$. Multiplying $n = b c + 1$ by $a d \ge 1$ in the ordered integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ yields
  $$
  n a d \ge n \cdot 1 = b c + 1 > b c.
  $$
  Because $b > 0$ and $d > 0$, dividing both sides of $n a d > b c$ by $b d > 0$ in the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ preserves the strict inequality:
  $$
  n x = \frac{n a}{b} = \frac{n a d}{b d} > \frac{b c}{b d} = \frac{c}{d} = y.
  $$
This proves $n x > y$ in all cases.

For the three consequences:
1. Setting $x = 1 > 0$ in $n x > y$ gives $n = n \cdot 1 > y$.
2. Given $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, apply the main statement with $x = \varepsilon > 0$ and $y = 1$ to obtain $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that $n \varepsilon > 1$. Multiplying both sides by $n^{-1} = 1 / n > 0$ gives $\varepsilon > 1 / n > 0$.
3. If $0 \le z < 1 / n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, suppose for contradiction that $z > 0$. By Consequence 2 applied to $\varepsilon = z > 0$, there exists $n_0 \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ with $1 / n_0 < z$, contradicting $z < 1 / n_0$. Thus $z = 0$. $\blacksquare$

In addition to being Archimedean, the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ possesses a remarkable property that distinguishes it sharply from the discrete ring of integers $\mathbb{Z}$: whereas in $\mathbb{Z}$ there is no integer strictly between $k$ and $k + 1$, in $\mathbb{Q}$ between any two distinct rationals $x < y$ there is *always* another rational number (and therefore infinitely many!).

> [!theorem] Theorem: Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in Itself
> For any two rational numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $x < y$, their arithmetic midpoint
> $$
> m := \frac{x + y}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}
> $$
> satisfies
> $$
> x < \frac{x + y}{2} < y.
> $$
> Consequently, between any two distinct rational numbers $x < y$, there exist infinitely many rational numbers.

**Proof:**
Since $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $2 = 1 + 1 \neq 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, the element $m = (x + y) \cdot 2^{-1}$ belongs to $\mathbb{Q}$ by field closure. Suppose $x < y$.
1. Adding $x$ to both sides of $x < y$ via Axiom (O3) gives
   $$
   2 x = x + x < x + y.
   $$
   Multiplying both sides by $2^{-1} > 0$ preserves the strict inequality, yielding
   $$
   x < \frac{x + y}{2}.
   $$
2. Adding $y$ to both sides of $x < y$ via Axiom (O3) gives
   $$
   x + y < y + y = 2 y.
   $$
   Multiplying both sides by $2^{-1} > 0$ yields
   $$
   \frac{x + y}{2} < y.
   $$
Combining both inequalities gives $x < \frac{x + y}{2} < y$. Finally, by inductively defining $m_1 := \frac{x + y}{2}$ and $m_{k+1} := \frac{x + m_k}{2}$ for $k \ge 1$, we obtain an infinite strictly decreasing sequence of distinct rationals $y > m_1 > m_2 > m_3 > \cdots > x$ all lying strictly between $x$ and $y$. $\blacksquare$

### The Crisis of Irrationality and Rudin's Gap Theorem

At first glance, the Archimedean Property and the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ might tempt us to believe that $\mathbb{Q}$ is already sufficient to serve as the continuous number line of geometry and calculus. After all, rational numbers are packed arbitrarily closely together: between any two rationals $x < y$, no matter how tiny the distance $y - x = \varepsilon > 0$, there are infinitely many rationals!

Yet one of the great shocks of classical Greek mathematics—attributed to the school of Pythagoras—was the discovery that despite being dense, the rational number line $\mathbb{Q}$ is riddled with "gaps" or "pinholes." If we construct a unit square with side length $1$, the Pythagorean theorem dictates that the length $d$ of its diagonal must satisfy the purely algebraic equation $d^2 = 1^2 + 1^2 = 2$. Does there exist *any* rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ whose square equals $2$?

Before we state and prove the negative answer, let us recall a simple parity fact about integers from Sections 0.2–0.3: by Euclidean division by $2$, every integer $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ is either **even** ($a = 2 k$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$) or **odd** ($a = 2 k + 1$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$), and never both. Moreover, if $a$ is odd ($a = 2 k + 1$), then its square is
$$
a^2 = (2 k + 1)^2 = 4 k^2 + 4 k + 1 = 2(2 k^2 + 2 k) + 1,
$$
which is also odd! Taking the contrapositive, we obtain the fundamental parity lemma: **for any $a \in \mathbb{Z}$, if $a^2$ is even, then $a$ itself must be even.**

> [!theorem] Theorem: Absence of a Rational Square Root of $2$
> There does not exist any rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that
> $$
> q^2 = 2.
> $$

**Proof:**
Seeking a contradiction, suppose there exists $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $q^2 = 2$. Since $(-q)^2 = q^2 = 2$ and $0^2 = 0 \neq 2$, we may assume without loss of generality that $q > 0$. Writing $q = [(m, n)] = m / n$ with positive integers $m, n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, the equation $(m / n)^2 = 2$ is equivalent to the integer equation
$$
m^2 = 2 n^2.
$$
Consider the set of all positive natural denominators that can appear in such a representation of $q$:
$$
D := \{n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\} : \text{there exists } m \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\} \text{ such that } m^2 = 2 n^2\}.
$$
Under our assumption that $q^2 = 2$ has a solution $q = m / n$ with $m, n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, the subset $D \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is nonempty. By the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$ (proved in Section 0.2), $D$ possesses a unique least element $n_0 \in D$. Because $n_0 \in D$, there exists a positive integer $m_0 \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that
$$
m_0^2 = 2 n_0^2.
$$
Now let us analyze the parity of $m_0$ and $n_0$:
1. Since $m_0^2 = 2 n_0^2$ is twice the integer $n_0^2$, the integer $m_0^2$ is even. By the parity lemma noted above, $m_0$ itself must be even! Thus there exists $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that
   $$
   m_0 = 2 k.
   $$
2. Substituting $m_0 = 2 k$ into $m_0^2 = 2 n_0^2$ gives $(2 k)^2 = 4 k^2 = 2 n_0^2$. Cancelling the nonzero factor $2$ in the integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ yields
   $$
   2 k^2 = n_0^2.
   $$
3. This equation shows that $n_0^2 = 2 k^2$ is even, which by the same parity lemma forces $n_0$ itself to be even! Hence there exists $l \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that
   $$
   n_0 = 2 l.
   $$
4. Substituting $n_0 = 2 l$ into $2 k^2 = n_0^2$ gives $2 k^2 = (2 l)^2 = 4 l^2$, and cancelling $2$ once more yields
   $$
   k^2 = 2 l^2.
   $$
Look at what we have uncovered: $k$ and $l$ are positive natural numbers ($k, l \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$) satisfying $k^2 = 2 l^2$, which means $l \in D$! Yet because $n_0 = 2 l$ and $l \ge 1$, we have
$$
l < 2 l = n_0,
$$
which directly contradicts the fact that $n_0$ is the *least* element of $D$! Therefore, no such rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ can exist. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Non-Circularity Check: Expressing the "Gap at $2$" Without Assuming $\sqrt{2}$ Exists
> Notice carefully how we must talk about this phenomenon at our current stage of the foundations: **the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ and the symbol $\sqrt{2}$ do not exist yet!** We cannot say "$\sqrt{2}$ is an irrational number that lies between two sets of rationals," because our universe of numbers currently ends at $\mathbb{Q}$. Instead, we must demonstrate the incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$ *internally*, using only rational numbers and the ordered field operations of $\mathbb{Q}$.

How do we expose the "gap" in $\mathbb{Q}$ where a solution to $q^2 = 2$ ought to live, using *only* elements of $\mathbb{Q}$? Following Walter Rudin's classic presentation, we split the positive rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ into two disjoint sets $A$ and $B$: those positive rationals whose square is strictly less than $2$, and those positive rationals whose square is strictly greater than $2$. By the theorem we just proved ($q^2 \neq 2$ for all $q \in \mathbb{Q}$), *every* positive rational number falls into either $A$ or $B$. We now prove that although $A$ lies entirely to the left of $B$, **$A$ has no largest element** and **$B$ has no smallest element**!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Rudin's Gap Theorem ($A$ Has No Maximum and $B$ Has No Minimum in $\mathbb{Q}$)
> Define two subsets $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ of the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> A := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q > 0 \text{ and } q^2 < 2\}, \qquad B := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q > 0 \text{ and } q^2 > 2\}.
> $$
> Then $A$ and $B$ are nonempty, $A \cap B = \emptyset$, $A \cup B = \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, and the following three properties hold:
> 1. **Strict Separation ($A < B$):** Every element $a \in A$ is strictly less than every element $b \in B$ ($a < b$).
> 2. **$A$ Has No Largest Element (No Maximum):** For every $q \in A$, there exists $p \in A$ such that $p > q$.
> 3. **$B$ Has No Smallest Element (No Minimum):** For every $q \in B$, there exists $p \in B$ such that $p < q$.

**Proof:**
First observe that $1 \in A$ (since $1 > 0$ and $1^2 = 1 < 2$) and $2 \in B$ (since $2 > 0$ and $2^2 = 4 > 2$), so $A$ and $B$ are both nonempty. By Trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$ and the theorem that $q^2 \neq 2$ for all $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, every $q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ satisfies either $q^2 < 2$ or $q^2 > 2$, and never both; hence $A \cap B = \emptyset$ and $A \cup B = \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$.

1. *Proof of Strict Separation ($a < b$ for all $a \in A, b \in B$):* Let $a \in A$ and $b \in B$. By definition, $a > 0$, $b > 0$, and $a^2 < 2 < b^2$, so transitivity gives $a^2 < b^2$, or equivalently
   $$
   b^2 - a^2 = (b - a)(b + a) > 0.
   $$
   Since $a > 0$ and $b > 0$, their sum satisfies $b + a > 0$, so $(b + a)^{-1} > 0$. Multiplying $(b - a)(b + a) > 0$ by $(b + a)^{-1} > 0$ yields $b - a > 0$, which means $a < b$.

2. & 3. *Explicit Rational Improvement Formula for $A$ and $B$:* Let $q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ be any positive rational number (so either $q \in A$ or $q \in B$). How can we construct a new positive rational number $p \in \mathbb{Q}$ whose square $p^2$ is strictly closer to $2$ than $q^2$ is, without crossing over $2$? We associate to $q$ the explicit rational number
   $$
   p := q - \frac{q^2 - 2}{q + 2} = \frac{q(q + 2) - (q^2 - 2)}{q + 2} = \frac{2 q + 2}{q + 2}.
   $$
   First, let us verify that $p \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $p > 0$: since $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $q > 0$, we have $q + 2 > 2 > 0$ and $2 q + 2 > 2 > 0$, so $p = \frac{2 q + 2}{q + 2}$ is a well-defined quotient of two strictly positive rationals, whence $p \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $p > 0$.

   Second, let us compute the exact algebraic difference $p^2 - 2$ in $\mathbb{Q}$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   p^2 - 2 &= \left(\frac{2 q + 2}{q + 2}\right)^2 - 2 \\
           &= \frac{(2 q + 2)^2 - 2(q + 2)^2}{(q + 2)^2} \\
           &= \frac{(4 q^2 + 8 q + 4) - (2 q^2 + 8 q + 8)}{(q + 2)^2} \\
           &= \frac{2 q^2 - 4}{(q + 2)^2} \\
           &= \frac{2(q^2 - 2)}{(q + 2)^2}.
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Look at the two key formulas we now hold in our hands:
   $$
   p - q = -\frac{q^2 - 2}{q + 2} = \frac{2 - q^2}{q + 2} \qquad \text{and} \qquad p^2 - 2 = \frac{2(q^2 - 2)}{(q + 2)^2}.
   $$
   Because $q > 0$, both denominators $q + 2 > 0$ and $(q + 2)^2 > 0$ are strictly positive in $\mathbb{Q}$! We can now read off both parts (2) and (3) immediately:
   - **If $q \in A$:** By definition of $A$, we have $q^2 < 2$, so $2 - q^2 > 0$ and $q^2 - 2 < 0$.
     - From $p - q = \frac{2 - q^2}{q + 2}$ with $2 - q^2 > 0$ and $q + 2 > 0$, we obtain $p - q > 0$, i.e., **$p > q$**.
     - From $p^2 - 2 = \frac{2(q^2 - 2)}{(q + 2)^2}$ with $q^2 - 2 < 0$ and $(q + 2)^2 > 0$, we obtain $p^2 - 2 < 0$, i.e., $p^2 < 2$. Since we already showed $p > 0$, this means **$p \in A$**.
     Thus for every $q \in A$, there exists $p \in A$ with $p > q$, proving that $A$ has no largest element.
   - **If $q \in B$:** By definition of $B$, we have $q^2 > 2$, so $q^2 - 2 > 0$ and $2 - q^2 < 0$.
     - From $p - q = -\frac{q^2 - 2}{q + 2}$ with $q^2 - 2 > 0$ and $q + 2 > 0$, we obtain $p - q < 0$, i.e., **$p < q$**.
     - From $p^2 - 2 = \frac{2(q^2 - 2)}{(q + 2)^2}$ with $q^2 - 2 > 0$ and $(q + 2)^2 > 0$, we obtain $p^2 - 2 > 0$, i.e., $p^2 > 2$. Since we already showed $p > 0$, this means **$p \in B$**.
     Thus for every $q \in B$, there exists $p \in B$ with $0 < p < q$, proving that $B$ has no smallest element. $\blacksquare$

> [!summary] Why Rudin's Gap Theorem Demands the Construction of $\mathbb{R}$
> Pause and appreciate the geometric catastrophe that Rudin's Gap Theorem reveals inside $\mathbb{Q}$. The set $A = \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 < 2\}$ is non-empty and **bounded above** in $\mathbb{Q}$ (every element of $B$, such as $2$, is an upper bound for $A$). If the rational line $\mathbb{Q}$ were truly unbroken, then as the elements of $A$ march upward toward $B$, there would have to be a *least upper bound* (a smallest rational number $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying $a \le s$ for all $a \in A$).
>
> Yet no such rational number $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ can exist! Why? If $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ is an upper bound for $A$, then $s \ge 1 > 0$, so either $s \in A$ or $s \in B$:
> - $s$ **cannot belong to $A$**, because $A$ has no maximum (by Part 2, there is some $p \in A$ with $p > s$, so $s$ is not even an upper bound for $A$!).
> - $s$ **cannot be the *least* upper bound if $s \in B$**, because $B$ has no minimum (by Part 3, there is a strictly smaller $p \in B$ with $p < s$, and by Part 1 every element of $B$ is still an upper bound for $A$, so $p < s$ is a smaller upper bound for $A$!).
>
> Between $A$ and $B$, the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ is severed by an invisible pinhole. Because the foundational theorems of real analysis—the Intermediate Value Theorem, the Extreme Value Theorem, the convergence of bounded monotone sequences, and the existence of roots and integrals—all break down in an ordered field with such pinholes, our mission in **Section 0.5** is clear: we must rigorously construct the complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$ that seals every single gap in $\mathbb{Q}$.

## 0.4 Exercises

### Problem 0.4.1: Canonical Prime Subfield of Any Ordered Field

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0_F, 1_F, <)$ be an arbitrary ordered field. For each integer $n \in \mathbb{Z}$, define the integer multiple $n \cdot 1_F \in F$ inductively by $0 \cdot 1_F := 0_F$, $(k + 1) \cdot 1_F := (k \cdot 1_F) + 1_F$ for $k \in \mathbb{N}$, and $(-k) \cdot 1_F := -(k \cdot 1_F)$ for $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$.
> 1. Prove that for all $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, $(a + b) \cdot 1_F = (a \cdot 1_F) + (b \cdot 1_F)$, $(a b) \cdot 1_F = (a \cdot 1_F) \cdot (b \cdot 1_F)$, and $a <_{\mathbb{Z}} b \iff a \cdot 1_F < b \cdot 1_F$. Deduce that $b \cdot 1_F \neq 0_F$ whenever $b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$.
> 2. Define the map $\iota_F : \mathbb{Q} \to F$ by setting, for each equivalence class $[(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $(a, b) \in \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$,
>    $$
>    \iota_F([(a, b)]) := (a \cdot 1_F) \cdot (b \cdot 1_F)^{-1}.
>    $$
>    Prove that $\iota_F$ is well-defined (independent of the choice of representative $(a, b)$) and is an injective, strictly order-preserving field homomorphism from $\mathbb{Q}$ into $F$.

### Problem 0.4.2: Impossibility of Ordering the Complex Field or Finite Fields

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ be an abstract field.
> 1. Suppose $F$ contains an element $i \in F$ satisfying $i^2 = -1$ (as in the field of complex numbers $\mathbb{C}$). Prove directly from the Ordered Field Axioms (O1)–(O4) that it is impossible to define a binary relation $<$ on $F$ such that $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ is an ordered field.
> 2. Suppose $F$ is a *finite* field (that is, $F$ has finitely many elements, such as $\mathbb{Z} / p\mathbb{Z}$ for a prime $p$). Prove that $F$ cannot be equipped with any binary relation $<$ making $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ an ordered field.

### Problem 0.4.3: Irrationality of Roots via the Rational Root Criterion

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $P(x) = c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0$ be a polynomial of degree $n \ge 1$ with integer coefficients $c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $c_n \neq 0$.
> 1. Suppose $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ is a rational root of $P$ ($P(q) = 0$), and write $q = a / b$ in lowest terms, where $a \in \mathbb{Z}$, $b \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, and $\gcd(a, b) = 1$ (so by Bézout's identity / Euclid's lemma, there exist integers $u, v \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $a u + b v = 1$). Prove that
>    $$
>    a \mid c_0 \quad \text{and} \quad b \mid c_n
>    $$
>    in $\mathbb{Z}$.
> 2. Deduce that for any positive integers $m, n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, if a rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $x^n = m$, then $x$ must be an integer ($x \in \mathbb{Z}$). Use this to prove that there exists no $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying $q^3 = 6$ or $q^2 = 12$.

### Problem 0.4.4: Construction of a Non-Archimedean Ordered Field $\mathbb{Q}(t)$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\mathbb{Q}[t]$ denote the integral domain of polynomials in one indeterminate $t$ with coefficients in $\mathbb{Q}$, where a nonzero polynomial $f(t) = a_m t^m + a_{m-1} t^{m-1} + \dots + a_0$ ($a_m \neq 0$) has degree $\deg(f) = m$ and leading coefficient $\operatorname{lc}(f) := a_m \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \{0\}$. Let $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ be the field of fractions of $\mathbb{Q}[t]$, whose elements are equivalence classes of rational functions $f(t) / g(t)$ with $f, g \in \mathbb{Q}[t]$ and $g \neq 0$.
> 1. Define a subset $P \subseteq \mathbb{Q}(t)$ by declaring $f(t) / g(t) \in P$ if and only if $f \neq 0$ and $\operatorname{lc}(f) \cdot \operatorname{lc}(g) >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0$. Prove that membership in $P$ is well-defined on equivalence classes and that the relation $r_1 < r_2 \iff r_2 - r_1 \in P$ makes $(\mathbb{Q}(t), +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ an **ordered field**.
> 2. Prove that in this ordered field $\mathbb{Q}(t)$, the element $t = t / 1$ satisfies
>    $$
>    t > n \cdot 1 \quad \text{for every } n \in \mathbb{N},
>    $$
>    and consequently its reciprocal $\varepsilon := 1 / t \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$ is a strictly positive **infinitesimal** satisfying
>    $$
>    0 < \frac{1}{t} < \frac{1}{n} \quad \text{for every } n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}.
>    $$
>    Conclude that the Archimedean Property is *independent* of the Ordered Field Axioms (O1)–(O4).

### Problem 0.4.5: Density of the Dyadic Rationals in $\mathbb{Q}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> A rational number $d \in \mathbb{Q}$ is called a **dyadic rational** if it can be written in the form
> $$
> d = \frac{m}{2^k}
> $$
> for some integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and some natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Let $\mathbb{D} := \{m / 2^k : m \in \mathbb{Z}, \, k \in \mathbb{N}\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ denote the set of all dyadic rationals.
> 1. Prove that $\mathbb{D}$ is a subring of $\mathbb{Q}$ (closed under $+$, $-$, and $\cdot$, with $0, 1 \in \mathbb{D}$), but is *not* a subfield of $\mathbb{Q}$ (explicitly prove that $1 / 3 \notin \mathbb{D}$).
> 2. Using Bernoulli's Inequality ($2^k = (1 + 1)^k \ge 1 + k > k$ for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$) together with the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{Q}$ and the Euclidean division / order properties of $\mathbb{Z}$, prove that $\mathbb{D}$ is **dense in $\mathbb{Q}$**: for any two rational numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $x < y$, there exists a dyadic rational $d \in \mathbb{D}$ such that
>    $$
>    x < d < y.
>    $$

### Problem 0.4.6: Cauchy's Forward–Backward Induction for AM–GM in an Ordered Field

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Because arbitrary $n$-th roots $\sqrt[n]{x_1 \cdots x_n}$ do not generally exist in an arbitrary ordered field $F$ (for example, $F = \mathbb{Q}$), the classical Arithmetic Mean–Geometric Mean (AM–GM) inequality must be formulated in a root-free form. Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be any ordered field.
> 1. **Base Case ($n = 2$):** Prove that for any $x_1, x_2 \in F$, if $x_1 + x_2 = 2$, then $x_1 x_2 \le 1$, with equality if and only if $x_1 = x_2 = 1$.
> 2. **Forward Dyadic Induction ($m = 2^k$):** Prove by mathematical induction on $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ that for any $m = 2^k$ non-negative elements $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_m \in F$ ($x_i \ge 0$), if
>    $$
>    x_1 + x_2 + \dots + x_m = m,
>    $$
>    then their product satisfies
>    $$
>    x_1 x_2 \cdots x_m \le 1,
>    $$
>    with equality if and only if $x_1 = x_2 = \dots = x_m = 1$.
> 3. **Backward Induction and General Homogeneous Form:** Suppose the property in Part 2 holds for some integer $m \ge 2$, and let $n = m - 1$. Given non-negative elements $x_1, \dots, x_n \in F$ with $x_1 + \dots + x_n = n$, apply the hypothesis for $m$ variables to $x_1, \dots, x_n$ together with $x_m := 1$ to deduce that $x_1 x_2 \cdots x_n \le 1$ (with equality iff $x_1 = \dots = x_n = 1$). Conclude that for *every* positive integer $n \ge 1$ and any non-negative $a_1, \dots, a_n \in F$,
>    $$
>    a_1 a_2 \cdots a_n \le \left(\frac{a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n}{n}\right)^n,
>    $$
>    with equality if and only if $a_1 = a_2 = \dots = a_n$.

### Problem 0.4.7: The Cauchy–Schwarz Inequality in an Arbitrary Ordered Field

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be an arbitrary ordered field, and let $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n \in F$ and $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_n \in F$ be two $n$-tuples of elements in $F$ ($n \ge 1$).
> 1. Prove **Lagrange's Algebraic Identity** in $F$:
>    $$
>    \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k^2\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^n b_k^2\right) - \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k b_k\right)^2 = \sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} (a_i b_j - a_j b_i)^2.
>    $$
> 2. Deduce—or prove independently by analyzing the non-negative quadratic polynomial $Q(t) := \sum_{k=1}^n (a_k t + b_k)^2 \ge 0$ for $t \in F$—the root-free **Cauchy–Schwarz Inequality** in $F$:
>    $$
>    \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k b_k\right)^2 \le \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k^2\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^n b_k^2\right),
>    $$
>    and determine the exact necessary and sufficient condition on $(a_1, \dots, a_n)$ and $(b_1, \dots, b_n)$ for equality to hold.

### Problem 0.4.8: Babylonian Rational Approximation Sequence and Absence of an Infimum in $\mathbb{Q}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Define a sequence $(q_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ of positive rational numbers in $\mathbb{Q}$ recursively by setting $q_1 := 1$ and, for each $n \ge 1$,
> $$
> q_{n+1} := \frac{1}{2}\left(q_n + \frac{2}{q_n}\right).
> $$
> Working *purely within the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$* (without assuming the existence of $\mathbb{R}$ or $\sqrt{2}$), prove each of the following statements:
> 1. **Quadratic Overshoot ($q_n \in B$ for $n \ge 2$):** Establish the algebraic identity
>    $$
>    q_{n+1}^2 - 2 = \frac{(q_n^2 - 2)^2}{4 q_n^2}
>    $$
>    for all $n \ge 1$, and deduce that $q_n > 0$ and $q_n^2 > 2$ for every integer $n \ge 2$.
> 2. **Strict Monotonicity and Quadratic Convergence Rate:** Prove that for every $n \ge 2$,
>    $$
>    q_{n+1} < q_n \quad \text{and} \quad 0 < q_{n+1}^2 - 2 < \frac{(q_n^2 - 2)^2}{4}.
>    $$
>    Deduce by induction that $0 < q_n^2 - 2 \le 4^{-(2^{n-2})}$ for all $n \ge 2$.
> 3. **Absence of a Greatest Lower Bound in $\mathbb{Q}$:** Consider the set $S := \{q_n : n \ge 2\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$. Prove that $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ is a lower bound for $S$ ($q \le q_n$ for all $n \ge 2$) if and only if $q \le 0$ or $q \in A = \{r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : r^2 < 2\}$, and conclude using Rudin's Gap Theorem that $S$ possesses **no greatest lower bound (infimum)** in $\mathbb{Q}$.

---

# 0.5 Construction and Completeness of the Real Numbers ($\mathbb{R}$)

In Section 0.4, we discovered a profound structural flaw in the ordered field of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$: although $\mathbb{Q}$ is algebraically closed under all four arithmetic operations and its elements are packed densely along the number line, it is riddled with microscopic "punctures" or gaps. Specifically, Rudin's Gap Theorem demonstrated that the set $A = \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 < 2\}$ has no largest element, while its upper complement $B = \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 > 2\}$ has no smallest element. Between $A$ and $B$ sits a geometric location where $\sqrt{2}$ ought to live, yet no rational number resides there.

To build a rigorous foundation for calculus and real analysis—where limits of sequences, intermediate values of continuous functions, and areas under curves must actually exist as numbers—we must first formulate an exact order-theoretic language to describe what it means for an ordered field to have "no gaps." Once we isolate this **Least Upper Bound Property** (or **Dedekind Completeness**), we will construct the real number system $\mathbb{R}$ directly from $\mathbb{Q}$ using two classic, complementary pathways:
1. **Method I (Richard Dedekind's Geometric/Order-Theoretic Construction, 1872):** We fill the gaps in $\mathbb{Q}$ by identifying each real number directly with the downward-closed slice (or *cut*) of all rational numbers lying strictly to its left.
2. **Method II (Georg Cantor's Analytical Construction via Rational Cauchy Sequences, 1872):** We fill the gaps dynamically by adjoining formal limits $\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n$ of sequences of rational numbers whose terms cluster arbitrarily close together.

Finally, we will prove the **Categoricity Theorem for Complete Ordered Fields**: any two complete ordered fields are canonically and uniquely order-isomorphic. Thus, Dedekind's cuts and Cantor's Cauchy sequences produce the exact same mathematical structure up to unique isomorphism, allowing us henceforth to work simply with **the** complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$.

## Order Completeness: Upper Bounds, Supremum, Infimum, and the Least Upper Bound Property

Before constructing $\mathbb{R}$, let us examine how subsets behave inside an arbitrary strict linearly ordered set $(X, <)$, and more specifically inside an **Ordered Field** $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ as axiomatized in Section 0.4.

### Upper Bounds, Lower Bounds, Maximum, and Minimum

Suppose we are given a subset $E \subseteq X$ of an ordered set $(X, <)$. How do we formalize the idea that $E$ does not stretch infinitely far to the right or to the left?

> [!abstract] Definition: Upper Bound, Lower Bound, and Bounded Sets
> Let $(X, <)$ be an ordered set (where $x \le y$ denotes $x < y$ or $x = y$), and let $E \subseteq X$ be a subset.
> 1. **Bounded Above and Upper Bound:** We say that $E$ is **bounded above** in $X$ if there exists an element $b \in X$ such that
>    $$
>    \forall x \in E, \quad x \le b
>    $$
>    Any such element $b \in X$ is called an **upper bound** of $E$ in $X$.
> 2. **Bounded Below and Lower Bound:** We say that $E$ is **bounded below** in $X$ if there exists an element $a \in X$ such that
>    $$
>    \forall x \in E, \quad a \le x
>    $$
>    Any such element $a \in X$ is called a **lower bound** of $E$ in $X$.
> 3. **Bounded Set:** We say that $E$ is **bounded** in $X$ if $E$ is both bounded above and bounded below in $X$—that is, if there exist $a, b \in X$ such that $E \subseteq [a, b] := \{x \in X : a \le x \le b\}$.

Notice carefully that an upper bound $b$ or a lower bound $a$ of $E$ is **not** required to belong to the set $E$ itself; it merely sits in the ambient ordered set $X$ and guards the boundary of $E$ from the outside. In the special case where an upper bound or lower bound actually belongs to $E$, we obtain a maximum or minimum element.

> [!abstract] Definition: Maximum ($\max E$) and Minimum ($\min E$)
> Let $(X, <)$ be an ordered set and let $E \subseteq X$.
> 1. An element $m \in X$ is called the **maximum** (or **largest element**) of $E$, denoted $m = \max E$, if
>    $$
>    m \in E \quad \text{and} \quad \forall x \in E, \; x \le m
>    $$
> 2. An element $l \in X$ is called the **minimum** (or **least element**) of $E$, denoted $l = \min E$, if
>    $$
>    l \in E \quad \text{and} \quad \forall x \in E, \; l \le x
>    $$

By the antisymmetry of $\le$ (which follows directly from trichotomy and irreflexivity of $<$), if $\max E$ exists, it is unique: if $m_1, m_2 \in E$ both satisfy $\forall x \in E, x \le m_i$, then $m_1 \le m_2$ (since $m_1 \in E$) and $m_2 \le m_1$ (since $m_2 \in E$), forcing $m_1 = m_2$. The exact same argument guarantees uniqueness of $\min E$ whenever it exists.

However, even very simple bounded sets in an ordered field frequently fail to possess a maximum or a minimum. For instance, in $\mathbb{Q}$, the open rational interval $E = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 < q < 1\}$ is bounded below by $0$ and bounded above by $1$, yet it has neither a minimum nor a maximum: for any $q \in E$, the midpoint $\frac{q}{2} \in E$ is strictly smaller than $q$, and $\frac{q + 1}{2} \in E$ is strictly larger than $q$.

### Supremum ($\sup E$) and Infimum ($\inf E$)

When a bounded set $E$ lacks a maximum element because its right-hand boundary is "open" (like $\{q \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 < q < 1\}$), we can still ask for the **tightest possible upper bound** in the ambient space $X$—an upper bound $s \in X$ that sits flush against the right edge of $E$ with no room to spare between $E$ and $s$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Supremum (Least Upper Bound) and Infimum (Greatest Lower Bound)
> Let $(X, <)$ be an ordered set and let $E \subseteq X$.
> 1. **Supremum ($\sup E$):** Suppose $E$ is bounded above in $X$. An element $s \in X$ is called the **supremum** (or **least upper bound**) of $E$ in $X$, denoted $s = \sup E$ (or $\sup_X E$, $\text{lub } E$), if $s$ satisfies two conditions:
>    - **(S1) $s$ is an upper bound of $E$:** For every $x \in E$, we have $x \le s$.
>    - **(S2) $s$ is the least among all upper bounds of $E$:** If $b \in X$ is any upper bound of $E$ (meaning $\forall x \in E, x \le b$), then $s \le b$. Equivalently, by contraposition and trichotomy, no element strictly smaller than $s$ is an upper bound of $E$:
>      $$
>      \forall y \in X, \quad y < s \implies \exists x \in E \text{ such that } y < x \le s
>      $$
> 2. **Infimum ($\inf E$):** Suppose $E$ is bounded below in $X$. An element $i \in X$ is called the **infimum** (or **greatest lower bound**) of $E$ in $X$, denoted $i = \inf E$ (or $\inf_X E$, $\text{glb } E$), if $i$ satisfies two dual conditions:
>    - **(I1) $i$ is a lower bound of $E$:** For every $x \in E$, we have $i \le x$.
>    - **(I2) $i$ is the greatest among all lower bounds of $E$:** If $a \in X$ is any lower bound of $E$ (meaning $\forall x \in E, a \le x$), then $a \le i$. Equivalently, no element strictly larger than $i$ is a lower bound of $E$:
>      $$
>      \forall y \in X, \quad y > i \implies \exists x \in E \text{ such that } i \le x < y
>      $$

Let us immediately verify that a set can have at most one supremum and at most one infimum, and clarify the exact relationship between $\sup E$ and $\max E$.

> [!theorem] Proposition: Uniqueness of Supremum and Infimum
> Let $(X, <)$ be an ordered set and let $E \subseteq X$.
> 1. If $\sup E$ exists in $X$, it is unique. Similarly, if $\inf E$ exists in $X$, it is unique.
> 2. The maximum $\max E$ exists if and only if $\sup E$ exists and $\sup E \in E$, in which case $\max E = \sup E$. Dually, $\min E$ exists if and only if $\inf E$ exists and $\inf E \in E$, in which case $\min E = \inf E$.

**Proof:**
1. Suppose $s_1, s_2 \in X$ are both suprema of $E$. By condition (S1), both $s_1$ and $s_2$ are upper bounds of $E$. Since $s_1$ is a least upper bound (condition S2) and $s_2$ is an upper bound of $E$, we have $s_1 \le s_2$. Reversing their roles, since $s_2$ is a least upper bound and $s_1$ is an upper bound of $E$, we also have $s_2 \le s_1$. By trichotomy on $(X, <)$, $s_1 < s_2$ and $s_2 < s_1$ cannot hold simultaneously, so $s_1 = s_2$. The uniqueness of $\inf E$ follows by the exact same argument: if $i_1, i_2$ are infima of $E$, then $i_1 \le i_2$ and $i_2 \le i_1$, whence $i_1 = i_2$.
2. Suppose $m = \max E$ exists. Then $m \in E$ and $\forall x \in E, x \le m$, so $m$ satisfies (S1). Moreover, if $b \in X$ is any upper bound of $E$, then because $m$ itself is an element of $E$, the condition $\forall x \in E, x \le b$ applied to $x = m$ yields $m \le b$, establishing (S2). Thus $\sup E$ exists and equals $m \in E$. Conversely, if $s = \sup E$ exists and happens to satisfy $s \in E$, then $s \in E$ and $\forall x \in E, x \le s$ (by S1), which is precisely the definition of $s = \max E$. The dual equivalence for $\min E$ and $\inf E$ is proved identically. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Conceptual Distinction Between $\max E$ and $\sup E$
> If we denote by $U(E) := \{b \in X : \forall x \in E, x \le b\}$ the set of all upper bounds of $E$ in $X$, then the definition of supremum says simply that
> $$
> s = \sup E \iff s = \min U(E)
> $$
> That is, $\sup E$ is the *minimum of the set of upper bounds* of $E$. Whether $s$ belongs to $E$ itself depends on whether $E$ contains its right endpoint: for instance, in $\mathbb{Q}$, both $E_1 = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le q < 1\}$ and $E_2 = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le q \le 1\}$ have the exact same set of upper bounds $U(E_1) = U(E_2) = \{b \in \mathbb{Q} : b \ge 1\}$, so $\sup_{\mathbb{Q}} E_1 = \sup_{\mathbb{Q}} E_2 = 1$. However, $1 \in E_2$ so $\max E_2 = 1$, whereas $1 \notin E_1$ so $\max E_1$ does not exist.

### The $\varepsilon$-Characterization of Supremum and Infimum in an Ordered Field

When our ambient ordered set is an **Ordered Field** $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$, we can express the condition "nothing strictly smaller than $s$ is an upper bound of $E$" algebraically by stepping leftward from $s$ by an arbitrarily small positive amount $\varepsilon \in F_{>0}$. This yields the most frequently used working tool in all of real analysis.

> [!theorem] Theorem: $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum and Infimum in an Ordered Field
> Let $F$ be an Ordered Field and let $E \subseteq F$ be a non-empty subset.
> 1. Suppose $E$ is bounded above in $F$, and let $s \in F$ be an upper bound of $E$ (so $x \le s$ for all $x \in E$). Then $s = \sup E$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists an element $x \in E$ such that
>    $$
>    s - \varepsilon < x \le s
>    $$
> 2. Dually, suppose $E$ is bounded below in $F$, and let $i \in F$ be a lower bound of $E$ (so $i \le x$ for all $x \in E$). Then $i = \inf E$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists an element $x \in E$ such that
>    $$
>    i \le x < i + \varepsilon
>    $$

**Proof:**
We prove part (1); part (2) follows by an identical argument with inequalities reversed. Since $s \in F$ is already assumed to be an upper bound of $E$, condition (S1) holds automatically, and we only need to show that condition (S2) is equivalent to the $\varepsilon$-condition.

$(\implies)$ Suppose $s = \sup E$, and let $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$ be given. By compatibility of $<$ with addition in an ordered field, adding $s - \varepsilon$ to $0 < \varepsilon$ yields $s - \varepsilon < s$. Because $s$ is the *least* upper bound of $E$, the strictly smaller element $y := s - \varepsilon < s$ cannot be an upper bound of $E$. The negation of "$s - \varepsilon$ is an upper bound of $E$" ($\forall x \in E, x \le s - \varepsilon$) is precisely that there exists at least one element $x \in E$ such that $x > s - \varepsilon$ (by trichotomy). Since $s$ is an upper bound of $E$, this $x$ satisfies $s - \varepsilon < x \le s$, as required.

$(\Longleftarrow)$ Conversely, suppose that for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $x \in E$ with $x > s - \varepsilon$. To prove condition (S2), let $b \in F$ be any upper bound of $E$; we must show $s \le b$. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $s \not\le b$, so by trichotomy $b < s$. Then the difference $\varepsilon := s - b \in F$ satisfies $\varepsilon > 0$. By our hypothesis applied to this $\varepsilon = s - b > 0$, there exists an element $x \in E$ such that
$$
x > s - \varepsilon = s - (s - b) = b
$$
Thus $x \in E$ satisfies $x > b$, which directly contradicts the assumption that $b$ is an upper bound of $E$! By contradiction, $b < s$ is impossible, so $s \le b$. Hence $s = \sup E$. $\blacksquare$

### The Least Upper Bound Property and Its Equivalence with the Greatest Lower Bound Property

In an arbitrary ordered set or ordered field, a non-empty subset $E$ that is bounded above is *not* guaranteed to have a supremum. When every such subset does have a supremum, the ordered structure is free of gaps.

> [!abstract] Definition: Least Upper Bound Property (Dedekind Completeness) and Greatest Lower Bound Property
> Let $(X, <)$ be an ordered set.
> 1. We say that $(X, <)$ has the **Least Upper Bound Property** (or is **Dedekind complete** / **order-complete**) if every non-empty subset $E \subseteq X$ that is bounded above in $X$ possesses a supremum $\sup E \in X$.
> 2. We say that $(X, <)$ has the **Greatest Lower Bound Property** if every non-empty subset $E \subseteq X$ that is bounded below in $X$ possesses an infimum $\inf E \in X$.
> 3. An Ordered Field $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ is called a **Complete Ordered Field** if it satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property.

> [!warning] Why Must $E$ Be Both Non-Empty and Bounded Above?
> Neither hypothesis in the Least Upper Bound Property can be omitted in an ordered field $F$:
> - If $E = \emptyset$ is the empty set, then *every* element $b \in F$ vacuously satisfies $\forall x \in \emptyset, x \le b$. Thus the set of upper bounds of $\emptyset$ is $U(\emptyset) = F$, which has no least element in an ordered field (since for any $b \in F$, $b - 1 < b$).
> - If $E \subseteq F$ is not bounded above, then its set of upper bounds is $U(E) = \emptyset$, so no upper bound—let alone a least upper bound—can exist in $F$.

At first glance, one might wonder whether a Complete Ordered Field requires a separate axiom to guarantee the existence of infima for sets bounded below. Happily, in any ordered field (or even any ordered set), the Least Upper Bound Property and the Greatest Lower Bound Property are completely equivalent! In an ordered field, we also get a clean reflection formula relating $\inf$ and $\sup$ via additive negation.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Equivalence of the LUB and GLB Properties in an Ordered Field
> Let $F$ be an Ordered Field. Then $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property if and only if $F$ satisfies the Greatest Lower Bound Property. Specifically, if $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property and $E \subseteq F$ is any non-empty subset that is bounded below in $F$, then the reflected set
> $$
> -E := \{-x : x \in E\} \subseteq F
> $$
> is non-empty and bounded above in $F$, and the infimum of $E$ exists in $F$ and is given explicitly by
> $$
> \inf E = -\sup(-E)
> $$

**Proof:**
Suppose $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property, and let $E \subseteq F$ be a non-empty subset that is bounded below in $F$.
1. **Non-emptiness and upper boundedness of $-E$:** Since $E \neq \emptyset$, there is some $x_0 \in E$, so $-x_0 \in -E$ and thus $-E \neq \emptyset$. Since $E$ is bounded below, there exists a lower bound $a \in F$ such that $a \le x$ for all $x \in E$. In an ordered field, multiplying or negating both sides of an inequality reverses its direction ($a \le x \iff -x \le -a$). Therefore, for every element $y = -x \in -E$ (with $x \in E$), we have $y = -x \le -a$. This shows that $-a \in F$ is an upper bound for $-E$, so $-E$ is non-empty and bounded above in $F$.
2. **Existence of $\sup(-E)$ and verification that $-\sup(-E) = \inf E$:** By the Least Upper Bound Property of $F$, the set $-E$ has a supremum $s := \sup(-E) \in F$. We claim that $i := -s = -\sup(-E) \in F$ is the infimum of $E$:
   - **(I1) $i = -s$ is a lower bound of $E$:** Let $x \in E$ be arbitrary. Then $-x \in -E$. Since $s = \sup(-E)$ is an upper bound of $-E$, we have $-x \le s$. Negating both sides in $F$ reverses the inequality and gives $-s \le -(-x) = x$, so $i \le x$ for all $x \in E$.
   - **(I2) $i = -s$ is the greatest lower bound of $E$:** Let $a \in F$ be any lower bound of $E$. As shown in Step 1, $\forall x \in E, a \le x \implies \forall x \in E, -x \le -a$, which means that $-a$ is an upper bound of $-E$. Because $s = \sup(-E)$ is the *least* upper bound of $-E$, we must have $s \le -a$. Negating both sides reverses the inequality once more, yielding $a = -(-a) \le -s = i$.
   Thus $i = -s = -\sup(-E)$ satisfies both (I1) and (I2), proving that $\inf E$ exists in $F$ and equals $-\sup(-E)$.

The converse implication (that the Greatest Lower Bound Property implies the Least Upper Bound Property via $\sup E = -\inf(-E)$) follows by the exact same reflection argument. $\blacksquare$

### Failure of the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{Q}$

We can now recast Rudin's Gap Theorem from Section 0.4 into the language of order completeness and prove rigorously that the rational field $\mathbb{Q}$ is *not* Dedekind complete.

> [!theorem] Theorem: $\mathbb{Q}$ Does Not Satisfy the Least Upper Bound Property
> In the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$, consider the subset
> $$
> A := \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 < 2\}
> $$
> Then $A$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$, yet $\sup_{\mathbb{Q}} A$ does not exist in $\mathbb{Q}$. Consequently, $\mathbb{Q}$ does not satisfy the Least Upper Bound Property.

**Proof:**
First, $1 \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $1^2 = 1 < 2$, so $1 \in A$ and $A$ is non-empty. Second, $2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ is an upper bound for $A$: if $q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ satisfies $q \ge 2$, then by positivity and order compatibility of multiplication in $\mathbb{Q}$, $q^2 \ge 2q \ge 4 > 2$, so $q \notin A$; by contraposition and trichotomy, every $q \in A$ satisfies $q < 2$. Thus $A$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $s = \sup_{\mathbb{Q}} A$ exists in $\mathbb{Q}$. Since $1 \in A$ and $s$ is an upper bound of $A$, we have $s \ge 1 > 0$, so $s \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$. Recall from Section 0.4 the complementary upper set $B := \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 > 2\}$ and the three properties proved in Rudin's Gap Theorem:
1. No rational $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $s^2 = 2$; hence by trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$, either $s^2 < 2$ (so $s \in A$) or $s^2 > 2$ (so $s \in B$).
2. $A$ has no largest element: for every $p \in A$, there exists $r \in A$ with $p < r$.
3. $B$ has no smallest element: for every $p \in B$, there exists $r \in B$ with $r < p$.

We now rule out both possibilities for $s \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$:
- **Case 1 ($s \in A$, i.e., $s^2 < 2$):** Because $A$ has no largest element, there exists $r \in A$ such that $s < r$. Wait—if $r \in A$ and $s < r$, then $s$ fails condition (S1) to be an upper bound of $A$! Contradiction.
- **Case 2 ($s \in B$, i.e., $s^2 > 2$):** Because $B$ has no smallest element, there exists $r \in B$ such that $0 < r < s$. We claim that this strictly smaller element $r$ is *also* an upper bound of $A$: indeed, for any $q \in A$, we have $q^2 < 2 < r^2$, so $r^2 - q^2 = (r - q)(r + q) > 0$. Since $r > 0$ and $q > 0$, we have $r + q > 0$, and multiplying by $(r + q)^{-1} > 0$ yields $r - q > 0$, i.e., $q < r$. Thus $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ is an upper bound of $A$ satisfying $r < s$, which contradicts condition (S2) that $s$ is the *least* upper bound of $A$!

In both cases we reach a contradiction. Therefore, no rational number $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ can be the supremum of $A$. $\blacksquare$

## Method I: Construction of $\mathbb{R}$ via Dedekind Cuts

How can we manufacture a new number system from $\mathbb{Q}$ in which sets like $A = \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 < 2\}$ actually possess a least upper bound? In 1872, Richard Dedekind made a brilliant observation: even though the "missing point" $\sqrt{2}$ does not exist inside $\mathbb{Q}$, its *footprint* inside $\mathbb{Q}$—namely, the entire downward-closed collection of rational numbers $\{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q \le 0 \text{ or } (q > 0 \text{ and } q^2 < 2)\}$ lying strictly to its left—already exists as a well-defined subset of $\mathbb{Q}$ in Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory (specifically, as an element of the power set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q})$)!

If we represent *every* point on the continuous line (both the old rational points and the new irrational gap-points) by the open left ray of all rational numbers strictly smaller than that point, then every gap in $\mathbb{Q}$ is automatically filled by the subset of $\mathbb{Q}$ that carves it out.

### Definition of a Dedekind Cut and Rational Cuts

Let us distill the exact three properties that characterize an "open left ray" of rational numbers.

> [!abstract] Definition: Dedekind Cut and the Set $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$
> A subset $\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ (i.e., an element $\alpha \in \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q})$) is called a **Dedekind cut** (or simply a **cut**) if it satisfies the following three axioms:
> - **(C1) Non-Triviality:** $\alpha \neq \emptyset$ and $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$ (so there exists at least one $p \in \alpha$ and at least one $q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$).
> - **(C2) Downward Closure (Left-Ray Property):** If $p \in \alpha$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $q < p$, then $q \in \alpha$.
> - **(C3) No Largest Element (Open Right Endpoint):** If $p \in \alpha$, then there exists some $r \in \alpha$ such that $p < r$.
>
> We define the set of **Dedekind real numbers**, denoted $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, to be the collection of all Dedekind cuts:
> $$
> \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}} := \{\alpha \in \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q}) : \alpha \text{ satisfies (C1), (C2), and (C3)}\}
> $$

Why do we impose each of these three axioms?
- **Axiom (C1)** prevents $\alpha = \emptyset$ (which would correspond to "$-\infty$") and $\alpha = \mathbb{Q}$ (which would correspond to "$+\infty$"); a real number must slice $\mathbb{Q}$ into a non-empty lower piece $\alpha$ and a non-empty upper piece $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$.
- **Axiom (C2)** guarantees that $\alpha$ has no internal holes: once $p \in \alpha$, every rational number to the left of $p$ is also in $\alpha$. Notice an immediate, indispensable consequence of (C2): **every element inside $\alpha$ is strictly smaller than every rational outside $\alpha$**. Indeed, if $p \in \alpha$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$, we cannot have $s \le p$, because $s = p$ would mean $s \in \alpha$, and $s < p$ would force $s \in \alpha$ by (C2). By trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$, $p \in \alpha$ and $s \notin \alpha$ always implies $p < s$.
- **Axiom (C3)** resolves a potential ambiguity at rational numbers: without (C3), a rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ could be represented either by the open left ray $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q\}$ or by the closed left ray $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le q\}$. By forbidding a cut from containing a maximum element, we force every cut to be open on the right, making the representation of every rational number unique (and, as you will prove in Problem 0.5.8, making addition of cuts a group!).

Let us verify that every rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ canonically defines a Dedekind cut.

> [!abstract] Definition: Rational Cuts ($q^*$, $0^*$, and $1^*$)
> For each rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, we define the **rational cut** $q^* \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> q^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q\}
> $$
> In particular, the additive identity cut and multiplicative identity cut are defined as
> $$
> 0^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 0\} \quad \text{and} \quad 1^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 1\}
> $$

Let us quickly check that $q^*$ is indeed a Dedekind cut for every $q \in \mathbb{Q}$:
- **(C1):** Since $q - 1 < q$, we have $q - 1 \in q^*$ so $q^* \neq \emptyset$. Since $q \not< q$, we have $q \notin q^*$ so $q^* \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
- **(C2):** If $p \in q^*$ (so $p < q$) and $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $r < p$, then by transitivity in $\mathbb{Q}$, $r < q$, so $r \in q^*$.
- **(C3):** If $p \in q^*$ (so $p < q$), then the average $r := \frac{p + q}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $p < \frac{p + q}{2} < q$, which means $r \in q^*$ and $p < r$.

### Order on Cuts and the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$

How should we compare the sizes of two Dedekind cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$? Geometrically, if the point represented by $\beta$ lies strictly to the right of the point represented by $\alpha$, then the open left ray $\beta$ contains every rational in $\alpha$ together with additional rationals sitting between $\alpha$ and $\beta$. Thus, the order relation $<$ on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is nothing other than **proper set inclusion** $\subsetneq$!

> [!abstract] Definition: Strict Linear Order on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$
> For any two Dedekind cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, we write $\alpha < \beta$ (and equivalently $\beta > \alpha$) if $\alpha$ is a proper subset of $\beta$:
> $$
> \alpha < \beta \iff \alpha \subsetneq \beta \iff (\alpha \subseteq \beta \text{ and } \alpha \neq \beta)
> $$
> As usual, we write $\alpha \le \beta$ to mean $\alpha < \beta$ or $\alpha = \beta$, which is simply set inclusion: $\alpha \le \beta \iff \alpha \subseteq \beta$.

Notice how astounding it is that set inclusion $\subsetneq$—which is normally only a *partial* order on $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q})$—becomes a **strict total order** when restricted to Dedekind cuts! Let us prove this rigorously.

> [!theorem] Proposition: $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, <)$ is a Strictly Linearly Ordered Set
> The relation $\alpha < \beta \iff \alpha \subsetneq \beta$ defines a strict linear order on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, satisfying both **Transitivity** and **Trichotomy**:
> 1. **Transitivity:** If $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ satisfy $\alpha < \beta$ and $\beta < \gamma$, then $\alpha < \gamma$.
> 2. **Trichotomy:** For any $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, exactly one of $\alpha < \beta$, $\alpha = \beta$, or $\beta < \alpha$ holds.

**Proof:**
1. **Transitivity:** Suppose $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$ and $\beta \subsetneq \gamma$. Then trivially $\alpha \subseteq \beta \subseteq \gamma$, so $\alpha \subseteq \gamma$. Since $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$, there exists an element $p \in \beta \setminus \alpha$. Because $\beta \subseteq \gamma$, we have $p \in \gamma$, so $p \in \gamma \setminus \alpha$. Thus $\alpha \neq \gamma$, and hence $\alpha \subsetneq \gamma$, i.e., $\alpha < \gamma$.
2. **Trichotomy:** First, at most one of $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$, $\alpha = \beta$, and $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$ can hold, because $\alpha = \beta$ explicitly contradicts $\alpha \neq \beta$ in $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$ and $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$, while $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$ and $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$ together would imply $\alpha \subseteq \beta$ and $\beta \subseteq \alpha$, forcing $\alpha = \beta$ by the Axiom of Extensionality, a contradiction.

   It remains to prove that at least one of the three cases must hold. Suppose that $\alpha < \beta$ and $\alpha = \beta$ both fail. That means $\alpha \not\subseteq \beta$. By the definition of subset, there exists a rational number $p \in \alpha$ such that $p \notin \beta$. We claim that $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$:
   - Let $q \in \beta$ be arbitrary. Because $q \in \beta$ and $p \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \beta$, downward closure (C2) applied to the cut $\beta$ guarantees that $q < p$.
   - Now, since $p \in \alpha$ and $q < p$, downward closure (C2) applied to the cut $\alpha$ guarantees that $q \in \alpha$!
   Since $q \in \beta$ was arbitrary, we have proved $\beta \subseteq \alpha$. Furthermore, since $p \in \alpha \setminus \beta$, we have $\beta \neq \alpha$. Therefore $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$, which means $\beta < \alpha$. $\blacksquare$

Before even defining arithmetic on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, we can already prove the crowning achievement of Dedekind's construction: because $\alpha \le \beta$ is simply $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, the least upper bound of any bounded non-empty family of cuts is literally their **set-theoretic union**!

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$
> The ordered set $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, <)$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property. Specifically, if $\mathcal{F} \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is any non-empty collection of Dedekind cuts that is bounded above by some cut $\beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, then the union
> $$
> \gamma := \bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : \exists \alpha \in \mathcal{F} \text{ such that } p \in \alpha\}
> $$
> is a Dedekind cut ($\gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$), and $\gamma = \sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}} \mathcal{F}$.

**Proof:**
Let $\mathcal{F} \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ be non-empty, and let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ be an upper bound for $\mathcal{F}$ (meaning $\alpha \le \beta$, i.e., $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, for every $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}$). We first verify that $\gamma := \bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies the three Dedekind cut axioms (C1), (C2), and (C3):
- **(C1) Non-Triviality:** Since $\mathcal{F}$ is non-empty, we can pick at least one cut $\alpha_0 \in \mathcal{F}$. By (C1) for $\alpha_0$, $\alpha_0 \neq \emptyset$, so $\emptyset \neq \alpha_0 \subseteq \gamma$, which proves $\gamma \neq \emptyset$. Next, because $\alpha \subseteq \beta$ for every $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}$, we have $\gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha \subseteq \beta$. By (C1) for the cut $\beta$, $\beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$, so there exists some rational $q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \beta$. Since $\gamma \subseteq \beta$, we also have $q \notin \gamma$, which proves $\gamma \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
- **(C2) Downward Closure:** Suppose $p \in \gamma$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $q < p$. By definition of $\gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha$, there exists some cut $\alpha_1 \in \mathcal{F}$ such that $p \in \alpha_1$. Because $\alpha_1$ is a Dedekind cut and $q < p \in \alpha_1$, downward closure (C2) for $\alpha_1$ implies $q \in \alpha_1$. Consequently, $q \in \bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha = \gamma$.
- **(C3) No Largest Element:** Suppose $p \in \gamma$. Again, by definition of the union, there exists some cut $\alpha_1 \in \mathcal{F}$ such that $p \in \alpha_1$. By axiom (C3) for the cut $\alpha_1$, there exists a rational $r \in \alpha_1$ such that $p < r$. Since $\alpha_1 \subseteq \gamma$, this same $r$ belongs to $\gamma$ and satisfies $p < r$.

Thus $\gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a valid Dedekind cut! It remains to verify the two supremum conditions (S1) and (S2):
- **(S1) $\gamma$ is an upper bound of $\mathcal{F}$:** For every $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}$, basic set theory gives $\alpha \subseteq \bigcup_{\alpha' \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha' = \gamma$, which by definition of order on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ means $\alpha \le \gamma$.
- **(S2) $\gamma$ is the least upper bound of $\mathcal{F}$:** Let $\delta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ be *any* upper bound of $\mathcal{F}$. Then $\alpha \le \delta$, i.e., $\alpha \subseteq \delta$, for every $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}$. By the universal property of set union, if every member $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}$ is a subset of $\delta$, then their union is also a subset of $\delta$: $\gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha \subseteq \delta$. In the order of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, this says precisely that $\gamma \le \delta$.

Therefore, $\gamma = \sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}} \mathcal{F}$, and $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property. $\blacksquare$

### Addition, the Straddling Lemma, and Additive Inverses in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$

Next, we turn $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ into an ordered abelian group under addition. If $\alpha$ represents all rationals strictly less than $x$ and $\beta$ represents all rationals strictly less than $y$, then the rationals strictly less than $x + y$ are simply all pairwise sums $r + s$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Addition of Dedekind Cuts
> For any two Dedekind cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, we define their **sum** $\alpha + \beta \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ to be the set of all pairwise rational sums:
> $$
> \alpha + \beta := \{r + s : r \in \alpha \text{ and } s \in \beta\}
> $$

> [!theorem] Proposition: Well-Definedness and Group Axioms (Except Inverse) for Cut Addition
> For any $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$:
> 1. $\alpha + \beta$ is a Dedekind cut ($\alpha + \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$).
> 2. Addition is commutative ($\alpha + \beta = \beta + \alpha$) and associative ($(\alpha + \beta) + \gamma = \alpha + (\beta + \gamma)$).
> 3. The rational cut $0^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 0\}$ is the additive identity: $\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$.
> 4. Addition is compatible with the order $<$: if $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and $\alpha < \beta$, then $\alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$ (Wait: to prove strict inequality $\alpha + \gamma \subsetneq \beta + \gamma$ in full generality, we will use additive inverses once established; right now $\alpha \subseteq \beta \implies \alpha + \gamma \subseteq \beta + \gamma$ is immediate).

**Proof:**
1. **Verification of (C1), (C2), (C3) for $\alpha + \beta$:**
   - **(C1):** Since $\alpha \neq \emptyset$ and $\beta \neq \emptyset$, picking $r_0 \in \alpha$ and $s_0 \in \beta$ gives $r_0 + s_0 \in \alpha + \beta$, so $\alpha + \beta \neq \emptyset$. Next, since $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$ and $\beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$, pick $u \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ and $v \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \beta$. By (C2), every $r \in \alpha$ satisfies $r < u$ and every $s \in \beta$ satisfies $s < v$. Adding these inequalities in $\mathbb{Q}$ shows that $r + s < u + v$ for all $r \in \alpha, s \in \beta$. Thus $u + v \notin \alpha + \beta$, proving $\alpha + \beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
   - **(C2):** Suppose $p \in \alpha + \beta$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q < p$. Write $p = r + s$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$. Then $q < r + s \iff q - s < r$. Since $r \in \alpha$ and $q - s \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $q - s < r$, downward closure (C2) for $\alpha$ gives $r' := q - s \in \alpha$. Therefore, $q = (q - s) + s = r' + s$ with $r' \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$, so $q \in \alpha + \beta$.
   - **(C3):** Suppose $p = r + s \in \alpha + \beta$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$. By (C3) for $\alpha$, there exists $r' \in \alpha$ with $r < r'$. Then $p = r + s < r' + s \in \alpha + \beta$, so $\alpha + \beta$ has no largest element.
2. **Commutativity and Associativity:** These follow directly from the commutativity ($r + s = s + r$) and associativity ($(r + s) + t = r + (s + t)$) of addition in $\mathbb{Q}$ together with the Axiom of Extensionality:
   $$
   \alpha + \beta = \{r + s : r \in \alpha, s \in \beta\} = \{s + r : s \in \beta, r \in \alpha\} = \beta + \alpha
   $$
   and similarly both $(\alpha + \beta) + \gamma$ and $\alpha + (\beta + \gamma)$ equal $\{r + s + t : r \in \alpha, s \in \beta, t \in \gamma\}$.
3. **Additive Identity ($\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$):** We prove both inclusions $\alpha + 0^* \subseteq \alpha$ and $\alpha \subseteq \alpha + 0^*$:
   - ($\alpha + 0^* \subseteq \alpha$): Let $p = r + s \in \alpha + 0^*$, where $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in 0^*$ (so $s < 0$). Because $s < 0$, we have $p = r + s < r + 0 = r \in \alpha$. By downward closure (C2) of $\alpha$, $p < r \in \alpha$ implies $p \in \alpha$.
   - ($\alpha \subseteq \alpha + 0^*$): Let $r \in \alpha$. By (C3) for $\alpha$, there exists a slightly larger element $r' \in \alpha$ such that $r < r'$. Set $s := r - r' \in \mathbb{Q}$. Because $r < r'$, we have $s = r - r' < 0$, which means $s \in 0^*$! Thus we can decompose $r = r' + (r - r') = r' + s$ with $r' \in \alpha$ and $s \in 0^*$, proving $r \in \alpha + 0^*$.
   By mutual inclusion, $\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$. $\blacksquare$

Now we come to the most delicate step in constructing additive (and later multiplicative) inverses of cuts. Why can we not simply define $-\alpha$ to be $\{-q : q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha\}$? Because if $\alpha = q_0^*$ is a rational cut $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q_0\}$, its complement is $\mathbb{Q} \setminus q_0^* = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q \ge q_0\}$, which has a smallest element $q_0$. Negating all elements of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus q_0^*$ produces $\{-q : q \ge q_0\} = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le -q_0\}$, which contains a **largest element** $-q_0$ and therefore violates (C3)!

To strip away that potential right-hand endpoint cleanly for *every* cut $\alpha$, we define $-\alpha$ to consist of all rationals $p \in \mathbb{Q}$ that sit strictly to the left of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ after negation—meaning there is a positive rational buffer $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $-p - r \notin \alpha$. And to prove that $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$, we need a fundamental lemma guaranteeing that any cut $\alpha$ can be "straddled" by two rationals $r \in \alpha$ and $s \notin \alpha$ that are arbitrarily close together.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Straddling a Cut by Arbitrarily Small Rational Intervals
> Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ be a Dedekind cut, and let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ be any positive rational number. Then there exist rational numbers $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ such that:
> 1. $s - r = \varepsilon$, and
> 2. $s$ is **not** the smallest element of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ (meaning there exists $s' \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ with $s' < s$, or equivalently there exists $\delta \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $s - \delta \notin \alpha$).

**Proof:**
Let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ be given, and set $h := \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$. By non-triviality (C1) of $\alpha$, we can choose a point $p_0 \in \alpha$ and a point $q_0 \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$. By downward closure (C2), $p_0 < q_0$, so $q_0 - p_0 \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$.

Applying the **Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{Q}$** (proved in Section 0.4) to the positive rationals $h = \frac{\varepsilon}{2} > 0$ and $q_0 - p_0 > 0$, there exists a positive integer $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $N h > q_0 - p_0$, i.e., $p_0 + N h > q_0$. Because $q_0 \notin \alpha$ and $p_0 + N h > q_0$, downward closure (C2) implies that $p_0 + N h \notin \alpha$.

Consider the finite sequence of rational numbers $x_k := p_0 + k h$ for $k \in \{0, 1, 2, \dots, N\}$. Notice that $x_0 = p_0 \in \alpha$, while $x_N = p_0 + N h \notin \alpha$. Therefore, the non-empty subset of natural numbers
$$
S := \{k \in \mathbb{N} : p_0 + k h \notin \alpha\} \subseteq \mathbb{N}
$$
contains $N$ (so $S \neq \emptyset$) and does not contain $0$ (since $x_0 = p_0 \in \alpha$). By the **Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$** (Section 0.2), $S$ has a least element $m := \min S \ge 1$. Because $m \ge 1$ is the *first* index for which $x_m \notin \alpha$, the preceding index $m - 1 \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfies $m - 1 \notin S$, which means
$$
r := x_{m-1} = p_0 + (m - 1)h \in \alpha, \qquad x_m = p_0 + m h \notin \alpha
$$
Now set $s := x_{m+1} = p_0 + (m + 1)h \in \mathbb{Q}$. We verify that $r$ and $s$ satisfy all required properties:
1. First, $r \in \alpha$ by construction. Since $s = x_{m+1} > x_m$ and $x_m \notin \alpha$, downward closure (C2) forces $s \notin \alpha$ (so $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$).
2. Their difference is $s - r = \big(p_0 + (m + 1)h\big) - \big(p_0 + (m - 1)h\big) = 2h = 2\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) = \varepsilon$.
3. Finally, $s$ is not the minimum of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ because $x_m = s - h \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ is strictly smaller than $s$! $\blacksquare$

Equipped with the Straddling Lemma, we can now define the additive inverse $-\alpha$ and prove that $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, +, 0^*)$ is an abelian group.

> [!abstract] Definition: Additive Inverse ($-\alpha$) of a Dedekind Cut
> For any Dedekind cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, we define its **additive inverse** (or **negation**) $-\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> -\alpha := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : \exists r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} \text{ such that } -p - r \notin \alpha\}
> $$
> As usual, we write $\alpha - \beta := \alpha + (-\beta)$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Additive Inverse and Order Compatibility of Addition on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$
> For every $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, the set $-\alpha$ is a Dedekind cut ($-\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$), and it satisfies
> $$
> \alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*
> $$
> Consequently, $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, +, 0^*)$ is a commutative group, and for all $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, $\alpha < \beta \iff \alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$.

**Proof:**
1. **Verification that $-\alpha$ is a Dedekind cut:**
   - **(C1) Non-Triviality:** Pick $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ and $q \in \alpha$. Setting $p := -s - 1 \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $r := 1 \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, we have $-p - r = -(-s - 1) - 1 = s \notin \alpha$, so $p \in -\alpha$ and $-\alpha \neq \emptyset$. On the other hand, we claim $-q \notin -\alpha$: if $-q \in -\alpha$, there would exist $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $-(-q) - r = q - r \notin \alpha$; however, since $q \in \alpha$ and $q - r < q$, downward closure (C2) for $\alpha$ forces $q - r \in \alpha$, a contradiction! Thus $-q \notin -\alpha$ and $-\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
   - **(C2) Downward Closure:** Suppose $p \in -\alpha$ and $p' \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $p' < p$. By definition of $-\alpha$, there exists $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $-p - r \notin \alpha$. Since $p' < p$, we have $-p < -p'$, so $-p - r < -p' - r$. Because $-p - r \notin \alpha$, downward closure (C2) of $\alpha$ implies that the strictly larger rational $-p' - r$ also satisfies $-p' - r \notin \alpha$. Using the same witness $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, we conclude $p' \in -\alpha$.
   - **(C3) No Largest Element:** Suppose $p \in -\alpha$, so there exists $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ with $-p - r \notin \alpha$. Set $p' := p + \frac{r}{2} > p$ and $r' := \frac{r}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$. Then
     $$
     -p' - r' = -\left(p + \frac{r}{2}\right) - \frac{r}{2} = -p - r \notin \alpha
     $$
     Thus $p' \in -\alpha$ and $p < p'$, establishing (C3).

2. **Proof that $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$:** We verify both inclusions $\alpha + (-\alpha) \subseteq 0^*$ and $0^* \subseteq \alpha + (-\alpha)$:
   - ($\alpha + (-\alpha) \subseteq 0^*$): Let $x \in \alpha + (-\alpha)$, so $x = q + p$ with $q \in \alpha$ and $p \in -\alpha$. By definition of $-\alpha$, there exists $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $-p - r \notin \alpha$. Because $q \in \alpha$ and $-p - r \notin \alpha$, property (C2) of $\alpha$ guarantees that $q < -p - r$. Adding $p$ to both sides yields
     $$
     x = q + p < -r < 0
     $$
     Thus $x < 0$, which means $x \in 0^*$.
   - ($0^* \subseteq \alpha + (-\alpha)$): Let $x \in 0^*$, so $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $x < 0$. Then $\varepsilon := -x \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ is a positive rational number! By the **Straddling Lemma** applied to the cut $\alpha$ and $\varepsilon = -x > 0$, there exist $q \in \alpha$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ such that $s - q = \varepsilon = -x$, and such that $s$ is *not* the minimum of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$—meaning there exists $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ with $s - r \notin \alpha$. Now set $p := -s \in \mathbb{Q}$. Because $-p - r = s - r \notin \alpha$ with $r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, we have $p \in -\alpha$! And the sum of $q \in \alpha$ and $p \in -\alpha$ is
     $$
     q + p = q + (-s) = -(s - q) = -(-x) = x
     $$
     Therefore, $x = q + p \in \alpha + (-\alpha)$. By mutual inclusion, $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$.

3. **Strict Order Preservation Under Addition:** Suppose $\alpha < \beta$ (i.e., $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$) and $\gamma \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$. Clearly $\alpha \subseteq \beta \implies \alpha + \gamma \subseteq \beta + \gamma$. If equality $\alpha + \gamma = \beta + \gamma$ held, adding $-\gamma$ to both sides and using associativity and $\gamma + (-\gamma) = 0^*$ would give $\alpha = (\alpha + \gamma) + (-\gamma) = (\beta + \gamma) + (-\gamma) = \beta$, contradicting $\alpha < \beta$. Thus $\alpha + \gamma \subsetneq \beta + \gamma$, i.e., $\alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$. $\blacksquare$

### Multiplication of Cuts and the Complete Ordered Field $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$

Why can we not define the product of two arbitrary cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ simply as $\{rs : r \in \alpha, s \in \beta\}$? Because every cut contains arbitrarily large negative rational numbers! Since the product of two negative rationals is a positive rational, $\{rs : r \in \alpha, s \in \beta\}$ would contain arbitrarily large positive rationals and equal $\mathbb{Q}$ (violating C1).

To avoid sign reversals from negative rationals, we first define multiplication for **positive cuts** $\alpha > 0^*$ and $\beta > 0^*$ using only their positive rational elements, and then extend multiplication to all cuts by the standard rule of signs. Notice that $\alpha > 0^*$ ($0^* \subsetneq \alpha$) means precisely that $\alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0} \neq \emptyset$ (there exists $r \in \alpha$ with $r > 0$, because if $0 \in \alpha$, then by C3 some $r > 0$ is also in $\alpha$).

> [!abstract] Definition: Multiplication and Multiplicative Inverse of Dedekind Cuts
> 1. **Product of Positive Cuts:** For any two positive cuts $\alpha > 0^*$ and $\beta > 0^*$, we define their product $\alpha \cdot \beta \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
>    $$
