>    \alpha \cdot \beta := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0\} \cup \{p \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : \exists r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}, \, \exists s \in \beta \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0} \text{ such that } p = rs\}
>    $$
> 2. **Extension to Arbitrary Cuts:** For arbitrary $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, we define $\alpha \cdot \beta$ by trichotomy on signs:
>    $$
>    \alpha \cdot \beta := \begin{cases}
>    0^* & \text{if } \alpha = 0^* \text{ or } \beta = 0^*, \\
>    \alpha \cdot \beta & \text{if } \alpha > 0^* \text{ and } \beta > 0^*, \\
>    -\big((-\alpha) \cdot \beta\big) & \text{if } \alpha < 0^* \text{ and } \beta > 0^*, \\
>    -\big(\alpha \cdot (-\beta)\big) & \text{if } \alpha > 0^* \text{ and } \beta < 0^*, \\
>    (-\alpha) \cdot (-\beta) & \text{if } \alpha < 0^* \text{ and } \beta < 0^*.
>    \end{cases}
>    $$
> 3. **Multiplicative Inverse ($\alpha^{-1}$):** For any positive cut $\alpha > 0^*$, we define its reciprocal $\alpha^{-1} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
>    $$
>    \alpha^{-1} := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0\} \cup \left\{p \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : \exists r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} \text{ such that } \frac{1}{p} - r \notin \alpha\right\}
>    $$
>    and for $\alpha < 0^*$, we define $\alpha^{-1} := -((-\alpha)^{-1})$.

Notice how the definition of $\alpha^{-1}$ for $\alpha > 0^*$ mirrors the definition of $-\alpha$, replacing additive negation $-p$ on $\mathbb{Q}$ with multiplicative inversion $\frac{1}{p}$ on $\mathbb{Q}_{>0}$! Let us verify the core multiplicative properties and establish that $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a Complete Ordered Field containing an isomorphic copy of $\mathbb{Q}$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a Complete Ordered Field Extending $\mathbb{Q}$
> Equipped with $+$, $\cdot$, $0^*$, $1^*$, and $<$, the system $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a **Complete Ordered Field**. Furthermore, the rational cut map $\iota: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ defined by $\iota(q) := q^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q\}$ is an order-preserving field embedding: for all $p, q \in \mathbb{Q}$,
> $$
> (p + q)^* = p^* + q^*, \quad (-q)^* = -(q^*), \quad (pq)^* = p^* \cdot q^*, \quad \text{and} \quad p < q \iff p^* < q^*
> $$
> Finally, $\iota(\mathbb{Q})$ is **dense** in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$: for any $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ with $\alpha < \beta$, there exists $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $\alpha < q^* < \beta$.

**Proof:**
We have already proved that $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, <)$ is a strict total order satisfying the Least Upper Bound Property, and that $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, +, 0^*)$ is an abelian group with $\alpha < \beta \implies \alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$. Let us verify the multiplicative axioms for positive cuts $\alpha, \beta, \gamma > 0^*$ (the general sign cases follow by routine case analysis using $(-\alpha) = -\alpha$, and the full verification of $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$ is guided step-by-step in Problem 0.5.2):
1. **$\alpha \cdot \beta$ is a positive cut:** Since $\alpha, \beta > 0^*$, there exist $r_0 \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $s_0 \in \beta \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, so $r_0 s_0 > 0$ belongs to $\alpha \cdot \beta$, showing $0^* \subsetneq \alpha \cdot \beta$ (and $\alpha \cdot \beta \neq \emptyset$). Choosing $u \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ and $v \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \beta$ (so $u, v > 0$), every $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $s \in \beta \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ satisfies $0 < r < u$ and $0 < s < v$, whence $rs < uv$; thus $uv \notin \alpha \cdot \beta$ and (C1) holds. If $p \in \alpha \cdot \beta$ and $q < p$, then either $q \le 0$ (so $q \in \alpha \cdot \beta$ by definition) or $0 < q < p = rs$ with $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}, s \in \beta \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$; then $r' := \frac{q}{s} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ satisfies $r' < \frac{rs}{s} = r \in \alpha$, so $r' \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ by (C2) and $q = r' s \in \alpha \cdot \beta$, establishing (C2). For (C3), if $p \le 0$ then $r_0 s_0 > 0 \ge p$ is in $\alpha \cdot \beta$, and if $p = rs > 0$, choosing $r' \in \alpha$ with $r' > r$ by (C3) gives $r' s \in \alpha \cdot \beta$ with $p = rs < r' s$.
2. **Commutativity, Associativity, Distributivity, and Multiplicative Identity ($\alpha \cdot 1^* = \alpha$):** Commutativity and associativity for positive cuts follow immediately from $rs = sr$ and $(rs)t = r(st)$ in $\mathbb{Q}_{>0}$. For distributivity $\alpha \cdot (\beta + \gamma) = \alpha \cdot \beta + \alpha \cdot \gamma$ (with $\alpha, \beta, \gamma > 0^*$), any positive element of $\alpha \cdot (\beta + \gamma)$ has the form $r(s + t) = rs + rt \in \alpha \cdot \beta + \alpha \cdot \gamma$ (choosing $s > 0, t > 0$ by C3), and conversely if $r_1 s + r_2 t \in (\alpha \cdot \beta + \alpha \cdot \gamma) \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ with $r_1, r_2 \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, setting $r := \max\{r_1, r_2\} \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ gives $r_1 s + r_2 t \le r(s + t) \in \alpha \cdot (\beta + \gamma)$, so downward closure (C2) gives $r_1 s + r_2 t \in \alpha \cdot (\beta + \gamma)$. For $\alpha \cdot 1^* = \alpha$ (where $\alpha > 0^*$), if $p = rs$ with $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $0 < s < 1$, then $p = rs < r \in \alpha \implies p \in \alpha$; conversely, if $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, choosing $r' \in \alpha$ with $r' > r$ by (C3) allows us to write $r = r' \cdot \left(\frac{r}{r'}\right)$ where $\frac{r}{r'} \in 1^* \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, so $\alpha \subseteq \alpha \cdot 1^*$.
3. **Embedding of $\mathbb{Q}$ and Density:** For $p, q \in \mathbb{Q}$, $r \in p^* + q^* \iff r = p' + q'$ with $p' < p, q' < q \iff r < p + q$ (using $r = \left(p - \frac{p+q-r}{2}\right) + \left(q - \frac{p+q-r}{2}\right)$ for the reverse direction), so $p^* + q^* = (p + q)^*$, and similarly for negation, multiplication, and order ($p < q \iff p^* \subsetneq q^*$). Finally, if $\alpha < \beta$ in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, then $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$, so there exists $r \in \beta \setminus \alpha$. By (C3) for $\beta$, there exists $q \in \beta$ with $r < q$. Then $q^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q\}$ satisfies $\alpha \subsetneq q^*$ (since every $p \in \alpha$ has $p < r < q$ so $\alpha \subseteq q^*$, and $r \in q^* \setminus \alpha$) and $q^* \subsetneq \beta$ (by C2 for $\beta$ since $q \in \beta$, and $q \in \beta \setminus q^*$). Thus $\alpha < q^* < \beta$! $\blacksquare$

## Method II: Construction of $\mathbb{R}$ via Rational Cauchy Sequences

While Dedekind's cut construction is marvelously suited to order theory—turning $\sup \mathcal{F}$ into a plain set union $\bigcup_{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}} \alpha$—its definition of multiplication requires an awkward case split over signs. Let us now explore the second great construction of the real numbers, pioneered by Georg Cantor (and presented with crystal-clear algebraic notation by Terence Tao): constructing $\mathbb{R}$ via **Rational Cauchy Sequences**.

What is the guiding analytical intuition here? Think about how we approximate $\sqrt{2}$ in decimal arithmetic: we form a sequence of rational numbers
$$
a_1 = 1.4, \quad a_2 = 1.41, \quad a_3 = 1.414, \quad a_4 = 1.4142, \quad \dots
$$
such that $a_n^2$ gets closer and closer to $2$. Inside $\mathbb{Q}$, this sequence $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ has no limit because the point $\sqrt{2}$ is missing from $\mathbb{Q}$. Yet we can recognize *purely within $\mathbb{Q}$*—without ever mentioning $\sqrt{2}$—that the terms $a_n$ and $a_m$ of this sequence are clustering arbitrarily close to one another as $n, m \to \infty$! Such an internally clustering sequence is called a **Cauchy sequence**. If we manufacture a new number system whose elements are formal limits $\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n$ of rational Cauchy sequences (identifying two Cauchy sequences whenever their difference shrinks to zero), then every arithmetic operation—addition, negation, and multiplication—can be defined *term-by-term* in a single stroke without any sign-splitting!

### Rational Sequences, Boundedness, and Rational Cauchy Sequences

To avoid any circularity, remember that $\mathbb{R}$ **does not exist yet** in this construction. Every sequence, bound, and tolerance $\varepsilon$ we write down in this subsection must live strictly inside the already-constructed sets $\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Rational Sequence and Bounded Rational Sequence
> 1. A **rational sequence** is a function $a: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to \mathbb{Q}$, denoted $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ or simply $(a_n)$, where $a_n := a(n) \in \mathbb{Q}$ is the $n$-th term of the sequence.
> 2. A rational sequence $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is said to be **bounded** if there exists a positive rational number $M \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that
>    $$
>    \forall n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \quad |a_n| \le M
>    $$

Now we formalize what it means for the terms of a rational sequence $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ to cluster arbitrarily close together.

> [!abstract] Definition: Rational Cauchy Sequence
> A rational sequence $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ is called a **rational Cauchy sequence** if for every positive rational number $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, there exists a threshold index $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$,
> $$
> n, m \ge N \implies |a_n - a_m| \le \varepsilon
> $$
> We denote the set of all rational Cauchy sequences by $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$.

> [!warning] Crucial Non-Circularity Check: $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$!
> Notice that in the definition of a rational Cauchy sequence, the tolerance $\varepsilon$ is restricted to **positive rational numbers** ($\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$), because real numbers have not been constructed yet! Similarly, $|a_n - a_m|$ is the rational absolute value studied in Section 0.4.

Before forming equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences, we need a vital structural lemma: every rational Cauchy sequence is automatically bounded, and the collection $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ of rational Cauchy sequences is closed under termwise addition, negation, and multiplication (making $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ a commutative ring!).

> [!theorem] Lemma: Boundedness and Algebraic Closure of Rational Cauchy Sequences
> Let $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ and $(b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ be rational Cauchy sequences in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$. Then:
> 1. **Boundedness:** $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is bounded in $\mathbb{Q}$ (there exists $M \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n| \le M$ for all $n \ge 1$).
> 2. **Sum and Negation:** The termwise sum $(a_n + b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ and termwise negation $(-a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ are rational Cauchy sequences.
> 3. **Product:** The termwise product $(a_n b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is a rational Cauchy sequence.

**Proof:**
1. **Boundedness of $(a_n)$:** Apply the definition of a rational Cauchy sequence to the specific rational tolerance $\varepsilon := 1 \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$. There exists an integer $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that for all $n, m \ge N$, $|a_n - a_m| \le 1$. Fixing $m = N$, we see that for every tail index $n \ge N$, the Triangle Inequality in $\mathbb{Q}$ (Section 0.4) yields
   $$
   |a_n| = |(a_n - a_N) + a_N| \le |a_n - a_N| + |a_N| \le 1 + |a_N|
   $$
   What about the finitely many initial terms $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_{N-1}$? By induction on $N$, any finite non-empty set of rational numbers has a maximum element, so we may define
   $$
   M := \max\big\{|a_1|, |a_2|, \dots, |a_{N-1}|, |a_N| + 1\big\} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}
   $$
   (Note $M \ge |a_N| + 1 \ge 1 > 0$). Then for $1 \le n \le N - 1$ we have $|a_n| \le M$ directly from the maximum, and for $n \ge N$ we have $|a_n| \le |a_N| + 1 \le M$. Thus $|a_n| \le M$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$.

2. **Closure under Addition and Negation:** Let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ be given. Because $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, the Cauchy property of $(a_n)$ and $(b_n)$ provides thresholds $N_1, N_2 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n - a_m| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for all $n, m \ge N_1$ and $|b_n - b_m| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for all $n, m \ge N_2$. Set $N := \max\{N_1, N_2\} \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. Then for all $n, m \ge N$, both inequalities hold simultaneously, so by the Triangle Inequality,
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   |(a_n + b_n) - (a_m + b_m)| &= |(a_n - a_m) + (b_n - b_m)| \\
   &\le |a_n - a_m| + |b_n - b_m| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Thus $(a_n + b_n) \in \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$. For $(-a_n)$, since $|(-a_n) - (-a_m)| = |-(a_n - a_m)| = |a_n - a_m|$, $(-a_n)$ is immediately Cauchy with the exact same threshold $N$ as $(a_n)$.

3. **Closure under Multiplication:** Why is $|a_n b_n - a_m b_m|$ small when $n, m$ are large? We use the classic algebraic trick of **adding and subtracting a cross-term** $a_n b_m$, together with the boundedness proved in part (1)! By part (1), since $(a_n)$ and $(b_n)$ are Cauchy, there exist positive rational bounds $M_1, M_2 \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n| \le M_1$ and $|b_n| \le M_2$ for all $n \ge 1$. Set $M := \max\{M_1, M_2\} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, so both $|a_n| \le M$ and $|b_n| \le M$ hold for all $n \ge 1$.

   Now let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ be given. Since $2M \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, the quotient $\frac{\varepsilon}{2M} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ is a valid positive rational tolerance! Therefore, there exist $N_1, N_2 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that
   $$
   \forall n, m \ge N_1, \; |a_n - a_m| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2M} \quad \text{and} \quad \forall n, m \ge N_2, \; |b_n - b_m| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2M}
   $$
   Set $N := \max\{N_1, N_2\} \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. Then for all $n, m \ge N$, the Triangle Inequality and multiplicative compatibility of $|\cdot|$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ yield
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   |a_n b_n - a_m b_m| &= |a_n b_n - a_n b_m + a_n b_m - a_m b_m| \\
   &= |a_n(b_n - b_m) + b_m(a_n - a_m)| \\
   &\le |a_n| \, |b_n - b_m| + |b_m| \, |a_n - a_m| \\
   &\le M \cdot \frac{\varepsilon}{2M} + M \cdot \frac{\varepsilon}{2M} = \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Hence $(a_n b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is a rational Cauchy sequence. $\blacksquare$

### Equivalence of Cauchy Sequences and the Quotient Ring $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$

Two distinct rational Cauchy sequences can easily represent the *same* target limit: for example, the sequences $a_n = \frac{1}{n}$ and $b_n = -\frac{1}{n}$ both cluster toward $0$. In general, two Cauchy sequences $(a_n)$ and $(b_n)$ should define the same real number if and only if their termwise distance $|a_n - b_n|$ becomes arbitrarily small for all sufficiently large $n$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Equivalence of Rational Cauchy Sequences and $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$
> Let $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ and $(b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ be two rational Cauchy sequences in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$. We say that $(a_n)$ and $(b_n)$ are **equivalent**, written $(a_n) \sim (b_n)$, if for every $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, there exists $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that
> $$
> \forall n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \quad n \ge N \implies |a_n - b_n| \le \varepsilon
> $$
> We define the set of **Cauchy real numbers** to be the quotient set of $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ modulo $\sim$:
> $$
> \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}} := \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q}) / {\sim} = \{[(a_n)]_\sim : (a_n) \in \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})\}
> $$
> Following Terence Tao's notation, we write the equivalence class of a rational Cauchy sequence $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ as its **formal limit**:
> $$
> \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n := [(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty]_\sim
> $$
> For each rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, the constant sequence $(q, q, q, \dots)$ is trivially Cauchy (with $|q - q| = 0 \le \varepsilon$ for all $n, m \ge 1$), and we embed $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ into $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ as the formal limit $\iota(q) := \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} q$.

> [!theorem] Proposition: Equivalence Relation and Well-Defined Ring Operations on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$
> 1. The relation $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$.
> 2. If we define addition, negation, and multiplication on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ via representatives by
>    $$
>    \begin{aligned}
>    (\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n) + (\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} b_n) &:= \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} (a_n + b_n) \\
>    -(\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n) &:= \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} (-a_n) \\
>    (\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n) \cdot (\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} b_n) &:= \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} (a_n b_n)
>    \end{aligned}
>    $$
>    then these operations are **well-defined** (independent of the choice of representative Cauchy sequences) and make $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}, +, \cdot, \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} 0, \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} 1)$ a commutative ring with unity.

**Proof:**
1. **$\sim$ is an Equivalence Relation:**
   - **Reflexivity:** For any $(a_n) \in \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ and $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, taking $N = 1$ gives $|a_n - a_n| = 0 \le \varepsilon$ for all $n \ge 1$, so $(a_n) \sim (a_n)$.
   - **Symmetry:** Since $|b_n - a_n| = |a_n - b_n|$, $(a_n) \sim (b_n) \implies (b_n) \sim (a_n)$ immediately.
   - **Transitivity:** Suppose $(a_n) \sim (b_n)$ and $(b_n) \sim (c_n)$, and let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$. Then there exist $N_1, N_2 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n - b_n| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for $n \ge N_1$ and $|b_n - c_n| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for $n \ge N_2$. For all $n \ge N := \max\{N_1, N_2\}$, the Triangle Inequality gives
     $$
     |a_n - c_n| \le |a_n - b_n| + |b_n - c_n| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon
     $$
     so $(a_n) \sim (c_n)$.

2. **Well-Definedness of $+$, $-$, and $\cdot$:** Suppose $(a_n) \sim (a_n')$ and $(b_n) \sim (b_n')$ in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$. We must verify that $(a_n + b_n) \sim (a_n' + b_n')$, $(-a_n) \sim (-a_n')$, and $(a_n b_n) \sim (a_n' b_n')$:
   - **Addition and Negation:** Given $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, choose $N_1, N_2 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ so that $|a_n - a_n'| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for $n \ge N_1$ and $|b_n - b_n'| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for $n \ge N_2$. For $n \ge \max\{N_1, N_2\}$,
     $$
     |(a_n + b_n) - (a_n' + b_n')| \le |a_n - a_n'| + |b_n - b_n'| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon
     $$
     so $(a_n + b_n) \sim (a_n' + b_n')$. Similarly, $|(-a_n) - (-a_n')| = |a_n - a_n'|$, so $(-a_n) \sim (-a_n')$.
   - **Multiplication:** Because $(a_n)$ and $(b_n')$ are Cauchy sequences, the Boundedness Lemma gives $M \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n| \le M$ and $|b_n'| \le M$ for all $n \ge 1$. Given $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, since $(a_n) \sim (a_n')$ and $(b_n) \sim (b_n')$, there exist $N_1, N_2 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n - a_n'| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2M}$ for $n \ge N_1$ and $|b_n - b_n'| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2M}$ for $n \ge N_2$. For all $n \ge N := \max\{N_1, N_2\}$, adding and subtracting $a_n b_n'$ yields
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     |a_n b_n - a_n' b_n'| &= |a_n(b_n - b_n') + b_n'(a_n - a_n')| \\
     &\le |a_n| \, |b_n - b_n'| + |b_n'| \, |a_n - a_n'| \\
     &\le M \cdot \frac{\varepsilon}{2M} + M \cdot \frac{\varepsilon}{2M} = \varepsilon
     \end{aligned}
     $$
     Thus $(a_n b_n) \sim (a_n' b_n')$, proving that multiplication on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is well-defined!
   - **Ring Axioms:** Because all ring axioms (commutativity and associativity of $+$ and $\cdot$, distributivity, additive identity $0 + a_n = a_n$, additive inverse $a_n + (-a_n) = 0$, and multiplicative identity $1 \cdot a_n = a_n$) hold term-by-term in the field $\mathbb{Q}$, they automatically hold for formal limits $\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n$ in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$. $\blacksquare$

### Multiplicative Inverses, Positivity, and the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$

How do we divide by a non-zero Cauchy real number $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \neq \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} 0$, and how do we tell whether $x > 0$ or $x < 0$? Notice a minor snag: even if $(a_n)$ is not equivalent to the zero sequence $(0)$, finitely many terms $a_n$ might accidentally equal $0$ (preventing us from writing $\frac{1}{a_n}$ for all $n \ge 1$).

The key to overcoming this snag—and simultaneously defining the order relation on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$—is the **Separation Lemma**: any Cauchy sequence $(a_n)$ that is *not* equivalent to $(0)$ must eventually stay strictly bounded away from zero on one side (either $a_n \ge c > 0$ for all $n \ge N$, or $a_n \le -c < 0$ for all $n \ge N$).

> [!theorem] Lemma: Key Separation Lemma (Sequences Bounded Away from Zero)
> Let $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty \in \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ be a rational Cauchy sequence. Then $(a_n)$ is **not** equivalent to the zero sequence $(0)$ (i.e., $\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \neq 0$) if and only if there exist a positive rational number $c \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and an integer $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that either:
> - **(Positively Bounded Away from Zero):** $a_n \ge c$ for all $n \ge N$, or
> - **(Negatively Bounded Away from Zero):** $a_n \le -c$ for all $n \ge N$.
>
> Moreover, these two possibilities are mutually exclusive and are preserved under equivalence $\sim$ of Cauchy sequences.

**Proof:**
$(\Longleftarrow)$ Suppose there exist $c \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $a_n \ge c$ for all $n \ge N$ (or $a_n \le -c$ for all $n \ge N$). In either case, $|a_n - 0| = |a_n| \ge c$ for all $n \ge N$. Taking the positive rational $\varepsilon := \frac{c}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, no index $n \ge N$ can satisfy $|a_n - 0| \le \frac{c}{2} < c$, so $(a_n) \not\sim (0)$.

$(\implies)$ Conversely, suppose $(a_n) \not\sim (0)$. By negating the definition of $(a_n) \sim (0)$ ($\forall \varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}, \exists N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \forall n \ge N, |a_n| \le \varepsilon$), there exists at least one specific positive rational $\varepsilon_0 \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that for *every* $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, there is some index $m \ge N$ with $|a_m| > \varepsilon_0$.

Now apply the Cauchy property of $(a_n)$ to the smaller positive rational tolerance $c := \frac{\varepsilon_0}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$: there exists a threshold $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that for all $n, m \ge N$,
$$
|a_n - a_m| \le \frac{\varepsilon_0}{2} = c
$$
For this specific $N$, our negation of $(a_n) \sim (0)$ guarantees the existence of an index $m_0 \ge N$ such that $|a_{m_0}| > \varepsilon_0 = 2c$. By trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$, either $a_{m_0} > 2c$ or $a_{m_0} < -2c$:
- **Case 1 ($a_{m_0} > 2c$):** For every $n \ge N$, we have $|a_n - a_{m_0}| \le c$, which unfolds as $-c \le a_n - a_{m_0} \le c$. Therefore, for all $n \ge N$,
  $$
  a_n \ge a_{m_0} - c > 2c - c = c > 0
  $$
  so $(a_n)$ is positively bounded away from zero!
- **Case 2 ($a_{m_0} < -2c$):** For every $n \ge N$, unfolding $a_n - a_{m_0} \le c$ yields
  $$
  a_n \le a_{m_0} + c < -2c + c = -c < 0
  $$
  so $(a_n)$ is negatively bounded away from zero!

Finally, if $(a_n)$ is positively bounded away from zero ($a_n \ge c > 0$ for $n \ge N_1$) and $(a_n) \sim (a_n')$, then there exists $N_2 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n - a_n'| \le \frac{c}{2}$ for all $n \ge N_2$. Thus for all $n \ge \max\{N_1, N_2\}$, we have $a_n' \ge a_n - \frac{c}{2} \ge c - \frac{c}{2} = \frac{c}{2} > 0$, so $(a_n')$ is also positively bounded away from zero (with constant $\frac{c}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$). $\blacksquare$

With the Key Separation Lemma in hand, both multiplicative inversion and positivity on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ fall into place immediately!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Multiplicative Inverses and Ordered Field Structure of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$
> 1. **Multiplicative Inverses:** Let $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ with $x \neq 0$. By the Key Separation Lemma, there exist $c \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n| \ge c > 0$ for all $n \ge N$. Define a sequence $(b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ by
>    $$
>    b_n := \begin{cases} 0 & \text{if } 1 \le n < N, \\ a_n^{-1} & \text{if } n \ge N. \end{cases}
>    $$
>    Then $(b_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is a rational Cauchy sequence, and $x^{-1} := \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} b_n$ satisfies $x \cdot x^{-1} = 1$.
> 2. **Positivity and Total Order:** We define $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ to be **positive** ($x > 0$) if $(a_n)$ is positively bounded away from zero ($\exists c \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}, \exists N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \forall n \ge N, a_n \ge c$), and write $x < y \iff y - x > 0$. This makes $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ an **Archimedean Ordered Field** containing $\iota(\mathbb{Q}) = \{\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} q : q \in \mathbb{Q}\}$ as a dense subfield.

**Proof:**
1. **Verification that $(b_n)$ is Cauchy and $x \cdot x^{-1} = 1$:** Let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ be given. Since $c \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, the number $c^2 \varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ is a positive rational tolerance. By the Cauchy property of $(a_n)$, there exists $N_1 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|a_n - a_m| \le c^2 \varepsilon$ for all $n, m \ge N_1$. Set $N^* := \max\{N, N_1\}$. For all $n, m \ge N^*$, we have $b_n = \frac{1}{a_n}$, $b_m = \frac{1}{a_m}$, $|a_n| \ge c$, and $|a_m| \ge c$, so
   $$
   |b_n - b_m| = \left|\frac{1}{a_n} - \frac{1}{a_m}\right| = \frac{|a_m - a_n|}{|a_n| \, |a_m|} \le \frac{1}{c^2} |a_n - a_m| \le \frac{1}{c^2} (c^2 \varepsilon) = \varepsilon
   $$
   Thus $(b_n)_{n=1}^\infty \in \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ is a rational Cauchy sequence! Furthermore, for every $n \ge N$, the product term is $a_n b_n = a_n \cdot a_n^{-1} = 1$, so $|a_n b_n - 1| = 0 \le \varepsilon$ for all $n \ge N$. Therefore $(a_n b_n) \sim (1)$, which means $x \cdot x^{-1} = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} (a_n b_n) = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} 1 = 1$.

2. **Ordered Field and Archimedean Properties:** By the Key Separation Lemma, positivity $x > 0$ is well-defined on equivalence classes, and for every $x \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$, exactly one of $x > 0$ (positively bounded away from $0$), $x = 0$ ($(a_n) \sim (0)$), or $-x > 0$ (negatively bounded away from $0$) holds. Moreover, if $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n > 0$ ($a_n \ge c_1 > 0$ for $n \ge N_1$) and $y = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} b_n > 0$ ($b_n \ge c_2 > 0$ for $n \ge N_2$), then for all $n \ge \max\{N_1, N_2\}$ we have $a_n + b_n \ge c_1 + c_2 > 0$ and $a_n b_n \ge c_1 c_2 > 0$, so $x + y > 0$ and $xy > 0$. Thus $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is an Ordered Field!

   Why is $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ Archimedean and why is $\mathbb{Q}$ dense in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$? Given $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$, the Boundedness Lemma gives $M \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $a_n \le M$ for all $n \ge 1$. By the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{Q}$, there exists an integer $K \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $K \ge M + 1$, so $K - a_n \ge 1 > 0$ for all $n \ge 1$, which means $x < K$. Thus $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is Archimedean, and as proved in Section 0.4 for any Archimedean ordered field, its prime rational subfield $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$. $\blacksquare$

Finally, let us prove directly that $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ satisfies the **Least Upper Bound Property** by constructing the supremum of any non-empty bounded-above set $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ through binary (dyadic) bisection of upper bounds!

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$
> The ordered field $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property: every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ that is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ has a supremum $\sup E \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$.

**Proof:**
Let $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ be non-empty (pick $x_0 \in E$) and bounded above by $B \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$. Because $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is Archimedean, for each positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ we can scale by $2^n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and choose integers $L_n, U_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that
$$
\frac{L_n}{2^n} < x_0 \quad \text{and} \quad B \le \frac{U_n}{2^n}
$$
Notice that $\frac{U_n}{2^n}$ is an upper bound for $E$, while $\frac{L_n}{2^n}$ is *not* an upper bound for $E$ (since $x_0 \in E$ exceeds $\frac{L_n}{2^n}$). Because there are only finitely many integers $k \in \mathbb{Z}$ between $L_n$ and $U_n$, there is a **unique least integer** $M_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that the dyadic rational
$$
u_n := \frac{M_n}{2^n} \in \mathbb{Q}
$$
is an upper bound for $E$, while the immediately preceding dyadic rational
$$
l_n := \frac{M_n - 1}{2^n} = u_n - \frac{1}{2^n} \in \mathbb{Q}
$$
is **not** an upper bound for $E$!

1. **$(u_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is a rational Cauchy sequence:** For any two indices $n, m \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, since $u_m$ is an upper bound for $E$ and $l_n = u_n - \frac{1}{2^n}$ is *not* an upper bound for $E$ (meaning there exists some $x \in E$ with $l_n < x \le u_m$), we must have $u_n - \frac{1}{2^n} = l_n < u_m$, i.e., $u_n - u_m < \frac{1}{2^n}$. By symmetry, $u_m - u_n < \frac{1}{2^m}$ as well. Thus, for all $n, m \ge N$,
   $$
   |u_n - u_m| < \max\left\{\frac{1}{2^n}, \frac{1}{2^m}\right\} \le \frac{1}{2^N} \le \frac{1}{N}
   $$
   (using Bernoulli's Inequality $2^N = (1 + 1)^N \ge 1 + N > N$ from Section 0.4). Given any $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{Q}$ gives $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ with $\frac{1}{N} \le \varepsilon$, so $|u_n - u_m| \le \varepsilon$ for all $n, m \ge N$. Hence $(u_n)_{n=1}^\infty \in \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ is a rational Cauchy sequence!

2. **$s := \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} u_n \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is the supremum of $E$:**
   - **(S1) $s$ is an upper bound of $E$:** Suppose for contradiction that there exists $x \in E$ with $x > s$. By density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ (or the definition of $x - s > 0$), there exists $c \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $x - s \ge c$, and choosing $k \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ large enough that $|u_k - s| \le \frac{c}{2}$ (which holds since $|u_n - u_k| \le \frac{1}{k} \le \frac{c}{2}$ for all $n \ge k$), we obtain $u_k \le s + \frac{c}{2} < s + c \le x$, contradicting the fact that $u_k$ is an upper bound for $E$! Thus $x \le s$ for all $x \in E$.
   - **(S2) $s$ is the least upper bound of $E$:** Let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ with $\varepsilon > 0$ be given. By the Archimedean property, choose $k \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ large enough that $\frac{1}{2^{k-1}} = \frac{2}{2^k} < \varepsilon$. Since $|u_n - u_k| \le \frac{1}{2^k}$ for all $n \ge k$, we have $s - u_k \le \frac{1}{2^k}$, so
     $$
     l_k = u_k - \frac{1}{2^k} \ge \left(s - \frac{1}{2^k}\right) - \frac{1}{2^k} = s - \frac{2}{2^k} > s - \varepsilon
     $$
     Because $l_k$ is **not** an upper bound of $E$, there exists an element $x \in E$ such that $x > l_k > s - \varepsilon$. By the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum, $s = \sup E$! $\blacksquare$

## Uniqueness (Categoricity) of Complete Ordered Fields

We now have two completely distinct set-theoretic models of a Complete Ordered Field:
- In Method I, a real number $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a downward-closed subset of $\mathbb{Q}$.
- In Method II, a real number $x \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is an equivalence class of rational Cauchy sequences.

Why are mathematicians justified in forgetting both internal representations and speaking simply of **the** field of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$? Because the axioms of a Complete Ordered Field are **categorical**: any two Complete Ordered Fields are linked by a *unique* order-preserving field isomorphism!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Categoricity (Uniqueness up to Unique Isomorphism) of Complete Ordered Fields
> Let $(\mathbb{F}_1, +_1, \cdot_1, 0_1, 1_1, <_1)$ and $(\mathbb{F}_2, +_2, \cdot_2, 0_2, 1_2, <_2)$ be any two Complete Ordered Fields. Let $\mathbb{Q}_1 \subseteq \mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{Q}_2 \subseteq \mathbb{F}_2$ be their canonical prime rational subfields (constructed in Section 0.4), and let $\iota: \mathbb{Q}_1 \to \mathbb{Q}_2$ be the unique order-preserving field isomorphism between $\mathbb{Q}_1$ and $\mathbb{Q}_2$.
>
> Then there exists a **unique** order-preserving field isomorphism $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ extending $\iota$, and it is given explicitly for every $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$ by the Dedekind-cut supremum formula
> $$
> \Phi(x) := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} \big\{\iota(q) : q \in \mathbb{Q}_1 \text{ and } q <_1 x\big\}
> $$

**Proof:**
First, remember from Section 0.4 that every Complete Ordered Field is **Archimedean**: if $\mathbb{N}_1 \subseteq \mathbb{F}_1$ were bounded above in $\mathbb{F}_1$, then $s := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_1} \mathbb{N}_1$ would exist by the Least Upper Bound Property, and by the $\varepsilon$-characterization with $\varepsilon = 1_1 >_1 0_1$, there would exist $n \in \mathbb{N}_1$ with $n >_1 s - 1_1$, forcing $n +_1 1_1 \in \mathbb{N}_1$ to satisfy $n +_1 1_1 >_1 s$, a contradiction! Because both $\mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{F}_2$ are Archimedean ordered fields, $\mathbb{Q}_1$ is dense in $\mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{Q}_2$ is dense in $\mathbb{F}_2$ (Section 0.4). To simplify notation, let us suppress the subscripts on $+, \cdot, 0, 1, <$ when clear from context.

1. **Uniqueness of $\Phi$:** Suppose $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ is *any* order-preserving field isomorphism between $\mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{F}_2$. Because $\Phi(1_1) = 1_2$ and $\Phi$ is a field homomorphism, $\Phi$ must agree with the canonical rational isomorphism $\iota$ on $\mathbb{Q}_1$ ($\Phi(q) = \iota(q)$ for all $q \in \mathbb{Q}_1$). For any $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, consider the non-empty set $L_x := \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_1 : q < x\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}_1$. Since $\Phi$ preserves order, for every $q \in L_x$ we have $\iota(q) = \Phi(q) < \Phi(x)$, so $\Phi(x)$ is an upper bound for $\iota(L_x) = \{\iota(q) : q \in \mathbb{Q}_1, q < x\}$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$. Moreover, if $y \in \mathbb{F}_2$ satisfies $y < \Phi(x)$, then by density of $\mathbb{Q}_2$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$ there exists $r = \iota(q) \in \mathbb{Q}_2$ (with $q \in \mathbb{Q}_1$) such that $y < \iota(q) = \Phi(q) < \Phi(x)$. Applying $\Phi^{-1}$ gives $q < x$, so $\iota(q) \in \iota(L_x)$ and $y$ is not an upper bound for $\iota(L_x)$. Thus $\Phi(x)$ *must* equal $\sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} \iota(L_x)$, proving uniqueness!

2. **Existence, Order-Preservation, and Bijectivity of $\Phi$:** For each $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, define $L_x := \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_1 : q < x\}$. By the Archimedean property (density of $\mathbb{Q}_1$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$), there exist rationals $q_1, q_2 \in \mathbb{Q}_1$ with $q_1 < x < q_2$. Thus $\iota(q_1) \in \iota(L_x)$ (so $\iota(L_x) \neq \emptyset$) and $\iota(q_2)$ is an upper bound for $\iota(L_x)$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$ (since $q < x < q_2 \implies \iota(q) < \iota(q_2)$). By the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{F}_2$, $\Phi(x) := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} \iota(L_x)$ exists in $\mathbb{F}_2$. Notice also that for any $r \in \mathbb{Q}_1$, if $r < x$ then $\iota(r) \le \Phi(x)$ (in fact, choosing $r' \in \mathbb{Q}_1$ with $r < r' < x$ gives $\iota(r) < \iota(r') \le \Phi(x)$), and if $x < r$ then $\iota(r)$ is an upper bound for $\iota(L_x)$, so $\Phi(x) \le \iota(r)$.
   - **Strict Order Preservation ($x < y \implies \Phi(x) < \Phi(y)$):** If $x < y$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$, density of $\mathbb{Q}_1$ gives $q_1, q_2 \in \mathbb{Q}_1$ such that $x < q_1 < q_2 < y$. Then $\Phi(x) \le \iota(q_1) < \iota(q_2) \le \Phi(y)$, so $\Phi(x) < \Phi(y)$! Since $\mathbb{F}_1$ is linearly ordered, strict order preservation immediately implies that $\Phi$ is **injective**.
   - **Surjectivity:** Given any $y \in \mathbb{F}_2$, the set $M_y := \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_1 : \iota(q) < y\} \subseteq \mathbb{F}_1$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{F}_1$, so $x := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_1} M_y \in \mathbb{F}_1$ exists by the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{F}_1$. For any $q \in \mathbb{Q}_1$, $q < x \iff \exists q' \in M_y, q < q' \iff \iota(q) < y$, so $\iota(L_x) = \{\iota(q) : \iota(q) < y\}$, whose supremum in $\mathbb{F}_2$ (by density of $\mathbb{Q}_2$) is precisely $y$. Thus $\Phi(x) = y$, and $\Phi$ is a bijection!

3. **Preservation of Addition and Multiplication:**
   - **Addition ($\Phi(x + y) = \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$):** Let $x, y \in \mathbb{F}_1$. For any $q \in L_x$ ($q < x$) and $r \in L_y$ ($r < y$), we have $q + r < x + y$, so $q + r \in L_{x+y}$ and therefore $\iota(q) + \iota(r) = \iota(q + r) \le \Phi(x + y)$. Fixing $r$ and taking the supremum over $q \in L_x$ gives $\Phi(x) + \iota(r) \le \Phi(x + y)$, and then taking the supremum over $r \in L_y$ yields $\Phi(x) + \Phi(y) \le \Phi(x + y)$. Conversely, for any $p \in L_{x+y}$ ($p < x + y$), let $\varepsilon := \frac{(x + y) - p}{2} > 0$. By density of $\mathbb{Q}_1$, choose $q, r \in \mathbb{Q}_1$ such that $x - \varepsilon < q < x$ and $y - \varepsilon < r < y$. Then $p = (x + y) - 2\varepsilon < q + r$, so $\iota(p) < \iota(q + r) = \iota(q) + \iota(r) \le \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$. Taking the supremum over $p \in L_{x+y}$ gives $\Phi(x + y) \le \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$, whence $\Phi(x + y) = \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$ (which also implies $\Phi(-x) = -\Phi(x)$).
   - **Multiplication ($\Phi(xy) = \Phi(x)\Phi(y)$):** For $x, y > 0$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$, repeating the exact same argument over positive rationals $0 < q < x$ and $0 < r < y$ shows that $\iota(q)\iota(r) = \iota(qr) \le \Phi(xy)$ for all such $q, r$ and every $0 < p < xy$ is bounded above by some $qr$ with $0 < q < x, 0 < r < y$, yielding $\Phi(xy) = \Phi(x)\Phi(y)$ for $x, y > 0$. The remaining sign cases follow immediately from $\Phi(-z) = -\Phi(z)$ and $\Phi(0_1) = 0_2$. $\blacksquare$

> [!summary] Summary: The Real Number System $\mathbb{R}$
> Because $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ are both Complete Ordered Fields, the Categoricity Theorem guarantees that $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}} \cong \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ via a unique order-preserving field isomorphism. From this point onward in our study of Real Analysis, we no longer need to distinguish between Dedekind cuts and equivalence classes of rational Cauchy sequences: we simply write **$\mathbb{R}$** for **the** Complete Ordered Field of real numbers, containing $\mathbb{Q}$ as a dense subfield ($\mathbb{N} \subset \mathbb{Z} \subset \mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{R}$) and satisfying the **Least Upper Bound Property**.

## 0.5 Exercises

### Problem 0.5.1: Existence of $\sqrt{2}$ as an Explicit Dedekind Cut

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Define the subset $\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> \alpha := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q \le 0 \text{ or } (q > 0 \text{ and } q^2 < 2)\}
> $$
> Using Rudin's Gap Theorem from Section 0.4, prove directly from the definitions in Section 0.5 that:
> 1. $\alpha$ satisfies the three Dedekind cut axioms (C1), (C2), and (C3), so $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and $\alpha > 0^*$.
> 2. In $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, the product $\alpha \cdot \alpha$ equals the rational cut $2^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 2\}$. (Hint: To show $2^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha$, for any rational $0 < p < 2$, apply Rudin's Gap Theorem or the Archimedean Property to construct $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $p < r^2 < 2$, and note that $p = r \cdot \left(\frac{p}{r}\right)$ with $\frac{p}{r} < r \in \alpha$.)

### Problem 0.5.2: Multiplicative Inverse in the Dedekind Cut Construction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ be a positive Dedekind cut ($\alpha > 0^*$), and recall the definition of its reciprocal cut:
> $$
> \alpha^{-1} := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0\} \cup \left\{p \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : \exists r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} \text{ such that } \frac{1}{p} - r \notin \alpha\right\}
> $$
> 1. Prove that $\alpha^{-1}$ satisfies the Dedekind cut axioms (C1), (C2), and (C3), and that $\alpha^{-1} > 0^*$.
> 2. Prove that for every integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and any rational $q_0 \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, there exist $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ (not the minimum of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$) such that $\frac{s}{r} < 1 + \frac{1}{n}$.
> 3. Conclude that $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 1\}$.

### Problem 0.5.3: Explicit Isomorphism Between $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ and $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For each Cauchy real number $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$, define the subset $\Psi(x) \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> \Psi(x) := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : \iota(q) < x\}
> $$
> where $\iota(q) = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} q$ is the canonical embedding of $\mathbb{Q}$ into $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$.
> 1. Prove directly from the Cauchy construction of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ that $\Psi(x)$ satisfies (C1), (C2), and (C3), so $\Psi(x) \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.
> 2. Prove directly (without invoking the general Categoricity Theorem) that $\Psi: \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}} \to \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a bijective, order-preserving field isomorphism. (Hint: For surjectivity, given a cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, use the Straddling Lemma with $\varepsilon = \frac{1}{n}$ to choose $a_n \in \alpha$ and $b_n \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ with $b_n - a_n = \frac{1}{n}$, prove $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is a rational Cauchy sequence, and show $\Psi(\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n) = \alpha$.)

### Problem 0.5.4: Rigidity of the Real Field (No Non-Trivial Field Automorphisms)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\sigma: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be any field homomorphism—meaning that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$,
> $$
> \sigma(x + y) = \sigma(x) + \sigma(y), \quad \sigma(xy) = \sigma(x)\sigma(y), \quad \text{and} \quad \sigma(1) = 1
> $$
> **without** assuming beforehand that $\sigma$ is order-preserving or continuous!
> 1. Prove that $\sigma(q) = q$ for every rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$.
> 2. Using the fact that every positive real number $z > 0$ has a real square root $w \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ with $w^2 = z$ (which follows from the Least Upper Bound Property as in Problem 0.5.1), prove that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$, $x < y \implies \sigma(x) < \sigma(y)$.
> 3. Using the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$, conclude that $\sigma(x) = x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$. Thus the identity map $\text{id}_{\mathbb{R}}$ is the **only** field automorphism of $\mathbb{R}$!

### Problem 0.5.5: Dedekind's Cut Theorem in $\mathbb{R}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $F$ be an Ordered Field. We say that $F$ satisfies **Dedekind's Cut Property** if for every pair of subsets $A, B \subseteq F$ such that:
> - $A \neq \emptyset$ and $B \neq \emptyset$,
> - $A \cup B = F$ and $A \cap B = \emptyset$, and
> - for every $a \in A$ and every $b \in B$, we have $a < b$,
>
> there exists a unique element $\xi \in F$ such that $a \le \xi \le b$ for all $a \in A$ and all $b \in B$ (equivalently, either $A$ has a maximum element $\xi = \max A$ or $B$ has a minimum element $\xi = \min B$).
> 1. Prove from the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ that $\mathbb{R}$ satisfies Dedekind's Cut Property.
> 2. Conversely, prove that if an Ordered Field $F$ satisfies Dedekind's Cut Property, then $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property.

### Problem 0.5.6: Why Cauchy Completeness Alone Does Not Imply Dedekind Completeness

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In an arbitrary Ordered Field $F$, a sequence $(x_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ of elements in $F$ is called **$F$-Cauchy** if for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|x_n - x_m| < \varepsilon$ for all $n, m \ge N$. Similarly, $(x_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is said to **$F$-converge** to a limit $L \in F$ if for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|x_n - L| < \varepsilon$ for all $n \ge N$. We say that $F$ is **$F$-Cauchy complete** if every $F$-Cauchy sequence in $F$ $F$-converges to some limit $L \in F$.
> 1. Prove that if $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property, then $F$ is **both** Archimedean **and** $F$-Cauchy complete. (Hint: For an $F$-Cauchy sequence $(x_n)$, show $(x_n)$ is bounded in $F$, define $u_k := \sup_{n \ge k} x_n$, and take $L := \inf_{k \ge 1} u_k$.)
> 2. Conversely, prove that if an Ordered Field $F$ is **both** Archimedean **and** $F$-Cauchy complete, then $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property. Explain precisely where the Archimedean Property is essential in constructing an $F$-Cauchy sequence that converges to $\sup E$ for a bounded non-empty set $E \subseteq F$.

### Problem 0.5.7: Order-Completeness Characterization via Monotone Bounded Functions on $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $F$ be an Ordered Field. A function $f: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to F$ (i.e., a sequence $(f(n))_{n=1}^\infty$ in $F$) is called **non-decreasing** if $f(n) \le f(n+1)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, and **bounded above** if there exists $M \in F$ such that $f(n) \le M$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$.
> 1. Prove that if every bounded-above non-decreasing function $f: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to F$ has a supremum $\sup_{n \ge 1} f(n) \in F$, then $F$ is necessarily **Archimedean**. (Hint: Suppose $\mathbb{N}_{>0}$ were bounded above in $F$, apply the hypothesis to $f(n) = n$, and derive a contradiction.)
> 2. Prove that an Ordered Field $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property if and only if every bounded-above non-decreasing function $f: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to F$ has a supremum in $F$.

### Problem 0.5.8: Failure of Dedekind Cuts Without the No-Maximum Condition (C3)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Suppose we modified the definition of a Dedekind cut in Section 0.5 by dropping axiom (C3) ("no largest element"), and defined the set $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$ of **modified cuts** to be all subsets $\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying only (C1) non-triviality ($\alpha \neq \emptyset, \alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$) and (C2) downward closure ($p \in \alpha \text{ and } q < p \implies q \in \alpha$).
> 1. Show that for every rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, both the open ray $q^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q\}$ and the closed ray $\overline{q}^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le q\}$ belong to $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$, and satisfy $q^* \subsetneq \overline{q}^*$ with no modified cut strictly between them.
> 2. If we define addition on $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$ by the same pairwise formula $\alpha + \beta := \{r + s : r \in \alpha \text{ and } s \in \beta\}$, calculate $0^* + 0^*$, $0^* + \overline{0}^*$, and $\overline{0}^* + \overline{0}^*$, and prove that $(\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}, +)$ **fails to form a group**! (Hint: Determine whether $0^*$ or $\overline{0}^*$ would have to be the identity element $e$, and show that one of them fails to have an inverse in $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$.)

---

# 0.6 Working in $\mathbb{R}$ — Supremum Calculus, Archimedean Consequences, Roots, and Extended Reals

With the construction of the real number system $\mathbb{R}$ complete and its uniqueness as a **Complete Ordered Field** established, we no longer need to peek inside Dedekind cuts or Cauchy equivalence classes to do analysis. From this point onward, we work entirely from the axioms of a complete ordered field: the algebraic and order axioms of an ordered field together with the **Least Upper Bound Property** (and its dual, the **Greatest Lower Bound Property**), along with the canonical chain of embeddings $\mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z} \subseteq \mathbb{Q} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$.

However, knowing that a supremum or infimum *exists* is only the starting point. In day-to-day analytical arguments, we constantly manipulate suprema and infima across subsets, sums of sets, scalar multiples, and real-valued functions. Moreover, the Least Upper Bound Property unlocks foundational structural properties of the real line that fail in the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$: the **Archimedean property** of $\mathbb{R}$, the simultaneous **density of both the rationals and the irrationals**, the **existence of $n$-th roots** $x^{1/n}$ (which we must prove purely algebraically from completeness, since continuity and the Intermediate Value Theorem do not yet exist!), and **Cantor's Nested Interval Property**. Finally, we will see how adjoining two formal boundary symbols $-\infty$ and $+\infty$ yields the **extended real number system** $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, a bookkeeping framework in which *every* subset possesses both a supremum and an infimum without exception.

## The Algebra and Calculus of Supremum and Infimum

How do the supremum and infimum behave when we shrink a set, compare two sets element-by-element, translate or rescale a set, or add two sets together? Let us build a systematic "calculus of bounds" that allows us to pass inequalities through $\sup$ and $\inf$ effortlessly.

### Monotonicity and Comparison Principles

Whenever we enlarge a set, we introduce new elements that might push the least upper bound higher or pull the greatest lower bound lower. Conversely, restricting attention to a smaller subset can only tighten the bounds inward.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Monotonicity of Supremum and Infimum under Subset Inclusion
> Let $A \subseteq B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ with $A \neq \emptyset$ (and hence $B \neq \emptyset$).
> 1. If $B$ is bounded above, then $A$ is bounded above and
>    $$
>    \sup A \le \sup B.
>    $$
> 2. If $B$ is bounded below, then $A$ is bounded below and
>    $$
>    \inf B \le \inf A.
>    $$
> 3. For any non-empty bounded subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$,
>    $$
>    \inf A \le \sup A.
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. Assume $B$ is bounded above. By the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$, the supremum $\beta := \sup B \in \mathbb{R}$ exists and is an upper bound for $B$, meaning $b \le \beta$ for all $b \in B$. Since $A \subseteq B$, every element $a \in A$ also belongs to $B$ and therefore satisfies $a \le \beta$. Thus $\beta$ is an upper bound for $A$. Because $A$ is non-empty and bounded above by $\beta$, $\sup A$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$; furthermore, because $\sup A$ is the *least* upper bound of $A$ and $\beta = \sup B$ is *an* upper bound of $A$, we immediately obtain $\sup A \le \sup B$.
2. Assume $B$ is bounded below. By the Greatest Lower Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$, $\gamma := \inf B \in \mathbb{R}$ exists and satisfies $\gamma \le b$ for all $b \in B$. Since $A \subseteq B$, we have $\gamma \le a$ for every $a \in A$, showing that $\gamma$ is a lower bound for $A$. Because $A$ is non-empty and bounded below, $\inf A$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$, and since $\inf A$ is the *greatest* lower bound of $A$ while $\gamma = \inf B$ is *a* lower bound of $A$, it follows that $\inf B \le \inf A$.
3. Suppose $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is non-empty and bounded (both above and below). Since $A \neq \emptyset$, we may choose at least one element $a_0 \in A$. By definition of infimum and supremum, $\inf A$ is a lower bound of $A$ and $\sup A$ is an upper bound of $A$, so
   $$
   \inf A \le a_0 \le \sup A.
   $$
   By transitivity of the order relation $\le$ in $\mathbb{R}$, we conclude that $\inf A \le \sup A$. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Caution: The Non-Emptiness Hypothesis is Essential
> Notice how part (3) of the theorem above relies crucially on picking an element $a_0 \in A$. Without $A \neq \emptyset$, there is no element bridging the lower bounds of $A$ to the upper bounds of $A$—indeed, when we extend $\sup$ and $\inf$ to the empty set in the extended reals $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ at the end of this section, we will see that the inequality reverses for $\emptyset$!

A natural follow-up question arises when comparing two distinct sets $A$ and $B$: how do elementwise inequalities between $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ translate into inequalities between $\sup A$, $\inf A$, $\sup B$, and $\inf B$? There are two fundamentally different situations to distinguish: one where *every* element of $A$ lies to the left of *every* element of $B$, and a much weaker one where each element of $A$ is merely dominated by *some* element of $B$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Comparison Criteria for Two Sets
> Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty subsets of $\mathbb{R}$.
> 1. **Uniform Separation ($A$ lies entirely to the left of $B$):** Suppose that for every $a \in A$ and every $b \in B$, we have $a \le b$. Then $A$ is bounded above, $B$ is bounded below, and
>    $$
>    \sup A \le \inf B.
>    $$
> 2. **Domination of $A$ by $B$:** Suppose $B$ is bounded above, and for every $a \in A$ there exists at least one $b \in B$ such that $a \le b$. Then $A$ is bounded above and
>    $$
>    \sup A \le \sup B.
>    $$
>    Dually, if $A$ is bounded below and for every $b \in B$ there exists at least one $a \in A$ such that $a \le b$, then $B$ is bounded below and $\inf A \le \inf B$.

**Proof:**
1. Since $B \neq \emptyset$, we can fix an arbitrary element $b_0 \in B$. The hypothesis states that $a \le b_0$ for all $a \in A$, which means that $b_0$ is an upper bound for $A$. Since $A$ is non-empty and bounded above, $\sup A$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$. Moreover, because $b_0 \in B$ was an arbitrary upper bound for $A$ and $\sup A$ is the *least* upper bound for $A$, we have
   $$
   \sup A \le b_0 \quad \text{for every } b_0 \in B.
   $$
   Now view this inequality from the perspective of the set $B$: it says that the real number $\sup A$ is a *lower bound* for the set $B$! Since $B$ is non-empty and bounded below by $\sup A$, its greatest lower bound $\inf B$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$, and because $\inf B$ is the *greatest* lower bound of $B$, we must have $\sup A \le \inf B$.
2. Suppose $B$ is bounded above, so $\sup B \in \mathbb{R}$ exists, and assume that for every $a \in A$ there exists some $b_a \in B$ with $a \le b_a$. Since $b_a \in B$, we have $b_a \le \sup B$. By transitivity,
   $$
   a \le b_a \le \sup B \quad \text{for all } a \in A.
   $$
   This shows that $\sup B$ is an upper bound for $A$. Since $A \neq \emptyset$, $\sup A$ exists and is the least upper bound of $A$, whence $\sup A \le \sup B$. The dual statement for infima follows by an identical argument: for each $b \in B$, choosing $a_b \in A$ with $a_b \le b$ gives $\inf A \le a_b \le b$, making $\inf A$ a lower bound for $B$ and yielding $\inf A \le \inf B$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: The Double-Quantifier Elimination Trick
> Look closely at the two-step strategy in the proof of Part (1) above. We began with a doubly quantified statement:
> $$
> \forall a \in A, \, \forall b \in B, \quad a \le b.
> $$
> First, we froze $b \in B$ and varied $a \in A$ to turn $b$ into an upper bound of $A$, collapsing $\forall a \in A$ into $\sup A \le b$. Second, we varied $b \in B$ to turn $\sup A$ into a lower bound of $B$, collapsing $\forall b \in B$ into $\sup A \le \inf B$. This "freeze one variable, take the extremum over the other, then unfreeze" technique is one of the most frequently used devices in real analysis!

### Minkowski Sums and Scalar Dilations of Sets

Next, let us investigate how $\sup$ and $\inf$ interact with the algebraic operations of the field $\mathbb{R}$. Given two subsets $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ and a scalar $c \in \mathbb{R}$, how do we form their sum and scalar multiple?

> [!abstract] Definition: Minkowski Sum, Difference, and Scalar Dilation of Sets
> Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, and let $c \in \mathbb{R}$.
> 1. The **Minkowski sum** (or **pointwise sum**) of $A$ and $B$ is the set of all sums of pairs of elements, one from $A$ and one from $B$:
>    $$
>    A + B := \{a + b : a \in A, \, b \in B\}.
>    $$
> 2. The **scalar dilation** (or **scalar multiple**) of $A$ by $c$ is the set:
>    $$
>    cA := \{ca : a \in A\}.
>    $$
>    In the special case $c = -1$, we write $-A := (-1)A = \{-a : a \in A\}$ and call $-A$ the **reflection** of $A$ across the origin.
> 3. The **Minkowski difference** of $A$ and $B$ is defined as:
>    $$
>    A - B := A + (-B) = \{a - b : a \in A, \, b \in B\}.
>    $$

Notice that $A + A = \{a_1 + a_2 : a_1, a_2 \in A\}$ is generally strictly larger than $2A = \{2a : a \in A\}$, because in $A + A$ the two summands $a_1$ and $a_2$ may be chosen independently from $A$, whereas in $2A$ both summands must be the same element $a$. Nevertheless, as the next theorem reveals, the supremum and infimum are completely linear with respect to Minkowski addition and positive scalar multiplication, while negative scalar multiplication swaps $\sup$ and $\inf$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Linearity and Reflection Laws for Supremum and Infimum
> Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, and let $c \in \mathbb{R}$. Then $A + B$ and $cA$ are non-empty and bounded, and the following identities hold:
> 1. **Additivity over Minkowski Sums:**
>    $$
>    \sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A + B) = \inf A + \inf B.
>    $$
> 2. **Positive Homogeneity ($c \ge 0$):** If $c \ge 0$, then
>    $$
>    \sup(cA) = c \sup A \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(cA) = c \inf A.
>    $$
> 3. **Order-Reversing Homogeneity ($c < 0$):** If $c < 0$, then
>    $$
>    \sup(cA) = c \inf A \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(cA) = c \sup A.
>    $$
>    In particular, setting $c = -1$ gives the **reflection duality laws**:
>    $$
>    \sup(-A) = -\inf A \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(-A) = -\sup A,
>    $$
>    and combining (1) and (3) yields the **Minkowski difference formulas**:
>    $$
>    \sup(A - B) = \sup A - \inf B \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A - B) = \inf A - \sup B.
>    $$

**Proof:**
We prove each part in full detail using both the definition of least upper bound and the $\varepsilon$-characterization of supremum and infimum established in Section 0.4.

**Proof of (1):** Since $A$ and $B$ are non-empty and bounded, $\alpha := \sup A$ and $\beta := \sup B$ exist in $\mathbb{R}$.
First, for any element $x \in A + B$, we can write $x = a + b$ with $a \in A$ and $b \in B$. Since $a \le \alpha$ and $b \le \beta$, the compatibility of addition with order in $\mathbb{R}$ gives
$$
a + b \le \alpha + \beta.
$$
Because this holds for all $a \in A$ and $b \in B$, the number $\alpha + \beta$ is an upper bound for the non-empty set $A + B$. Therefore, $\sup(A + B)$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$ and satisfies the inequality
$$
\sup(A + B) \le \alpha + \beta = \sup A + \sup B.
$$
To establish the reverse inequality, let $\varepsilon > 0$ be an arbitrary positive real number. By the ordered field axioms, $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} > 0$. Applying the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum to $\alpha = \sup A$ and $\beta = \sup B$ with tolerance $\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$, there exist elements $a_\varepsilon \in A$ and $b_\varepsilon \in B$ such that
$$
a_\varepsilon > \alpha - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \quad \text{and} \quad b_\varepsilon > \beta - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}.
$$
Adding these two strict inequalities gives an element $a_\varepsilon + b_\varepsilon \in A + B$ satisfying
$$
\sup(A + B) \ge a_\varepsilon + b_\varepsilon > \left(\alpha - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) + \left(\beta - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) = (\alpha + \beta) - \varepsilon.
$$
Notice what we have shown: for every $\varepsilon > 0$, $\sup(A + B) > (\alpha + \beta) - \varepsilon$, or equivalently $\alpha + \beta < \sup(A + B) + \varepsilon$. Since $\varepsilon > 0$ is arbitrary, we must have $\alpha + \beta \le \sup(A + B)$ (for if $\alpha + \beta > \sup(A + B)$, setting $\varepsilon := (\alpha + \beta) - \sup(A + B) > 0$ would yield the contradiction $\alpha + \beta < \alpha + \beta$). Combining both inequalities gives $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$.

The identity $\inf(A + B) = \inf A + \inf B$ can be proved either by the exact mirror-image $\varepsilon/2$ argument (showing $\inf A + \inf B \le a + b$ for all $a \in A, b \in B$, and finding $a_\varepsilon \in A, b_\varepsilon \in B$ with $a_\varepsilon + b_\varepsilon < \inf A + \inf B + \varepsilon$), or immediately deduced from the reflection duality in Part (3) once Part (3) is proved.

**Proof of (2):** Let $c \ge 0$. If $c = 0$, then since $A \neq \emptyset$, we have $0A = \{0\}$, so $\sup(0A) = 0 = 0 \cdot \sup A$ and $\inf(0A) = 0 = 0 \cdot \inf A$.
Now suppose $c > 0$. For every $a \in A$, we have $\inf A \le a \le \sup A$. Multiplying by the positive real $c > 0$ preserves order:
$$
c \inf A \le ca \le c \sup A \quad \text{for all } a \in A.
$$
Thus $c \sup A$ is an upper bound for $cA$, which implies $\sup(cA) \le c \sup A$. Conversely, because $\sup(cA)$ is an upper bound for $cA$, we have $ca \le \sup(cA)$ for every $a \in A$. Multiplying both sides by $c^{-1} > 0$ yields
$$
a \le \frac{1}{c} \sup(cA) \quad \text{for all } a \in A.
$$
This means $\frac{1}{c} \sup(cA)$ is an upper bound for $A$, so $\sup A \le \frac{1}{c} \sup(cA)$, or $c \sup A \le \sup(cA)$. Combining the two inequalities gives $\sup(cA) = c \sup A$. By the identical argument applied to lower bounds, $c \inf A \le ca$ implies $c \inf A \le \inf(cA)$, while $\inf(cA) \le ca \implies \frac{1}{c}\inf(cA) \le a \implies \frac{1}{c}\inf(cA) \le \inf A \implies \inf(cA) \le c \inf A$, proving $\inf(cA) = c \inf A$.

**Proof of (3):** First consider the reflection $c = -1$. An element $x \in -A$ has the form $x = -a$ for some $a \in A$. Since $\inf A \le a \le \sup A$ for all $a \in A$, multiplying by $-1$ reverses the inequalities:
$$
-\sup A \le -a \le -\inf A \quad \text{for all } a \in A.
$$
This shows that $-\inf A$ is an upper bound for $-A$, so $\sup(-A) \le -\inf A$. Conversely, since $\sup(-A)$ is an upper bound for $-A$, every $a \in A$ satisfies $-a \le \sup(-A)$, which upon multiplying by $-1$ becomes
$$
-\sup(-A) \le a \quad \text{for all } a \in A.
$$
Thus $-\sup(-A)$ is a lower bound for $A$. Because $\inf A$ is the *greatest* lower bound of $A$, we have $-\sup(-A) \le \inf A$, and multiplying by $-1$ yields $-\inf A \le \sup(-A)$. Therefore, $\sup(-A) = -\inf A$.
To obtain $\inf(-A) = -\sup A$, we simply apply the identity just proved to the set $-A$ in place of $A$ (noting that $-(-A) = A$):
$$
\sup A = \sup(-(-A)) = -\inf(-A) \implies \inf(-A) = -\sup A.
$$
Finally, for any $c < 0$, write $c = (-c)(-1)$ where $-c = |c| > 0$. Since $cA = |c|(-A)$, combining Part (2) with the reflection laws gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\sup(cA) &= \sup(|c|(-A)) = |c| \sup(-A) = (-c)(-\inf A) = c \inf A, \\
\inf(cA) &= \inf(|c|(-A)) = |c| \inf(-A) = (-c)(-\sup A) = c \sup A.
\end{aligned}
$$
Lastly, since $A - B = A + (-B)$, combining (1) and (3) gives $\sup(A - B) = \sup A + \sup(-B) = \sup A - \inf B$ and $\inf(A - B) = \inf A + \inf(-B) = \inf A - \sup B$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Intuition for $\sup(A - B) = \sup A - \inf B$
> Why does the supremum of a difference $A - B$ involve subtracting the *infimum* of $B$ rather than the supremum of $B$? Think of it as an optimization problem: to make the difference $a - b$ as *large* as possible when $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ can be chosen independently, you want to make the positive contribution $a \in A$ as *large* as possible (approaching $\sup A$) while making the subtracted penalty $b \in B$ as *small* as possible (approaching $\inf B$).

### Supremum and Infimum of Real-Valued Functions

In analysis, we most often take suprema and infima not of abstract subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, but of the values attained by a real-valued function $f: D \to \mathbb{R}$ as its input $x$ varies over a non-empty domain $D$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Bounded Functions and Extremal Values on a Domain
> Let $D$ be a non-empty set and let $f: D \to \mathbb{R}$ be a function with range (image) $f(D) := \{f(x) : x \in D\} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$.
> 1. We say that $f$ is **bounded above on $D$** if its range $f(D)$ is a bounded-above subset of $\mathbb{R}$, i.e., there exists $M \in \mathbb{R}$ such that $f(x) \le M$ for all $x \in D$. In this case, we define the **supremum of $f$ on $D$** to be
>    $$
>    \sup_{x \in D} f(x) := \sup f(D) = \sup \{f(x) : x \in D\}.
>    $$
> 2. We say that $f$ is **bounded below on $D$** if $f(D)$ is bounded below in $\mathbb{R}$, i.e., there exists $m \in \mathbb{R}$ such that $m \le f(x)$ for all $x \in D$. In this case, we define the **infimum of $f$ on $D$** to be
>    $$
>    \inf_{x \in D} f(x) := \inf f(D) = \inf \{f(x) : x \in D\}.
>    $$
> 3. We say that $f$ is **bounded on $D$** if it is both bounded above and bounded below on $D$; equivalently, there exists $K \ge 0$ such that $|f(x)| \le K$ for all $x \in D$.

Given two bounded functions $f, g: D \to \mathbb{R}$ on the same non-empty domain $D$, we can form their pointwise sum $(f + g): D \to \mathbb{R}$ defined by $(f + g)(x) := f(x) + g(x)$. A tempting mistake for beginners is to assume that $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x))$ equals $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ by analogy with $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$. Why does equality fail for functions in general, and what is the exact chain of inequalities connecting the extrema of $f$, $g$, and $f + g$?

> [!theorem] Theorem: Fundamental Chain of Inequalities for Pointwise Sums of Functions
> Let $D$ be a non-empty set and let $f, g: D \to \mathbb{R}$ be bounded functions on $D$. Then their pointwise sum $f + g$ is bounded on $D$, and its infimum and supremum satisfy the complete chain of inequalities:
> $$
> \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x) \le \sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x).
> $$
> By symmetry (interchanging the roles of $f$ and $g$), the middle term $\inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ can also be replaced by $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x)$.

**Proof:**
We establish the four inequalities in the chain from left to right.

1. **First inequality ($\inf_D f + \inf_D g \le \inf_D (f + g)$):** For every $x \in D$, by definition of infimum we have $\inf_{t \in D} f(t) \le f(x)$ and $\inf_{t \in D} g(t) \le g(x)$. Adding these two inequalities yields
   $$
   \inf_{t \in D} f(t) + \inf_{t \in D} g(t) \le f(x) + g(x) \quad \text{for all } x \in D.
   $$
   This shows that the real number $\inf_{t \in D} f(t) + \inf_{t \in D} g(t)$ is a lower bound for the set $\{(f + g)(x) : x \in D\}$. Since $\inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x))$ is the *greatest* lower bound of that set, we obtain
   $$
   \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)).
   $$

2. **Second inequality ($\inf_D (f + g) \le \inf_D f + \sup_D g$):** For every $x \in D$, we have $\inf_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t)) \le f(x) + g(x)$ and $g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} g(t)$. Therefore, for every $x \in D$,
   $$
   \inf_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t)) \le f(x) + g(x) \le f(x) + \sup_{t \in D} g(t).
   $$
   Subtracting the constant $\sup_{t \in D} g(t)$ from both ends gives
   $$
   \inf_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t)) - \sup_{t \in D} g(t) \le f(x) \quad \text{for all } x \in D.
   $$
   That is, the constant $\inf_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t)) - \sup_{t \in D} g(t)$ is a lower bound for $f(D)$. Because $\inf_{x \in D} f(x)$ is the greatest lower bound of $f(D)$, we deduce that
   $$
   \inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) - \sup_{x \in D} g(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} f(x),
   $$
   which rearranges immediately to $\inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$.

3. **Third inequality ($\inf_D f + \sup_D g \le \sup_D (f + g)$):** For every $x \in D$, we have $\inf_{t \in D} f(t) \le f(x)$ and $f(x) + g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t))$. Combining these gives
   $$
   \inf_{t \in D} f(t) + g(x) \le f(x) + g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t)) \quad \text{for all } x \in D.
   $$
   Subtracting $\inf_{t \in D} f(t)$ from both sides shows that for every $x \in D$,
   $$
   g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} (f(t) + g(t)) - \inf_{t \in D} f(t).
   $$
   Thus the right-hand side is an upper bound for $g(D)$. Since $\sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ is the least upper bound of $g(D)$, we have $\sup_{x \in D} g(x) \le \sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) - \inf_{x \in D} f(x)$, which rearranges to
   $$
   \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x) \le \sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)).
   $$

4. **Fourth inequality ($\sup_D (f + g) \le \sup_D f + \sup_D g$):** Finally, for every $x \in D$, we have $f(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} f(t)$ and $g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} g(t)$, so adding them gives
   $$
   f(x) + g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} f(t) + \sup_{t \in D} g(t) \quad \text{for all } x \in D.
   $$
   Hence $\sup_{t \in D} f(t) + \sup_{t \in D} g(t)$ is an upper bound for $(f + g)(D)$, and since $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x))$ is the least upper bound of $(f + g)(D)$, we conclude that $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Caution: Why $\sup_D (f + g)$ Can Be Strictly Less Than $\sup_D f + \sup_D g$
> Why did $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$ hold with equality for sets, whereas $\sup_{x \in D}(f(x) + g(x)) \le \sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ is only an inequality for functions?
>
> Look at the sets involved:
> $$
> (f + g)(D) = \{f(x) + g(x) : x \in D\} \subseteq \{f(x) + g(y) : x \in D, \, y \in D\} = f(D) + g(D).
> $$
> In the Minkowski sum $f(D) + g(D)$, we are allowed to choose $x \in D$ to make $f(x)$ near its peak $\sup_D f$ and **independently** choose a potentially different point $y \in D$ to make $g(y)$ near its peak $\sup_D g$. In contrast, in the pointwise sum $(f + g)(D)$, both functions are evaluated at the **exact same input $x \in D$**! If $f$ attains its peak where $g$ is small, and $g$ attains its peak where $f$ is small, then $f(x) + g(x)$ can never simultaneously realize both peaks.
>
> For a concrete illustration, take $D = \{0, 1\}$ (or $D = [0, 1]$), let $f(x) = x$, and let $g(x) = 1 - x$. Then $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) = 1$ (attained at $x = 1$) and $\sup_{x \in D} g(x) = 1$ (attained at $x = 0$), so $\sup_D f + \sup_D g = 1 + 1 = 2$. Yet for every $x \in D$, $f(x) + g(x) = x + (1 - x) = 1$, so $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) = 1 < 2$! Notice that the monotonicity of $\sup$ under subset inclusion immediately explains the inequality: since $(f + g)(D) \subseteq f(D) + g(D)$, we have $\sup (f + g)(D) \le \sup(f(D) + g(D)) = \sup f(D) + \sup g(D)$.

## The Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$ and Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$

In Section 0.4, we saw that the rational field $\mathbb{Q}$ fails the Least Upper Bound Property. Now that we have passed to the complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$, what does the Least Upper Bound Property tell us about how the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$ and integers $\mathbb{Z}$ sit inside $\mathbb{R}$?

Intuitively, we picture the natural numbers $0, 1, 2, 3, \dots$ marching off arbitrarily far to the right along the real line, so that no real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ can ever stand to the right of *all* natural numbers. It is a remarkable fact of axiomatic analysis that this geometric intuition—known as the **Archimedean Property**—is not an independent axiom of $\mathbb{R}$, but an automatic consequence of the **Least Upper Bound Property**!

### The Archimedean Property and Its Equivalent Formulations

Let us prove the Archimedean Property purely from the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$
> The subset of natural numbers $\mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is **not bounded above** in $\mathbb{R}$. That is, for every real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists a natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $n > x$.

**Proof:**
Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that $\mathbb{N}$ were bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$. Since $\mathbb{N}$ is non-empty ($0 \in \mathbb{N}$ and $1 \in \mathbb{N}$) and bounded above, the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ guarantees the existence of a least upper bound
$$
\alpha := \sup \mathbb{N} \in \mathbb{R}.
$$
Because $1 > 0$ in the ordered field $\mathbb{R}$, we have $\alpha - 1 < \alpha$. Since $\alpha$ is the *least* upper bound of $\mathbb{N}$, the strictly smaller real number $\alpha - 1$ cannot be an upper bound for $\mathbb{N}$. Therefore, there must exist at least one natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
$$
n > \alpha - 1.
$$
Adding $1$ to both sides of this inequality using the order compatibility of addition in $\mathbb{R}$ gives
$$
n + 1 > \alpha.
$$
However, by the inductive closure of the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$ under the successor operation ($n \in \mathbb{N} \implies n + 1 \in \mathbb{N}$), the number $n + 1$ is itself an element of $\mathbb{N}$! Thus we have found an element $n + 1 \in \mathbb{N}$ that is strictly greater than $\alpha = \sup \mathbb{N}$, contradicting the fact that $\alpha$ is an upper bound for $\mathbb{N}$. Therefore, $\mathbb{N}$ is not bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Why the Proof Needs Completeness
> Look at how effortlessly the proof above worked: if $\mathbb{N}$ had *any* upper bound $x \in \mathbb{R}$, completeness would force $\mathbb{N}$ to have a *tightest* upper bound $\alpha = \sup \mathbb{N}$, and stepping back by $1$ from $\alpha$ leaves a gap too narrow to prevent $n + 1$ from jumping past $\alpha$. In an ordered field that lacks the Least Upper Bound Property, it is entirely possible to have "infinitely large" elements $x$ satisfying $n < x$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ (such ordered fields are called *non-Archimedean* ordered fields), because in such a field the set $\mathbb{N}$ is bounded above by $x$ without possessing a least upper bound $\alpha$! Completeness rules out such infinite elements completely.

In practice, the Archimedean Property is invoked in several equivalent forms: stepping past a large target $y$ using integer multiples of a tiny step $x > 0$, squeezing $\frac{1}{n}$ below a tiny tolerance $\varepsilon > 0$, or trapping any real number $x$ between two consecutive integers $m \le x < m + 1$. Let us prove all of these equivalent formulations rigorously.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Equivalent Formulations of the Archimedean Property and the Floor Function
> In the complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$, the following statements hold and are consequences of the Archimedean Property:
> 1. **Arbitrary Step Multiples:** For every pair of real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x > 0$, there exists a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that
>    $$
>    nx > y.
>    $$
> 2. **Reciprocal Squeezing and Infimum of $\{1/n\}$:** For every real number $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that
>    $$
>    0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon.
>    $$
>    Consequently, the set $S := \left\{\frac{1}{n} : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\right\}$ satisfies
>    $$
>    \inf \left\{\frac{1}{n} : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\right\} = 0.
>    $$
> 3. **Existence and Uniqueness of the Floor Function $\lfloor x \rfloor$:** For every real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists a **unique** integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that
>    $$
>    m \le x < m + 1.
>    $$
>    This unique integer $m$ is denoted $\lfloor x \rfloor$ and is called the **floor** (or **greatest integer part**) of $x$.

**Proof:**
1. Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x > 0$. Applying the Archimedean Property to the real number $\frac{y}{x} \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists a natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $k > \frac{y}{x}$. To ensure our integer is strictly positive ($n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ even if $y < 0$ and $k = 0$ happened to work), set $n := k + 1 \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. Then $n > k > \frac{y}{x}$, and multiplying both sides by $x > 0$ yields $nx > y$.

2. Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be given. Applying Part (1) with $x = \varepsilon > 0$ and $y = 1$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $n\varepsilon > 1$. Since $n \ge 1 > 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, multiplying the inequality $1 < n\varepsilon$ by $n^{-1} > 0$ gives $0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon$.
   To see that $\inf S = 0$ for $S = \{1/n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}$, observe first that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, we have $n > 0 \implies \frac{1}{n} > 0$, so $0$ is a lower bound for $S$. Second, for any $\varepsilon > 0$, we have just shown that there exists $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ with $\frac{1}{n} < 0 + \varepsilon$. By the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the infimum, $0 = \inf S$.

3. Let $x \in \mathbb{R}$ be arbitrary. We first prove the **uniqueness** of an integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $m \le x < m + 1$. Suppose $m_1, m_2 \in \mathbb{Z}$ both satisfy
   $$
   m_1 \le x < m_1 + 1 \quad \text{and} \quad m_2 \le x < m_2 + 1.
   $$
   Then $m_1 \le x < m_2 + 1$, so $m_1 < m_2 + 1$, and symmetrically $m_2 \le x < m_1 + 1$, so $m_2 < m_1 + 1$. Equivalently,
   $$
   m_2 - 1 < m_1 < m_2 + 1 \implies -1 < m_1 - m_2 < 1.
   $$
   However, as proved in Section 0.3 on the discrete order structure of $\mathbb{Z}$, there is no integer strictly between $0$ and $1$ (nor between $-1$ and $0$). Since $m_1 - m_2 \in \mathbb{Z}$, the only integer satisfying $-1 < m_1 - m_2 < 1$ is $m_1 - m_2 = 0$, so $m_1 = m_2$.

   Next, we prove the **existence** of $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ using the Archimedean Property and the **Bounded Well-Ordering Principle for $\mathbb{Z}$** established in Section 0.3. Consider the subset of integers strictly exceeding $x$:
   $$
   E := \{k \in \mathbb{Z} : k > x\} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}.
   $$
   First, by the Archimedean Property applied to $x$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ such that $n > x$. Thus $n \in E$, which proves that $E$ is **non-empty**.
   Second, by the Archimedean Property applied to $-x$, there exists a natural number $p \in \mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ such that $p > -x$, which upon multiplying by $-1$ becomes $-p < x$. For every $k \in E$, we have $k > x > -p$, so the integer $-p \in \mathbb{Z}$ is a **lower bound** in $\mathbb{Z}$ for $E$.
   Because $E$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{Z}$ that is bounded below in $\mathbb{Z}$, the Bounded Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{Z}$ guarantees that $E$ possesses a **minimum** (least) element $k_0 := \min E \in E$.
   Let us define $m := k_0 - 1 \in \mathbb{Z}$. Since $k_0 \in E$, we have $k_0 > x$, which is to say
   $$
   x < m + 1.
   $$
   On the other hand, because $m = k_0 - 1 < k_0$ and $k_0$ is the *least* element of $E$, the integer $m$ cannot belong to $E$. By definition of $E = \{k \in \mathbb{Z} : k > x\}$, saying $m \notin E$ (combined with trichotomy in $\mathbb{R}$) means precisely that
   $$
   m \le x.
   $$
   Combining both inequalities gives $m \le x < m + 1$, completing the proof of existence and uniqueness. $\blacksquare$

> [!abstract] Definition: The Floor Function $\lfloor x \rfloor$ and Fractional Part $\{x\}$
> For every $x \in \mathbb{R}$:
> 1. The **floor** of $x$, denoted $\lfloor x \rfloor \in \mathbb{Z}$, is the unique integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $m \le x < m + 1$; equivalently, $\lfloor x \rfloor = \max \{m \in \mathbb{Z} : m \le x\}$.
> 2. The **fractional part** of $x$, denoted $\{x\}$ (or $\operatorname{frac}(x)$), is defined by
>    $$
>    \{x\} := x - \lfloor x \rfloor \in [0, 1).
>    $$

### Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$

Even though the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ are full of "pinholes" (missing suprema such as $\sqrt{2}$), how finely are the rationals distributed inside $\mathbb{R}$?
