Using the Archimedean Property and the floor function $\lfloor \cdot \rfloor$, we can now prove that between any two distinct real numbers—no matter how close together they are—there lies at least one rational number (and in fact infinitely many).

> [!abstract] Definition: Dense Subset of $\mathbb{R}$
> A subset $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is said to be **dense in $\mathbb{R}$** if for every pair of real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exists an element $d \in D$ such that
> $$
> x < d < y.
> $$

Before reading the formal proof that $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$, let us visualize the geometric mechanism behind it. We are given an open interval $(x, y)$ of length $y - x > 0$, and we want to land a fraction $\frac{m}{n}$ strictly inside $(x, y)$, meaning $x < \frac{m}{n} < y$. Clearing the denominator $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, this is equivalent to landing an integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ strictly inside the dilated interval $(nx, ny)$:
$$
nx < m < ny.
$$
How can we be 100% sure that the dilated interval $(nx, ny)$ contains at least one integer $m$? The distance between consecutive integers is $1$. If we choose the scaling factor $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ large enough that the length of the dilated interval, $ny - nx = n(y - x)$, is **strictly greater than $1$**, then any step of length $1$ cannot leap completely across $(nx, ny)$ without landing inside it! Specifically, if we take $m = \lfloor nx \rfloor + 1$—the very first integer strictly to the right of $nx$—it cannot overshoot $ny$ because $m \le nx + 1 < ny$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Density of the Rational Numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$
> The set of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$: for any two real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exists a rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that
> $$
> x < q < y.
> $$

**Proof:**
Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$. Then $y - x > 0$. By the Archimedean Property (in the form of arbitrary step multiples with step $y - x > 0$ and target $1$), there exists a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that
$$
n(y - x) > 1,
$$
which rearranges to
$$
nx + 1 < ny.
$$
Now let $m := \lfloor nx \rfloor + 1 \in \mathbb{Z}$. By the defining property of the floor function applied to the real number $nx$, the integer $\lfloor nx \rfloor$ satisfies
$$
\lfloor nx \rfloor \le nx < \lfloor nx \rfloor + 1 = m.
$$
From the first inequality $\lfloor nx \rfloor \le nx$, adding $1$ to both sides gives $m = \lfloor nx \rfloor + 1 \le nx + 1$. Combining this with $nx < m$ and $nx + 1 < ny$ yields the chain of inequalities
$$
nx < m \le nx + 1 < ny,
$$
and hence
$$
nx < m < ny.
$$
Finally, dividing this strict inequality through by the positive integer $n > 0$ produces the rational number $q := \frac{m}{n} \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying
$$
x < \frac{m}{n} < y.
$$
Thus $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ lies strictly between $x$ and $y$. $\blacksquare$

What about the **irrational numbers** $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$? Once we know that at least one non-zero irrational number $\xi \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ exists (for instance, the positive real number $\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$, whose existence in $\mathbb{R}$ we prove rigorously in the very next subsection, and whose irrationality was already proved in Section 0.4 via $t^2 = 2 \implies t \notin \mathbb{Q}$), we can piggyback on the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ to show that the irrationals $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ are *also* dense in $\mathbb{R}$!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Density of the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$
> Let $\xi \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ be any positive irrational real number (such as $\xi = \sqrt{2}$). Then for any two real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exists an irrational number $z \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ (in fact, of the form $z = q\xi$ with $q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \{0\}$, or of the form $z = q + \xi$ with $q \in \mathbb{Q}$) such that
> $$
> x < z < y.
> $$

**Proof:**
We give two equally instructive proofs—one via additive translation by $\xi$, and one via multiplicative scaling by $\xi > 0$.

**Method 1 (Additive Translation):** Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, and let $\xi \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ be an irrational number. Since $x < y$, subtracting $\xi$ from both sides preserves the strict inequality:
$$
x - \xi < y - \xi.
$$
By the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ applied to the two real numbers $x - \xi$ and $y - \xi$, there exists a rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that
$$
x - \xi < q < y - \xi.
$$
Adding $\xi$ across this inequality yields the real number $z := q + \xi \in \mathbb{R}$ satisfying
$$
x < q + \xi < y.
$$
We claim that $z = q + \xi$ is irrational ($z \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$). Indeed, if $z \in \mathbb{Q}$, then since $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field (and thus closed under subtraction), we would have $\xi = z - q \in \mathbb{Q}$, contradicting the fact that $\xi \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$. Therefore, $z \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ and $x < z < y$.

**Method 2 (Multiplicative Scaling):** Alternatively, since $\xi > 0$, multiplying $x < y$ by $\xi^{-1} > 0$ gives $\frac{x}{\xi} < \frac{y}{\xi}$. By the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$, we can choose a rational number $q_1 \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $\frac{x}{\xi} < q_1 < \frac{y}{\xi}$, and then a second rational number $q_2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q_1 < q_2 < \frac{y}{\xi}$, ensuring that at least one of $q_1, q_2$ is non-zero; let $q \in \{q_1, q_2\} \setminus \{0\}$ be that non-zero rational number. Multiplying $\frac{x}{\xi} < q < \frac{y}{\xi}$ by $\xi > 0$ gives $x < q\xi < y$, and $z := q\xi$ is irrational because if $z \in \mathbb{Q}$, then $q \neq 0 \implies \xi = z q^{-1} \in \mathbb{Q}$, a contradiction. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Iterated Density Gives Infinitely Many Rationals and Irrationals in $(x, y)$
> Notice that once we find a single rational $q_1 \in (x, y)$, we can apply the density theorem again to the subinterval $(x, q_1)$ to find $q_2 \in (x, q_1) \cap \mathbb{Q}$, and then to $(x, q_2)$ to find $q_3 \in (x, q_2) \cap \mathbb{Q}$, and so on by induction. Thus every non-degenerate interval $(x, y)$ with $x < y$ contains **infinitely many** rational numbers and **infinitely many** irrational numbers!

## Existence of $n$-th Roots, Rational Exponents, and Cantor's Nested Interval Property

In Section 0.4, we motivated the incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$ by proving that the equation $t^2 = 2$ has no solution in $\mathbb{Q}$, because the bounded-above set $\{t \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : t^2 < 2\}$ lacks a least upper bound in $\mathbb{Q}$. Now that we are working in the complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$, does $\sup \{t \in \mathbb{R}_{>0} : t^2 < 2\}$ actually square to $2$? More generally, for every positive real number $x > 0$ and every integer $n \ge 1$, does there exist a unique positive real number $y > 0$ such that $y^n = x$?

> [!warning] Caution: Avoiding Circularity (No Continuity or IVT Allowed!)
> In an introductory calculus course, one often waves hands and says: *"The function $f(t) = t^n$ is continuous on $[0, \infty)$, starts at $0^n = 0 < x$, and grows large for large $t$, so by the Intermediate Value Theorem there exists $y > 0$ with $y^n = x$."*
>
> In a rigorous foundational treatment, invoking the Intermediate Value Theorem here would be circular or premature: we have not yet defined limits, continuity, or topological connectedness! Worse yet, how does one prove the Intermediate Value Theorem later in the course? By taking the supremum of a bounded set! Instead of waiting several chapters for continuity, we can prove the existence of $n$-th roots **right now**, directly from the **Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$** and elementary polynomial algebra (following Walter Rudin's classic argument).

### Existence and Uniqueness of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$

Before proving the main theorem, let us establish a simple algebraic lemma that gives us tight linear control over how fast the difference $b^n - a^n$ can grow when $b$ is slightly larger than $a$.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Algebraic Factorization and Upper Bound for $b^n - a^n$
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$ and let $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ be a positive integer.
> 1. **Cyclotomic Factorization Identity:**
>    $$
>    b^n - a^n = (b - a)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1} b^{n-1-k} a^k = (b - a)\left(b^{n-1} + b^{n-2}a + \dots + b a^{n-2} + a^{n-1}\right).
>    $$
> 2. **Strict Monotonicity and Linear Perturbation Bound:** If $0 < a < b$, then every term $b^{n-1-k}a^k$ in the sum satisfies $0 < a^{n-1} \le b^{n-1-k}a^k \le b^{n-1}$, and summing over all $n$ terms yields the two-sided estimate
>    $$
>    n(b - a)a^{n-1} \le b^n - a^n \le n(b - a)b^{n-1}.
>    $$
>    In particular, for $a, b > 0$, we have $a < b \iff a^n < b^n$, and $a = b \iff a^n = b^n$.

**Proof:**
1. Using the distributive law and index shifting in $\mathbb{R}$, we expand the right-hand side as a telescoping sum:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (b - a)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1} b^{n-1-k}a^k &= \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} b^{n-k}a^k - \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} b^{n-1-k}a^{k+1} \\
   &= \left(b^n + \sum_{k=1}^{n-1} b^{n-k}a^k\right) - \left(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1} b^{n-j}a^j + a^n\right) = b^n - a^n.
   \end{aligned}
   $$
2. Suppose $0 < a < b$. As proved by induction from the ordered field axioms in Section 0.4, for each $k \in \{0, 1, \dots, n-1\}$ we have $a^k \le b^k$ and $a^{n-1-k} \le b^{n-1-k}$ with positive factors, so multiplying gives
   $$
   a^{n-1} = a^{n-1-k}a^k \le b^{n-1-k}a^k \le b^{n-1-k}b^k = b^{n-1}.
   $$
   Summing these $n$ inequalities from $k = 0$ to $k = n - 1$ gives
   $$
   n a^{n-1} \le \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} b^{n-1-k}a^k \le n b^{n-1}.
   $$
   Since $b - a > 0$, multiplying through by $(b - a)$ and using the factorization in Part (1) yields $n(b - a)a^{n-1} \le b^n - a^n \le n(b - a)b^{n-1}$. Because $n(b - a)a^{n-1} > 0$, this immediately shows that $0 < a < b \implies a^n < b^n$. By trichotomy on $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, it follows that $a < b \iff a^n < b^n$ and $a = b \iff a^n = b^n$. $\blacksquare$

Why is the upper estimate $b^n - a^n \le n(b - a)b^{n-1}$ so powerful? Suppose we want to nudge $y > 0$ upward to $y + h$ (so $a = y$ and $b = y + h$ with $0 < h < 1$), and we want to keep the increase $(y + h)^n - y^n$ small. The inequality tells us that
$$
(y + h)^n - y^n \le n h (y + h)^{n-1} < n h (y + 1)^{n-1}.
$$
Notice that on the right-hand side, the perturbation $h$ appears **linearly** multiplied by the fixed constant $n(y + 1)^{n-1}$! That makes it trivial to solve for how small $h > 0$ needs to be to keep $(y + h)^n - y^n$ below any desired gap $x - y^n > 0$. Similarly, if we nudge $y$ downward to $y - k$ (so $a = y - k$ and $b = y$ with $0 < k < y$), the same inequality gives
$$
y^n - (y - k)^n \le n k y^{n-1},
$$
which again is linear in $k$! With this estimate in hand, we are ready to prove the existence and uniqueness of $n$-th roots in $\mathbb{R}$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Existence and Uniqueness of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$
> For every real number $x > 0$ and every positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, there exists a **unique** positive real number $y > 0$ such that
> $$
> y^n = x.
> $$
> This unique real number $y$ is denoted by $x^{1/n}$ or $\sqrt[n]{x}$, and is called the **positive $n$-th root** of $x$.

**Proof:**
Let $x > 0$ and $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ be given. If $n = 1$, then $y = x > 0$ trivially satisfies $y^1 = x$ and is unique, so we may assume $n \ge 1$ generally.

**Step 1 (Uniqueness):** Suppose $y_1, y_2 \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ both satisfy $y_1^n = x$ and $y_2^n = x$. By the Lemma above, if $0 < y_1 < y_2$ then $y_1^n < y_2^n$, and if $0 < y_2 < y_1$ then $y_2^n < y_1^n$. By trichotomy, $y_1^n = y_2^n$ forces $y_1 = y_2$. Thus at most one positive real number $y$ can satisfy $y^n = x$.

**Step 2 (Construction of the Candidate Supremum):** Define the subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ of positive real numbers whose $n$-th power is strictly less than $x$:
$$
E := \{t \in \mathbb{R}_{>0} : t^n < x\}.
$$
To apply the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ to $E$, we must verify that $E$ is **non-empty** and **bounded above**:
- **$E$ is non-empty:** Consider the real number
  $$
  t_0 := \frac{x}{1 + x}.
  $$
  Since $x > 0$, we have $1 + x > x > 0$ and $1 + x > 1$, so dividing by $1 + x > 0$ gives
  $$
  0 < t_0 < 1 \quad \text{and} \quad t_0 = \frac{x}{1 + x} < \frac{x}{1} = x.
  $$
  Because $0 < t_0 < 1$, induction on $n \ge 1$ shows that $t_0^n \le t_0$ (since multiplying $t_0^k \le t_0$ by $t_0 \in (0, 1)$ gives $t_0^{k+1} \le t_0^2 < t_0$). Therefore,
  $$
  t_0^n \le t_0 < x,
  $$
  which proves that $t_0 \in E$, so $E \neq \emptyset$.
- **$E$ is bounded above:** Consider the real number
  $$
  M := 1 + x.
  $$
  Since $x > 0$, we have $M = 1 + x > 1$ and $M > x$. For any $t > M = 1 + x$, we have $t > 1$, so induction on $n \ge 1$ gives $t^n \ge t$. Consequently, for every $t > 1 + x$,
  $$
  t^n \ge t > 1 + x > x,
  $$
  which means $t \notin E$. Taking the contrapositive, every element $t \in E$ must satisfy $t \le 1 + x$. Thus $M = 1 + x$ is an upper bound for $E$.

Since $E$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{R}$ that is bounded above, the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ guarantees the existence of
$$
y := \sup E \in \mathbb{R}.
$$
Moreover, since $t_0 \in E$ and $t_0 > 0$, we have $y = \sup E \ge t_0 > 0$, so $y \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$.

**Step 3 (Eliminating $y^n < x$ and $y^n > x$ by Trichotomy):** By trichotomy in $\mathbb{R}$, exactly one of the three relations $y^n < x$, $y^n > x$, or $y^n = x$ holds. We show that both $y^n < x$ and $y^n > x$ lead to contradictions.

- **Case 3a (Suppose $y^n < x$):** Then the gap $x - y^n$ is strictly positive ($x - y^n > 0$). We will construct a small positive step $h \in (0, 1)$ such that $(y + h)^n < x$, which will put $y + h \in E$ and contradict $y = \sup E$.
  For any $h \in (0, 1)$, applying the Lemma to $a = y > 0$ and $b = y + h > y$ gives
  $$
  (y + h)^n - y^n \le n h (y + h)^{n-1} < n h (y + 1)^{n-1},
  $$
  where the second inequality follows because $0 < y + h < y + 1 \implies (y + h)^{n-1} \le (y + 1)^{n-1}$. Therefore, to make $(y + h)^n - y^n < x - y^n$, it suffices to choose $h \in (0, 1)$ so that
  $$
  n h (y + 1)^{n-1} < x - y^n \iff h < \frac{x - y^n}{n(y + 1)^{n-1}}.
  $$
  Explicitly, define
  $$
  h := \frac{1}{2} \min \left\{1, \, \frac{x - y^n}{n(y + 1)^{n-1}}\right\}.
  $$
  Since $x - y^n > 0$ and $n(y + 1)^{n-1} > 0$, we have $0 < h < 1$ and $h < \frac{x - y^n}{n(y + 1)^{n-1}}$. Consequently,
  $$
  (y + h)^n - y^n \le n h (y + h)^{n-1} \le n h (y + 1)^{n-1} < x - y^n,
  $$
  which implies $(y + h)^n < x$. Since $y + h > y > 0$, this means $y + h \in E$. However, $h > 0 \implies y + h > y = \sup E$, contradicting the fact that $y$ is an upper bound for $E$! Thus $y^n < x$ is impossible.

- **Case 3b (Suppose $y^n > x$):** Then the gap $y^n - x$ is strictly positive ($y^n - x > 0$). We will construct a small positive step $k \in (0, y)$ such that $(y - k)^n > x$, which will make $y - k$ a strictly smaller upper bound for $E$ than $y = \sup E$, yielding a contradiction.
  For any $k \in (0, y)$, we have $0 < y - k < y$, so applying the Lemma to $a = y - k$ and $b = y$ gives
  $$
  y^n - (y - k)^n \le n k y^{n-1}.
  $$
  Explicitly, define
  $$
  k := \frac{1}{2} \min \left\{y, \, \frac{y^n - x}{n y^{n-1}}\right\}.
  $$
  Since $y > 0$ and $y^n - x > 0$, we have $0 < k < y$ and $n k y^{n-1} < y^n - x$. Therefore,
  $$
  y^n - (y - k)^n \le n k y^{n-1} < y^n - x,
  $$
  which upon subtracting $y^n$ and multiplying by $-1$ yields
  $$
  (y - k)^n > x.
  $$
  Now let $t \in E$ be arbitrary. By definition of $E$, we have $t^n < x < (y - k)^n$. By the strict monotonicity of $n$-th powers on $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$ (from the Lemma, since $t > 0$ and $y - k > 0$), $t^n < (y - k)^n$ implies $t < y - k$. Since this holds for every $t \in E$, the number $y - k$ is an upper bound for $E$! Because $k > 0$, we have $y - k < y = \sup E$, contradicting the fact that $y$ is the *least* upper bound of $E$. Thus $y^n > x$ is also impossible.

By trichotomy, the only remaining possibility is $y^n = x$, completing the proof. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Extending $x^{1/n}$ to $x = 0$ and Odd $n$ for $x < 0$
> For $x = 0$ and any $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, $y = 0$ is the unique real number satisfying $y^n = 0$ (since $\mathbb{R}$ is an integral domain), so we set $0^{1/n} := 0$. Furthermore, if $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ is **odd** and $x < 0$, then $-x > 0$ has a unique positive $n$-th root $(-x)^{1/n} > 0$, and setting $y := -(-x)^{1/n} < 0$ gives $y^n = (-1)^n \left((-x)^{1/n}\right)^n = -(-x) = x$, so every real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ has a unique real $n$-th root when $n$ is odd. When $n$ is **even**, however, $y^n \ge 0$ for all $y \in \mathbb{R}$, so negative real numbers have no real $n$-th roots, while every $x > 0$ has exactly two real solutions to $t^n = x$, namely $t = x^{1/n} > 0$ and $t = -x^{1/n} < 0$. By universal convention, the radical symbol $\sqrt[n]{x} = x^{1/n}$ for $x > 0$ and even $n$ **always** denotes the unique *positive* root!

### Algebraic Laws of Roots and Rational Exponents

Because the positive $n$-th root $x^{1/n}$ is *uniquely* determined by the equation $(x^{1/n})^n = x$ and the requirement $x^{1/n} > 0$, proving algebraic identities for $n$-th roots requires zero new supremum arguments! To prove that a positive expression $Z > 0$ equals $W^{1/n}$, we simply raise $Z$ to the $n$-th power using the integer exponent laws of an ordered field and verify that $Z^n = W$; uniqueness of the positive $n$-th root immediately forces $Z = W^{1/n}$.

> [!theorem] Corollary: Algebraic Laws of Positive $n$-th Roots
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ be positive real numbers and let $m, n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ be positive integers. Then:
> 1. **Product Rule:** $(ab)^{1/n} = a^{1/n} b^{1/n}$.
> 2. **Quotient Rule:** $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{1/n} = \frac{a^{1/n}}{b^{1/n}}$, and in particular $(a^{-1})^{1/n} = (a^{1/n})^{-1}$.
> 3. **Iterated Root Rule:** $(a^{1/n})^{1/m} = a^{1/(mn)}$.
> 4. **Commutativity of Integer Power and Root:** For any integer $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, $(a^k)^{1/n} = (a^{1/n})^k$.
> 5. **Cancellation of Common Factors in Fractional Indices:** For any positive integer $p \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and integer $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, $(a^{1/(pn)})^{pk} = (a^{1/n})^k$.

**Proof:**
1. Set $u := a^{1/n} > 0$ and $v := b^{1/n} > 0$, so $u^n = a$ and $v^n = b$. Then $uv > 0$, and by the integer exponent laws in the field $\mathbb{R}$,
   $$
   (uv)^n = u^n v^n = ab.
   $$
   Since $(ab)^{1/n}$ is the unique positive real number whose $n$-th power equals $ab$, uniqueness gives $(ab)^{1/n} = uv = a^{1/n} b^{1/n}$.
2. Applying Part (1) to $a = \frac{a}{b} \cdot b$ gives $a^{1/n} = \left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{1/n} b^{1/n}$, and dividing by $b^{1/n} > 0$ yields $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{1/n} = \frac{a^{1/n}}{b^{1/n}}$.
3. Set $w := (a^{1/n})^{1/m} > 0$. By definition of $m$-th root, $w^m = a^{1/n}$. Raising both sides to the $n$-th power and using the integer exponent rule $(w^m)^n = w^{mn}$ yields
   $$
   w^{mn} = (w^m)^n = (a^{1/n})^n = a.
   $$
   Since $w > 0$, uniqueness of the positive $(mn)$-th root of $a$ implies $w = a^{1/(mn)}$.
4. Set $z := (a^{1/n})^k > 0$. Then using the integer exponent rules $(z^n = ((a^{1/n})^k)^n = ((a^{1/n})^n)^k)$, we find
   $$
   z^n = \left((a^{1/n})^n\right)^k = a^k.
   $$
   By uniqueness of the positive $n$-th root of $a^k$, we have $z = (a^k)^{1/n}$.
5. Using Part (3) and the integer exponent laws, we have $a^{1/n} = \left(a^{1/(pn)}\right)^p$. Raising both sides to the integer power $k \in \mathbb{Z}$ gives
   $$
   (a^{1/n})^k = \left(\left(a^{1/(pn)}\right)^p\right)^k = \left(a^{1/(pn)}\right)^{pk}. \quad \blacksquare
   $$

Part (5) of this Corollary allows us to define **rational powers** $x^q$ for any positive real base $x > 0$ and any rational exponent $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ unambiguously.

> [!abstract] Definition: Rational Exponents $x^q$ ($x > 0, q \in \mathbb{Q}$)
> Let $x \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ be a positive real number and let $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ be a rational number, written as $q = \frac{k}{n}$ with $k \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. We define the **rational power** $x^q \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ by
> $$
> x^q = x^{k/n} := \left(x^{1/n}\right)^k = \left(x^k\right)^{1/n}.
> $$

> [!theorem] Theorem: Well-Definedness and Exponent Laws for Rational Powers
> For every $x, y \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ and all rational numbers $p, q \in \mathbb{Q}$:
> 1. **Well-Definedness:** The value of $x^q$ does not depend on the choice of fraction representation $q = \frac{k}{n} = \frac{k'}{n'}$ ($k, k' \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $n, n' \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$).
> 2. **Laws of Rational Exponents:**
>    $$
>    x^{p+q} = x^p x^q, \qquad x^{-q} = \frac{1}{x^q}, \qquad (x^p)^q = x^{pq}, \qquad (xy)^q = x^q y^q.
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. Suppose $q = \frac{k}{n} = \frac{k'}{n'}$ with $k, k' \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $n, n' \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. Then $k n' = k' n$ in $\mathbb{Z}$. Applying Part (5) of the preceding Corollary twice—first with factor $n'$ and then with factor $n$—gives
   $$
   \left(x^{1/n}\right)^k = \left(x^{1/(n n')}\right)^{k n'} = \left(x^{1/(n n')}\right)^{k' n} = \left(x^{1/n'}\right)^{k'}.
   $$
   Thus $x^q$ is well-defined.
2. Given $p, q \in \mathbb{Q}$, choose a common positive denominator $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ so that $p = \frac{j}{n}$ and $q = \frac{k}{n}$ with $j, k \in \mathbb{Z}$. Then $p + q = \frac{j + k}{n}$, and setting $u := x^{1/n} > 0$, the integer exponent law $u^{j+k} = u^j u^k$ directly yields
   $$
   x^{p+q} = \left(x^{1/n}\right)^{j+k} = \left(x^{1/n}\right)^j \left(x^{1/n}\right)^k = x^p x^q.
   $$
   Setting $p = q$ and $q = -q$ gives $x^q x^{-q} = x^0 = 1$, so $x^{-q} = 1 / x^q$. Next, writing $p = \frac{j}{m}$ and $q = \frac{k}{n}$ with $m, n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, Parts (3) and (4) of the Corollary give
   $$
   (x^p)^q = \left(\left((x^j)^{1/m}\right)^k\right)^{1/n} = \left(\left(x^{jk}\right)^{1/m}\right)^{1/n} = \left(x^{jk}\right)^{1/(mn)} = x^{pq}.
   $$
   Finally, for $q = \frac{k}{n}$, Part (1) of the Corollary gives $(xy)^q = ((xy)^{1/n})^k = (x^{1/n} y^{1/n})^k = (x^{1/n})^k (y^{1/n})^k = x^q y^q$. $\blacksquare$

What about irrational exponents, such as $2^{\sqrt{2}}$? With rational exponents $a^q$ now rigorously defined, defining $a^x$ for arbitrary real $x \in \mathbb{R}$ (and $a > 1$) is achieved by taking the supremum $\sup \{a^q : q \in \mathbb{Q}, \, q \le x\}$—a construction you will carry out step-by-step in **Problem 0.6.5**!

### Cantor's Nested Interval Property

Let us now turn to a geometric manifestation of the completeness of $\mathbb{R}$ that will serve as a workhorse throughout topology and analysis (for example, in proving the Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem, the uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$, and the Heine–Borel theorem).

First, let us record the standard notation for intervals in $\mathbb{R}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Bounded Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$ with $a \le b$. We define:
> 1. The **closed bounded interval** $[a, b] := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : a \le x \le b\}$.
> 2. The **open bounded interval** $(a, b) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : a < x < b\}$ (which is empty if $a = b$).
> 3. The **half-open bounded intervals** $[a, b) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : a \le x < b\}$ and $(a, b] := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : a < x \le b\}$.
>
> The non-negative real number $\ell(I) := b - a \ge 0$ is called the **length** of the interval $I$.

Suppose we have an infinite sequence of closed bounded intervals $I_1, I_2, I_3, \dots$ such that each interval is contained inside the previous one:
$$
I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \cdots \supseteq I_n \supseteq I_{n+1} \supseteq \cdots
$$
where $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ with $a_n \le b_n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. What does the nesting condition $[a_n, b_n] \supseteq [a_{n+1}, b_{n+1}]$ mean in terms of the endpoints? Since $a_{n+1}$ and $b_{n+1}$ both belong to $[a_{n+1}, b_{n+1}] \subseteq [a_n, b_n]$, they must lie between $a_n$ and $b_n$, so
$$
a_n \le a_{n+1} \le b_{n+1} \le b_n \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}.
$$
Unrolling this chain shows that the left endpoints move monotonically to the right while the right endpoints move monotonically to the left, and **every left endpoint $a_m$ lies to the left of every right endpoint $b_k$**:
$$
a_1 \le a_2 \le \cdots \le a_n \le \cdots \le b_n \le \cdots \le b_2 \le b_1.
$$
Because the real line $\mathbb{R}$ has no gaps, these shrinking closed intervals cannot squeeze down onto an "empty pinhole": there must be at least one real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ trapped inside *all* of the intervals $I_n$ simultaneously! Moreover, if the lengths $b_n - a_n$ shrink down to $0$, then the intersection of all the intervals narrows down to a **single unique point**.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Cantor's Nested Interval Property (Nested Interval Theorem)
> Let $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ ($a_n \le b_n$) for $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ be a nested sequence of non-empty closed bounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$, meaning that
> $$
> I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \cdots \supseteq I_n \supseteq I_{n+1} \supseteq \cdots.
> $$
> Then the following statements hold:
> 1. **Non-Empty Closed Interval Intersection:** The supremum of the left endpoints and the infimum of the right endpoints,
>    $$
>    \eta := \sup \{a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} \quad \text{and} \quad \xi := \inf \{b_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\},
>    $$
>    both exist in $\mathbb{R}$, satisfy $\eta \le \xi$, and the intersection of the entire infinite family of intervals is the non-empty closed interval
>    $$
>    \bigcap_{n=1}^\infty I_n = \bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] = [\eta, \xi] \neq \emptyset.
>    $$
> 2. **Uniqueness under Vanishing Lengths:** If, in addition, the lengths of the intervals have infimum zero, i.e.,
>    $$
>    \inf \{b_n - a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0,
>    $$
>    then $\eta = \xi$, and the intersection consists of the single point $\eta$:
>    $$
>    \bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] = \{\eta\}.
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. Define the two non-empty sets of endpoints:
   $$
   A := \{a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} \quad \text{and} \quad B := \{b_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}.
   $$
   We first claim that **for every $m \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and every $k \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, we have $a_m \le b_k$**. To verify this for arbitrary indices $m$ and $k$ (which need not be equal!), let $N := \max\{m, k\} \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. Since $m \le N$ and $k \le N$, the nesting hypothesis $I_m \supseteq I_N$ and $I_k \supseteq I_N$ implies that $[a_N, b_N] \subseteq [a_m, b_m]$ and $[a_N, b_N] \subseteq [a_k, b_k]$. Therefore, $a_m \le a_N$ and $b_N \le b_k$. Since $I_N = [a_N, b_N]$ is non-empty ($a_N \le b_N$), we obtain the chain
   $$
   a_m \le a_N \le b_N \le b_k.
   $$
   Thus every element of $A$ is less than or equal to every element of $B$. By Part (1) of the **Comparison Criteria for Two Sets** proved at the beginning of this section, $A$ is bounded above, $B$ is bounded below, their extrema $\eta := \sup A \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\xi := \inf B \in \mathbb{R}$ exist, and they satisfy
   $$
   \eta = \sup A \le \inf B = \xi.
   $$
   In particular, since $\eta \le \xi$, the closed interval $[\eta, \xi]$ is non-empty (containing at least $\eta$ and $\xi$).
   Finally, let us show that $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] = [\eta, \xi]$. For any real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we have the chain of logical equivalences:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   x \in \bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] &\iff \forall n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \quad a_n \le x \le b_n \\
   &\iff (\forall n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \, a_n \le x) \quad \text{and} \quad (\forall n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}, \, x \le b_n) \\
   &\iff (x \text{ is an upper bound for } A) \quad \text{and} \quad (x \text{ is a lower bound for } B) \\
   &\iff \sup A \le x \quad \text{and} \quad x \le \inf B \\
   &\iff \eta \le x \le \xi \iff x \in [\eta, \xi].
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Therefore, $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] = [\eta, \xi] \neq \emptyset$.

2. Now suppose in addition that $\inf \{b_n - a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0$. From Part (1), we know that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, both $\eta$ and $\xi$ belong to $[a_n, b_n]$, so
   $$
   a_n \le \eta \le \xi \le b_n \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}.
   $$
   Subtracting the inequalities $a_n \le \eta$ and $\xi \le b_n$ gives
   $$
   0 \le \xi - \eta \le b_n - a_n \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}.
   $$
   This says that the non-negative real number $\xi - \eta$ is a **lower bound** for the set of interval lengths $\{b_n - a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}$. Since the greatest lower bound (infimum) of that set is $0$ by hypothesis, we must have
   $$
   0 \le \xi - \eta \le \inf \{b_n - a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0.
   $$
   By antisymmetry of $\le$, we conclude that $\xi - \eta = 0$, so $\eta = \xi$, and thus $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] = [\eta, \eta] = \{\eta\}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Caution: Every Single Hypothesis in Cantor's Nested Interval Property is Indispensable!
> If you drop even *one* of the hypotheses of Cantor's Nested Interval Property—that the intervals be **closed**, **bounded**, and lie in the **complete** field $\mathbb{R}$—the intersection $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty I_n$ can easily be empty:
> - **Open bounded intervals can have empty intersection:** For $I_n = \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right)$, we have $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq \cdots$, yet $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right) = \emptyset$ because any $x > 0$ fails $x < \frac{1}{n}$ for large $n$ by the Archimedean property, while $0 \notin \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right)$. (Here the candidate point $\eta = 0$ was excluded from the open intervals!)
> - **Closed unbounded intervals can have empty intersection:** For $I_n = [n, \infty) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x \ge n\}$, we have $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq \cdots$, yet $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [n, \infty) = \emptyset$ because $\mathbb{N}$ is not bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$.
> - **Closed bounded intervals in $\mathbb{Q}$ can have empty intersection:** If we choose rational endpoints $a_n < \sqrt{2} < b_n$ with $b_n - a_n \le \frac{1}{n}$, then inside $\mathbb{Q}$ the nested closed intervals $[a_n, b_n] \cap \mathbb{Q}$ squeeze down on the missing irrational $\sqrt{2} \notin \mathbb{Q}$, leaving $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty ([a_n, b_n] \cap \mathbb{Q}) = \emptyset$. You will explore all three of these counterexamples in detail in **Problem 0.6.4**.

## The Extended Real Number System ($\overline{\mathbb{R}}$)

Whenever we apply the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{R}$, we have to pause and verify two preconditions: that our subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is **non-empty** and **bounded above**. If $E$ is not bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$ (like $E = \mathbb{N}$), we have to say in words that "$\sup E$ does not exist in $\mathbb{R}$ because $E$ grows arbitrarily large." Wouldn't it be far cleaner if we had a unified notation that allowed us to write $\sup E = +\infty$ when $E$ is unbounded above, and $\inf E = -\infty$ when $E$ is unbounded below, so that *every* subset has a supremum and an infimum without case-by-case caveats?

This is the exact role of the **extended real number system** $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$.

### Definition and Order Structure of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$

> [!abstract] Definition: The Extended Real Number System $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$
> The **extended real number system**, denoted $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ (or $[-\infty, +\infty]$), is the set formed by adjoining two distinct formal symbols $-\infty$ ("minus infinity") and $+\infty$ ("plus infinity", often written simply as $\infty$), neither of which belongs to $\mathbb{R}$, to the field of real numbers:
> $$
> \overline{\mathbb{R}} := \mathbb{R} \cup \{-\infty, +\infty\}.
> $$
> We extend the total order $<$ on $\mathbb{R}$ to a total order on $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ by retaining the usual order $x < y$ for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ and declaring that
> $$
> -\infty < x < +\infty \quad \text{for every } x \in \mathbb{R}, \quad \text{and} \quad -\infty < +\infty.
> $$
> As usual, for $u, v \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$, we write $u \le v$ to mean $u < v$ or $u = v$. Real numbers $x \in \mathbb{R}$ are called **finite** elements of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, while $-\infty$ and $+\infty$ are called **infinite** elements.

With this extended order, $+\infty$ is the **maximum** (greatest element) of the entire space $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, and $-\infty$ is the **minimum** (least element) of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$. Because every subset $E \subseteq \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ now has $+\infty$ as an upper bound in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and $-\infty$ as a lower bound in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, no subset of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ is ever "unbounded" inside $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Universal Existence of Supremum and Infimum in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$
> In the extended real number system $(\overline{\mathbb{R}}, \le)$, **every** subset $E \subseteq \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ (including unbounded sets and the empty set $\emptyset$) possesses a unique least upper bound $\sup E \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and a unique greatest lower bound $\inf E \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$, characterized explicitly as follows:
> 1. **For a non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ of finite reals:**
>    - If $E$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, then $\sup E \in \mathbb{R}$ is its usual real supremum; if $E$ is **not** bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, then $\sup E = +\infty$.
>    - If $E$ is bounded below in $\mathbb{R}$, then $\inf E \in \mathbb{R}$ is its usual real infimum; if $E$ is **not** bounded below in $\mathbb{R}$, then $\inf E = -\infty$.
> 2. **For subsets containing $+\infty$ or $-\infty$:**
>    - If $+\infty \in E$, then $\sup E = +\infty$. If $-\infty \in E$, then $\inf E = -\infty$.
>    - If $E = \{-\infty\}$, then $\sup E = \inf E = -\infty$; if $E = \{+\infty\}$, then $\sup E = \inf E = +\infty$. Otherwise, removing $-\infty$ does not change $\sup E$ ($\sup E = \sup(E \setminus \{-\infty\})$), and removing $+\infty$ does not change $\inf E$ ($\inf E = \inf(E \setminus \{+\infty\})$).
> 3. **For the empty set $E = \emptyset$:**
>    $$
>    \sup \emptyset = -\infty \quad \text{and} \quad \inf \emptyset = +\infty.
>    $$

**Proof:**
Let $E \subseteq \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ be any subset, and denote by $U(E) := \{u \in \overline{\mathbb{R}} : \forall x \in E, \, x \le u\}$ the set of all upper bounds of $E$ in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$. Note that $+\infty \in U(E)$ always holds, so $U(E) \neq \emptyset$.

- **Case 1 ($E = \emptyset$):** Look at the definition of an upper bound for $\emptyset$: an element $u \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ belongs to $U(\emptyset)$ if and only if $\forall x \in \emptyset, \, x \le u$. Because the domain of quantification $\emptyset$ is empty, this universal statement is **vacuously true** for every $u \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$! Therefore, the set of upper bounds of $\emptyset$ is the entire extended real line:
  $$
  U(\emptyset) = \overline{\mathbb{R}}.
  $$
  What is the *least* element of $U(\emptyset) = \overline{\mathbb{R}}$? Since $-\infty$ is the minimum element of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, the least upper bound of $\emptyset$ is
  $$
  \sup \emptyset = \min U(\emptyset) = \min \overline{\mathbb{R}} = -\infty.
  $$
  By the exact same logic, every $l \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ is vacuously a lower bound for $\emptyset$, so the set of lower bounds of $\emptyset$ is all of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, whose *greatest* element is $\inf \emptyset = \max \overline{\mathbb{R}} = +\infty$.

- **Case 2 ($E \neq \emptyset$):**
  - If $+\infty \in E$, then any upper bound $u \in U(E)$ must satisfy $+\infty \le u \le +\infty$, so $U(E) = \{+\infty\}$ and $\sup E = +\infty$.
  - If $E = \{-\infty\}$, then every element of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ is an upper bound for $\{-\infty\}$, so $U(\{-\infty\}) = \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and $\sup \{-\infty\} = -\infty$.
  - Otherwise, $+\infty \notin E$ and the finite part $E_{\text{fin}} := E \cap \mathbb{R} = E \setminus \{-\infty\}$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{R}$. Since $-\infty < x$ for all $x \in E_{\text{fin}}$, an element $u \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ is an upper bound for $E$ if and only if it is an upper bound for $E_{\text{fin}}$. If $E_{\text{fin}}$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, then by the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ it has a least upper bound $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$, and since $\alpha < +\infty$, $\alpha$ is also the least element of $U(E)$ in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$. If $E_{\text{fin}}$ is *not* bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, then no finite real number belongs to $U(E)$, leaving $U(E) = \{+\infty\}$, whose least (and only) element is $\sup E = +\infty$.
  The proof for $\inf E$ is completely symmetric by reversing the order. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Why $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$ and $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$ Preserve Monotonicity
> Beyond being a direct logical consequence of vacuous truth, the conventions $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$ and $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$ are the **unique** values that preserve the monotonicity law $A \subseteq B \implies \sup A \le \sup B$ and $\inf B \le \inf A$ across all subsets of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$! Indeed, since $\emptyset \subseteq B$ for every subset $B \subseteq \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ (even $B = \{-\infty\}$ or $B = \{+\infty\}$), we need $\sup \emptyset \le \sup B$ for all $B$, which forces $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$, and we need $\inf B \le \inf \emptyset$ for all $B$, which forces $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$. Similarly, if $E = A \cup B$, we always want $\sup(A \cup B) = \max\{\sup A, \sup B\}$; taking $B = \emptyset$ requires $-\infty$ to act as the neutral element for $\max$, so $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$.

### Arithmetic Conventions and Indeterminate Forms in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$

While $(\overline{\mathbb{R}}, \le)$ is a complete lattice in which every subset has a supremum and infimum, it is **not** a field! Nevertheless, we can extend addition, multiplication, and division partially to $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ whenever the behavior of large numbers is unambiguous.

> [!abstract] Definition: Standard Arithmetic Conventions on $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$
> Let $x \in \mathbb{R}$ be any finite real number. We define the following operations involving $+\infty$ and $-\infty$:
> 1. **Negation:** $-(+\infty) := -\infty$ and $-(-\infty) := +\infty$.
> 2. **Addition and Subtraction:**
>    $$
>    \begin{aligned}
>    x + (+\infty) &= (+\infty) + x = +\infty, \qquad & (+\infty) + (+\infty) &= +\infty, \\
>    x + (-\infty) &= (-\infty) + x = -\infty, \qquad & (-\infty) + (-\infty) &= -\infty, \\
>    x - (+\infty) &= -\infty, \qquad & x - (-\infty) &= +\infty.
>    \end{aligned}
>    $$
> 3. **Multiplication (for $x \neq 0$):**
>    - If $x \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x > 0$ (or $x = +\infty$):
>      $$
>      x \cdot (+\infty) = (+\infty) \cdot x = +\infty, \qquad x \cdot (-\infty) = (-\infty) \cdot x = -\infty.
>      $$
>    - If $x \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < 0$ (or $x = -\infty$):
>      $$
>      x \cdot (+\infty) = (+\infty) \cdot x = -\infty, \qquad x \cdot (-\infty) = (-\infty) \cdot x = +\infty.
>      $$
> 4. **Quotients with Infinite Denominator:** For every finite $x \in \mathbb{R}$,
>    $$
>    \frac{x}{+\infty} = \frac{x}{-\infty} = 0.
>    $$

> [!warning] Caution: Indeterminate Forms That Must Remain Strictly Undefined in Analysis!
> Notice which combinations are conspicuously absent from the definition above:
> $$
> (+\infty) + (-\infty), \qquad (-\infty) + (+\infty), \qquad 0 \cdot (\pm\infty), \qquad \frac{\pm\infty}{\pm\infty}, \qquad \frac{0}{0}.
> $$
> In real analysis, these expressions are called **indeterminate forms**, and they are left **strictly undefined** in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$. Why can we not simply define $(+\infty) + (-\infty) = 0$ or $0 \cdot (+\infty) = 0$?
>
> 1. **Algebraic Collapse (Failure of Associativity and Cancellation):** Suppose we tried to define $(+\infty) + (-\infty) = 0$. Then for any finite real number $x \neq 0$ (say $x = 5$), the associative law of addition would immediately break down:
>    $$
>    \big(5 + (+\infty)\big) + (-\infty) = (+\infty) + (-\infty) = 0, \quad \text{whereas} \quad 5 + \big((+\infty) + (-\infty)\big) = 5 + 0 = 5 \neq 0!
>    $$
>    Similarly, if we tried to define $0 \cdot (+\infty) = 0$, then since $\frac{1}{+\infty} = 0$, associativity of multiplication would fail for $\left(2 \cdot \frac{1}{n}\right) \cdot n = 2$ as $n$ becomes infinite.
> 2. **Analytical Ambiguity (Competing Rates of Growth):** More fundamentally, the symbols $+\infty$ and $-\infty$ in analysis represent the limiting behavior of unbounded quantities. If $a_n = n + c$ (growing like $+\infty$) and $b_n = -n$ (growing like $-\infty$), their sum $a_n + b_n = c$ can equal *any* real number $c \in \mathbb{R}$ (or $+\infty$ if $a_n = n^2$, or $-\infty$ if $b_n = -n^2$)! Likewise, if $a_n = \frac{c}{n} \to 0$ and $b_n = n \to +\infty$, their product $a_n b_n = c$ can equal any number $c \in \mathbb{R}$. Because the outcome depends entirely on the relative speed at which the two terms approach $0$ or $\pm\infty$, no single value in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ can consistently represent $(+\infty) + (-\infty)$, $0 \cdot (\pm\infty)$, or $\frac{\pm\infty}{\pm\infty}$. (Note: in advanced measure theory and Lebesgue integration, one sometimes adopts the specialized convention $0 \cdot (\pm\infty) = 0$ purely for integrating the zero function over a set of infinite measure, but in classical real analysis and limit theory, $0 \cdot (\pm\infty)$ is always treated as an indeterminate form!)

> [!summary] Summary: Working in the Complete Ordered Field $\mathbb{R}$
> 1. **Supremum & Infimum Calculus:** Subset inclusion reverses or preserves bounds ($A \subseteq B \implies \sup A \le \sup B$ and $\inf B \le \inf A$). Minkowski sums and scalar multiples obey $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$, $\sup(cA) = c \sup A$ for $c \ge 0$, and $\sup(cA) = c \inf A$ for $c < 0$ (so $\sup(-A) = -\inf A$ and $\sup(A - B) = \sup A - \inf B$). For functions $f, g: D \to \mathbb{R}$, $\sup_D (f + g) \le \sup_D f + \sup_D g$ can be strict because $f$ and $g$ are evaluated at the same $x \in D$.
> 2. **Archimedean Consequences & Density:** Completeness forces $\mathbb{N}$ to be unbounded above in $\mathbb{R}$. Consequently, $\inf \{1/n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0$, every $x \in \mathbb{R}$ has a unique floor $\lfloor x \rfloor \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $\lfloor x \rfloor \le x < \lfloor x \rfloor + 1$, and both $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ are dense in $\mathbb{R}$.
> 3. **Roots & Nested Intervals:** Every $x > 0$ has a unique positive $n$-th root $y = x^{1/n} = \sup \{t > 0 : t^n < x\}$, enabling well-defined rational powers $x^q$ ($q \in \mathbb{Q}$). Every nested sequence of closed bounded intervals $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq \cdots$ ($I_n = [a_n, b_n]$) has non-empty intersection $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n] = [\sup_n a_n, \inf_n b_n]$, which reduces to a singleton when $\inf_n (b_n - a_n) = 0$.
> 4. **Extended Reals $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$:** Adjoining $-\infty < x < +\infty$ ensures that *every* subset $E \subseteq \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ has $\sup E, \inf E \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$, with $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$ and $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$.

## 0.6 Exercises

### Problem 0.6.1: Supremum and Infimum of Elementwise Products and Reciprocals of Sets

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, and define their elementwise product by
> $$
> A \cdot B := \{ab : a \in A, \, b \in B\}.
> $$
> 1. Suppose first that $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ consist entirely of strictly positive real numbers. Prove that $A \cdot B$ is bounded and that
>    $$
>    \sup(A \cdot B) = (\sup A)(\sup B) \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A \cdot B) = (\inf A)(\inf B).
>    $$
> 2. Suppose $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ is a non-empty bounded set of positive real numbers, and define $A^{-1} := \{1/a : a \in A\}$. Prove that $A^{-1}$ is bounded above if and only if $\inf A > 0$, in which case
>    $$
>    \sup(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\inf A} \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\sup A}.
>    $$
> 3. Now let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be arbitrary non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ (which may contain negative numbers, zero, and positive numbers). Let $\alpha_1 := \inf A$, $\alpha_2 := \sup A$, $\beta_1 := \inf B$, and $\beta_2 := \sup B$. Prove that
>    $$
>    \sup(A \cdot B) = \max \{\alpha_1 \beta_1, \, \alpha_1 \beta_2, \, \alpha_2 \beta_1, \, \alpha_2 \beta_2\} \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A \cdot B) = \min \{\alpha_1 \beta_1, \, \alpha_1 \beta_2, \, \alpha_2 \beta_1, \, \alpha_2 \beta_2\}.
>    $$

### Problem 0.6.2: Lipschitz Continuity of the Supremum and Infimum Functionals

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $D$ be a non-empty set and let $f, g: D \to \mathbb{R}$ be bounded real-valued functions on $D$. Prove that $|f - g|: D \to \mathbb{R}$ is bounded on $D$, and establish the inequalities
> $$
> \left|\sup_{x \in D} f(x) - \sup_{x \in D} g(x)\right| \le \sup_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|
> $$
> and
> $$
> \left|\inf_{x \in D} f(x) - \inf_{x \in D} g(x)\right| \le \sup_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|.
> $$
> Furthermore, give an example showing that $\sup_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|$ on the right-hand side cannot be replaced by $\inf_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|$.

### Problem 0.6.3: The Minimax (Saddle-Point) Inequality for Functions of Two Variables

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $X$ and $Y$ be non-empty sets, and let $f: X \times Y \to \mathbb{R}$ be a bounded function (meaning there exists $M \in \mathbb{R}$ such that $|f(x, y)| \le M$ for all $(x, y) \in X \times Y$).
> 1. Prove that for each fixed $x_0 \in X$, the quantity $g(x_0) := \inf_{y \in Y} f(x_0, y)$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$; for each fixed $y_0 \in Y$, the quantity $h(y_0) := \sup_{x \in X} f(x, y_0)$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$; and the functions $g: X \to \mathbb{R}$ and $h: Y \to \mathbb{R}$ are bounded.
> 2. Prove the **Minimax Inequality**:
>    $$
>    \sup_{x \in X} \inf_{y \in Y} f(x, y) \le \inf_{y \in Y} \sup_{x \in X} f(x, y).
>    $$
> 3. Construct an explicit bounded function $f: \{0, 1\} \times \{0, 1\} \to \mathbb{R}$, and also an explicit bounded function $F: [0, 1] \times [0, 1] \to \mathbb{R}$, for which the strict inequality $\sup_x \inf_y f(x, y) < \inf_y \sup_x f(x, y)$ holds.

### Problem 0.6.4: Necessity of Hypotheses in Cantor's Nested Interval Property

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Construct explicit nested sequences of intervals $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \cdots$ (with $I_n \neq \emptyset$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$) and prove rigorously that $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty I_n = \emptyset$ in each of the following three settings:
> 1. **Open Bounded Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$:** $I_n = (a_n, b_n) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ are open bounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$.
> 2. **Closed Unbounded Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$:** $I_n = [a_n, +\infty) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x \ge a_n\} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ are closed unbounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$.
> 3. **Closed Bounded Intervals in $\mathbb{Q}$:** $I_n = [a_n, b_n]_{\mathbb{Q}} := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : a_n \le q \le b_n\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ are closed bounded intervals in the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ with rational endpoints $a_n, b_n \in \mathbb{Q}$.

### Problem 0.6.5: Construction of Real Exponents $a^x$ via Supremum

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $a \in \mathbb{R}$ with $a > 1$. In this problem, you will extend the definition of rational powers $a^q$ ($q \in \mathbb{Q}$) to arbitrary real exponents $x \in \mathbb{R}$ using the Least Upper Bound Property, without invoking logarithms or continuity.
> 1. **Strict Monotonicity and Bernoulli Estimate on $\mathbb{Q}$:** Prove that for all rational numbers $p, q \in \mathbb{Q}$, $p < q \iff a^p < a^q$. Furthermore, using Bernoulli's inequality $(1 + \delta)^n \ge 1 + n\delta$, prove that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$,
>    $$
>    1 < a^{1/n} \le 1 + \frac{a - 1}{n},
>    $$
>    and deduce that $\inf \{a^{1/n} - 1 : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0$.
> 2. **Definition and Consistency of $a^x$:** For each $x \in \mathbb{R}$, define the subset $E_x := \{a^q : q \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ and } q \le x\}$. Prove that $E_x$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, so that
>    $$
>    a^x := \sup \{a^q : q \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ and } q \le x\}
>    $$
>    is well-defined in $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$. Prove also that if $x \in \mathbb{Q}$, this supremum coincides with the rational power $a^x$ already defined.
> 3. **Strict Monotonicity on $\mathbb{R}$:** Prove that for any $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, we have $a^x < a^y$.
> 4. **Additivity Law $a^{x+y} = a^x a^y$:** Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$. Using the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and Part (1), prove that $a^x = \inf \{a^r : r \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ and } r > x\}$, and prove that
>    $$
>    a^{x+y} = a^x a^y \quad \text{for all } x, y \in \mathbb{R}.
>    $$

### Problem 0.6.6: Kronecker's Approximation Theorem (1D Density of $\mathbb{Z} + \alpha \mathbb{Z}$)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ be an irrational real number, and define the subset of $\mathbb{R}$ generated additively by $1$ and $\alpha$:
> $$
> G_\alpha := \mathbb{Z} + \alpha\mathbb{Z} = \{m + n\alpha : m, n \in \mathbb{Z}\}.
> $$
> 1. Prove that for distinct integers $n_1, n_2 \in \mathbb{Z}$, their fractional parts $\{n_1 \alpha\} = n_1 \alpha - \lfloor n_1 \alpha \rfloor$ and $\{n_2 \alpha\} = n_2 \alpha - \lfloor n_2 \alpha \rfloor$ in $[0, 1)$ are distinct and strictly positive whenever $n_i \neq 0$.
> 2. Let $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ be any positive integer. By partitioning the half-open unit interval $[0, 1)$ into $N$ subintervals $\left[\frac{j-1}{N}, \frac{j}{N}\right)$ for $j = 1, 2, \dots, N$ and applying the **Pigeonhole Principle** to the $N + 1$ points $\{0\alpha\}, \{1\alpha\}, \{2\alpha\}, \dots, \{N\alpha\} \in [0, 1)$, prove that there exist integers $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $1 \le |n| \le N$ such that
>    $$
>    0 < m + n\alpha < \frac{1}{N}.
>    $$
> 3. Deduce that for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists an element $g \in G_\alpha$ with $0 < g < \varepsilon$, and prove that $G_\alpha = \{m + n\alpha : m, n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ is **dense in $\mathbb{R}$** (meaning that for every $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exist integers $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $x < m + n\alpha < y$).

### Problem 0.6.7: Complete Classification of Additive Subgroups of $(\mathbb{R}, +)$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $G \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be an **additive subgroup** of $(\mathbb{R}, +)$, meaning that $G \neq \emptyset$ and for all $x, y \in G$, we have $x - y \in G$ (which implies $0 \in G$, $-x \in G$, and $x + y \in G$). Assume $G \neq \{0\}$, define the set of positive elements of $G$ by $G^+ := G \cap \mathbb{R}_{>0}$, and show that $G^+ \neq \emptyset$ so that
> $$
> c := \inf G^+ \ge 0
> $$
> exists in $\mathbb{R}$. Prove the following dichotomy classifying all non-trivial additive subgroups of $\mathbb{R}$:
> 1. **Discrete Cyclic Case ($c > 0$):** If $c > 0$, prove that $c \in G^+$ (i.e., $c = \min G^+$) and that
>    $$
>    G = c\mathbb{Z} := \{nc : n \in \mathbb{Z}\}.
>    $$
> 2. **Dense Case ($c = 0$):** If $c = 0$, prove that $G$ is **dense in $\mathbb{R}$**.
> 3. **Application to Kronecker's Theorem:** Use this classification to give an alternative proof that for any $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$, the subgroup $G_\alpha := \{m + n\alpha : m, n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $\alpha \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$.

### Problem 0.6.8: Equivalence of the Nested Interval Property + Archimedean Property to the Least Upper Bound Property

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> We proved in this section that the Least Upper Bound Property implies both the **Archimedean Property** and **Cantor's Nested Interval Property**. In this problem, you will prove the converse using the **method of bisection**.
>
> Let $F$ be an ordered field that satisfies both:
> - **(A) The Archimedean Property:** For every $x \in F$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $n \cdot 1_F > x$ (and hence $\inf \{2^{-n} : n \in \mathbb{N}\} = 0$).
> - **(NIP) Cantor's Nested Interval Property:** For every nested sequence $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \cdots$ of closed bounded intervals $I_n = [a_n, b_n] := \{x \in F : a_n \le x \le b_n\}$ ($a_n \le b_n$ in $F$), the intersection $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n]$ is non-empty in $F$.
>
> Let $E \subseteq F$ be any non-empty subset of $F$ that is bounded above in $F$.
> 1. Choose $a_1 \in E$ and an upper bound $b_1 \in F$ of $E$ (so $I_1 = [a_1, b_1]$ satisfies $I_1 \cap E \neq \emptyset$ and $b_1$ is an upper bound of $E$). Define a nested sequence of closed intervals $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ inductively via bisection: given $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$, let $m_n := \frac{a_n + b_n}{2} \in F$ be the midpoint; if $m_n$ is an upper bound for $E$, set $[a_{n+1}, b_{n+1}] := [a_n, m_n]$, and otherwise set $[a_{n+1}, b_{n+1}] := [m_n, b_n]$. Prove by induction on $n \ge 1$ that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$:
>    - $b_n$ is an upper bound for $E$,
>    - there exists at least one element $e_n \in E$ with $a_n \le e_n \le b_n$, and
>    - $b_n - a_n = \frac{b_1 - a_1}{2^{n-1}}$.
> 2. Using (NIP) and (A), prove that there exists a unique element $\alpha \in \bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n]$ in $F$, and prove that $\alpha = \sup E$ in $F$. Conclude that $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property.

---

# 0.7 Cardinality, Countability, Uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$, and the Axiom of Choice

Up to this point in our foundational tour, we have constructed the number systems $\mathbb{N} \subsetneq \mathbb{Z} \subsetneq \mathbb{Q} \subsetneq \mathbb{R}$ and uncovered two profound structural differences between the rationals $\mathbb{Q}$ and the reals $\mathbb{R}$: algebraically, $\mathbb{R}$ contains irrational quantities like $\sqrt{2}$ that close algebraic gaps, and analytically, $\mathbb{R}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property (and consequently Cantor's Nested Interval Property), whereas $\mathbb{Q}$ is riddled with holes.

Yet a nagging philosophical question remains. Since $\mathbb{Q}$ is *dense* in $\mathbb{R}$—meaning that between any two distinct real numbers $a < b$, no matter how microscopically close, there sits a rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$—how much "larger" can $\mathbb{R}$ really be than $\mathbb{Q}$? Are the irrational numbers merely a scattered collection of missing points comparable in size to the rationals, or do they overwhelm the rationals so completely that $\mathbb{Q}$ is virtually invisible inside $\mathbb{R}$?

To answer this question rigorously, we must first ask what it even means for two *infinite* sets to have the "same size." In the late nineteenth century, Georg Cantor revolutionized mathematics by realizing that the ancient act of counting does not fundamentally require numbers at all—it only requires *pairing*. When a shepherd checks whether a flock of sheep matches a pile of pebbles without knowing how to count, they simply pair each sheep with exactly one pebble. Cantor elevated this primitive idea of one-to-one correspondence into a breathtaking calculus of the infinite, discovering that infinity is not a single monolithic concept, but an endless hierarchy of strictly larger infinite sizes—and that passing from $\mathbb{Q}$ to $\mathbb{R}$ leaps across the first great chasm of that hierarchy.

## Equinumerosity, Cardinal Comparison, and the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem

### Equinumerosity and Cardinal Equivalence

How do we compare the sizes of two sets $A$ and $B$ without counting their elements one by one? If we can match every element of $A$ with a unique partner in $B$ so that no elements of $A$ share a partner and no elements of $B$ are left unmatched, then $A$ and $B$ must have the exact same abundance of elements. In the language of functions developed in Section 0.1, such a perfect matching is precisely a *bijection*.

> [!abstract] Definition: Equinumerosity (Cardinal Equivalence)
> Two sets $A$ and $B$ are said to be **equinumerous** (or **cardinally equivalent**, or to have the **same cardinality**), written $A \sim B$ or $|A| = |B|$, if there exists a bijection $f: A \to B$.

Notice that at this foundational stage, we do not need to define $|A|$ as an independent set-theoretic object (in advanced Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory, cardinals are defined as initial ordinals); rather, the equation $|A| = |B|$ is simply a vivid, intuitive shorthand for the relation $A \sim B$. For this notion of "same size" to behave coherently, equinumerosity must act as an equivalence relation across any collection of sets.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Equinumerosity is an Equivalence Relation
> Let $\mathcal{C}$ be any collection of sets. The relation $\sim$ of equinumerosity on $\mathcal{C}$ is an equivalence relation; that is, for all sets $A, B, C \in \mathcal{C}$:
> 1. **Reflexivity:** $A \sim A$.
> 2. **Symmetry:** If $A \sim B$, then $B \sim A$.
> 3. **Transitivity:** If $A \sim B$ and $B \sim C$, then $A \sim C$.

**Proof:**
We verify each of the three properties directly using the fundamental properties of bijections established in Section 0.1:

1. **Reflexivity:** For any set $A$, the identity map $\text{id}_A: A \to A$ defined by $\text{id}_A(a) = a$ for all $a \in A$ is both injective and surjective, hence a bijection. Thus $A \sim A$.
2. **Symmetry:** Suppose $A \sim B$, so there exists a bijection $f: A \to B$. Because $f$ is bijective, its inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$ exists and is itself a bijection from $B$ to $A$. Therefore, $B \sim A$.
3. **Transitivity:** Suppose $A \sim B$ and $B \sim C$, witnessed by bijections $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to C$. As proved in Section 0.1, the composition of two bijections $g \circ f: A \to C$ is again a bijection (with inverse $(g \circ f)^{-1} = f^{-1} \circ g^{-1}$). Hence $A \sim C$. $\blacksquare$

### Cardinal Inequality, Finite Sets, and Infinite Sets

What if we cannot match $A$ onto all of $B$, but we can still match $A$ bijectively onto a *subset* of $B$? Then $A$ contains at most as many elements as $B$. Just as bijections capture equality of size, injections capture inequality of size.

> [!abstract] Definition: Cardinal Inequality ($|A| \le |B|$ and $|A| < |B|$)
> Let $A$ and $B$ be sets.
> 1. We write $|A| \le |B|$ (and say that the cardinality of $A$ is **less than or equal to** the cardinality of $B$, or that $B$ **dominates** $A$) if there exists an injection $f: A \to B$. Equivalently, $A$ is equinumerous with a subset $f(A) \subseteq B$.
> 2. We write $|A| < |B|$ (and say that the cardinality of $A$ is **strictly less than** the cardinality of $B$) if $|A| \le |B|$ and $A \not\sim B$—that is, there exists an injection $f: A \to B$, but there is **no** bijection between $A$ and $B$.

Notice immediately that cardinal inequality $\le$ is **reflexive** (since $\text{id}_A: A \to A$ is injective, so $|A| \le |A|$) and **transitive** (since if $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to C$ are injective, their composition $g \circ f: A \to C$ is injective, so $|A| \le |B|$ and $|B| \le |C|$ imply $|A| \le |C|$). Furthermore, if $A \subseteq B$, the inclusion map $\iota: A \hookrightarrow B$ given by $\iota(a) = a$ is an injection, which gives the foundational monotonicity principle:

$$
A \subseteq B \implies |A| \le |B|
$$

Before venturing into infinite sets, let us anchor our terminology by defining *finite* and *infinite* sets precisely in terms of the positive integers $\mathbb{N}_{>0} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ studied in Section 0.2. For each $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, let us denote the standard initial segment of positive integers by:

$$
I_n := \{k \in \mathbb{N} : 1 \le k \le n\} = \{1, 2, \dots, n\}
$$

> [!abstract] Definition: Finite and Infinite Sets
> Let $A$ be a set.
> 1. We say that $A$ is **finite** if either $A = \emptyset$ (in which case we say $A$ has cardinality $|A| = 0$) or there exists some $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $A \sim \{1, 2, \dots, n\}$ (in which case we say $A$ has cardinality $|A| = n$).
> 2. We say that $A$ is **infinite** if $A$ is not finite.

> [!note] Uniqueness of Finite Cardinality and the Pigeonhole Principle
> Why is the integer $n$ in the definition of a finite set $|A| = n$ uniquely determined by $A$? Suppose $A \sim \{1, \dots, m\}$ and $A \sim \{1, \dots, n\}$ for some $m, n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$. By symmetry and transitivity of $\sim$, we would have $\{1, \dots, m\} \sim \{1, \dots, n\}$. However, by the **Finite Pigeonhole Principle** proved by mathematical induction in Section 0.2, if $m > n$, there exists no injection from $\{1, \dots, m\}$ into $\{1, \dots, n\}$, and similarly if $n > m$. Thus $m = n$ is forced!
>
> Moreover, the Finite Pigeonhole Principle guarantees that **no finite set can ever be equinumerous with a proper subset of itself**. In stark contrast, we saw in Section 0.2 that the successor map $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ given by $S(n) = n + 1$ is a bijection from $\mathbb{N}$ onto its proper subset $\mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. As we will explore later in this section, this "Hilbert's Hotel" phenomenon—being equinumerous with a proper subset—is the hallmark of infinity.

### The Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem

When we want to prove that two infinite sets $A$ and $B$ have the exact same cardinality ($A \sim B$), constructing an explicit single formula for a bijection $h: A \to B$ is often painfully awkward. For instance, suppose we want to show that the closed interval $[0, 1]$ and the open interval $(0, 1)$ have the same cardinality. Because $[0, 1]$ has two endpoints $0$ and $1$ while $(0, 1)$ has none, any continuous map cannot be a bijection, and writing down an explicit piecewise bijection requires manually shuffling an infinite sequence of points to absorb the endpoints.

On the other hand, constructing *two separate injections* $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to A$ is usually effortless! For $A = (0, 1)$ and $B = [0, 1]$, the inclusion map $f(x) = x$ injects $(0, 1) \hookrightarrow [0, 1]$, while the affine scaling map $g(y) = \frac{1}{2}y + \frac{1}{4}$ injects $[0, 1] \hookrightarrow (0, 1)$ (mapping $[0, 1]$ onto $[\frac{1}{4}, \frac{3}{4}] \subsetneq (0, 1)$).

If we know that $|A| \le |B|$ and $|B| \le |A|$, can we immediately conclude that $|A| = |B|$? For numbers, the antisymmetry of $\le$ is an axiom; for arbitrary sets, it is a deep and remarkable theorem! Conjectured by Georg Cantor in 1887 and proved independently by Ernst Schröder (whose initial 1896 proof contained a flaw later fixed) and Felix Bernstein (in his 1897 doctoral dissertation at age nineteen), the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem** is one of the most indispensable tools in all of set theory and real analysis. What makes it doubly extraordinary is that its proof is **completely constructive within ZF set theory**—it does not require the Axiom of Choice!

> [!theorem] Theorem: The Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem
> Let $A$ and $B$ be arbitrary sets. If there exist an injection $f: A \to B$ and an injection $g: B \to A$ (that is, if $|A| \le |B|$ and $|B| \le |A|$), then there exists a bijection $h: A \to B$ (so $A \sim B$, or $|A| = |B|$).

**Proof:**
We present two illuminating, closely related proofs: first, a completely explicit proof via **ancestor chains** that constructs the bijection directly by decomposing $A$, and second, a conceptual five-line proof via the **Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem** from Section 0.1 that reveals why this decomposition works.

**Method I (Explicit Construction via Ancestor Chains):**
Suppose $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to A$ are injections. Our goal is to partition $A$ into two disjoint subsets, $C$ and $A \setminus C$, such that we can safely define $h: A \to B$ by using $f$ on $C$ and $g^{-1}$ on $A \setminus C$:

$$
h(x) := \begin{cases} f(x) & \text{if } x \in C, \\ g^{-1}(x) & \text{if } x \in A \setminus C. \end{cases}
$$

Why can we not choose $C$ arbitrarily? First, in order for $g^{-1}(x)$ to be well-defined for every $x \in A \setminus C$, every element outside $C$ must lie in the image $g(B) \subseteq A$. Equivalently, the complement of $g(B)$ in $A$—namely the set $C_0 := A \setminus g(B)$ of elements in $A$ that have no preimage under $g$—*must* be included in $C$, where we apply $f$ instead of $g^{-1}$.

Second, once we decide to apply $f$ to every $x \in C_0$, their images $f(C_0) \subseteq B$ are already hit by $h$. Therefore, if we looks at where $g$ sends $f(C_0)$ back into $A$—namely the set $C_1 := g(f(C_0)) = (g \circ f)(C_0)$—we **cannot** apply $g^{-1}$ to elements of $C_1$, because doing so would send them back into $f(C_0)$ and cause a collision with $f(C_0)$, destroying injectivity! Instead, we are forced to include $C_1$ inside $C$ and apply $f$ to $C_1$ as well.

Continuing this chain reaction inductively by recursion on $\mathbb{N}$, we define a sequence of subsets $(C_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ of $A$ by:

$$
\begin{aligned}
C_0 &:= A \setminus g(B), \\
C_{n+1} &:= g(f(C_n)) = (g \circ f)(C_n) \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N},
\end{aligned}
$$

and we define $C \subseteq A$ to be the union of all these generations:

$$
C := \bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty} C_n.
$$

Intuitively, if we trace the "ancestry" of an element $x \in A$ backward by alternately applying $g^{-1}$ and $f^{-1}$, the set $C$ consists precisely of those elements $x \in A$ whose ancestry eventually terminates in $A \setminus g(B)$ after an even number of steps!

Let us prove a crucial structural identity for $C$:

$$
A \setminus C = g(B \setminus f(C)).
$$

*Proof of the structural identity:*
- $(\subseteq)$ Let $x \in A \setminus C$. Since $C_0 = A \setminus g(B) \subseteq C$ and $x \notin C$, we have $x \notin A \setminus g(B)$, which means $x \in g(B)$. Because $g: B \to A$ is injective, there exists a unique $y \in B$ such that $x = g(y)$. Could $y$ belong to $f(C)$? If $y \in f(C) = f\left(\bigcup_{n=0}^\infty C_n\right) = \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty f(C_n)$, then $y \in f(C_n)$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, which implies $x = g(y) \in g(f(C_n)) = C_{n+1} \subseteq C$, contradicting $x \notin C$! Thus $y \in B \setminus f(C)$, so $x = g(y) \in g(B \setminus f(C))$.
- $(\supseteq)$ Conversely, let $x \in g(B \setminus f(C))$, so $x = g(y)$ for some $y \in B \setminus f(C)$. Since $x = g(y) \in g(B)$, we certainly have $x \notin A \setminus g(B) = C_0$. Moreover, for any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, could $x \in C_{n+1} = g(f(C_n))$? If $x = g(y) \in g(f(C_n))$, then by injectivity of $g$, we would have $y \in f(C_n) \subseteq f(C)$, contradicting $y \in B \setminus f(C)$! Hence $x \notin C_k$ for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $x \in A \setminus C$.

Now that we know $A \setminus C = g(B \setminus f(C))$ and $g$ is injective, the restriction of $g$ to $B \setminus f(C)$ is a bijection onto $A \setminus C$, so for every $x \in A \setminus C$ there exists a unique element $g^{-1}(x) \in B \setminus f(C)$ such that $g(g^{-1}(x)) = x$. Thus the piecewise function $h: A \to B$ given by:

$$
h(x) = \begin{cases} f(x) & \text{if } x \in C, \\ g^{-1}(x) & \text{if } x \in A \setminus C \end{cases}
$$

is completely well-defined! Let us verify step-by-step that $h$ is both injective and surjective:

1. **Injectivity of $h$:** Take any $x_1, x_2 \in A$ with $h(x_1) = h(x_2)$.
   - *Case 1:* Both $x_1, x_2 \in C$. Then $f(x_1) = h(x_1) = h(x_2) = f(x_2)$, which implies $x_1 = x_2$ because $f$ is injective.
   - *Case 2:* Both $x_1, x_2 \in A \setminus C$. Then $g^{-1}(x_1) = h(x_1) = h(x_2) = g^{-1}(x_2)$. Applying $g$ to both sides yields $x_1 = g(g^{-1}(x_1)) = g(g^{-1}(x_2)) = x_2$.
   - *Case 3:* One element lies in $C$ and the other lies in $A \setminus C$, say $x_1 \in C$ and $x_2 \in A \setminus C$. Then $h(x_1) = f(x_1) \in f(C)$, whereas by our structural identity $x_2 \in A \setminus C = g(B \setminus f(C))$, we have $h(x_2) = g^{-1}(x_2) \in B \setminus f(C)$. Since $f(C)$ and $B \setminus f(C)$ are disjoint, $h(x_1) = h(x_2)$ is impossible!
   In all cases, $h(x_1) = h(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2$, so $h$ is injective.

2. **Surjectivity of $h$:** Let $y \in B$ be arbitrary. Either $y \in f(C)$ or $y \in B \setminus f(C)$.
   - If $y \in f(C)$, then there exists $x \in C$ such that $f(x) = y$, and since $x \in C$, we have $h(x) = f(x) = y$.
   - If $y \in B \setminus f(C)$, consider $x := g(y) \in g(B \setminus f(C)) = A \setminus C$. Since $x \in A \setminus C$, the definition of $h$ gives $h(x) = g^{-1}(x) = g^{-1}(g(y)) = y$.
   Thus $h(A) = f(C) \cup (B \setminus f(C)) = B$, proving that $h$ is surjective and hence a bijection.

**Method II (Connection to the Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem):**
Notice that the entire proof above hinged on finding a subset $C \subseteq A$ satisfying $A \setminus C = g(B \setminus f(C))$, or equivalently $C = A \setminus g(B \setminus f(C))$. In Section 0.1, we proved the **Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem**: every inclusion-preserving map $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(A) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ has a fixed point! If we define $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(A) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ by:

$$
\Phi(E) := A \setminus g(B \setminus f(E)) \quad \text{for each } E \subseteq A,
$$

then whenever $E_1 \subseteq E_2 \subseteq A$, taking images and complements reverses inclusion twice:

$$
\begin{aligned}
E_1 \subseteq E_2 &\implies f(E_1) \subseteq f(E_2) \\
&\implies B \setminus f(E_1) \supseteq B \setminus f(E_2) \\
&\implies g(B \setminus f(E_1)) \supseteq g(B \setminus f(E_2)) \\
&\implies A \setminus g(B \setminus f(E_1)) \subseteq A \setminus g(B \setminus f(E_2)),
\end{aligned}
$$

so $\Phi(E_1) \subseteq \Phi(E_2)$. By the Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem, there exists $C \subseteq A$ with $\Phi(C) = C$, which immediately gives $A \setminus C = g(B \setminus f(C))$ and makes $h$ a bijection! In fact, the set $C = \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty C_n$ constructed in Method I is precisely the *least* fixed point $\bigcap \{E \subseteq A : \Phi(E) \subseteq E\}$ of $\Phi$. $\blacksquare$

## Countable Sets and Countable Unions

### Denumerable and Countable Sets

What is the smallest possible size of an infinite set? Since we start our counting numbers with $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ (or equivalently $\mathbb{N}_{>0} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, which is equinumerous with $\mathbb{N}$ via the successor bijection $n \mapsto n + 1$), the natural benchmark for infinite sets is the cardinality of $\mathbb{N}$ itself. Any set that can be placed in one-to-one correspondence with $\mathbb{N}$ can be listed in an unending sequence $a_0, a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots$ in which every element appears at a specific finite step.

> [!abstract] Definition: Denumerable (Countably Infinite) and Countable Sets
> Let $A$ be a set.
> 1. We say that $A$ is **denumerable** (or **countably infinite**), and write $|A| = \aleph_0$ (read "aleph-naught" or "aleph-null"), if $A \sim \mathbb{N}$ (equivalently, $A \sim \mathbb{N}_{>0}$).
> 2. We say that $A$ is **countable** (or **at most countable**) if $A$ is either **finite** or **denumerable**.
> 3. We say that $A$ is **uncountable** if $A$ is not countable—that is, $A$ is infinite and $A \not\sim \mathbb{N}$.

> [!warning] Terminological Convention: "Countable" vs. "Countably Infinite"
> In mathematical literature, most modern analysts (including Rudin, Folland, and Tao) use **countable** to mean *at most countable* (finite or countably infinite), reserving **countably infinite** or **denumerable** for sets strictly equinumerous with $\mathbb{N}$. A few older texts use "countable" to mean strictly countably infinite. Throughout these notes, **countable** always means *at most countable* ($|A| \le \aleph_0$), and when we specifically mean $A \sim \mathbb{N}$ we will say **countably infinite** or **denumerable** where any ambiguity could arise.

### Fundamental Theorems of Countability

Our first fundamental theorem shows that there is no intermediate infinity strictly between finite sets and $\mathbb{N}$ among the subsets of $\mathbb{N}$: *every* infinite subset of $\mathbb{N}$ has the exact same cardinality as $\mathbb{N}$ itself! Better yet, because $\mathbb{N}$ satisfies the **Well-Ordering Principle** (Section 0.2), we can prove this completely constructively without invoking the Axiom of Choice: whenever we have a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$, we do not need to make an arbitrary choice of an element—we simply pick its *minimum*!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Every Infinite Subset of $\mathbb{N}$ is Denumerable
> Every infinite subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is countably infinite ($S \sim \mathbb{N}$). Consequently, every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is countable (either finite or denumerable).

**Proof:**
Let $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ be an infinite subset. We construct a strictly increasing bijection $f: \mathbb{N} \to S$ recursively using the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2), which guarantees that every non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$ possesses a unique least element:

- **Base Step ($n = 0$):** Since $S$ is infinite, $S \neq \emptyset$. By the Well-Ordering Principle, $S$ has a unique minimum element, which we define to be:
  $$
  s_0 := \min S.
  $$
- **Recursive Step ($n \to n + 1$):** Suppose $s_0, s_1, \dots, s_n \in S$ have been defined. Because $S$ is infinite whereas $\{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_n\}$ is finite (having size at most $n + 1$), the complement $S \setminus \{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_n\}$ cannot be empty (otherwise $S = \{s_0, \dots, s_n\}$ would be finite). Therefore, by the Well-Ordering Principle, $S \setminus \{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_n\}$ has a unique minimum element, and we define:
  $$
  s_{n+1} := \min\bigl(S \setminus \{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_n\}\bigr).
  $$

Let $f: \mathbb{N} \to S$ be the function $f(n) := s_n$. We prove that $f$ is strictly increasing ($s_0 < s_1 < s_2 < \cdots$) and surjective onto $S$:

1. **Strict Monotonicity and Injectivity:** For any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, by definition $s_{n+1}$ belongs to $S \setminus \{s_0, \dots, s_n\}$, which is a subset of $S \setminus \{s_0, \dots, s_{n-1}\}$ (interpreting $\{s_0, \dots, s_{-1}\} = \emptyset$ for $n = 0$). Since $s_n = \min(S \setminus \{s_0, \dots, s_{n-1}\})$ is the *least* element of the larger set and $s_{n+1} \neq s_n$ belongs to that same set, we must have $s_n < s_{n+1}$. By a straightforward induction on $k \ge 1$, this implies $s_m < s_{m+k}$ whenever $m < m + k$, so $f$ is strictly increasing ($m < n \implies f(m) < f(n)$) and therefore injective! Moreover, another immediate induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ shows that $s_n \ge n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ (since $s_0 \ge 0$, and $s_{n+1} > s_n \ge n \implies s_{n+1} \ge n + 1$).

2. **Surjectivity:** Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $f(\mathbb{N}) \subsetneq S$, so there exists some element $x \in S \setminus f(\mathbb{N})$. Since $x \in \mathbb{N}$ and $s_k \ge k$ for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$, taking $k = x$ gives $s_x \ge x$, and since $x \notin f(\mathbb{N})$, we actually have $s_x > x$. Thus the set $\{k \in \mathbb{N} : s_k > x\}$ is non-empty (it contains $k = x$). By the Well-Ordering Principle, let $m := \min \{k \in \mathbb{N} : s_k > x\}$.
   - Could $m = 0$? If $m = 0$, then $s_0 = \min S > x$, which is impossible since $x \in S$!
   - Thus $m \ge 1$. By minimality of $m$, for every $j \in \{0, 1, \dots, m - 1\}$ we have $s_j \le x$, and since $x \notin f(\mathbb{N})$, we have $s_j < x$. In particular, $x \notin \{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_{m-1}\}$, which means $x \in S \setminus \{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_{m-1}\}$.
   - However, $s_m$ was defined as $\min(S \setminus \{s_0, s_1, \dots, s_{m-1}\})$, which forces $s_m \le x$, directly contradicting $s_m > x$!

Hence no such $x$ can exist: $f(\mathbb{N}) = S$, so $f: \mathbb{N} \to S$ is a bijection and $S \sim \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

This theorem immediately yields a powerful, flexible characterization of countability via injections and surjections, freeing us from ever needing to verify bijectivity when proving a set is countable!

> [!theorem] Corollary: Injective and Surjective Characterizations of Countability
> Let $A$ be any set. The following three statements are equivalent:
> 1. $A$ is countable (finite or denumerable).
> 2. $|A| \le |\mathbb{N}|$—that is, there exists an injection $f: A \to \mathbb{N}$.
> 3. Either $A = \emptyset$, or there exists a surjection $g: \mathbb{N} \to A$.

**Proof:**
- $(1 \implies 2)$ Suppose $A$ is countable. If $A = \emptyset$, the empty function $\emptyset \to \mathbb{N}$ is vacuously injective. If $A$ is finite and non-empty, there is a bijection $h: A \to \{1, \dots, n\} \subseteq \mathbb{N}$, which is an injection into $\mathbb{N}$. If $A$ is denumerable, there is a bijection $h: A \to \mathbb{N}$, which is also an injection into $\mathbb{N}$.
- $(2 \implies 1)$ Suppose $f: A \to \mathbb{N}$ is an injection. Then $A$ is bijective with its image $S := f(A) \subseteq \mathbb{N}$. By our theorem on subsets of $\mathbb{N}$, the subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is either finite or denumerable, and since $A \sim S$, the set $A$ is likewise either finite or denumerable—hence countable!
- $(2 \implies 3)$ Suppose $f: A \to \mathbb{N}$ is an injection and $A \neq \emptyset$. Fix an element $a_0 \in A$. Since $f$ is injective, for each $n \in f(A)$ there is a unique $a \in A$ with $f(a) = n$, which we denote by $f^{-1}(n)$. Define $g: \mathbb{N} \to A$ by:
  $$
  g(n) := \begin{cases} f^{-1}(n) & \text{if } n \in f(A), \\ a_0 & \text{if } n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus f(A). \end{cases}
  $$
  For every $a \in A$, taking $n = f(a) \in f(A)$ gives $g(n) = f^{-1}(f(a)) = a$, so $g: \mathbb{N} \to A$ is surjective.
- $(3 \implies 2)$ Suppose $A \neq \emptyset$ and there exists a surjection $g: \mathbb{N} \to A$. For each $a \in A$, because $g$ is surjective, the preimage fiber $g^{-1}(\{a\}) = \{n \in \mathbb{N} : g(n) = a\}$ is a **non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$**. By the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$ (no Axiom of Choice required!), we can canonically define $f: A \to \mathbb{N}$ by picking the least index at which $a$ appears:
  $$
  f(a) := \min \{n \in \mathbb{N} : g(n) = a\}.
  $$
  By definition of $f(a)$, we have $g(f(a)) = a$ for all $a \in A$. Consequently, if $f(a_1) = f(a_2)$, applying $g$ to both sides yields $a_1 = g(f(a_1)) = g(f(a_2)) = a_2$, proving that $f: A \to \mathbb{N}$ is an injection! $\blacksquare$

Next, let us ask what happens when we form the Cartesian product of two countably infinite sets: if we arrange an infinite two-dimensional grid $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ of ordered pairs $(m, n)$, can a single one-dimensional list $\mathbb{N}$ sweep through the entire two-dimensional grid without missing a single entry? If we foolishly try to read across the first row $(0, 0), (0, 1), (0, 2), \dots$ forever, we will never even reach the second row $(1, 0)$!

Cantor's brilliant geometric insight was to traverse the grid $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ along its **finite anti-diagonals**, where the sum of coordinates $k = m + n$ is constant:
- Diagonal $k = 0$: $(0, 0)$ (1 pair);
- Diagonal $k = 1$: $(0, 1), (1, 0)$ (2 pairs);
- Diagonal $k = 2$: $(0, 2), (1, 1), (2, 0)$ (3 pairs);
- Diagonal $k = 3$: $(0, 3), (1, 2), (2, 1), (3, 0)$ (4 pairs).
