Because each diagonal $k$ contains only finitely many pairs ($k + 1$ pairs, ordered by increasing first coordinate $m = 0, 1, \dots, k$), before reaching the $k$-th diagonal $m + n = k$ we have traversed exactly $1 + 2 + \dots + k = \frac{k(k + 1)}{2}$ pairs, and along the $k$-th diagonal itself we take $m$ additional steps to reach $(m, n)$. This produces an exact polynomial bijection $\pi: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Countability of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ and Finite Cartesian Products
> The Cartesian product $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ is countably infinite ($\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}$). Specifically, the **Cantor Pairing Function** $\pi: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ defined by
> $$
> \pi(m, n) := \frac{(m + n)(m + n + 1)}{2} + m
> $$
> is a bijection. More generally, for any finite integer $k \ge 1$, if $A_1, A_2, \dots, A_k$ are countable sets, then their Cartesian product $A_1 \times A_2 \times \dots \times A_k$ is countable.

**Proof:**
We give two independent proofs that $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}$: first by verifying that the Cantor pairing function $\pi$ is an explicit bijection, and second via a two-line prime-power argument using the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem.

**Method I (Explicit Bijectivity of the Cantor Pairing Function $\pi$):**
For each $k \in \mathbb{N}$, define the $k$-th triangular number $T_k := \frac{k(k + 1)}{2} \in \mathbb{N}$ (with $T_0 = 0$), and observe that:

$$
T_{k+1} - T_k = \frac{(k + 1)(k + 2) - k(k + 1)}{2} = k + 1, \quad \text{so } T_{k+1} = T_k + k + 1.
$$

For any pair $(m, n) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, let $k := m + n \in \mathbb{N}$. Since $0 \le m \le m + n = k$, the value $\pi(m, n) = T_k + m$ satisfies:

$$
T_k \le \pi(m, n) = T_k + m \le T_k + k = T_{k+1} - 1 < T_{k+1}.
$$

Thus $\pi$ maps the $k$-th diagonal $D_k := \{(m, n) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} : m + n = k\}$ into the integer interval $[T_k, T_{k+1} - 1] \cap \mathbb{N}$.
1. **Injectivity of $\pi$:** Suppose $\pi(m_1, n_1) = \pi(m_2, n_2)$, and set $k_1 := m_1 + n_1$ and $k_2 := m_2 + n_2$. If $k_1 \neq k_2$, say $k_1 < k_2$, then $k_1 + 1 \le k_2$, and since $T_k$ is strictly increasing in $k$, we have:
   $$
   \pi(m_1, n_1) < T_{k_1 + 1} \le T_{k_2} \le \pi(m_2, n_2),
   $$
   contradicting $\pi(m_1, n_1) = \pi(m_2, n_2)$! Therefore $k_1 = k_2$. Subtracting $T_{k_1} = T_{k_2}$ from $\pi(m_1, n_1) = \pi(m_2, n_2)$ immediately yields $m_1 = m_2$, and consequently $n_1 = k_1 - m_1 = k_2 - m_2 = n_2$. Thus $\pi$ is injective.
2. **Surjectivity of $\pi$:** Let $z \in \mathbb{N}$ be arbitrary. Since $T_0 = 0 \le z$ and $T_k \ge k > z$ for $k = z + 1$, the set $\{k \in \mathbb{N} : T_k > z\}$ is non-empty, so by the Well-Ordering Principle we can let $k + 1 := \min \{j \in \mathbb{N} : T_j > z\} \ge 1$. Then $T_k \le z < T_{k+1} = T_k + k + 1$, which implies $0 \le z - T_k \le k$. Setting $m := z - T_k \in \mathbb{N}$ and $n := k - m \in \mathbb{N}$ gives $m + n = k$ and $\pi(m, n) = T_k + m = z$, proving $\pi$ is surjective!

**Method II (Via Prime Powers and Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein):**
Alternatively, define $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ by $f(m) := (m, 0)$, which is trivially injective, and define $g: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ by $g(m, n) := 2^m 3^n$. To see that $g$ is injective without even needing the full Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic, suppose $2^{m_1} 3^{n_1} = 2^{m_2} 3^{n_2}$. If $m_1 \neq m_2$, say $m_1 > m_2$, dividing by $2^{m_2}$ yields $2^{m_1 - m_2} 3^{n_1} = 3^{n_2}$, where the left-hand side is an even integer (since $m_1 - m_2 \ge 1$) and the right-hand side $3^{n_2}$ is an odd integer (as proved inductively from $3 \equiv 1 \pmod 2$), a contradiction! Hence $m_1 = m_2$, so $3^{n_1} = 3^{n_2}$, and since $n \mapsto 3^n$ is strictly increasing, $n_1 = n_2$. By the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem**, $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}$.

**Extension to Finite Cartesian Products $A_1 \times \dots \times A_k$:**
First, if $A$ and $B$ are countable sets, there exist injections $f_A: A \to \mathbb{N}$ and $f_B: B \to \mathbb{N}$ by our characterization of countability. Then the product map $F: A \times B \to \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ defined by $F(a, b) := (f_A(a), f_B(b))$ is injective, and composing with the Cantor pairing bijection $\pi: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ gives an injection $\pi \circ F: A \times B \to \mathbb{N}$, proving $A \times B$ is countable.

For general $k \ge 1$, we proceed by mathematical induction on $k$: the base case $k = 1$ holds by hypothesis, and if $A_1 \times \dots \times A_k$ is countable and $A_{k+1}$ is countable, the canonical bijection $(A_1 \times \dots \times A_k) \times A_{k+1} \sim A_1 \times \dots \times A_k \times A_{k+1}$ combined with the two-factor result proves that $A_1 \times \dots \times A_{k+1}$ is countable. $\blacksquare$

Armed with the countability of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, we can immediately settle the cardinality of the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ and the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$. Even though $\mathbb{Q}$ is densely packed throughout the real line—so that every open interval $(a, b) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ contains infinitely many rationals—the entire field $\mathbb{Q}$ has no more elements than the discrete set of natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Countability of $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$
> Both the ring of integers $\mathbb{Z}$ and the field of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ are countably infinite:
> $$
> |\mathbb{Z}| = |\mathbb{Q}| = |\mathbb{N}| = \aleph_0.
> $$

**Proof:**
1. **Countability of $\mathbb{Z}$:** We construct an explicit bijection $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$ by alternating between non-negative and negative integers ($0, 1, -1, 2, -2, 3, -3, \dots$):
   $$
   f(n) := \begin{cases} \frac{n}{2} & \text{if } n \text{ is even } (n = 2k), \\ -\frac{n + 1}{2} & \text{if } n \text{ is odd } (n = 2k - 1). \end{cases}
   $$
   To verify that $f$ is a bijection, observe that its explicit inverse $f^{-1}: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{N}$ is given by $f^{-1}(m) = 2m$ for $m \ge 0$ and $f^{-1}(m) = -2m - 1$ for $m < 0$. Since non-negative integers map bijectively onto the even naturals $\{0, 2, 4, \dots\}$ and negative integers map bijectively onto the odd naturals $\{1, 3, 5, \dots\}$, $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$ is a bijection, so $\mathbb{Z} \sim \mathbb{N}$.

2. **Countability of $\mathbb{Q}$:** Since $\mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$, the inclusion map $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$ shows that $\mathbb{Q}$ is infinite ($|\mathbb{N}| \le |\mathbb{Q}|$). To prove $|\mathbb{Q}| \le |\mathbb{N}|$, recall that every rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ can be written as a fraction $q = \frac{a}{b}$ with $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b \in \mathbb{N}_{>0} = \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$.
   - Even without invoking unique prime factorization to reduce fractions to lowest terms, we can define a map $\Phi: \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to \mathbb{Q}$ by:
     $$
     \Phi(a, b) := \frac{a}{b}.
     $$
   - By the definition of $\mathbb{Q}$, $\Phi: \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to \mathbb{Q}$ is **surjective**.
   - Since $\mathbb{Z}$ is countable and $\mathbb{N}_{>0} \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is countable, their Cartesian product $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ is countably infinite by the previous theorem, so there exists a bijection $\psi: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{N}_{>0}$.
   - Composing $\Phi$ with $\psi$ produces a surjection $\Phi \circ \psi: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Q}$. By our surjective characterization of countability (which selects for each $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ the minimum $n \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $(\Phi \circ \psi)(n) = q$), there exists an injection $\mathbb{Q} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}$. Since $\mathbb{Q}$ is infinite, we conclude $\mathbb{Q} \sim \mathbb{N}$! $\blacksquare$

What if instead of taking a finite union or a two-set product, we take the union of a *countably infinite family* of countable sets $A_0, A_1, A_2, \dots$? Can a countable union of countable sets suddenly jump to uncountability, or does it remain countable?

> [!theorem] Theorem: Countable Union of Countable Sets
> Let $(A_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence (or more generally, let $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ be a family indexed by a countable set $I$) of countable sets. Then their union
> $$
> A := \bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n
> $$
> is countable.

**Proof:**
If $A_n = \emptyset$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then $A = \emptyset$ is finite and hence countable. Otherwise, let $J := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : A_n \neq \emptyset\}$ be the non-empty subset of indices for which $A_n$ is non-empty. Since $A = \bigcup_{n \in J} A_n$, for each $n \in J$ the non-empty countable set $A_n$ admits a surjection $g_n: \mathbb{N} \to A_n$ by our surjective characterization of countability. (If $n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus J$, pick any fixed element $a_* \in A$ and let $g_n: \mathbb{N} \to A$ be the constant map $g_n(m) := a_*$.)

Now define a master map $G: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to A$ by:

$$
G(n, m) := g_n(m) \quad \text{for all } (n, m) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}.
$$

We claim that $G$ is surjective onto $A = \bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$. Indeed, given any $x \in A$, by definition of the union there exists at least one $n \in J \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ such that $x \in A_n$. Because $g_n: \mathbb{N} \to A_n$ is surjective, there exists $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $g_n(m) = x$, and thus $G(n, m) = g_n(m) = x$.

Finally, let $\pi^{-1}: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ be the inverse of the Cantor pairing bijection $\pi: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$. Then the composite map:

$$
G \circ \pi^{-1}: \mathbb{N} \to \bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n
$$

is a surjection from $\mathbb{N}$ onto $A$. By the surjective characterization of countability, $A = \bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$ is countable! $\blacksquare$

> [!note] A Subtle Foundational Observation on Countable Choice
> Look closely at the first paragraph of the proof above: for each $n \in J$, we knew that *there exists* at least one surjection $g_n: \mathbb{N} \to A_n$, and we simultaneously selected one such surjection $g_n$ for every $n \in J$ to assemble the two-variable function $G(n, m) = g_n(m)$. When the sets $A_n$ are not given with a canonical enumeration in advance, choosing one enumeration $g_n$ from the non-empty collection of all possible enumerations of $A_n$ for each $n \in \mathbb{N}$ implicitly invokes the **Axiom of Countable Choice ($\text{AC}_\omega$)**, which we will examine in detail at the end of this section! Notice, however, that whenever each $A_n$ comes equipped with a specified enumeration or injection into $\mathbb{N}$ (as in all concrete applications to $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$, finite tuples, and algebraic numbers below), no appeal to Choice is needed at all.

Let us showcase the remarkable reach of the Countable Union Theorem by proving that the set of all finite sequences of elements from a countable set, the set of all integer polynomials $\mathbb{Z}[x]$, and the set of all **real algebraic numbers** (including all $n$-th roots $\sqrt[n]{q}$, nested radicals, and roots of arbitrary high-degree polynomial equations like $x^5 - x - 1 = 0$) are all merely *countable*!

> [!abstract] Definition: Algebraic and Transcendental Real Numbers
> A real number $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is called an **algebraic real number** if there exists a non-zero polynomial $P(x) = a_d x^d + a_{d-1} x^{d-1} + \dots + a_1 x + a_0$ with integer coefficients $a_0, a_1, \dots, a_d \in \mathbb{Z}$ (where $d \ge 1$ and $a_d \neq 0$) such that $P(\alpha) = 0$.
>
> A real number $\tau \in \mathbb{R}$ is called **transcendental** if it is not algebraic—that is, if $P(\tau) \neq 0$ for every non-zero polynomial $P(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Finite Sequences, Finite Subsets, and Real Algebraic Numbers are Countable
> 1. Let $A$ be any countable set. Then the set $A^{<\omega} := \bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty} A^k$ of all finite tuples of elements of $A$, as well as the set $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(A) := \{F \subseteq A : F \text{ is finite}\}$ of all finite subsets of $A$, are countable.
> 2. The set $\mathbb{Z}[x]$ of all polynomials with integer coefficients is countably infinite, and the set $\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}$ of all **real algebraic numbers** is countably infinite.

**Proof:**
1. **Finite Tuples and Finite Subsets:** Suppose $A$ is countable. By the finite product theorem proved above, for each fixed positive integer $k \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, the $k$-fold Cartesian product $A^k = A \times A \times \dots \times A$ is countable. Therefore, $A^{<\omega} = \bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty} A^k$ is a countable union of countable sets, so by the Countable Union Theorem, $A^{<\omega}$ is countable!
   Similarly, for any non-empty finite subset $F \subseteq A$ of cardinality $k \ge 1$, there exists a tuple $(a_1, \dots, a_k) \in A^k$ such that $F = \{a_1, \dots, a_k\}$. Thus the map $(a_1, \dots, a_k) \mapsto \{a_1, \dots, a_k\}$ is a surjection from $A^{<\omega}$ onto $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(A) \setminus \{\emptyset\}$. Since $A^{<\omega}$ is countable, $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(A) \setminus \{\emptyset\}$ (and hence $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(A)$ after adjoining the single element $\emptyset$) is countable.

2. **Integer Polynomials and Real Algebraic Numbers:** For each degree $d \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, a polynomial $P(x) = a_d x^d + \dots + a_1 x + a_0 \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ of degree $d$ is uniquely determined by its $(d + 1)$-tuple of coefficients $(a_0, a_1, \dots, a_d) \in \mathbb{Z}^d \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}) \subseteq \mathbb{Z}^{d+1}$. Since $\mathbb{Z}$ is countable, the set $\mathcal{P}_d$ of all integer polynomials of degree $d$ is in bijection with $\mathbb{Z}^d \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$ and is therefore countable. Consequently, the set of all non-constant integer polynomials:
   $$
   \mathcal{P}_{*} := \bigcup_{d=1}^{\infty} \mathcal{P}_d
   $$
   is a countable union of countable sets, hence countably infinite!
   Now, for each polynomial $P \in \mathcal{P}_d$ of degree $d \ge 1$, let $R(P) := \{\alpha \in \mathbb{R} : P(\alpha) = 0\}$ denote its set of real roots. By a standard inductive application of polynomial division in the field $\mathbb{R}$ (if $P(\alpha) = 0$, then $P(x) = (x - \alpha) Q(x)$ where $\deg Q = d - 1$, as $x^k - \alpha^k = (x - \alpha)(x^{k-1} + x^{k-2}\alpha + \dots + \alpha^{k-1})$), a non-zero polynomial $P$ of degree $d$ has **at most $d$ roots** in $\mathbb{R}$, so $|R(P)| \le d$ is finite! (Indeed, since $R(P) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is finite of size $m \le d$, its elements can even be canonically listed in strictly increasing order $\alpha_1 < \alpha_2 < \dots < \alpha_m$ using the total order of $\mathbb{R}$ without any appeal to Choice.)
   Therefore, the set of all real algebraic numbers is:
   $$
   \mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R} = \bigcup_{P \in \mathcal{P}_{*}} R(P),
   $$
   which is a countable union of finite sets, and is thus countable! Finally, since every rational number $q = \frac{a}{b} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (with $a \in \mathbb{Z}, b \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$) is a root of the degree-$1$ integer polynomial $P(x) = bx - a$, we have $\mathbb{Q} \subseteq \mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}$, so $\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}$ is infinite and hence countably infinite ($|\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}| = \aleph_0$). $\blacksquare$

## Cantor's Theorem and the Uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$

### Cantor's Theorem and the Hierarchy of Infinities

Up to this moment, every infinite set we have constructed—$\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$, $\mathbb{N}^k$, $\mathbb{Z}[x]$, and even the set of all real algebraic numbers $\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}$—has turned out to be countably infinite! One might begin to suspect that *all* infinite sets are equinumerous with $\mathbb{N}$.

In 1891, Georg Cantor shattered this illusion with one of the simplest yet deepest arguments in the history of human thought: **Cantor's Diagonal Argument**. Cantor proved that for *any* set $X$ whatsoever, the power set $\mathcal{P}(X)$ of all subsets of $X$ always has a strictly larger cardinality than $X$ itself! Notice the uncanny parallel between Cantor's diagonal subset $D = \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\}$ below and Russell's Paradox from Section 0.1: Russell applied the diagonal construction to the non-existent "set of all sets" to derive a contradiction, whereas Cantor applied it to any function $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ to prove that $f$ can never be surjective!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Cantor's Theorem ($|X| < |\mathcal{P}(X)|$)
> For every set $X$, the cardinality of $X$ is strictly less than the cardinality of its power set $\mathcal{P}(X)$:
> $$
> |X| < |\mathcal{P}(X)|.
> $$
> Explicitly:
> 1. There exists an injection $\iota: X \hookrightarrow \mathcal{P}(X)$ given by $\iota(x) := \{x\}$, so $|X| \le |\mathcal{P}(X)|$.
> 2. There exists **no surjection** $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ (and consequently no bijection between $X$ and $\mathcal{P}(X)$, even if $X = \emptyset$).

**Proof:**
1. **Existence of an Injection:** Define $\iota: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ by mapping each element $x \in X$ to its singleton subset $\iota(x) := \{x\} \in \mathcal{P}(X)$. If $\iota(x_1) = \iota(x_2)$, then $\{x_1\} = \{x_2\}$, so $x_1 \in \{x_2\}$, which forces $x_1 = x_2$. Thus $\iota$ is injective, proving $|X| \le |\mathcal{P}(X)|$.

2. **Non-Existence of a Surjection (Cantor's Diagonal Argument):** Let $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ be **any** function from $X$ to $\mathcal{P}(X)$. We will explicitly construct a subset $D \subseteq X$ (so $D \in \mathcal{P}(X)$) that does not belong to the image $f(X)$, proving that $f$ cannot be surjective.
   For each $x \in X$, its image $f(x)$ is a subset of $X$, so it makes complete sense to ask whether $x$ is a member of the subset $f(x)$ or not. By the Axiom Schema of Specification (Section 0.1), define the **diagonal set** $D \subseteq X$ by collecting precisely those elements of $X$ that do *not* belong to their own assigned subset:
   $$
   D := \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\} \in \mathcal{P}(X).
   $$
   Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $D$ lies in the image of $f$, so there exists some element $x_0 \in X$ such that:
   $$
   f(x_0) = D.
   $$
   Now we ask the fatal question: **Does $x_0$ belong to $D$?**
   - If $x_0 \in D$, then by the defining condition of $D$, we must have $x_0 \notin f(x_0)$. But $f(x_0) = D$, so this says $x_0 \notin D$—a contradiction!
   - If $x_0 \notin D$, then since $f(x_0) = D$, we have $x_0 \notin f(x_0)$. By the defining condition of $D$, any element $x \in X$ satisfying $x \notin f(x)$ belongs to $D$, so $x_0 \in D$—again a contradiction!

   In compact logical notation:
   $$
   x_0 \in D \iff x_0 \notin f(x_0) \iff x_0 \notin D,
   $$
   which is impossible! Therefore, no such $x_0 \in X$ can exist: $D \notin f(X)$, so $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ is not surjective. In particular, there can be no bijection between $X$ and $\mathcal{P}(X)$, so $|X| < |\mathcal{P}(X)|$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] The Endless Hierarchy of Infinities
> Take a moment to appreciate what Cantor's Theorem immediately implies when we start with $X = \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. The power set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ is **uncountable**: since $|\mathbb{N}| < |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|$, there is no bijection between $\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$.
> 2. Even more astonishingly, there is no "largest" infinity! By repeatedly applying the power set operation, we generate an endless, strictly ascending tower of uncountable infinities:
>    $$
>    |\mathbb{N}| < |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| < |\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}))| < |\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})))| < \cdots
>    $$
>    No matter how vast an infinite set $X$ you stand upon, its power set $\mathcal{P}(X)$ towers strictly above it.

### Uncountability of $[0, 1]$ and $\mathbb{R}$ — Proof I via Cantor's Nested Interval Property

Now we turn to the central question of real analysis: **Is the set of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ countable or uncountable?**

Many elementary textbooks prove the uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$ by writing real numbers in base-$10$ decimal expansions $0.d_1 d_2 d_3 \dots$ and flipping the $n$-th digit of the $n$-th number. While vivid, that argument requires developing the entire theory of infinite series and decimal expansions first, and then carefully tiptoeing around the annoying technicality that numbers like $0.5000\dots = 0.4999\dots$ have two distinct decimal representations!

We will do something much cleaner, more rigorous, and completely non-circular:
1. First, in **Proof I** below, we follow Cantor's original 1874 topological insight and prove that every non-degenerate closed interval $[a_0, b_0] \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ (and hence $\mathbb{R}$ itself) is uncountable using **zero decimal or binary expansion machinery whatsoever**—relying directly on **Cantor's Nested Interval Property** proved in Section 0.6!
2. Second, in **Proof II** immediately afterward, we go further and determine the *exact* cardinality of $\mathbb{R}$, proving via the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem** that $|\mathbb{R}| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|$ without ever needing to worry about duplicate binary expansions!

> [!theorem] Theorem: Uncountability of $[0, 1]$ and $\mathbb{R}$ (Proof I via Cantor's Nested Interval Property)
> For any real numbers $a_0 < b_0$, the closed interval $I_0 = [a_0, b_0]$ is **uncountable**. Consequently, the set of all real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable.

**Proof:**
Since $a_0 < b_0$, the interval $I_0 = [a_0, b_0]$ is non-empty (in fact infinite), so to prove that $I_0$ is uncountable (and that $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable), it suffices by our characterization of countability to show that **no function $x: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to \mathbb{R}$ can have an image containing $I_0$**. Equivalently, given *any* sequence of real numbers $(x_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ in $\mathbb{R}$, we will construct a real number $\xi \in [a_0, b_0]$ such that $\xi \neq x_n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$!

Let $(x_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ be an arbitrary sequence in $\mathbb{R}$. We construct by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ a nested sequence of closed, bounded intervals $I_0 \supseteq I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq \cdots$, where $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ satisfies:
1. $a_n < b_n$ and $b_n - a_n = \frac{b_0 - a_0}{3^n}$ for all $n \ge 0$;
2. $x_n \notin I_n$ for all $n \ge 1$.

- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** We start with $I_0 = [a_0, b_0]$, which satisfies $a_0 < b_0$ and $b_0 - a_0 = \frac{b_0 - a_0}{3^0}$.
- **Inductive Step ($n - 1 \to n$):** Suppose $I_{n-1} = [a_{n-1}, b_{n-1}]$ with $a_{n-1} < b_{n-1}$ has been constructed. Let $\delta_n := \frac{b_{n-1} - a_{n-1}}{3} > 0$, and divide $I_{n-1}$ into three equal closed subintervals by trisecting at $a_{n-1} + \delta_n$ and $a_{n-1} + 2\delta_n$:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  J_1 &:= [a_{n-1},\, a_{n-1} + \delta_n], \\
  J_2 &:= [a_{n-1} + \delta_n,\, a_{n-1} + 2\delta_n], \\
  J_3 &:= [a_{n-1} + 2\delta_n,\, b_{n-1}].
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Notice that the first subinterval $J_1$ and the third subinterval $J_3$ are **completely disjoint**: since $\delta_n > 0$, every element $u \in J_1$ and $v \in J_3$ satisfy $u \le a_{n-1} + \delta_n < a_{n-1} + 2\delta_n \le v$, so $J_1 \cap J_3 = \emptyset$!
  Therefore, the single real number $x_n$ **cannot belong to both $J_1$ and $J_3$**. To make our choice completely deterministic (requiring no Axiom of Choice!), we define:
  $$
  I_n = [a_n, b_n] := \begin{cases} J_1 & \text{if } x_n \notin J_1, \\ J_3 & \text{if } x_n \in J_1 \text{ (which forces } x_n \notin J_3\text{)}. \end{cases}
  $$
  In either case, $I_n \subseteq I_{n-1}$ is a closed, bounded interval of length $b_n - a_n = \delta_n = \frac{b_{n-1} - a_{n-1}}{3} = \frac{b_0 - a_0}{3^n} > 0$, and $x_n \notin I_n$!

Now, since $I_0 \supseteq I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq \cdots$ is a nested sequence of non-empty, closed, bounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$, **Cantor's Nested Interval Property** (Section 0.6) guarantees that their intersection is non-empty: there exists a real number

$$
\xi \in \bigcap_{n=0}^{\infty} I_n.
$$

(In fact, since $\inf_{n \ge 1} (b_n - a_n) = \inf_{n \ge 1} \frac{b_0 - a_0}{3^n} = 0$, the intersection $\bigcap_{n=0}^\infty I_n = \{\xi\}$ consists of a single unique point $\xi = \sup_{n \ge 0} a_n = \inf_{n \ge 0} b_n$.)
- First, since $\xi \in I_0 = [a_0, b_0]$, the number $\xi$ lies in $[a_0, b_0]$.
- Second, for **every** $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, we have $\xi \in I_n$ while $x_n \notin I_n$ by construction, which immediately implies:
  $$
  \xi \neq x_n \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}!
  $$

Thus no sequence $(x_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ can exhaust $[a_0, b_0]$. It follows that $[a_0, b_0]$ (in particular $[0, 1]$) is uncountable, and since $[0, 1] \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, the set $\mathbb{R}$ cannot be countable either (as any injection $\mathbb{R} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}$ would restrict to an injection $[0, 1] \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}$). Hence $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable! $\blacksquare$

### Cardinality of the Continuum: $|\mathbb{R}| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| = \mathfrak{c}$ — Proof II via Binary/Ternary Maps and Schröder–Bernstein

Now we know that both $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ and $\mathbb{R}$ are uncountable. How do their uncountable cardinalities compare? Remarkably, they are **equal**: the cardinality of the real line $\mathbb{R}$, traditionally denoted by the Fraktur letter $\mathfrak{c}$ (for the **cardinality of the continuum**), is the exact same infinity as the power set of the natural numbers $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$, as well as the set $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}}$ of all infinite binary sequences!

Let us prove this chain of equinumerosities step-by-step using explicit, elementary injections and the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem**, completely avoiding any need to resolve ambiguous binary expansions.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Cardinality of the Continuum ($|\mathbb{R}| = |(0, 1)| = |[0, 1]| = |\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}}| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| = \mathfrak{c}$)
> The set of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$, every open interval $(a, b)$ and closed interval $[a, b]$ with $a < b$, the set $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$ of all infinite binary sequences, and the power set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{>0})$ (and $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$) are all equinumerous:
> $$
> |\mathbb{R}| = |(0, 1)| = |[0, 1]| = \bigl|\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}\bigr| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| =: \mathfrak{c} > \aleph_0.
> $$

**Proof:**
We establish the equinumerosities in four clean, modular steps:

**Step 1: $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{>0}) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$ via Characteristic Functions.**
Let $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$ denote the set of all functions $d: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to \{0, 1\}$ (i.e., infinite binary sequences $(d_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ where each $d_n \in \{0, 1\}$). As established in Section 0.1, the map $\Psi: \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{>0}) \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$ that sends each subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ to its **characteristic (indicator) function** $\chi_S: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to \{0, 1\}$ defined by:

$$
\chi_S(n) := \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if } n \in S, \\ 0 & \text{if } n \notin S \end{cases}
$$

is a bijection, with explicit inverse $\Psi^{-1}(d) = \{n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0} : d_n = 1\}$. Since $\mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, we have $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}) \sim \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{>0}) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$.

**Step 2: $(a, b) \sim (0, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$ and $[0, 1] \sim (0, 1)$ via Explicit Algebraic Maps.**
- First, for any real numbers $a < b$, the affine linear function $\ell: (0, 1) \to (a, b)$ given by $\ell(t) := a + (b - a)t$ is a strictly increasing bijection with inverse $\ell^{-1}(y) = \frac{y - a}{b - a}$ (and similarly $[0, 1] \sim [a, b]$).
- Second, to prove $(0, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$ without using trigonometric functions (since $\tan x$ will only be constructed later in analysis!), consider the elementary rational function $f: (0, 1) \to \mathbb{R}$ defined by:
  $$
  f(x) := \frac{2x - 1}{x(1 - x)} = \frac{1}{1 - x} - \frac{1}{x}.
  $$
  Let us check that $f: (0, 1) \to \mathbb{R}$ is a bijection:
  - *Injectivity (Strict Monotonicity):* Take any $0 < x_1 < x_2 < 1$. Then $0 < 1 - x_2 < 1 - x_1 < 1$, so taking reciprocals in the ordered field $\mathbb{R}$ gives $\frac{1}{1 - x_1} < \frac{1}{1 - x_2}$ and $-\frac{1}{x_1} < -\frac{1}{x_2}$. Adding these two strict inequalities yields $f(x_1) < f(x_2)$, so $f$ is strictly increasing and hence injective!
  - *Surjectivity:* Let $y \in \mathbb{R}$ be arbitrary. If $y = 0$, then $f(1/2) = 0$. If $y \neq 0$, solving $\frac{2x - 1}{x(1 - x)} = y \iff y x^2 - (y + 2)x + 1 = 0$ by the quadratic formula (which is valid in $\mathbb{R}$ since $(y + 2)^2 - 4y = y^2 + 4 > 0$ has a positive square root $\sqrt{y^2 + 4} > |y|$ in $\mathbb{R}$) gives:
    $$
    x := \frac{y + 2 - \sqrt{y^2 + 4}}{2y} = \frac{2}{y + 2 + \sqrt{y^2 + 4}}.
    $$
    Since $\sqrt{y^2 + 4} > \sqrt{y^2} = |y| \ge -y$, the denominator satisfies $y + 2 + \sqrt{y^2 + 4} > y + 2 - y = 2 > 0$, which immediately guarantees $0 < x < \frac{2}{2} = 1$ and $f(x) = y$! Thus $f: (0, 1) \to \mathbb{R}$ is a bijection.
- Third, since $(0, 1) \subseteq [0, 1] \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, we have $|(0, 1)| \le |[0, 1]| \le |\mathbb{R}| = |(0, 1)|$, so by the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem**, $|(0, 1)| = |[0, 1]| = |\mathbb{R}|$.

**Step 3: $|\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}| \le |[0, 1]|$ via the Ternary Cantor Injection.**
How do we inject $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$ into $[0, 1]$ without getting tripped up by duplicate binary representations like $0.1000\dots_2 = 0.0111\dots_2$? We work in **base $3$ (ternary)** using only the digits $0$ and $2$!
For each binary sequence $d = (d_n)_{n=1}^{\infty} \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$ and each $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, consider the finite partial sum:

$$
S_N(d) := \sum_{n=1}^{N} \frac{2 d_n}{3^n}.
$$

Since $d_n \in \{0, 1\}$, the partial sums satisfy $0 \le S_1(d) \le S_2(d) \le \cdots$, and by the elementary geometric sum identity $\sum_{n=1}^N \frac{1}{3^n} = \frac{1 - 3^{-N}}{2} < \frac{1}{2}$ (proved by induction on $N$), every partial sum is bounded above by $1$:

$$
0 \le S_N(d) \le \sum_{n=1}^{N} \frac{2}{3^n} = 1 - \frac{1}{3^N} < 1.
$$

By the **Least Upper Bound Property** of $\mathbb{R}$, the bounded-above non-empty set $\{S_N(d) : N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}$ has a supremum in $[0, 1]$, allowing us to define $\Phi: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}} \to [0, 1]$ by:

$$
\Phi(d) := \sup_{N \ge 1} \sum_{n=1}^{N} \frac{2 d_n}{3^n} \in [0, 1].
$$

Let us prove that $\Phi$ is **strictly injective**! Suppose $d = (d_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ and $e = (e_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ are two distinct sequences in $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}$. By the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$, let $k := \min \{n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0} : d_n \neq e_n\}$ be the very first index where $d$ and $e$ differ. Without loss of generality, suppose $d_k = 1$ and $e_k = 0$ (while $d_n = e_n$ for all $1 \le n < k$).
- For the sequence $d$, since $d_k = 1$ and all terms are non-negative, for every $N \ge k$ we have $S_N(d) \ge S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{2}{3^k}$, so:
  $$
  \Phi(d) \ge S_k(d) = S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{2}{3^k}.
  $$
- For the sequence $e$, since $e_n = d_n$ for $n < k$, $e_k = 0$, and $e_n \le 1$ for all $n > k$, for every $N > k$ we have:
  $$
  S_N(e) \le S_{k-1}(d) + 0 + \sum_{n=k+1}^{N} \frac{2}{3^n} = S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{1}{3^k}\left(1 - \frac{1}{3^{N-k}}\right) \le S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{1}{3^k}.
  $$
  Since $S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{1}{3^k}$ is an upper bound for $\{S_N(e) : N \ge 1\}$, taking the supremum gives $\Phi(e) \le S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{1}{3^k}$.
- Combining these two inequalities yields:
  $$
  \Phi(d) - \Phi(e) \ge \left(S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{2}{3^k}\right) - \left(S_{k-1}(d) + \frac{1}{3^k}\right) = \frac{1}{3^k} > 0,
  $$
  so $\Phi(d) > \Phi(e)$! Thus $\Phi: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}} \to [0, 1]$ is injective, proving $|\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| = |\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}| \le |[0, 1]| = |\mathbb{R}|$.

**Step 4: $|\mathbb{R}| \le |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q})| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|$ via the Dedekind Cut Map.**
To obtain the reverse inequality $|\mathbb{R}| \le |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|$, we exploit the **Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$** (Section 0.5)! Define the Dedekind-cut map $\Theta: \mathbb{R} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q})$ by sending each real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ to the set of all rational numbers strictly less than $x$:

$$
\Theta(x) := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q < x\} \in \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q}).
$$

If $x_1, x_2 \in \mathbb{R}$ are distinct, say $x_1 < x_2$, then by the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ there exists a rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $x_1 < r < x_2$. Then $r \in \Theta(x_2)$ (since $r < x_2$) whereas $r \notin \Theta(x_1)$ (since $r > x_1$), which means $\Theta(x_1) \neq \Theta(x_2)$! Thus $\Theta: \mathbb{R} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q})$ is injective.

Finally, because $\mathbb{Q} \sim \mathbb{N}$, any bijection $\beta: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{N}$ induces a bijection $B: \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Q}) \to \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ given by $B(E) := \beta(E) = \{\beta(q) : q \in E\}$ (with inverse $B^{-1}(S) = \beta^{-1}(S)$). Composing $B$ with $\Theta$ gives an injection $B \circ \Theta: \mathbb{R} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$, proving:

$$
|\mathbb{R}| \le |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|.
$$

Since we have proved both $|\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| \le |\mathbb{R}|$ (Step 3) and $|\mathbb{R}| \le |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|$ (Step 4), the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem** immediately delivers $|\mathbb{R}| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| = \mathfrak{c} > \aleph_0$! $\blacksquare$

### Existence and Uncountability of Transcendental Real Numbers

In 1844, Joseph Liouville worked tirelessly using delicate Diophantine approximation inequalities just to exhibit a *single* transcendental real number (such as $\sum_{n=1}^\infty 10^{-n!}$), and proving that specific constants like $e$ (Hermite, 1873) or $\pi$ (Lindemann, 1882) are transcendental required monumental analytic machinery.

Just one year after Hermite's proof for $e$, Georg Cantor stunned the mathematical community by proving in a few lines that **transcendental numbers not only exist, they are uncountably infinite**—in fact, almost all real numbers are transcendental! His proof did not construct a single digit of a transcendental number; instead, it simply compared the cardinality of the algebraic numbers ($|\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}| = \aleph_0$) against the cardinality of the continuum ($|\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c} > \aleph_0$).

> [!theorem] Corollary: Existence and Uncountability of Transcendental Real Numbers
> Let $\mathbb{I} := \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ denote the set of irrational real numbers, and let $\mathbb{T} := \mathbb{R} \setminus (\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R})$ denote the set of **transcendental real numbers**. Both $\mathbb{I}$ and $\mathbb{T}$ are uncountable; in fact, they have the full cardinality of the continuum:
> $$
> |\mathbb{I}| = |\mathbb{T}| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}.
> $$

**Proof:**
First, let us see why $\mathbb{T}$ (and hence $\mathbb{I} \supseteq \mathbb{T}$) cannot be countable: we can write $\mathbb{R}$ as the union of the algebraic real numbers and the transcendental real numbers:

$$
\mathbb{R} = (\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}) \cup \mathbb{T}.
$$

We already proved that $\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}$ is countable. If $\mathbb{T}$ were also countable, then $\mathbb{R} = (\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}) \cup \mathbb{T}$ would be the union of two countable sets, which is countable by the Countable Union Theorem—directly contradicting the uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$! Hence $\mathbb{T}$ must be uncountable (and in particular non-empty, guaranteeing the existence of transcendental numbers).

Even better, we can prove the exact cardinal equality $|\mathbb{T}| = |\mathbb{R}|$ (and thus $|\mathbb{I}| = |\mathbb{R}|$ since $\mathbb{T} \subseteq \mathbb{I} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$) constructively via Hilbert's Hotel without the Axiom of Choice!
Since $\mathbb{T}$ is uncountable and in particular infinite, and since we can explicitly inject $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{T}$—for instance, take any fixed $\tau_0 \in \mathbb{T}$ and consider the sequence of distinct numbers $x_n := \tau_0 + n$ for $n \in \mathbb{N}$: every $\tau_0 + n$ must be transcendental, because if $P(\tau_0 + n) = 0$ for some non-zero integer polynomial $P(x) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$, then $\tau_0$ would be a root of the non-zero integer polynomial $Q(x) := P(x + n) \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$, contradicting $\tau_0 \in \mathbb{T}$!
Thus $C := \{\tau_0 + n : n \in \mathbb{N}\} \subseteq \mathbb{T}$ is an explicit countably infinite subset of $\mathbb{T}$. Since $\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}$ and $C$ are both countably infinite ($|\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}| = |C| = \aleph_0$), the disjoint union $(\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}) \cup C$ is also countably infinite, so there exists a bijection $\beta: (\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}) \cup C \to C$. Extending $\beta$ to be the identity on $\mathbb{T} \setminus C$ defines a bijection $H: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{T}$:

$$
H(x) := \begin{cases} \beta(x) & \text{if } x \in (\mathbb{A} \cap \mathbb{R}) \cup C, \\ x & \text{if } x \in \mathbb{T} \setminus C. \end{cases}
$$

Hence $\mathbb{R} \sim \mathbb{T}$, and since $\mathbb{T} \subseteq \mathbb{I} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem yields $|\mathbb{T}| = |\mathbb{I}| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}$. $\blacksquare$

## The Axiom of Choice, Countable Choice in Analysis, and Zorn's Lemma

### Three Equivalent Formulations of the Axiom of Choice

In Section 0.1, when we laid down the Zermelo–Fraenkel (ZF) axioms of set theory, we noted that the standard foundation of modern mathematics is **ZFC**—ZF augmented with one final, uniquely non-constructive axiom formulated by Ernst Zermelo in 1904: the **Axiom of Choice (AC)**.

Why do we need a special axiom just to "choose" elements from sets? If we have a *single* non-empty set $A \neq \emptyset$, the logical rules of existential instantiation in first-order logic allow us to name an element $a \in A$; by induction, if we have any *finite* collection of non-empty sets $A_1, A_2, \dots, A_n$, we can pick $a_1 \in A_1, a_2 \in A_2, \dots, a_n \in A_n$ in $n$ steps within pure ZF set theory. Furthermore, even if we have an *infinite* family of non-empty sets $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$, if we possess an explicit rule that singles out a unique element of each $A_i$—for instance, if every $A_i$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$, where we can canonically define $c(A_i) := \min A_i$ via the Well-Ordering Principle—then the Axiom Schema of Replacement/Specification constructs the function $i \mapsto c(A_i)$ inside pure ZF without any extra axiom!

The crisis arises when we have an **infinite family of non-empty sets $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ with no canonical rule to distinguish one element of $A_i$ from another**. The ZF axioms only allow us to form a set by specifying a definite property $\phi(x)$ that its elements satisfy; they do not automatically guarantee the existence of a function $c$ that simultaneously reaches into infinitely many unstructured sets $A_i$ and plucks out one element $c(i) \in A_i$ from each. The **Axiom of Choice** postulates that such a simultaneous selection function always exists.

Let us first define the general Cartesian product of an arbitrary indexed family of sets before stating and proving the equivalence of the three most common formulations of the Axiom of Choice.

> [!abstract] Definition: Choice Functions and Arbitrary Cartesian Products
> 1. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a collection of non-empty sets (so $\emptyset \notin \mathcal{F}$). A **choice function** on $\mathcal{F}$ is a function
>    $$
>    c: \mathcal{F} \to \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A
>    $$
>    such that $c(A) \in A$ for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$.
> 2. Let $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ be a family of sets indexed by an arbitrary set $I$. The **Cartesian product** $\prod_{i \in I} A_i$ is the set of all functions $x: I \to \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$ such that $x(i) \in A_i$ for every $i \in I$:
>    $$
>    \prod_{i \in I} A_i := \left\{ x: I \to \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i \;\middle|\; \forall i \in I,\, x(i) \in A_i \right\}.
>    $$

> [!theorem] Theorem: Three Equivalent Formulations of the Axiom of Choice (AC)
> Within Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory (ZF), the following three principles are logically equivalent (and any one of them may be taken as the **Axiom of Choice**):
> 1. **Existence of Choice Functions:** For every collection $\mathcal{F}$ of non-empty sets, there exists a choice function $c: \mathcal{F} \to \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$ satisfying $c(A) \in A$ for all $A \in \mathcal{F}$.
> 2. **Non-Emptiness of Infinite Cartesian Products:** For every indexed family $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ of non-empty sets (where $A_i \neq \emptyset$ for all $i \in I$), the Cartesian product $\prod_{i \in I} A_i$ is non-empty.
> 3. **Existence of Right Inverses (Sections) for Surjections:** For every surjective function $f: X \to Y$, there exists a function $s: Y \to X$ (called a **right inverse** or **section** of $f$) such that $f \circ s = \text{id}_Y$—that is, $f(s(y)) = y$ for all $y \in Y$ (which immediately implies that $s: Y \to X$ is injective!).

**Proof:**
We prove the circular chain of implications $(1) \implies (2) \implies (3) \implies (1)$ in pure ZF:

- $(1 \implies 2)$ Suppose (1) holds, and let $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ be an indexed family of non-empty sets. If $I = \emptyset$, the empty function $\emptyset \to \emptyset$ belongs to $\prod_{i \in \emptyset} A_i$, so the product is non-empty. If $I \neq \emptyset$, let $\mathcal{F} := \{A_i : i \in I\}$ be the collection of these non-empty sets. By (1), there exists a choice function $c: \mathcal{F} \to \bigcup_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A = \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$ such that $c(A) \in A$ for all $A \in \mathcal{F}$. Define a function $x: I \to \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$ by setting $x(i) := c(A_i)$ for each $i \in I$. Then $x(i) = c(A_i) \in A_i$ for every $i \in I$, which means $x \in \prod_{i \in I} A_i$. Hence $\prod_{i \in I} A_i \neq \emptyset$.

- $(2 \implies 3)$ Suppose (2) holds, and let $f: X \to Y$ be any surjective function. For each $y \in Y$, consider the preimage fiber:
  $$
  A_y := f^{-1}(\{y\}) = \{x \in X : f(x) = y\} \subseteq X.
  $$
  Because $f: X \to Y$ is surjective, the fiber $A_y$ is **non-empty** for every $y \in Y$! Therefore, $\{A_y\}_{y \in Y}$ is an indexed family of non-empty sets indexed by $Y$. By (2), the Cartesian product $\prod_{y \in Y} A_y$ is non-empty, so there exists an element $s \in \prod_{y \in Y} A_y$. By definition of the Cartesian product, $s$ is a function $s: Y \to \bigcup_{y \in Y} A_y = X$ satisfying $s(y) \in A_y = f^{-1}(\{y\})$ for every $y \in Y$—which means precisely that $f(s(y)) = y$ for all $y \in Y$, so $f \circ s = \text{id}_Y$! (Moreover, if $s(y_1) = s(y_2)$, applying $f$ gives $y_1 = f(s(y_1)) = f(s(y_2)) = y_2$, so $s$ is automatically an injection $Y \hookrightarrow X$.)

- $(3 \implies 1)$ Suppose (3) holds, and let $\mathcal{F}$ be any collection of non-empty sets. Consider the set of ordered pairs:
  $$
  X := \left\{ (A, a) \in \mathcal{F} \times \bigcup_{B \in \mathcal{F}} B \;\middle|\; a \in A \right\},
  $$
  together with the first-coordinate projection map $\pi_1: X \to \mathcal{F}$ defined by $\pi_1(A, a) := A$, and the second-coordinate projection map $\pi_2: X \to \bigcup_{B \in \mathcal{F}} B$ defined by $\pi_2(A, a) := a$. Because every set $A \in \mathcal{F}$ is non-empty, for each $A \in \mathcal{F}$ there exists at least one $a \in A$, so $(A, a) \in X$ and $\pi_1(A, a) = A$; thus $\pi_1: X \to \mathcal{F}$ is **surjective**!
  By (3), $\pi_1$ has a right inverse $s: \mathcal{F} \to X$ satisfying $\pi_1(s(A)) = A$ for all $A \in \mathcal{F}$. This means that for each $A \in \mathcal{F}$, the pair $s(A) \in X$ must have first coordinate $A$, so $s(A) = (A, a)$ for some $a \in A$. Therefore, defining $c: \mathcal{F} \to \bigcup_{B \in \mathcal{F}} B$ by $c(A) := \pi_2(s(A))$ gives $c(A) \in A$ for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$, so $c$ is a choice function on $\mathcal{F}$. $\blacksquare$

### Why Real Analysis Needs Countable Choice ($\text{AC}_{\omega}$) and Dependent Choice ($\text{DC}$)

To clarify why Choice is sometimes needed and sometimes not, Bertrand Russell proposed a famous everyday analogy: **shoes vs. socks**.

Suppose a millionaire possesses an infinite sequence of pairs of **shoes** $(P_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$, and wants to select one shoe from each pair. Do they need the Axiom of Choice? **No!** In every pair of shoes $P_n$, the two shoes are intrinsically distinguishable—there is a unique *left shoe* and a unique *right shoe*—so the rule *"from each pair $P_n$, pick the left shoe"* explicitly defines a choice function inside pure ZF set theory. In mathematics, non-empty subsets $A_n \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ (or finite subsets $F_n \subseteq \mathbb{R}$) are like pairs of shoes: the total order and Well-Ordering Principle let us canonically specify *"pick the minimum element $\min A_n$"* without invoking Choice!

Now suppose instead that the millionaire possesses an infinite sequence of pairs of **indistinguishable socks** $(S_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$. Because the two socks in each pair $S_n$ have no intrinsic left/right distinction, there is no uniform formula that singles out one sock from every pair simultaneously; to select one sock from each $S_n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, one must invoke the **Axiom of Choice**!

In everyday real analysis, we almost never need the full strength of the Axiom of Choice over arbitrary uncountable index sets $I$; instead, virtually all of classical real analysis relies on two mild, natural countable versions of Choice:

> [!abstract] Definition: Axiom of Countable Choice ($\text{AC}_{\omega}$) and Principle of Dependent Choices ($\text{DC}$)
> 1. **Axiom of Countable Choice ($\text{AC}_{\omega}$):** For every **countable** family $\{A_n\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ of non-empty sets ($A_n \neq \emptyset$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$), there exists a choice function $c: \mathbb{N} \to \bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$ such that $c(n) \in A_n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. **Principle of Dependent Choices ($\text{DC}$):** Let $X$ be a non-empty set and let $R \subseteq X \times X$ be a binary relation on $X$ such that for every $x \in X$, there exists at least one $y \in X$ with $(x, y) \in R$ (we write $x \mathrel{R} y$). Then for any starting element $x_0 \in X$, there exists an infinite sequence $(x_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ in $X$ starting at $x_0$ such that
>    $$
>    x_n \mathrel{R} x_{n+1} \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}.
>    $$

> [!note] Where $\text{AC}_{\omega}$ and $\text{DC}$ Appear Across Real Analysis
> Why do analysts care so deeply about $\text{AC}_{\omega}$ and $\text{DC}$? Because without them, the sequential methods that form the lifeblood of real analysis would break down! Here are three ubiquitous places where you will use them:
> 1. **Sequential Approximation of Supremums ($\text{AC}_{\omega}$):** Let $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be a non-empty bounded-above subset with $s = \sup E$. By the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum (Section 0.4), for each $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, the set
>    $$
>    A_n := \left\{ x \in E : s - \frac{1}{n} < x \le s \right\}
>    $$
>    is non-empty. However, because $E$ is an arbitrary subset of $\mathbb{R}$ (not a subset of $\mathbb{N}$!), $A_n$ has no minimum element in general—its elements are like Russell's socks! To conclude that there exists a sequence $(x_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ in $E$ satisfying $s - \frac{1}{n} < x_n \le s$ for all $n \ge 1$ (so $x_n \to \sup E$), we apply $\text{AC}_{\omega}$ to the countable family $\{A_n\}_{n=1}^{\infty}$.
> 2. **Countable Union of Countable Sets ($\text{AC}_{\omega}$):** As we noted earlier, if $(A_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ is a sequence of non-empty countable sets, each $A_n$ has a non-empty set $\mathcal{S}_n$ of surjections $\mathbb{N} \to A_n$, and $\text{AC}_{\omega}$ lets us choose one surjection $g_n \in \mathcal{S}_n$ for each $n \in \mathbb{N}$ simultaneously. (Astonishingly, Paul Cohen and Solomon Feferman proved in the 1960s that in pure ZF without $\text{AC}_{\omega}$, it is logically consistent for $\mathbb{R}$ to be a countable union of countable sets!)
> 3. **Inductive Step-by-Step Sequence Constructions ($\text{DC}$):** Often in analysis, the choice of the $(n+1)$-st point $x_{n+1}$ depends on which point $x_n$ was chosen at step $n$ (for example, choosing a strictly decreasing sequence $x_0 > x_1 > x_2 > \cdots$ inside a set with no minimum, or extracting a subsequence with a desired property, or proving in Problem 0.7.5 that every infinite set $X$ contains a countably infinite subset by picking $x_{n+1} \in X \setminus \{x_0, \dots, x_n\}$). That step-by-step dependent selection is precisely what $\text{DC}$ guarantees (and in fact $\text{AC} \implies \text{DC} \implies \text{AC}_{\omega}$ in ZF).

### Partially Ordered Sets (Posets) and Zorn's Lemma

While $\text{AC}_{\omega}$ and $\text{DC}$ handle countable sequential constructions, what happens in higher analysis, linear algebra, and topology when we need to construct a maximal object over an *uncountable* domain—such as a basis for an infinite-dimensional vector space (like $\mathbb{R}$ over $\mathbb{Q}$), a maximal ideal in a ring of functions, or a Hahn–Banach extension of a linear functional?

In practice, mathematicians almost never invoke the raw Axiom of Choice by building transfinite recursive sequences directly. Instead, they invoke a vastly more convenient order-theoretic reformulation discovered by Kazimierz Kuratowski (1922) and Max Zorn (1935): **Zorn's Lemma**. To state Zorn's Lemma cleanly, let us introduce the language of **partially ordered sets (posets)**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Partially Ordered Sets (Posets), Chains, Upper Bounds, and Maximal Elements
> 1. **Partially Ordered Set (Poset):** A **partial order** on a set $P$ is a binary relation $\preceq$ on $P$ that satisfies three axioms for all $x, y, z \in P$:
>    - **Reflexivity:** $x \preceq x$.
>    - **Antisymmetry:** If $x \preceq y$ and $y \preceq x$, then $x = y$.
>    - **Transitivity:** If $x \preceq y$ and $y \preceq z$, then $x \preceq z$.
>    The pair $(P, \preceq)$ is called a **partially ordered set** (or **poset**). Unlike a total order (Section 0.3), two elements $x, y \in P$ in a general poset **do not need to be comparable**: it is entirely possible that neither $x \preceq y$ nor $y \preceq x$ holds! (The prototypical example is $(\mathcal{P}(X), \subseteq)$, the power set of a set $X$ ordered by set inclusion $\subseteq$.)
> 2. **Chain (Totally Ordered Subset):** A subset $C \subseteq P$ of a poset $(P, \preceq)$ is called a **chain** (or a **totally ordered subset**) if every two elements of $C$ are comparable:
>    $$
>    \forall x, y \in C, \quad x \preceq y \quad \text{or} \quad y \preceq x.
>    $$
> 3. **Upper Bound of a Subset:** Let $S \subseteq P$ be a subset of a poset $(P, \preceq)$. An element $u \in P$ is called an **upper bound** of $S$ in $P$ if $x \preceq u$ for all $x \in S$.
> 4. **Maximal Element:** An element $m \in P$ is called a **maximal element** of $(P, \preceq)$ if no element of $P$ is strictly larger than $m$—that is, for all $x \in P$:
>    $$
>    m \preceq x \implies x = m.
>    $$

> [!warning] Crucial Distinction: "Maximal Element" vs. "Greatest (Maximum) Element"
> Do **not** confuse a **maximal element** $m \in P$ with a **greatest (or maximum) element** $g \in P$!
> - A **greatest element** $g \in P$ must dominate *every* element of $P$: $x \preceq g$ for **all** $x \in P$ (which forces $g$ to be comparable to every element of $P$, and unique if it exists).
> - A **maximal element** $m \in P$ merely requires that *nothing in $P$ sits strictly above $m$*: if $x \in P$ is comparable to $m$ with $m \preceq x$, then $x = m$, while elements $y \in P$ that are incomparable to $m$ are completely allowed!
>
> For a concrete example, consider the poset $P = \{\{1\}, \{2\}, \{1, 2\}, \{1, 3\}\}$ ordered by inclusion $\subseteq$. Neither $\{1, 2\}$ nor $\{1, 3\}$ is a greatest element of $P$ (since $\{1, 2\} \not\subseteq \{1, 3\}$ and $\{1, 3\} \not\subseteq \{1, 2\}$), yet **both $\{1, 2\}$ and $\{1, 3\}$ are maximal elements of $P$**, because no set in $P$ strictly contains $\{1, 2\}$ and no set in $P$ strictly contains $\{1, 3\}$!

Now we can state **Zorn's Lemma** and **Zermelo's Well-Ordering Theorem**, which together with the Axiom of Choice form the famous "trinity" of equivalent set-theoretic principles in ZFC.

> [!theorem] Theorem: Zorn's Lemma and the Well-Ordering Theorem (Equivalence to AC in ZF)
> Within Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory (ZF), the following three foundational theorems are logically equivalent to one another:
> 1. **The Axiom of Choice (AC):** Every collection of non-empty sets admits a choice function.
> 2. **Zorn's Lemma:** Let $(P, \preceq)$ be a **non-empty** partially ordered set. If **every chain $C \subseteq P$ has an upper bound in $P$**, then $P$ possesses at least one **maximal element** $m \in P$.
> 3. **Zermelo's Well-Ordering Theorem:** Every set $X$ can be **well-ordered**—that is, there exists a total order $\le$ on $X$ such that every non-empty subset $S \subseteq X$ has a least element with respect to $\le$.

> [!note] How Zorn's Lemma is Used in Practice (The Standard Blueprint)
> Whenever you apply Zorn's Lemma in analysis or algebra (as you will do in Problem 0.7.8 to construct a Hamel basis of $\mathbb{R}$ over $\mathbb{Q}$), the proof always follows a clean four-step blueprint:
> 1. **Define the Poset $(P, \subseteq)$:** Let $P$ be the collection of all "candidate partial objects" (for example, all $\mathbb{Q}$-linearly independent subsets $L \subseteq \mathbb{R}$), ordered by set inclusion $\subseteq$.
> 2. **Verify $P \neq \emptyset$:** Exhibit at least one simple element in $P$ (for example, the singleton $\{1\} \in P$ or $\emptyset \in P$).
> 3. **Bound Every Chain via Union:** Take an arbitrary non-empty chain $\mathcal{C} \subseteq P$ and consider its union $U := \bigcup_{L \in \mathcal{C}} L$. Because any *finite* collection of elements $x_1, \dots, x_k \in U$ belongs to $L_1, \dots, L_k \in \mathcal{C}$, and because $\mathcal{C}$ is totally ordered by $\subseteq$, there is a largest set $L_{\max} \in \{L_1, \dots, L_k\}$ containing *all* of $x_1, \dots, x_k$ simultaneously! Thus any finitary condition (like linear independence) automatically transfers from $L_{\max} \in P$ to the union $U$, proving $U \in P$ is an upper bound for $\mathcal{C}$.
> 4. **Interpret Maximality:** Invoke Zorn's Lemma to obtain a maximal element $M \in P$, and show by contradiction that if $M$ were not a complete object (e.g., if $M$ did not span $\mathbb{R}$), you could adjoin one missing element $x \notin \text{span}(M)$ to form a strictly larger candidate $M \cup \{x\} \in P$ with $M \subsetneq M \cup \{x\}$, contradicting the maximality of $M$!

## 0.7 Exercises

### Problem 0.7.1: Disjoint Open Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$ (The Countable Chain Condition)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\mathcal{I} = \{(a_\alpha, b_\alpha) : \alpha \in \Lambda\}$ be an arbitrary family of pairwise disjoint non-empty open intervals in $\mathbb{R}$ (meaning that $a_\alpha < b_\alpha$ for every $\alpha \in \Lambda$, and $(a_\alpha, b_\alpha) \cap (a_\beta, b_\beta) = \emptyset$ whenever $\alpha \neq \beta$ in $\Lambda$). Using the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and the countability of $\mathbb{Q}$, prove **without using the Axiom of Choice** that the index set $\Lambda$ is at most countable.

### Problem 0.7.2: Monotone Functions on $\mathbb{R}$ Have At Most Countably Many Jump Discontinuities

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be a monotonically increasing function on $\mathbb{R}$ (meaning that $x < y \implies f(x) \le f(y)$ for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$). For each $x \in \mathbb{R}$, define the left-hand and right-hand order limits by:
> $$
> f(x^-) := \sup \{f(t) : t < x\} \quad \text{and} \quad f(x^+) := \inf \{f(t) : t > x\}.
> $$
> 1. Prove using the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ that $f(x^-)$ and $f(x^+)$ are well-defined real numbers satisfying $f(x^-) \le f(x) \le f(x^+)$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
> 2. Prove that whenever $x < y$ in $\mathbb{R}$, we have $f(x^+) \le f(y^-)$, and deduce that the open intervals $\bigl(f(x^-),\, f(x^+)\bigr)$ and $\bigl(f(y^-),\, f(y^+)\bigr)$ are disjoint.
> 3. Using Problem 0.7.1, deduce that the set of jump points
>    $$
>    J_f := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : f(x^-) < f(x^+)\}
>    $$
>    is at most countable.

### Problem 0.7.3: Cardinal Arithmetic of the Continuum ($|\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}| = |\mathbb{R}^k| = |\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}| = |\mathbb{R}|$)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Using the bijections $\mathbb{R} \sim \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}}$ and $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}$ established in Section 0.7:
> 1. Prove that $|\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}| = |\mathbb{R}|$, and deduce by mathematical induction that $|\mathbb{R}^k| = |\mathbb{R}|$ for every integer $k \ge 1$ (so the $k$-dimensional Euclidean space $\mathbb{R}^k$ has the exact same cardinality $\mathfrak{c}$ as the one-dimensional real line $\mathbb{R}$!).
> 2. More generally, let $\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}$ denote the set of all infinite sequences $(x_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ of real numbers (i.e., all functions $x: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{R}$). Prove by constructing a bijection between $\bigl(\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\bigr)^{\mathbb{N}}$ and $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}}$ that
>    $$
>    \bigl|\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\bigr| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}.
>    $$

### Problem 0.7.4: Finite vs. Cofinite and Infinite Subsets of $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N})$ denote the set of all finite subsets of $\mathbb{N}$, let $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cof}}(\mathbb{N}) := \{A \subseteq \mathbb{N} : \mathbb{N} \setminus A \in \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N})\}$ denote the set of all **cofinite** subsets of $\mathbb{N}$ (subsets whose complement in $\mathbb{N}$ is finite), and let $\mathcal{P}_{\text{inf}}(\mathbb{N}) := \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}) \setminus \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N})$ denote the set of all infinite subsets of $\mathbb{N}$.
> 1. Prove explicitly that the binary expansion map $\Phi: \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}) \to \mathbb{N}$ defined by $\Phi(\emptyset) := 0$ and
>    $$
>    \Phi(F) := \sum_{k \in F} 2^k \quad \text{for every non-empty } F \in \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
>    $$
>    is a bijection, so $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}) \sim \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. Deduce that $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cof}}(\mathbb{N}) \sim \mathbb{N}$ and that $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}) \cup \mathcal{P}_{\text{cof}}(\mathbb{N})$ is countably infinite.
> 3. Using the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem (for example, by mapping each $S \in \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ to $2S \cup (2\mathbb{N} + 1) \in \mathcal{P}_{\text{inf}}(\mathbb{N})$ where $2S = \{2n : n \in S\}$ and $2\mathbb{N} + 1 = \{2n + 1 : n \in \mathbb{N}\}$), prove directly that
>    $$
>    \bigl|\mathcal{P}_{\text{inf}}(\mathbb{N})\bigr| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}.
>    $$

### Problem 0.7.5: Dedekind-Infinite Sets and Countably Infinite Subsets

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $X$ be an arbitrary set. A set $X$ is called **Dedekind-infinite** if there exists a proper subset $Y \subsetneq X$ such that $X \sim Y$ (i.e., there exists an injection $f: X \to X$ that is not surjective).
> 1. Using the Principle of Dependent Choices / Axiom of Countable Choice, prove that every infinite set $X$ contains a countably infinite subset $C \subseteq X$ ($C \sim \mathbb{N}$).
> 2. Deduce that a set $X$ is infinite if and only if $X$ is Dedekind-infinite—and in fact, if $X$ is infinite and $x_0 \in X$ is any element, then $X \sim X \setminus \{x_0\}$ (Hilbert's Grand Hotel).

### Problem 0.7.6: Well-Ordered Chains vs. Uncountable Chains in $(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}), \subsetneq)$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Consider the power set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ ordered by set inclusion $\subseteq$. Because $\mathbb{N}$ is only countable, one might naively guess that any chain $\mathcal{C} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ of subsets of $\mathbb{N}$ (where for all $A, B \in \mathcal{C}$, either $A \subseteq B$ or $B \subseteq A$) must also be at most countable, since stepping from a smaller subset $A \subsetneq B$ to a strictly larger one must add at least one natural number from $\mathbb{N}$. This problem explores why that intuition fails for general chains, yet succeeds for **well-ordered** chains!
> 1. Fix a bijection $\phi: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Q}$. Using $\phi$ and Dedekind cuts of real numbers, construct an explicit **uncountable chain** $\mathcal{C} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ of cardinality $|\mathcal{C}| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}$.
> 2. In contrast, suppose $\mathcal{W} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ is a **well-ordered chain** under inclusion—meaning that $\mathcal{W}$ is a chain and every non-empty subfamily $\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{W}$ has a minimum element $M \in \mathcal{S}$ satisfying $M \subseteq A$ for all $A \in \mathcal{S}$. Prove that $\mathcal{W}$ must be **at most countable**! *(Hint: For each $A \in \mathcal{W}$ that is not the maximum element of $\mathcal{W}$, consider the minimum element $A^+ \in \mathcal{W}$ of the non-empty set $\{B \in \mathcal{W} : A \subsetneq B\}$, and look at $\min(A^+ \setminus A) \in \mathbb{N}$.)*

### Problem 0.7.7: Cardinality of Function Spaces ($\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{R}}$ vs. $\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Q}}$)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. Let $\mathcal{F} := \mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{R}}$ denote the set of all functions $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$. Prove using Cantor's Theorem and the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem that $|\mathcal{F}| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})| > |\mathbb{R}|$. *(Hint: To show $|\mathcal{F}| \le |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})|$, identify each function $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ with its graph $\Gamma_f = \{(x, f(x)) : x \in \mathbb{R}\} \subseteq \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}$ and use $|\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}| = |\mathbb{R}|$ from Problem 0.7.3.)*
> 2. In contrast, let $\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Q}} := \mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Q}}$ denote the set of all functions $g: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{R}$. Prove that $|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Q}}| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}$. (Later in real analysis, you will see that every continuous function $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ is uniquely determined by its restriction $f|_{\mathbb{Q}}: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{R}$ because $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$—which will immediately imply that the set $C(\mathbb{R})$ of all continuous functions on $\mathbb{R}$ has cardinality only $|\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}$, strictly smaller than $|\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{R}}| = |\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})|$!)

### Problem 0.7.8: Application of Zorn's Lemma — Hamel Basis of $\mathbb{R}$ as a $\mathbb{Q}$-Vector Space

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Consider the field of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ as a vector space over its subfield of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$. A subset $\mathcal{B} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is called **$\mathbb{Q}$-linearly independent** if $0 \notin \mathcal{B}$ and for every finite collection of distinct elements $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_k \in \mathcal{B}$ and rational scalars $q_1, q_2, \dots, q_k \in \mathbb{Q}$:
> $$
> q_1 b_1 + q_2 b_2 + \dots + q_k b_k = 0 \implies q_1 = q_2 = \dots = q_k = 0.
> $$
> A $\mathbb{Q}$-linearly independent subset $\mathcal{H} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is called a **Hamel basis** of $\mathbb{R}$ over $\mathbb{Q}$ if its $\mathbb{Q}$-linear span equals $\mathbb{R}$—that is, every real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ can be expressed uniquely (up to order of terms and omitting zero coefficients) as a finite rational linear combination $x = \sum_{i=1}^k q_i h_i$ with $h_1, \dots, h_k \in \mathcal{H}$ and $q_1, \dots, q_k \in \mathbb{Q}$.
> 1. Let $\mathcal{L}$ be the collection of all $\mathbb{Q}$-linearly independent subsets $\mathcal{B} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, partially ordered by set inclusion $\subseteq$. Prove that $\mathcal{L}$ is non-empty and that for every chain $\mathcal{C} \subseteq \mathcal{L}$, the union $\bigcup_{\mathcal{B} \in \mathcal{C}} \mathcal{B}$ belongs to $\mathcal{L}$.
> 2. Invoke **Zorn's Lemma** to conclude that $\mathcal{L}$ has a maximal element $\mathcal{H} \in \mathcal{L}$, and prove that $\mathcal{H}$ is a Hamel basis of $\mathbb{R}$ over $\mathbb{Q}$.
> 3. Using the theorems on countable unions and finite tuples from Section 0.7, prove that any Hamel basis $\mathcal{H}$ of $\mathbb{R}$ over $\mathbb{Q}$ must be **uncountable** (and in fact $|\mathcal{H}| = |\mathbb{R}| = \mathfrak{c}$).

---

# Skeleton Index

## 0.0 Introduction: How Modern Mathematics Works

### From Self-Evident Truths to Foundational Crises

**Euclid and the Classical Picture of Axioms**
- Describe the structure of Euclid's *Elements* and explain what the word "axiom" meant in the classical picture of mathematics.

**The First Crack: Non-Euclidean Geometry**
- State Euclid's five postulates, and explain why the fifth was regarded as less self-evident than the others.
- State the parallel postulate in Playfair's form and describe how its negation led to hyperbolic geometry.
- Explain what Beltrami's models established about hyperbolic geometry and Euclidean geometry.
- Explain the philosophical consequence of non-Euclidean geometry for the status of axioms.

**The Crisis in Calculus and the Arithmetization of Analysis**
- Explain the foundational problem with infinitesimals in early calculus and Berkeley's criticism of them.
- Describe the developments in Fourier series and Weierstrass's work that undermined geometric intuition in analysis.
- State the two parts of the program of arithmetization of analysis and identify the chain of reductions it produced.

**Paradoxes at the Bottom: Sets, Russell, and the Need for Formal Foundations**
- Describe the naive conception of set and the paradox that destroyed it, including its impact on Frege's program.
- Explain why an inconsistent theory makes the notion of proof worthless.
- List the foundational questions that mathematicians were forced to answer after the discovery of the set-theoretic paradoxes.

### The Modern Axiomatic Method

**Axioms as Specifications, Not Dogmas**
- Explain the meaning of Hilbert's "tables, chairs, and beer mugs" remark.
- Contrast the classical and modern meanings of an axiom, and explain the logical form of every theorem in the modern view.
- Explain why the axiomatic method does not make mathematics circular.

**Formal Systems: Language, Axioms, and Rules of Inference**
- Define a formal system, listing its three ingredients.
- Define a formal proof and the provability relation $\Gamma \vdash \varphi$.
- Explain why checking a formal proof requires no notion of meaning or truth, and why this makes proof objective.
- Identify the formal framework used by most mathematicians and in this course.

**Two Floors: Syntax and Semantics**
- Distinguish syntax from semantics and identify the central notion of each.
- Define a structure for a formal language, truth of a sentence in a structure ($\mathcal{M} \vDash \varphi$), a model of $\Gamma$, and logical consequence ($\Gamma \vDash \varphi$).
- Determine the truth value of $\exists x \, (x + x = x)$ in the real numbers and in the strictly positive integers under ordinary addition, and explain what this shows.
- Explain Tarski's view of truth and the relationship between truth tables and the recursive definition of truth.

**Soundness and Completeness: Where Proof Meets Truth**
- State the Soundness Theorem and Gödel's Completeness Theorem for first-order logic.
- State the characterization of consistency in terms of models.
- Explain the precise sense in which "provable from the axioms" relates to "true".

**Independence: Statements the Axioms Do Not Decide**
- Define independence of a sentence from $\Gamma$ and explain how soundness is used to establish independence.
- Give an example of a statement independent of the group axioms and justify its independence.
- Explain the independence of the parallel postulate in terms of models.
- Explain the relationship between being unprovable and being false.

**Gödel's Incompleteness Theorems: Truth Outruns Proof**
- Describe the goals of Hilbert's program.
- State Gödel's First and Second Incompleteness Theorems, including all hypotheses on the theory $T$.
- Reconcile the First Incompleteness Theorem with the Completeness Theorem.
- Give an example of a statement independent of ZFC.
- Explain what Gödel's theorems do and do not imply about the reliability of mathematics.

### So What Does "True" Mean in Mathematics?

**Escaping the Circle: Object Language and Metalanguage**
- Distinguish the object language from the metalanguage, and explain where the regress of justification stops.
- Explain why the metalanguage may count symbols and proof lines even though no number systems exist yet inside the formal theory.

**Consistency: The One Assumption We Cannot Remove**
- Identify the single remaining assumption underlying all theorems of real analysis, and explain why it cannot be proved within ZFC.
- Explain the grounds on which mathematicians trust the consistency of ZFC.

**What a Theorem of Real Analysis Really Says**
- Identify the axioms that form the "character sheet" of $\mathbb{R}$.
- Rephrase a theorem of real analysis as a statement about complete ordered fields, and explain what justifies speaking of *the* real numbers.

**Mathematics in Practice Today**
- Explain what makes an informal proof rigorous and the role of proof assistants in modern mathematics.
- List the habits of rigorous mathematical practice and explain the respective roles of intuition and proof.

### Roadmap of This Chapter
- Describe the chain of constructions followed in this chapter and the role of each section in it.

## 0.1 Mathematical Logic, Proof Architecture, and Axiomatic Set Theory

### Propositional and First-Order Predicate Logic for Analysis

**Propositions, Logical Connectives, and Truth Tables**
- Define a proposition and state the five fundamental logical connectives ($\neg, \land, \lor, \implies, \iff$) along with their complete joint truth tables.
- Define logical equivalence of propositional formulas, tautologies, and contradictions.
- Explain the sense in which a proposition is "true or false", and what truth tables do and do not determine.

**Material Implication and Vacuous Truth**
- State the disjunctive and negated-conjunctive equivalents of material implication $P \implies Q$.
- Define vacuous truth and explain conceptually and set-theoretically why an implication $P \implies Q$ is defined to be true whenever the antecedent $P$ is false.

**Contrapositive, Converse, and Inverse**
- Define the contrapositive, converse, and inverse of an implication $P \implies Q$.
- Construct the joint truth table comparing $P \implies Q$ with its contrapositive, converse, and inverse, and identify which pairs are logically equivalent.
- Identify the logical fallacies of affirming the consequent and denying the antecedent.

**First-Order Predicates, Quantifiers, and Bound vs. Free Variables**
- Define a predicate (open sentence) and state how universal quantification ($\forall x \in D, P(x)$) and existential quantification ($\exists x \in D, P(x)$) expand into unrestricted logical statements using $\implies$ and $\land$.
- Determine the truth values of $\forall x \in \emptyset, P(x)$ and $\exists x \in \emptyset, P(x)$ for an arbitrary predicate $P(x)$, and justify why they differ.
- Distinguish between bound (dummy) variables and free variables in a first-order formula.

**The Order of Quantifiers**
- Compare the meaning of commuting two quantifiers of the same type versus commuting mixed quantifiers $\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y)$ and $\exists y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y)$.
- State and prove the one-way implication between $\exists y \in B \, \forall x \in A, P(x, y)$ and $\forall x \in A \, \exists y \in B, P(x, y)$, and construct a purely logical counterexample showing that the converse implication fails.

**Systematic Rules for Negating Quantified Statements**
- State the propositional negation laws for double negation, conjunction, disjunction, and material implication.
- State and derive De Morgan's Laws for negating restricted universal and existential quantifiers over a domain $D$.
- State the general step-by-step algorithm for negating a deeply nested multi-quantifier statement involving implications.

**The Uniqueness Quantifier and Standard Proof Architectures**
- State two equivalent formal definitions of the uniqueness quantifier $\exists! x \in D, P(x)$ using only $\forall$, $\exists$, $\land$, $\implies$, and equality.
- Describe the logical structure of a direct proof, a proof by contraposition, a proof by contradiction (*reductio ad absurdum*), and a two-part existence-and-uniqueness proof.

### Why Naive Set Theory Fails and the Zermelo–Fraenkel (ZF) Axioms

**Naive Comprehension and Russell's Paradox**
- State the Unrestricted (Naive) Comprehension Principle of naive set theory.
- State and prove Russell's Paradox, deriving step by step why Unrestricted Comprehension yields a contradiction.

**Extensionality, the Empty Set, and Pairing**
- State the Axiom of Extensionality and define the subset ($A \subseteq B$) and proper subset ($A \subsetneq B$) relations.
- Explain how the Axiom of Extensionality justifies the method of proof by double inclusion, and verify reflexivity, antisymmetry, and transitivity of $\subseteq$.
- State the Axiom of the Empty Set, and prove both the uniqueness of the empty set $\emptyset$ and the universal subset property $\emptyset \subseteq A$ for every set $A$.
- State the Axiom of Pairing, define the unordered pair $\{a, b\}$ and the singleton $\{a\}$, and explain why $\emptyset \neq \{\emptyset\}$.

**The Axiom Schema of Specification (Restricted Comprehension)**
- State the Axiom Schema of Specification (Restricted Comprehension) and explain why it is an axiom schema rather than a single axiom.
- State and prove how the Axiom Schema of Specification resolves Russell's Paradox by showing that $\forall A, \exists B \notin A$, and deduce that no universal set of all sets can exist in ZF.
- Distinguish between a set and a proper class in the language of ZF set theory.

**The Axiom of Union**
- State the Axiom of Union for an arbitrary family of sets $\mathcal{F}$, and use it alongside Pairing to define the pairwise union $A \cup B$ and indexed family union $\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$.
- Determine $\bigcup \emptyset$ and justify your answer from the definition of union.

**Intersections, Relative Complements, and Symmetric Differences**
- Define the pairwise intersection $A \cap B$, the intersection $\bigcap \mathcal{F}$ of a non-empty family $\mathcal{F} \neq \emptyset$, and the indexed intersection $\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$ for $I \neq \emptyset$ using the Axiom Schema of Specification.
- Prove why the empty intersection $\bigcap \emptyset$ cannot exist as a set in ZF set theory.
- Define the relative complement $A \setminus B$ and the symmetric difference $A \triangle B$, and explain why absolute complements do not exist in ZF.

**The Axiom of Power Set**
- State the Axiom of Power Set, define $\mathcal{P}(A)$, and compute $\mathcal{P}(\emptyset)$ and $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\emptyset))$.

**Distributive Laws and De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Families of Sets**
- State and prove the distributive laws of intersection over arbitrary unions and of union over arbitrary non-empty intersections.
- State and prove De Morgan's Laws for the relative complement $B \setminus \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$ and $B \setminus \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$ of an arbitrary non-empty indexed family of sets.

### Ordered Pairs, Cartesian Products, Relations, and Functions

**Kuratowski's Ordered Pair**
- State Kuratowski's set-theoretic definition of the ordered pair $(a, b)$.
- State and prove the Characteristic Property of Kuratowski's Ordered Pair ($(a, b) = (c, d) \iff a = c \land b = d$) using only the Axiom of Extensionality and the Axiom of Pairing, carefully analyzing both $a = b$ and $a \neq b$.

**The Cartesian Product $A \times B$**
- Prove that for any two sets $A$ and $B$ and any $a \in A, b \in B$, the Kuratowski ordered pair $(a, b)$ belongs to $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(A \cup B))$, and use this to construct the Cartesian product $A \times B$ via the Axiom Schema of Specification.

**Formal Definitions of Relations and Functions**
- Define a binary relation $R$ from $A$ to $B$ and a binary relation on $A$.
- State the rigorous set-theoretic definition of a function $f: A \to B$ as an ordered triple $(A, B, G_f)$, including the functional condition on the graph $G_f$ and the definitions of domain, codomain, and range.
- State and justify the criterion for equality of two functions, explain why a unique empty function $\emptyset \to B$ exists for every $B$, and define the identity function $\operatorname{id}_A$ and restriction $f|_E$.

**Injections, Surjections, Bijections, Composition, and Inverses**
- Define injective, surjective, and bijective functions $f: A \to B$ in first-order predicate logic.
- Define the composition $g \circ f : A \to C$ of functions $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to C$ in terms of their graphs, and prove that function composition is associative.
- State and prove how injectivity, surjectivity, and bijectivity behave under composition $g \circ f$, both when $f, g$ have the property and when the composite $g \circ f$ has the property.
- Define left inverses (retractions), right inverses (sections), and two-sided inverse functions.
- State and prove the theorem characterizing bijections via the existence and uniqueness of a two-sided inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$.

**Direct Images and Preimages (Inverse Images) of Sets**
- Define the direct image $f(E) \subseteq B$ for $E \subseteq A$ and the preimage (inverse image) $f^{-1}(F) \subseteq A$ for $F \subseteq B$, and explain the distinction between the preimage map on $\mathcal{P}(B)$ and an inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$.
- State and prove how preimages $f^{-1}$ interact with arbitrary unions, arbitrary non-empty intersections, and relative complements.
- State and prove how direct images $f$ interact with arbitrary unions and arbitrary non-empty intersections, and prove that $f$ preserves intersections of all subsets if and only if $f$ is injective.
- State and prove the round-trip inclusions between $E$ and $f^{-1}(f(E))$ and between $f(f^{-1}(F))$ and $F$, and prove the exact conditions on $f$ under which each inclusion becomes an equality for all subsets.

### 0.1 Exercises

**Problem 0.1.1: Wiener's Alternative Ordered Pair Definition**
- Before Kuratowski introduced $(a, b) := \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$ in 1921, Norbert Wiener (1914) gave the first purely set-theoretic definition of an ordered pair by setting
  $$
  \langle a, b \rangle_W := \{\{\{a\}, \emptyset\}, \{\{b\}\}\}
  $$
  Using only the Axiom of Extensionality, the Axiom of the Empty Set, and the Axiom of Pairing, prove that Wiener's ordered pair satisfies the characteristic property of ordered pairs: for all sets $a, b, c, d$,
