  $$
  \langle a, b \rangle_W = \langle c, d \rangle_W \iff (a = c \land b = d)
  $$

**Problem 0.1.2: Algebraic Laws of Symmetric Difference**
- Recall that for any two sets $A$ and $B$, their symmetric difference is defined by $A \triangle B := (A \setminus B) \cup (B \setminus A)$. Prove the following algebraic identities for all sets $A, B, C$:
  1. $A \triangle B = (A \cup B) \setminus (A \cap B)$.
  2. **Commutativity and Identity / Self-Inverse Laws:** $A \triangle B = B \triangle A$, $A \triangle \emptyset = A$, and $A \triangle A = \emptyset$.
  3. **Associativity:** $(A \triangle B) \triangle C = A \triangle (B \triangle C)$.
  4. **Distributivity of Intersection over Symmetric Difference:** $A \cap (B \triangle C) = (A \cap B) \triangle (A \cap C)$.
  5. **Cancellation Law:** If $A \triangle C = B \triangle C$, then $A = B$.

**Problem 0.1.3: Characterizations of Injectivity and Surjectivity via Set Operations**
- Let $f: A \to B$ be a function.
  1. Prove that the following four conditions are logically equivalent:
     - (a) $f$ is injective.
     - (b) For every subset $E \subseteq A$, $f^{-1}(f(E)) = E$.
     - (c) For all subsets $X, Y \subseteq A$, $f(X \cap Y) = f(X) \cap f(Y)$.
     - (d) For all subsets $X, Y \subseteq A$, $f(X \setminus Y) = f(X) \setminus f(Y)$.
  2. Prove that the following three conditions are logically equivalent:
     - (a) $f$ is surjective.
     - (b) For every subset $F \subseteq B$, $f(f^{-1}(F)) = F$.
     - (c) For every subset $F \subseteq B$, $B \setminus f(f^{-1}(F)) = f(A \setminus f^{-1}(F))$.

**Problem 0.1.4: Saturated Subsets and Induced Power Set Maps**
- Let $f: A \to B$ be a function. A subset $E \subseteq A$ is said to be **$f$-saturated** if $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$. Let $\mathcal{S}_f := \{E \in \mathcal{P}(A) : E = f^{-1}(f(E))\}$ denote the set of all $f$-saturated subsets of $A$.
  1. Prove that for every subset $F \subseteq B$, its preimage $f^{-1}(F)$ is $f$-saturated (that is, $f^{-1}(f(f^{-1}(F))) = f^{-1}(F)$), and therefore $E \subseteq A$ is $f$-saturated if and only if there exists $F \subseteq B$ such that $E = f^{-1}(F)$.
  2. Let $f_* : \mathcal{P}(A) \to \mathcal{P}(B)$ be the direct image map $f_*(E) := f(E)$, and let $f^* : \mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ be the preimage map $f^*(F) := f^{-1}(F)$. Prove that:
     - $f_*$ is injective if and only if $f$ is injective, and $f_*$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is surjective;
     - $f^*$ is injective if and only if $f$ is surjective, and $f^*$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is injective.
  3. Prove that restricting the domain of $f_*$ to $\mathcal{S}_f$ and its codomain to $\mathcal{P}(f(A))$ yields a bijection $\widetilde{f}_* : \mathcal{S}_f \to \mathcal{P}(f(A))$.

**Problem 0.1.5: The Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem on $\mathcal{P}(X)$**
- Let $X$ be a set, and let $\Phi : \mathcal{P}(X) \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ be an **inclusion-preserving** (or **monotone**) map on the power set of $X$, meaning that for all $A, B \in \mathcal{P}(X)$,
  $$
  A \subseteq B \implies \Phi(A) \subseteq \Phi(B)
  $$
  A subset $S \subseteq X$ is called a **fixed point** of $\Phi$ if $\Phi(S) = S$. Define the collections of **pre-fixed points** and **post-fixed points** of $\Phi$ by
  $$
  \mathcal{A} := \{A \in \mathcal{P}(X) : \Phi(A) \subseteq A\} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \mathcal{B} := \{B \in \mathcal{P}(X) : B \subseteq \Phi(B)\}
  $$
  1. Verify that $\mathcal{A} \neq \emptyset$ and $\mathcal{B} \neq \emptyset$ by checking $X \in \mathcal{A}$ and $\emptyset \in \mathcal{B}$, so that $S_* := \bigcap \mathcal{A}$ and $S^* := \bigcup \mathcal{B}$ exist as subsets of $X$.
  2. Prove that $\Phi(S_*) \subseteq S_*$, and deduce by applying $\Phi$ to this inclusion that $S_* \subseteq \Phi(S_*)$, thereby establishing that $\Phi(S_*) = S_*$. Conclude that $S_*$ is the **smallest fixed point** of $\Phi$ (meaning $\Phi(S_*) = S_*$, and for every $S \subseteq X$ with $\Phi(S) = S$, we have $S_* \subseteq S$).
  3. By a dual argument, prove that $\Phi(S^*) = S^*$ and that $S^*$ is the **largest fixed point** of $\Phi$ (meaning $\Phi(S^*) = S^*$, and for every $S \subseteq X$ with $\Phi(S) = S$, we have $S \subseteq S^*$).

**Problem 0.1.6: Minimax Inclusion for Doubly-Indexed Set Families**
- Let $I$ and $J$ be non-empty index sets, and for each ordered pair $(i, j) \in I \times J$, let $A_{i, j}$ be a set.
  1. Prove the general set-theoretic minimax inclusion:
     $$
     \bigcup_{i \in I} \left(\bigcap_{j \in J} A_{i, j}\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{j \in J} \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_{i, j}\right)
     $$
  2. Construct an explicit purely set-theoretic example (using only sets built from $\emptyset$, such as $I = J = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$) showing that this inclusion can be strict:
     $$
     \bigcup_{i \in I} \left(\bigcap_{j \in J} A_{i, j}\right) \subsetneq \bigcap_{j \in J} \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_{i, j}\right)
     $$

**Problem 0.1.7: Categorical Monomorphisms and Epimorphisms in $\mathbf{Set}$**
- Let $f: A \to B$ be a function between sets $A$ and $B$.
  1. We say $f$ is **left-cancellative** (or a **monomorphism**) if for every set $Z$ and every pair of functions $g_1, g_2 : Z \to A$,
     $$
     f \circ g_1 = f \circ g_2 \implies g_1 = g_2
     $$
     Prove that $f: A \to B$ is injective if and only if $f$ is left-cancellative. (For the reverse implication, test left-cancellation using the singleton set $Z = \{\emptyset\}$.)
  2. We say $f$ is **right-cancellative** (or an **epimorphism**) if for every set $W$ and every pair of functions $h_1, h_2 : B \to W$,
     $$
     h_1 \circ f = h_2 \circ f \implies h_1 = h_2
     $$
     Prove that $f: A \to B$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is right-cancellative. (For the reverse implication, test right-cancellation using the two-element set $W = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$.)

**Problem 0.1.8: Set-Theoretic Sandwich Bijection (Schröder–Bernstein Core)**
- Suppose $A, B, C$ are sets satisfying the nested inclusion $A \subseteq B \subseteq C$, and suppose there exists a bijection $f: C \to A$.
  1. Define a map $\Phi : \mathcal{P}(C) \to \mathcal{P}(C)$ by setting, for each subset $E \subseteq C$,
     $$
     \Phi(E) := (C \setminus B) \cup f(E)
     $$
     Prove that $\Phi$ is inclusion-preserving ($E_1 \subseteq E_2 \implies \Phi(E_1) \subseteq \Phi(E_2)$), and apply Problem 0.1.5 (the Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem) to conclude that there exists a subset $S \subseteq C$ such that $\Phi(S) = S$, i.e., $S = (C \setminus B) \cup f(S)$.
  2. Prove that for this fixed point $S$, the sets $C \setminus B$ and $f(S)$ are disjoint, that $C \setminus S = B \setminus f(S)$, and that $B$ is the disjoint union $B = f(S) \cup (C \setminus S)$.
  3. Define a function $g: C \to B$ piecewise on the partition $C = S \cup (C \setminus S)$ by setting $g(x) := f(x)$ for $x \in S$ and $g(x) := x$ for $x \in C \setminus S$ (formally, $G_g := (G_f \cap (S \times B)) \cup (\operatorname{id}_C \cap ((C \setminus S) \times B))$). Prove that $g: C \to B$ is a bijection.

## 0.2 The Natural Numbers ($\mathbb{N}$) — Peano Axioms, Recursion, and Arithmetic

### The Peano Axioms and the Von Neumann Set-Theoretic Model

**The Essence of Counting and the Peano Postulates**
- Motivate why an axiomatic foundation for the natural numbers is required in Real Analysis rather than relying on informal counting intuition.
- Define a Peano system $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ and state all five Peano Axioms (P1–P5) formally.
- Define the standard numerals $1, 2, 3, 4$ in terms of $0$ and $S$, and explain how the Peano Axioms guarantee that these elements are distinct.
- Explain how proving a first-order logical predicate $\varphi(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ reduces to the subset formulation of Mathematical Induction (Axiom P5) via the Axiom Schema of Specification.

**Pathological Toy Models: Why Every Peano Axiom is Indispensable**
- Construct an explicit toy model that satisfies Axioms P1, P2, P4, and P5 but violates Axiom P3, and analyze how the structure fails to represent $\mathbb{N}$.
- Construct an explicit toy model that satisfies Axioms P1, P2, P3, and P5 but violates Axiom P4, and analyze how the structure fails to represent $\mathbb{N}$.
- Construct an explicit toy model that satisfies Axioms P1, P2, P3, and P4 but violates Axiom P5, and explain the role of Axiom P5 as a minimality condition.

**The Fundamental Predecessor Lemma**
- State and prove the Fundamental Predecessor Lemma characterizing the image of the successor map $S$ on $\mathbb{N}$.

**Existence in ZF Set Theory: The Axiom of Infinity and Von Neumann Ordinals**
- Define the Von Neumann successor operation on sets and write out the explicit set-theoretic representations of $0, 1, 2,$ and $3$.
- Define what it means for a set $I$ to be inductive, and state the ZF Axiom of Infinity.
- State and prove the theorem constructing $\mathbb{N}$ inside ZF set theory from an inductive set $I_0$, verifying that the resulting triple $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ is independent of $I_0$ and satisfies the Peano Axioms.

### Dedekind's Recursion Theorem and the Construction of Addition

**Why Recursive Definitions Require a Theorem**
- Contrast Mathematical Induction with Recursive Definition, and explain why the existence of a recursively specified function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ is not automatic in ZF set theory.

**Dedekind's Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$**
- State and prove Dedekind's Recursion Theorem for defining a unique function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ from an initial element $a \in X$ and a transition function $g: \mathbb{N} \times X \to X$.
- Identify the exact steps in the proof of Dedekind's Recursion Theorem where Axioms P3, P4, and P5 are each essential.

**Recursive Definition of Addition on $\mathbb{N}$**
- Define the binary operation of addition $+: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ recursively via Dedekind's Recursion Theorem, and evaluate $n + 1$ in terms of the successor map $S$.

**The Algebraic Laws of Addition (Strict Non-Circular Sequence)**
- Explain why Commutativity of Addition cannot be proved in a single inductive step directly from the right-recursive definition of addition without preliminary lemmas.
- State and prove the Left Additive Identity Lemma on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Left Successor Shift Lemma for addition on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Commutativity of Addition on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Associativity of Addition on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Additive Cancellation Law on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the necessary and sufficient conditions on $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ for their sum $a + b$ to equal $0$.

### Recursive Construction of Multiplication and Semiring Structure

**Recursive Definition of Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$**
- Define the binary operation of multiplication $\cdot : \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ recursively via Dedekind's Recursion Theorem.

**The Algebraic Laws of Multiplication (Strict Non-Circular Sequence)**
- State and prove the Left Zero Annihilation Lemma and the Left Successor Rule for multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Commutativity of Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Multiplicative Identity Proposition for $1 := S(0)$ on $\mathbb{N}$.
- Explain why the Distributive Law must be proved prior to the Associativity of Multiplication in the inductive development of arithmetic.
- State and prove both Left and Right Distributivity of multiplication over addition on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Associativity of Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$.
- Summarize the algebraic structure formed by $(\mathbb{N}, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$.
- State and prove the Absence of Zero Divisors Theorem in $\mathbb{N}$.

### Order on $\mathbb{N}$, Trichotomy, Strong Induction, and Well-Ordering

**Defining the Order Relation via Addition**
- Define the non-strict order relation $a \le b$ and the strict order relation $a < b$ on $\mathbb{N}$ in terms of addition.
- State and prove the Algebraic Characterization of Strict Inequality on $\mathbb{N}$ connecting $a < b$, addition of a successor, and $S(a) \le b$.

**Partial Order Properties, Trichotomy, and Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$**
- State and prove the Reflexivity, Antisymmetry, and Transitivity of the order relation $\le$ on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Trichotomy Law for $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$ Corollary concerning elements between $n$ and $S(n)$.

**Order Compatibility with Arithmetic and Multiplicative Cancellation**
- State and prove the Compatibility of Order ($\le$ and $<$) with Addition and Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Multiplicative Cancellation Law on $\mathbb{N}$.

**Strong Induction and the Well-Ordering Principle (WOP)**
- State the Principle of Strong Induction and the Well-Ordering Principle (WOP) on $\mathbb{N}$.
- Explain how the base case at $m = 0$ is embedded within the formal statement of Strong Induction.
- State and prove the logical equivalence between Peano Induction (Axiom P5), Strong Induction, and the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$.

**The Euclidean Division Algorithm on $\mathbb{N}$**
- State and prove the Euclidean Division Algorithm on $\mathbb{N}$ (both existence and uniqueness of the quotient and remainder).

### 0.2 Exercises

#### Problem 0.2.1: Categoricity of the Peano Axioms

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(N_1, 0_1, S_1)$ and $(N_2, 0_2, S_2)$ be any two Peano systems satisfying Axioms P1–P5.
> 1. Using **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** and **Mathematical Induction**, prove that there exists a **unique bijection** $\Phi: N_1 \to N_2$ such that $\Phi(0_1) = 0_2$ and $\Phi(S_1(n)) = S_2(\Phi(n))$ for all $n \in N_1$ (that is, $\Phi \circ S_1 = S_2 \circ \Phi$).
> 2. Let $+_1, \cdot_1, \le_1$ and $+_2, \cdot_2, \le_2$ denote the addition, multiplication, and order relations recursively constructed on $N_1$ and $N_2$, respectively. Prove by induction that $\Phi$ is an **ordered semiring isomorphism**: for all $a, b \in N_1$,
>    $$
>    \Phi(a +_1 b) = \Phi(a) +_2 \Phi(b), \quad \Phi(a \cdot_1 b) = \Phi(a) \cdot_2 \Phi(b), \quad \text{and} \quad a \le_1 b \iff \Phi(a) \le_2 \Phi(b)
>    $$

#### Problem 0.2.2: Recursive Construction and Laws of Exponentiation on $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For each fixed base $a \in \mathbb{N}$, use Dedekind's Recursion Theorem to define the **exponentiation function** $b \mapsto a^b$ from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{N}$ by the recursive rules:
> $$
> a^0 := 1 \quad (\text{where } 1 := S(0)), \qquad \text{and} \qquad a^{S(b)} := a^b \cdot a \quad \text{for all } b \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> (Note that under this recursive convention, $0^0 = 1$, while $0^{S(b)} = 0$ for all $b \in \mathbb{N}$.)
> Prove the following three **Laws of Exponents** strictly by Mathematical Induction on $\mathbb{N}$, stating clearly which previously proved laws of addition and multiplication are invoked at each step:
> 1. **Product of Powers Law:** For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$.
> 2. **Power of a Power Law:** For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$.
> 3. **Power of a Product Law:** For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$.

#### Problem 0.2.3: Fermat's Principle of Infinite Descent

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> **Fermat's Principle of Infinite Descent** states that there does **not** exist a function $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ that is strictly decreasing at every step; that is, there is no function $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying
> $$
> f(S(n)) < f(n) \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> Prove inside ZF set theory (assuming Axioms P1–P4 and the basic properties of order on $\mathbb{N}$, together with the Axiom of Dependent Choice or by constructing an explicit recursive descent function on a non-empty subset without a minimum) that **Fermat's Principle of Infinite Descent is logically equivalent to the Well-Ordering Principle (WOP)** on $\mathbb{N}$. Specifically:
> 1. Assuming WOP, prove that no function $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfies $f(S(n)) < f(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ by examining the image $f(\mathbb{N}) \subseteq \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. Conversely, assuming the Principle of Infinite Descent (or using Peano/Strong Induction to show that any strictly decreasing sequence must terminate), prove that every non-empty subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ possesses a minimum element.

#### Problem 0.2.4: Transitive Sets in the Von Neumann Construction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Recall from Section 0.1 and the Von Neumann model of $\mathbb{N}$ that $0 := \emptyset$ and $S(x) := x \cup \{x\}$. A set $T$ is called a **transitive set** if every element of $T$ is also a subset of $T$:
> $$
> \forall x \in T, \quad x \subseteq T \qquad (\text{equivalently, } \forall x, y, \, (y \in x \land x \in T) \implies y \in T)
> $$
> Using only ZF set theory and Mathematical Induction (Axiom P5) on the Von Neumann natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$ (without appealing to the Axiom of Foundation):
> 1. Prove by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ that every $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is a transitive set.
> 2. Using part (1), prove by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ that $n \notin n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and deduce directly that $S(n) = S(m) \implies n = m$ (Axiom P4).
> 3. Prove by induction that for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$, the algebraic order relations $<$ and $\le$ (defined via addition) coincide with set membership and set inclusion:
>    $$
>    m < n \iff m \in n, \qquad \text{and} \qquad m \le n \iff m \subseteq n
>    $$

#### Problem 0.2.5: The Finite Pigeonhole Principle via Peano Induction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For each $n \in \mathbb{N}$, define the **initial segment** $I_n := \{k \in \mathbb{N} : k < n\}$ (so $I_0 = \emptyset$, $I_1 = \{0\}$, $I_2 = \{0, 1\}$, and $I_{S(n)} = I_n \cup \{n\}$ by the Discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$).
> 1. **(Swap Lemma):** Let $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and let $f: I_{S(n)} \to I_{S(m)}$ be an injective function. Prove that there exists an injective function $g: I_{S(n)} \to I_{S(m)}$ such that $g(n) = m$, and deduce by restricting $g$ to $I_n$ that there exists an injective function $\tilde{g}: I_n \to I_m$.
> 2. **(Finite Pigeonhole Principle):** Prove by induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$ that for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$ with $m < n$, there does **not** exist an injective function $f: I_n \to I_m$.
> 3. **(Dedekind Finiteness of $I_n$):** Deduce that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, a function $h: I_n \to I_n$ is injective if and only if $h$ is surjective (and hence bijective). Contrast this with the successor map $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ on the infinite set $\mathbb{N}$.

#### Problem 0.2.6: Mutual Recursion and Simultaneous Induction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In many applications, two sequences or functions $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ and $g: \mathbb{N} \to Y$ are defined via **mutual recursion**, where each function's value at $S(n)$ depends on *both* $f(n)$ and $g(n)$.
> Let $X$ and $Y$ be non-empty sets, let $x_0 \in X$ and $y_0 \in Y$ be fixed initial elements, and let
> $$
> H_1: \mathbb{N} \times X \times Y \to X \quad \text{and} \quad H_2: \mathbb{N} \times X \times Y \to Y
> $$
> be given transition functions.
> 1. **(Simultaneous Recursion Theorem):** Prove that there exists a **unique** pair of functions $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ and $g: \mathbb{N} \to Y$ satisfying $f(0) = x_0$, $g(0) = y_0$, and for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    $$
>    f(S(n)) = H_1\big(n, f(n), g(n)\big) \quad \text{and} \quad g(S(n)) = H_2\big(n, f(n), g(n)\big)
>    $$
>    by reducing the problem to a single application of **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** on the Cartesian product $Z := X \times Y$ together with the canonical projection maps $\pi_1: X \times Y \to X$ and $\pi_2: X \times Y \to Y$.
> 2. **(Two-Step / Fibonacci-Style Recursion):** Let $X$ be a non-empty set, let $a_0, a_1 \in X$, and let $G: \mathbb{N} \times X \times X \to X$ be a function. Using part (1) with $Y = X$, prove that there exists a unique function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ such that $f(0) = a_0$, $f(1) = a_1$, and $f(S(S(n))) = G(n, f(n), f(S(n)))$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

#### Problem 0.2.7: Partial Subtraction on $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Although $\mathbb{N}$ is not closed under subtraction, we can define a rigorous **partial subtraction** operation whenever $b \le a$.
> 1. Let $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ with $b \le a$. Using the definition of $\le$ and the **Additive Cancellation Law**, prove that there exists a **unique** natural number $d \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $b + d = a$. We denote this unique element $d$ by $a \ominus b$.
> 2. Prove that for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    - If $b + c \le a$, then $b \le a$, $c \le a \ominus b$, and
>      $$
>      (a \ominus b) \ominus c = a \ominus (b + c)
>      $$
>    - If $b \le a$, then $c \cdot b \le c \cdot a$ and **Multiplication Distributes over Partial Subtraction**:
>      $$
>      c \cdot (a \ominus b) = (c \cdot a) \ominus (c \cdot b)
>      $$

#### Problem 0.2.8: Ackermann's Function via Higher-Order Recursion

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In 1928, Wilhelm Ackermann constructed a total computable function on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ that grows faster than any function definable by a single primitive recursion (such as addition, multiplication, exponentiation, or iterated towers of exponents).
> Consider the **Ackermann-Péter recurrence relations** for a function $\operatorname{Ack}: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$:
> - **(A1):** $\operatorname{Ack}(0, n) = S(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$;
> - **(A2):** $\operatorname{Ack}(S(m), 0) = \operatorname{Ack}(m, 1)$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$;
> - **(A3):** $\operatorname{Ack}(S(m), S(n)) = \operatorname{Ack}\big(m, \operatorname{Ack}(S(m), n)\big)$ for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 
> 1. By applying **Dedekind's Recursion Theorem** (or Peano Induction on $m$ to construct a sequence of functions $A_m: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ where $A_0(n) = S(n)$ and $A_{S(m)}$ is defined from $A_m$ via Dedekind's Recursion Theorem with $A_{S(m)}(0) = A_m(1)$ and $A_{S(m)}(S(n)) = A_m(A_{S(m)}(n))$), prove that there exists a **unique** function $\operatorname{Ack}: \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying (A1)–(A3).
> 2. Using **nested Peano Induction** (an outer induction on $m \in \mathbb{N}$ and an inner induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$), prove that for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    $$
>    n < \operatorname{Ack}(m, n) \quad \text{and} \quad \operatorname{Ack}(m, n) < \operatorname{Ack}(m, S(n))
>    $$
>    and deduce that $\operatorname{Ack}(m, n) < \operatorname{Ack}(S(m), n)$ for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$.

## 0.3 Equivalence Relations, Quotients, and the Construction of the Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$)

### Equivalence Relations, Partitions, and Quotient Sets

**Binary Relations and the Equivalence Axioms**
- State the formal set-theoretic definitions of reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity for a binary relation $\sim$ on a set $X$, and define an equivalence relation.
- Explain why reflexivity is an independent axiom that cannot be deduced from symmetry and transitivity alone, and construct an explicit counterexample on a non-empty set $X$.

**Equivalence Classes, Quotient Sets, and the Canonical Projection**
- Define the equivalence class $[x]_\sim$ of an element $x \in X$, a representative of an equivalence class, the quotient set $X / {\sim}$, and the canonical projection $\pi : X \to X / {\sim}$.
- Justify using the Zermelo-Fraenkel axioms why $[x]_\sim$ and $X / {\sim}$ are legitimate sets.

**Partitions and the Fundamental Correspondence**
- State the three axioms defining a partition $\mathcal{P}$ of a set $X$.
- State and prove the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations and Partitions, establishing the bijective correspondence between equivalence relations on $X$ and partitions of $X$.

**The Principle of Well-Definedness on Quotient Sets**
- Explain why defining a function or operation on a quotient set $X / {\sim}$ in terms of class representatives can fail to produce a valid function, and provide an explicit example of an ill-defined prescription on $(\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}) / {\sim}$.
- State and prove the Universal Property of Quotient Sets (Principle of Well-Definedness) characterizing when a function $f : X \to Y$ descends to a unique well-defined function $\tilde{f} : X / {\sim} \to Y$.
- Formulate the invariance condition required for a binary operation on $X$ to descend to a well-defined binary operation on $X / {\sim}$.

### Set-Theoretic Construction of the Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$)

**Motivation: Formal Differences of Natural Numbers**
- Explain why the commutative monoid $(\mathbb{N}, +, 0)$ fails to be a group and how ordered pairs $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ allow us to encode formal differences without circularly invoking subtraction or negative numbers.

**The Equivalence Relation on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$**
- State the subtraction-free definition of the integer equivalence relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the theorem that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, identifying the exact step where the Additive Cancellation Law on $\mathbb{N}$ is required.
- Define the set of integers $\mathbb{Z}$ as a quotient set.

### Ring Structure and Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$

**Defining Integer Addition, Negation, and Multiplication**
- Motivate and state the formal definitions of integer addition $+_\mathbb{Z}$, negation $-$, integer multiplication $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$, additive identity $0_\mathbb{Z}$, and multiplicative identity $1_\mathbb{Z}$ on equivalence classes in $\mathbb{Z}$.
- State and prove the Well-Definedness of Integer Arithmetic, verifying that $+_\mathbb{Z}$, $-$, and $\cdot_\mathbb{Z}$ are invariant under changes of class representatives.

**The Commutative Ring Axioms for $\mathbb{Z}$**
- State and prove the theorem that $(\mathbb{Z}, +_\mathbb{Z}, \cdot_\mathbb{Z}, 0_\mathbb{Z}, 1_\mathbb{Z})$ is a commutative ring with unity.
- Define subtraction on $\mathbb{Z}$ in terms of addition and negation, and derive its coordinate representation.
- State and prove the basic ring identities in $\mathbb{Z}$: the Additive Cancellation Law, uniqueness of additive inverses, double negation, unique solvability of linear equations $a + x = b$, multiplication by zero, and the sign rules for multiplication.

**The Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$ and Sign Trichotomy**
- Define the canonical embedding $\iota : \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$ and prove that it is an injective semiring homomorphism satisfying $[(a, b)] = \iota(a) - \iota(b)$ for all $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$.
- State and prove the Canonical Representatives and Sign Trichotomy Theorem for $\mathbb{Z}$, establishing the decomposition of $\mathbb{Z}$ into $\mathbb{Z}^+$, $\{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$, and $\mathbb{Z}^-$.

### Order Structure, Integral Domain Properties, and Absolute Value on $\mathbb{Z}$

**The Total Order on $\mathbb{Z}$**
- Define the order relations $\le_\mathbb{Z}$ and $<_\mathbb{Z}$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ in terms of $\iota(\mathbb{N})$ and $\mathbb{Z}^+$.
- State and prove the coordinate characterization of $\le_\mathbb{Z}$ and $<_\mathbb{Z}$, the closure of $\mathbb{Z}^+$ under addition and multiplication, the total order and trichotomy properties of $(\mathbb{Z}, \le)$, and the compatibility of the order with addition, negation, and multiplication.

**$\mathbb{Z}$ as an Ordered Integral Domain**
- Define an integral domain, and state and prove the theorem that $\mathbb{Z}$ has no nonzero zero divisors and satisfies the Multiplicative Cancellation Law.

**Absolute Value on $\mathbb{Z}$ and the Triangle Inequality**
- Define the absolute value $|x|$ of an integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ via Sign Trichotomy.
- State and prove the fundamental properties of the absolute value on $\mathbb{Z}$: non-negativity and positive definiteness, multiplicativity, order bound equivalence, the Triangle Inequality, and the Reverse Triangle Inequality.

### 0.3 Exercises

**Problem 0.3.1: Failure of Transitivity Without Additive Cancellation**
- Let $(M, +, 0)$ be a commutative monoid (that is, $+$ is an associative and commutative binary operation on $M$ with identity element $0 \in M$) in which the additive cancellation law fails.
  1. Let $X$ be a non-empty set, and consider the power set $M := \mathcal{P}(X)$ equipped with the binary operation $A + B := A \cup B$ and identity $0 := \emptyset$. Verify that $(\mathcal{P}(X), \cup, \emptyset)$ is a commutative monoid, and prove explicitly that the relation $\sim$ on $M \times M$ defined by
     $$
     (A, B) \sim (C, D) \iff A + D = B + C
     $$
     is reflexive and symmetric, but **fails to be transitive**.
  2. Now let $(M, +, 0)$ be *any* commutative monoid, and define a modified binary relation $\approx$ on $M \times M$ (called the **Grothendieck equivalence relation**) by
     $$
     (a, b) \approx (c, d) \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } a + d + k = b + c + k
     $$
     Prove that $\approx$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$ even when cancellation fails in $M$, and show that on $(\mathcal{P}(X), \cup, \emptyset)$ the quotient $(M \times M) / {\approx}$ collapses to a single one-element set!

**Problem 0.3.2: The Universal Property of the Grothendieck Group $\mathbb{Z}$**
- Let $(G, +_G, 0_G)$ be any abelian group (that is, a set with an associative, commutative binary operation $+_G$, identity $0_G$, and additive inverses $-g$ for all $g \in G$), and let $f : \mathbb{N} \to G$ be a monoid homomorphism, meaning that $f(0) = 0_G$ and
  $$
  f(m + n) = f(m) +_G f(n) \quad \text{for all } m, n \in \mathbb{N}
  $$
  Prove that there exists a **unique** group homomorphism $\tilde{f} : \mathbb{Z} \to G$ (satisfying $\tilde{f}(x +_\mathbb{Z} y) = \tilde{f}(x) +_G \tilde{f}(y)$ for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$) such that $\tilde{f} \circ \iota = f$, where $\iota : \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ is the canonical embedding $\iota(n) = [(n, 0)]$.

**Problem 0.3.3: Discreteness and Units of the Ring $\mathbb{Z}$**
- 1. Prove directly from the construction of $\mathbb{Z}$ and the properties of $\mathbb{N}$ established in Section 0.2 that the order on $\mathbb{Z}$ is **discrete**: there exists no integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $0_\mathbb{Z} < x < 1_\mathbb{Z}$, and more generally, for any $n \in \mathbb{Z}$, there exists no integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $n < x < n + 1_\mathbb{Z}$.
  2. An element $u \in \mathbb{Z}$ is called a **multiplicative unit** (or **invertible element**) of the ring $\mathbb{Z}$ if there exists $v \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $u \cdot v = 1_\mathbb{Z}$. Using Part 1 and the properties of the absolute value or order on $\mathbb{Z}$, prove that the only multiplicative units in $\mathbb{Z}$ are $1_\mathbb{Z}$ and $-1_\mathbb{Z}$.

**Problem 0.3.4: Bounded Well-Ordering and Two-Sided Induction on $\mathbb{Z}$**
- Although $\mathbb{Z}$ is not well-ordered (since $\mathbb{Z}$ itself has no minimum element), it inherits a powerful bounded well-ordering structure from $\mathbb{N}$:
  1. Let $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ be a non-empty subset that is **bounded below**, meaning there exists $B \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $B \le s$ for all $s \in S$. Prove using the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2) that $S$ has a unique **minimum element** $s_{\min} \in S$ (satisfying $s_{\min} \le s$ for all $s \in S$).
  2. Deduce by a symmetry argument that every non-empty subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ that is **bounded above** ($\exists U \in \mathbb{Z}, \forall t \in T, t \le U$) has a unique **maximum element** $t_{\max} \in T$.
  3. **Bidirectional Mathematical Induction:** Let $P(x)$ be a predicate defined for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$. Prove that if $P(0_\mathbb{Z})$ holds, and for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ both $P(x) \implies P(x + 1_\mathbb{Z})$ and $P(x) \implies P(x - 1_\mathbb{Z})$ hold, then $P(x)$ is true for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$.

**Problem 0.3.5: The Euclidean Division Algorithm Extended to All of $\mathbb{Z}$**
- Let $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ be arbitrary integers with $b \neq 0_\mathbb{Z}$. Prove that there exist **unique** integers $q, r \in \mathbb{Z}$ (called the **quotient** and **remainder**, respectively) such that
  $$
  a = q \cdot b + r \quad \text{and} \quad 0_\mathbb{Z} \le r < |b|
  $$
  *(Be sure to prove both existence and uniqueness for all four sign combinations of $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0_\mathbb{Z}\}$!)*

**Problem 0.3.6: Order-Preserving Ring Automorphisms of $\mathbb{Z}$**
- Let $\phi : \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$ be a **ring homomorphism**, meaning that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$,
  $$
  \phi(x + y) = \phi(x) + \phi(y) \quad \text{and} \quad \phi(x \cdot y) = \phi(x) \cdot \phi(y)
  $$
  1. Prove that $\phi(1_\mathbb{Z})$ must satisfy $(\phi(1_\mathbb{Z}))^2 = \phi(1_\mathbb{Z})$ (an **idempotent** in $\mathbb{Z}$), and use the Integral Domain property of $\mathbb{Z}$ to deduce that either $\phi(1_\mathbb{Z}) = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ or $\phi(1_\mathbb{Z}) = 1_\mathbb{Z}$.
  2. Prove that if $\phi$ is not the identically zero map ($\phi(x) = 0_\mathbb{Z}$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$), then $\phi$ is necessarily the identity map $\text{id}_\mathbb{Z}$ ($\phi(x) = x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$). In particular, $\text{id}_\mathbb{Z}$ is the unique ring automorphism of $\mathbb{Z}$, and $\phi$ automatically preserves the order relation $\le$ on $\mathbb{Z}$.

## 0.4 The Rational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}$), Ordered Fields, and the Incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$

### Construction of the Rational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}$) as the Field of Fractions of $\mathbb{Z}$

**[The Relation of Cross-Multiplication on Formal Fraction Pairs]**
- Explain why the ordered integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ fails to be closed under division by nonzero elements and why zero denominators must be excluded from any field of fractions construction.
- Define the set of formal fraction pairs $S = \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$ and state the cross-multiplication relation $\sim$ on $S$.
- State and prove the theorem that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$.
- Explain the exact point in the proof of transitivity where both the nonzero denominator restriction and the absence of zero divisors in $\mathbb{Z}$ are essential, and analyze what would happen if $(0, 0)$ were permitted in $S$.

**[The Quotient Set $\mathbb{Q}$ and Its Algebraic Operations]**
- Define the set of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ as a quotient set and state the definition of the equivalence class $[(a, b)]$.
- Define rational addition $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$, rational negation $-_{\mathbb{Q}}$, rational multiplication $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$, the distinguished constants $0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ and $1_{\mathbb{Q}}$, and the multiplicative inverse $[(a, b)]^{-1}$ in terms of equivalence class representatives.
- State and prove the theorem establishing the validity of denominators, the characterization of $0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, the well-definedness of $+_{\mathbb{Q}}$, $-_{\mathbb{Q}}$, $\cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}$, and $(\cdot)^{-1}$, and the full field structure of $(\mathbb{Q}, +_{\mathbb{Q}}, \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}, 0_{\mathbb{Q}}, 1_{\mathbb{Q}})$.
- Define the canonical map $j: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$ and prove that it is an injective ring homomorphism embedding $\mathbb{Z}$ into $\mathbb{Q}$.

### Abstract Ordered Fields and Their Fundamental Algebraic Consequences

**[The Axioms of an Abstract Field and an Ordered Field]**
- Explain the foundational rationale for proving algebraic and order inequalities in an arbitrary ordered field $F$ rather than solely in $\mathbb{Q}$.
- State the complete definition of an abstract field $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$, listing all five addition axioms (A1–A5), all five multiplication axioms (M1–M5), and the distributive axiom (D).
- Prove that every field is an integral domain and that multiplicative inverses of nonzero elements are unique.
- State the complete definition of an ordered field $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$, listing the four order axioms (O1–O4), and characterize the order relation in terms of the positive cone $P$.

**[Making $\mathbb{Q}$ an Ordered Field]**
- Define positivity $>_{\mathbb{Q}} 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ and the strict order relation $<_{\mathbb{Q}}$ on $\mathbb{Q}$ in terms of integer representatives.
- State and prove the theorem that positivity on $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined, that $(\mathbb{Q}, +_{\mathbb{Q}}, \cdot_{\mathbb{Q}}, 0_{\mathbb{Q}}, 1_{\mathbb{Q}}, <_{\mathbb{Q}})$ satisfies all ordered field axioms, and that the canonical embedding $j: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$ is strictly order-preserving.

**[Fundamental Algebraic and Order Theorems in Any Ordered Field $F$]**
- State and prove the fundamental laws in any ordered field $F$ governing sign reversal under negation, addition of inequalities, multiplication of inequalities by positive and negative elements, strict positivity of nonzero squares (including the sign of $1_F$), and positivity and order reversal of reciprocals.
- Explain why every ordered field must have characteristic zero and contain a canonical isomorphic copy of $\mathbb{Q}$.
- State and prove Bernoulli's Inequality in an arbitrary ordered field $F$, identifying the exact role of the hypothesis on $x$ in the inductive step.

**[Absolute Value and Distance in an Ordered Field]**
- Define the absolute value $|x|$ and the distance function $d(x, y)$ in an arbitrary ordered field $F$.
- State and prove the fundamental properties of absolute value in an ordered field: non-negativity and definiteness, symmetry and square identity, multiplicativity, fundamental sandwich bounds, symmetric interval characterization, the Triangle Inequality, and the Reverse Triangle Inequality.
- Verify that the distance function $d(x, y)$ on an ordered field satisfies the three axioms of a metric.

**[The $\varepsilon$-Principle in an Ordered Field]**
- State and prove both the inequality form and the equality/metric form of the $\varepsilon$-Principle in an arbitrary ordered field $F$.

### The Archimedean Property, Density, and the Incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$

**[The Archimedean Property and Density of $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- State and prove the Archimedean Property of the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$, together with its three consequences concerning the unboundedness of $\mathbb{N}$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, arbitrarily small reciprocals $1/n$, and the absence of positive infinitesimals.
- State and prove the theorem that $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in itself, showing that infinitely many rational numbers lie strictly between any two distinct rational numbers $x < y$.

**[The Crisis of Irrationality and Rudin's Gap Theorem]**
- Prove the parity lemma for integer squares and use the Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$ to prove that there exists no rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ whose square equals $2$.
- Explain why the incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$ must be formulated without referencing $\mathbb{R}$ or $\sqrt{2}$ at this stage of the foundations.
- Define the sets $A$ and $B$ of positive rationals in Rudin's Gap Theorem, and state and prove that every element of $A$ is strictly less than every element of $B$, that $A$ has no largest element, and that $B$ has no smallest element.
- Explain why Rudin's Gap Theorem proves that the bounded-above set $A \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ possesses no least upper bound in $\mathbb{Q}$ and why this motivates the construction of $\mathbb{R}$.

### 0.4 Exercises

**[Problem 0.4.1: Canonical Prime Subfield of Any Ordered Field]**
- Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0_F, 1_F, <)$ be an arbitrary ordered field. For each integer $n \in \mathbb{Z}$, define the integer multiple $n \cdot 1_F \in F$ inductively by $0 \cdot 1_F := 0_F$, $(k + 1) \cdot 1_F := (k \cdot 1_F) + 1_F$ for $k \in \mathbb{N}$, and $(-k) \cdot 1_F := -(k \cdot 1_F)$ for $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$.
  1. Prove that for all $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, $(a + b) \cdot 1_F = (a \cdot 1_F) + (b \cdot 1_F)$, $(a b) \cdot 1_F = (a \cdot 1_F) \cdot (b \cdot 1_F)$, and $a <_{\mathbb{Z}} b \iff a \cdot 1_F < b \cdot 1_F$. Deduce that $b \cdot 1_F \neq 0_F$ whenever $b \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$.
  2. Define the map $\iota_F : \mathbb{Q} \to F$ by setting, for each equivalence class $[(a, b)] \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $(a, b) \in \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\})$,
     $$
     \iota_F([(a, b)]) := (a \cdot 1_F) \cdot (b \cdot 1_F)^{-1}.
     $$
     Prove that $\iota_F$ is well-defined (independent of the choice of representative $(a, b)$) and is an injective, strictly order-preserving field homomorphism from $\mathbb{Q}$ into $F$.

**[Problem 0.4.2: Impossibility of Ordering the Complex Field or Finite Fields]**
- Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1)$ be an abstract field.
  1. Suppose $F$ contains an element $i \in F$ satisfying $i^2 = -1$ (as in the field of complex numbers $\mathbb{C}$). Prove directly from the Ordered Field Axioms (O1)–(O4) that it is impossible to define a binary relation $<$ on $F$ such that $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ is an ordered field.
  2. Suppose $F$ is a *finite* field (that is, $F$ has finitely many elements, such as $\mathbb{Z} / p\mathbb{Z}$ for a prime $p$). Prove that $F$ cannot be equipped with any binary relation $<$ making $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ an ordered field.

**[Problem 0.4.3: Irrationality of Roots via the Rational Root Criterion]**
- Let $P(x) = c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0$ be a polynomial of degree $n \ge 1$ with integer coefficients $c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $c_n \neq 0$.
  1. Suppose $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ is a rational root of $P$ ($P(q) = 0$), and write $q = a / b$ in lowest terms, where $a \in \mathbb{Z}$, $b \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, and $\gcd(a, b) = 1$ (so by Bézout's identity / Euclid's lemma, there exist integers $u, v \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $a u + b v = 1$). Prove that
     $$
     a \mid c_0 \quad \text{and} \quad b \mid c_n
     $$
     in $\mathbb{Z}$.
  2. Deduce that for any positive integers $m, n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, if a rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $x^n = m$, then $x$ must be an integer ($x \in \mathbb{Z}$). Use this to prove that there exists no $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying $q^3 = 6$ or $q^2 = 12$.

**[Problem 0.4.4: Construction of a Non-Archimedean Ordered Field $\mathbb{Q}(t)$]**
- Let $\mathbb{Q}[t]$ denote the integral domain of polynomials in one indeterminate $t$ with coefficients in $\mathbb{Q}$, where a nonzero polynomial $f(t) = a_m t^m + a_{m-1} t^{m-1} + \dots + a_0$ ($a_m \neq 0$) has degree $\deg(f) = m$ and leading coefficient $\operatorname{lc}(f) := a_m \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \{0\}$. Let $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ be the field of fractions of $\mathbb{Q}[t]$, whose elements are equivalence classes of rational functions $f(t) / g(t)$ with $f, g \in \mathbb{Q}[t]$ and $g \neq 0$.
  1. Define a subset $P \subseteq \mathbb{Q}(t)$ by declaring $f(t) / g(t) \in P$ if and only if $f \neq 0$ and $\operatorname{lc}(f) \cdot \operatorname{lc}(g) >_{\mathbb{Q}} 0$. Prove that membership in $P$ is well-defined on equivalence classes and that the relation $r_1 < r_2 \iff r_2 - r_1 \in P$ makes $(\mathbb{Q}(t), +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ an **ordered field**.
  2. Prove that in this ordered field $\mathbb{Q}(t)$, the element $t = t / 1$ satisfies
     $$
     t > n \cdot 1 \quad \text{for every } n \in \mathbb{N},
     $$
     and consequently its reciprocal $\varepsilon := 1 / t \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$ is a strictly positive **infinitesimal** satisfying
     $$
     0 < \frac{1}{t} < \frac{1}{n} \quad \text{for every } n \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}.
     $$
     Conclude that the Archimedean Property is *independent* of the Ordered Field Axioms (O1)–(O4).

**[Problem 0.4.5: Density of the Dyadic Rationals in $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- A rational number $d \in \mathbb{Q}$ is called a **dyadic rational** if it can be written in the form
  $$
  d = \frac{m}{2^k}
  $$
  for some integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and some natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Let $\mathbb{D} := \{m / 2^k : m \in \mathbb{Z}, \, k \in \mathbb{N}\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ denote the set of all dyadic rationals.
  1. Prove that $\mathbb{D}$ is a subring of $\mathbb{Q}$ (closed under $+$, $-$, and $\cdot$, with $0, 1 \in \mathbb{D}$), but is *not* a subfield of $\mathbb{Q}$ (explicitly prove that $1 / 3 \notin \mathbb{D}$).
  2. Using Bernoulli's Inequality ($2^k = (1 + 1)^k \ge 1 + k > k$ for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$) together with the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{Q}$ and the Euclidean division / order properties of $\mathbb{Z}$, prove that $\mathbb{D}$ is **dense in $\mathbb{Q}$**: for any two rational numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $x < y$, there exists a dyadic rational $d \in \mathbb{D}$ such that
     $$
     x < d < y.
     $$

**[Problem 0.4.6: Cauchy's Forward–Backward Induction for AM–GM in an Ordered Field]**
- Because arbitrary $n$-th roots $\sqrt[n]{x_1 \cdots x_n}$ do not generally exist in an arbitrary ordered field $F$ (for example, $F = \mathbb{Q}$), the classical Arithmetic Mean–Geometric Mean (AM–GM) inequality must be formulated in a root-free form. Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be any ordered field.
  1. **Base Case ($n = 2$):** Prove that for any $x_1, x_2 \in F$, if $x_1 + x_2 = 2$, then $x_1 x_2 \le 1$, with equality if and only if $x_1 = x_2 = 1$.
  2. **Forward Dyadic Induction ($m = 2^k$):** Prove by mathematical induction on $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ that for any $m = 2^k$ non-negative elements $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_m \in F$ ($x_i \ge 0$), if
     $$
     x_1 + x_2 + \dots + x_m = m,
     $$
     then their product satisfies
     $$
     x_1 x_2 \cdots x_m \le 1,
     $$
     with equality if and only if $x_1 = x_2 = \dots = x_m = 1$.
  3. **Backward Induction and General Homogeneous Form:** Suppose the property in Part 2 holds for some integer $m \ge 2$, and let $n = m - 1$. Given non-negative elements $x_1, \dots, x_n \in F$ with $x_1 + \dots + x_n = n$, apply the hypothesis for $m$ variables to $x_1, \dots, x_n$ together with $x_m := 1$ to deduce that $x_1 x_2 \cdots x_n \le 1$ (with equality iff $x_1 = \dots = x_n = 1$). Conclude that for *every* positive integer $n \ge 1$ and any non-negative $a_1, \dots, a_n \in F$,
     $$
     a_1 a_2 \cdots a_n \le \left(\frac{a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n}{n}\right)^n,
     $$
     with equality if and only if $a_1 = a_2 = \dots = a_n$.

**[Problem 0.4.7: The Cauchy–Schwarz Inequality in an Arbitrary Ordered Field]**
- Let $(F, +, \cdot, 0, 1, <)$ be an arbitrary ordered field, and let $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n \in F$ and $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_n \in F$ be two $n$-tuples of elements in $F$ ($n \ge 1$).
  1. Prove **Lagrange's Algebraic Identity** in $F$:
     $$
     \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k^2\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^n b_k^2\right) - \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k b_k\right)^2 = \sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} (a_i b_j - a_j b_i)^2.
     $$
  2. Deduce—or prove independently by analyzing the non-negative quadratic polynomial $Q(t) := \sum_{k=1}^n (a_k t + b_k)^2 \ge 0$ for $t \in F$—the root-free **Cauchy–Schwarz Inequality** in $F$:
     $$
     \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k b_k\right)^2 \le \left(\sum_{k=1}^n a_k^2\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^n b_k^2\right),
     $$
     and determine the exact necessary and sufficient condition on $(a_1, \dots, a_n)$ and $(b_1, \dots, b_n)$ for equality to hold.

**[Problem 0.4.8: Babylonian Rational Approximation Sequence and Absence of an Infimum in $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- Define a sequence $(q_n)_{n=1}^{\infty}$ of positive rational numbers in $\mathbb{Q}$ recursively by setting $q_1 := 1$ and, for each $n \ge 1$,
  $$
  q_{n+1} := \frac{1}{2}\left(q_n + \frac{2}{q_n}\right).
  $$
  Working *purely within the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$* (without assuming the existence of $\mathbb{R}$ or $\sqrt{2}$), prove each of the following statements:
  1. **Quadratic Overshoot ($q_n \in B$ for $n \ge 2$):** Establish the algebraic identity
     $$
     q_{n+1}^2 - 2 = \frac{(q_n^2 - 2)^2}{4 q_n^2}
     $$
     for all $n \ge 1$, and deduce that $q_n > 0$ and $q_n^2 > 2$ for every integer $n \ge 2$.
  2. **Strict Monotonicity and Quadratic Convergence Rate:** Prove that for every $n \ge 2$,
     $$
     q_{n+1} < q_n \quad \text{and} \quad 0 < q_{n+1}^2 - 2 < \frac{(q_n^2 - 2)^2}{4}.
     $$
     Deduce by induction that $0 < q_n^2 - 2 \le 4^{-(2^{n-2})}$ for all $n \ge 2$.
  3. **Absence of a Greatest Lower Bound in $\mathbb{Q}$:** Consider the set $S := \{q_n : n \ge 2\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$. Prove that $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ is a lower bound for $S$ ($q \le q_n$ for all $n \ge 2$) if and only if $q \le 0$ or $q \in A = \{r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : r^2 < 2\}$, and conclude using Rudin's Gap Theorem that $S$ possesses **no greatest lower bound (infimum)** in $\mathbb{Q}$.

## 0.5 Construction and Completeness of the Real Numbers ($\mathbb{R}$)

### Order Completeness: Upper Bounds, Supremum, Infimum, and the Least Upper Bound Property

**Upper Bounds, Lower Bounds, Maximum, and Minimum**
- Define what it means for a subset $E \subseteq X$ of an ordered set $(X, <)$ to be bounded above, bounded below, and bounded, and define upper bound and lower bound.
- Define the maximum ($\max E$) and minimum ($\min E$) of a subset $E \subseteq X$, and explain why they are unique whenever they exist.
- Give an example of a bounded subset of $\mathbb{Q}$ that possesses neither a maximum nor a minimum.

**Supremum ($\sup E$) and Infimum ($\inf E$)**
- Define the supremum (least upper bound, $\sup E$) and infimum (greatest lower bound, $\inf E$) of a subset $E \subseteq X$ in an ordered set $(X, <)$.
- State and prove the Proposition on the uniqueness of $\sup E$ and $\inf E$, and characterize when $\max E$ and $\min E$ exist in terms of $\sup E$ and $\inf E$.
- Explain how $\sup E$ can be interpreted in terms of the minimum of the set of upper bounds $U(E)$.

**The $\varepsilon$-Characterization of Supremum and Infimum in an Ordered Field**
- State and prove the $\varepsilon$-characterization of $\sup E$ and $\inf E$ for a non-empty bounded subset $E$ of an Ordered Field $F$.

**The Least Upper Bound Property and Its Equivalence with the Greatest Lower Bound Property**
- Define the Least Upper Bound Property (Dedekind completeness), the Greatest Lower Bound Property, and a Complete Ordered Field.
- Explain why both the non-emptiness hypothesis ($E \neq \emptyset$) and the upper-boundedness hypothesis on $E$ are indispensable in the Least Upper Bound Property.
- State and prove the Theorem establishing the equivalence of the Least Upper Bound Property and the Greatest Lower Bound Property in an Ordered Field, including the explicit relation between $\inf E$ and $\sup(-E)$.

**Failure of the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{Q}$**
- State and prove the Theorem showing that $A = \{q \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : q^2 < 2\}$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$ but has no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$, and deduce whether $\mathbb{Q}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property.

### Method I: Construction of $\mathbb{R}$ via Dedekind Cuts

**Definition of a Dedekind Cut and Rational Cuts**
- Explain the geometric and order-theoretic motivation behind Richard Dedekind's construction of $\mathbb{R}$ from $\mathbb{Q}$.
- State the three axioms (C1), (C2), and (C3) defining a Dedekind cut $\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$, and define the set $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.
- Explain the role of each cut axiom and deduce the order relationship between any $p \in \alpha$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$.
- Define the rational cut $q^*$ for each $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ (including $0^*$ and $1^*$) and prove that $q^*$ satisfies all three Dedekind cut axioms.

**Order on Cuts and the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$**
- Define the strict order relation $\alpha < \beta$ and weak order relation $\alpha \le \beta$ on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.
- State and prove the Proposition that $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, <)$ is a strictly linearly ordered set satisfying transitivity and trichotomy.
- State and prove the Theorem that $(\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}, <)$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property for any non-empty bounded-above collection $\mathcal{F} \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.

**Addition, the Straddling Lemma, and Additive Inverses in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$**
- Define the sum $\alpha + \beta$ of two Dedekind cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.
- State and prove the Proposition that $\alpha + \beta \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and that cut addition is commutative, associative, and has additive identity $0^*$.
- Explain why $-\alpha$ cannot simply be defined by negating all elements of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$.
- State and prove the Straddling Lemma for approximating a Dedekind cut $\alpha$ by rational intervals of arbitrarily small length $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$.
- Define the additive inverse $-\alpha$ of a cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, prove that $-\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$, and deduce strict order compatibility of cut addition.

**Multiplication of Cuts and the Complete Ordered Field $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$**
- Explain why cut multiplication cannot be defined directly as $\{rs : r \in \alpha, s \in \beta\}$ for arbitrary cuts, and define $\alpha \cdot \beta$ and $\alpha^{-1}$ across all sign cases in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.
- State and prove the Theorem that $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ forms a Complete Ordered Field and that $\iota(q) = q^*$ embeds $\mathbb{Q}$ as a dense subfield of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.

### Method II: Construction of $\mathbb{R}$ via Rational Cauchy Sequences

**Rational Sequences, Boundedness, and Rational Cauchy Sequences**
- Explain the analytical motivation behind Georg Cantor's construction of $\mathbb{R}$ via rational Cauchy sequences.
- Define a rational sequence $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$, a bounded rational sequence, and a rational Cauchy sequence in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$, explaining why the tolerance $\varepsilon$ must be restricted to $\mathbb{Q}_{>0}$.
- State and prove the Lemma that every rational Cauchy sequence is bounded and that $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ is closed under termwise addition, negation, and multiplication.

**Equivalence of Cauchy Sequences and the Quotient Ring $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$**
- Define the equivalence relation $(a_n) \sim (b_n)$ on $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$, the quotient set $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}} = \mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q}) / {\sim}$, the formal limit notation $\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n$, and the canonical embedding of $\mathbb{Q}$.
- State and prove the Proposition that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Q})$ and that termwise addition, negation, and multiplication are well-defined on $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ and satisfy the commutative ring axioms.

**Multiplicative Inverses, Positivity, and the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$**
- State and prove the Key Separation Lemma characterizing when a rational Cauchy sequence $(a_n)$ is not equivalent to $(0)$ in terms of being positively or negatively bounded away from zero.
- Define the multiplicative inverse $x^{-1}$ for $x \neq 0$ and positivity $x > 0$ in $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$, and prove that $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ is an Archimedean Ordered Field containing $\mathbb{Q}$ as a dense subfield.
- State and prove the Theorem that $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property.

### Uniqueness (Categoricity) of Complete Ordered Fields

**Categoricity of Complete Ordered Fields**
- State and prove the Categoricity Theorem for Complete Ordered Fields, establishing the existence and uniqueness of an order-preserving field isomorphism $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ between any two Complete Ordered Fields.
- Explain why the Categoricity Theorem justifies identifying $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ as the unique Complete Ordered Field $\mathbb{R}$.

### 0.5 Exercises

#### Problem 0.5.1: Existence of $\sqrt{2}$ as an Explicit Dedekind Cut

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Define the subset $\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> \alpha := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q \le 0 \text{ or } (q > 0 \text{ and } q^2 < 2)\}
> $$
> Using Rudin's Gap Theorem from Section 0.4, prove directly from the definitions in Section 0.5 that:
> 1. $\alpha$ satisfies the three Dedekind cut axioms (C1), (C2), and (C3), so $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ and $\alpha > 0^*$.
> 2. In $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, the product $\alpha \cdot \alpha$ equals the rational cut $2^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 2\}$. (Hint: To show $2^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha$, for any rational $0 < p < 2$, apply Rudin's Gap Theorem or the Archimedean Property to construct $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ such that $p < r^2 < 2$, and note that $p = r \cdot \left(\frac{p}{r}\right)$ with $\frac{p}{r} < r \in \alpha$.)

#### Problem 0.5.2: Multiplicative Inverse in the Dedekind Cut Construction

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ be a positive Dedekind cut ($\alpha > 0^*$), and recall the definition of its reciprocal cut:
> $$
> \alpha^{-1} := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0\} \cup \left\{p \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} : \exists r \in \mathbb{Q}_{>0} \text{ such that } \frac{1}{p} - r \notin \alpha\right\}
> $$
> 1. Prove that $\alpha^{-1}$ satisfies the Dedekind cut axioms (C1), (C2), and (C3), and that $\alpha^{-1} > 0^*$.
> 2. Prove that for every integer $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and any rational $q_0 \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$, there exist $r \in \alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}_{>0}$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ (not the minimum of $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$) such that $\frac{s}{r} < 1 + \frac{1}{n}$.
> 3. Conclude that $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 1\}$.

#### Problem 0.5.3: Explicit Isomorphism Between $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ and $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For each Cauchy real number $x = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$, define the subset $\Psi(x) \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> \Psi(x) := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : \iota(q) < x\}
> $$
> where $\iota(q) = \text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} q$ is the canonical embedding of $\mathbb{Q}$ into $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$.
> 1. Prove directly from the Cauchy construction of $\mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}}$ that $\Psi(x)$ satisfies (C1), (C2), and (C3), so $\Psi(x) \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$.
> 2. Prove directly (without invoking the general Categoricity Theorem) that $\Psi: \mathbb{R}_{\text{Cauchy}} \to \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$ is a bijective, order-preserving field isomorphism. (Hint: For surjectivity, given a cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}_{\text{Ded}}$, use the Straddling Lemma with $\varepsilon = \frac{1}{n}$ to choose $a_n \in \alpha$ and $b_n \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ with $b_n - a_n = \frac{1}{n}$, prove $(a_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is a rational Cauchy sequence, and show $\Psi(\text{LIM}_{n \to \infty} a_n) = \alpha$.)

#### Problem 0.5.4: Rigidity of the Real Field (No Non-Trivial Field Automorphisms)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\sigma: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be any field homomorphism—meaning that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$,
> $$
> \sigma(x + y) = \sigma(x) + \sigma(y), \quad \sigma(xy) = \sigma(x)\sigma(y), \quad \text{and} \quad \sigma(1) = 1
> $$
> **without** assuming beforehand that $\sigma$ is order-preserving or continuous!
> 1. Prove that $\sigma(q) = q$ for every rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$.
> 2. Using the fact that every positive real number $z > 0$ has a real square root $w \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ with $w^2 = z$ (which follows from the Least Upper Bound Property as in Problem 0.5.1), prove that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$, $x < y \implies \sigma(x) < \sigma(y)$.
> 3. Using the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$, conclude that $\sigma(x) = x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$. Thus the identity map $\text{id}_{\mathbb{R}}$ is the **only** field automorphism of $\mathbb{R}$!

#### Problem 0.5.5: Dedekind's Cut Theorem in $\mathbb{R}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $F$ be an Ordered Field. We say that $F$ satisfies **Dedekind's Cut Property** if for every pair of subsets $A, B \subseteq F$ such that:
> - $A \neq \emptyset$ and $B \neq \emptyset$,
> - $A \cup B = F$ and $A \cap B = \emptyset$, and
> - for every $a \in A$ and every $b \in B$, we have $a < b$,
>
> there exists a unique element $\xi \in F$ such that $a \le \xi \le b$ for all $a \in A$ and all $b \in B$ (equivalently, either $A$ has a maximum element $\xi = \max A$ or $B$ has a minimum element $\xi = \min B$).
> 1. Prove from the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ that $\mathbb{R}$ satisfies Dedekind's Cut Property.
> 2. Conversely, prove that if an Ordered Field $F$ satisfies Dedekind's Cut Property, then $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property.

#### Problem 0.5.6: Why Cauchy Completeness Alone Does Not Imply Dedekind Completeness

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In an arbitrary Ordered Field $F$, a sequence $(x_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ of elements in $F$ is called **$F$-Cauchy** if for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|x_n - x_m| < \varepsilon$ for all $n, m \ge N$. Similarly, $(x_n)_{n=1}^\infty$ is said to **$F$-converge** to a limit $L \in F$ if for every $\varepsilon \in F$ with $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ such that $|x_n - L| < \varepsilon$ for all $n \ge N$. We say that $F$ is **$F$-Cauchy complete** if every $F$-Cauchy sequence in $F$ $F$-converges to some limit $L \in F$.
> 1. Prove that if $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property, then $F$ is **both** Archimedean **and** $F$-Cauchy complete. (Hint: For an $F$-Cauchy sequence $(x_n)$, show $(x_n)$ is bounded in $F$, define $u_k := \sup_{n \ge k} x_n$, and take $L := \inf_{k \ge 1} u_k$.)
> 2. Conversely, prove that if an Ordered Field $F$ is **both** Archimedean **and** $F$-Cauchy complete, then $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property. Explain precisely where the Archimedean Property is essential in constructing an $F$-Cauchy sequence that converges to $\sup E$ for a bounded non-empty set $E \subseteq F$.

#### Problem 0.5.7: Order-Completeness Characterization via Monotone Bounded Functions on $\mathbb{N}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $F$ be an Ordered Field. A function $f: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to F$ (i.e., a sequence $(f(n))_{n=1}^\infty$ in $F$) is called **non-decreasing** if $f(n) \le f(n+1)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, and **bounded above** if there exists $M \in F$ such that $f(n) \le M$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$.
> 1. Prove that if every bounded-above non-decreasing function $f: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to F$ has a supremum $\sup_{n \ge 1} f(n) \in F$, then $F$ is necessarily **Archimedean**. (Hint: Suppose $\mathbb{N}_{>0}$ were bounded above in $F$, apply the hypothesis to $f(n) = n$, and derive a contradiction.)
> 2. Prove that an Ordered Field $F$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property if and only if every bounded-above non-decreasing function $f: \mathbb{N}_{>0} \to F$ has a supremum in $F$.

#### Problem 0.5.8: Failure of Dedekind Cuts Without the No-Maximum Condition (C3)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Suppose we modified the definition of a Dedekind cut in Section 0.5 by dropping axiom (C3) ("no largest element"), and defined the set $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$ of **modified cuts** to be all subsets $\alpha \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying only (C1) non-triviality ($\alpha \neq \emptyset, \alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$) and (C2) downward closure ($p \in \alpha \text{ and } q < p \implies q \in \alpha$).
> 1. Show that for every rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, both the open ray $q^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < q\}$ and the closed ray $\overline{q}^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le q\}$ belong to $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$, and satisfy $q^* \subsetneq \overline{q}^*$ with no modified cut strictly between them.
> 2. If we define addition on $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$ by the same pairwise formula $\alpha + \beta := \{r + s : r \in \alpha \text{ and } s \in \beta\}$, calculate $0^* + 0^*$, $0^* + \overline{0}^*$, and $\overline{0}^* + \overline{0}^*$, and prove that $(\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}, +)$ **fails to form a group**! (Hint: Determine whether $0^*$ or $\overline{0}^*$ would have to be the identity element $e$, and show that one of them fails to have an inverse in $\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}$.)

## 0.6 Working in $\mathbb{R}$ — Supremum Calculus, Archimedean Consequences, Roots, and Extended Reals

### The Algebra and Calculus of Supremum and Infimum

**Monotonicity and Comparison Principles**
- State and prove the theorem on the monotonicity of supremum and infimum under subset inclusion for $A \subseteq B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ with $A \neq \emptyset$, and establish the relation between $\inf A$ and $\sup A$ for any non-empty bounded subset $A$.
- Explain why the non-emptiness hypothesis $A \neq \emptyset$ is essential when comparing $\inf A$ and $\sup A$.
- State and prove the two comparison criteria for non-empty subsets $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ (both under uniform elementwise separation between $A$ and $B$, and under elementwise domination of $A$ by $B$).
- Explain the double-quantifier elimination technique used to convert a doubly quantified elementwise inequality across two sets into an inequality between their extrema.

**Minkowski Sums and Scalar Dilations of Sets**
- Define the Minkowski sum $A + B$, scalar dilation $cA$, reflection $-A$, and Minkowski difference $A - B$ for non-empty subsets $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ and $c \in \mathbb{R}$, and explain why $A + A$ and $2A$ need not coincide.
- State and prove the linearity and reflection laws for the supremum and infimum of $A + B$, $cA$ (for $c \ge 0$ and for $c < 0$), $-A$, and $A - B$.
- Explain intuitively why the supremum and infimum of a Minkowski difference $A - B$ mix $\sup$ and $\inf$ across the two sets.

**Supremum and Infimum of Real-Valued Functions**
- Define what it means for a function $f: D \to \mathbb{R}$ on a non-empty domain $D$ to be bounded above, bounded below, or bounded on $D$, and define $\sup_{x \in D} f(x)$ and $\inf_{x \in D} f(x)$.
- State and prove the four-step chain of inequalities relating the infima and suprema of two bounded functions $f, g: D \to \mathbb{R}$ and their pointwise sum $f + g$ on $D$.
- Explain conceptually why the inequality between $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x))$ and $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ can be strict, relate $(f + g)(D)$ to the Minkowski sum $f(D) + g(D)$, and construct an explicit counterexample to equality.

### The Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$ and Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$

**The Archimedean Property and Its Equivalent Formulations**
- State and prove the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$ directly from the Least Upper Bound Property.
- Explain why completeness is required to rule out unboundedly large elements dominating all of $\mathbb{N}$ in an ordered field.
- State and prove the three equivalent consequences of the Archimedean Property: arbitrary step multiples $nx > y$ for $x > 0$, reciprocal squeezing $0 < 1/n < \varepsilon$ and the value of $\inf \{1/n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}$, and the existence and uniqueness of the floor integer $\lfloor x \rfloor \in \mathbb{Z}$ for every $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
- Define the floor function $\lfloor x \rfloor$ and the fractional part $\{x\}$ of a real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$**
- Define what it means for a subset $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ to be dense in $\mathbb{R}$.
- State and prove the Density of the Rational Numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$, explaining the geometric scaling mechanism behind choosing the denominator and numerator.
- State and prove the Density of the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ using both additive translation and multiplicative scaling by a known positive irrational number.
- Explain why density implies that every non-degenerate open interval $(x, y)$ with $x < y$ contains infinitely many rational numbers and infinitely many irrational numbers.

### Existence of $n$-th Roots, Rational Exponents, and Cantor's Nested Interval Property

**Existence and Uniqueness of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$**
- Explain why invoking continuity or the Intermediate Value Theorem to prove the existence of $n$-th roots in $\mathbb{R}$ is circular at this foundational stage.
- State and prove the cyclotomic factorization identity for $b^n - a^n$ and derive the two-sided linear perturbation bounds for $b^n - a^n$ when $0 < a < b$.
- State and prove the Existence and Uniqueness of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$ ($y^n = x$ for $x > 0$ and $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$) using the Least Upper Bound Property on the candidate set $E := \{t \in \mathbb{R}_{>0} : t^n < x\}$.
- Discuss how $n$-th roots extend to $x = 0$ and to $x < 0$ when $n$ is odd versus even.

**Algebraic Laws of Roots and Rational Exponents**
- State and prove the algebraic laws for positive $n$-th roots (product rule, quotient rule, iterated root rule, commutativity of integer powers and roots, and cancellation of common factors in fractional indices) using uniqueness.
- Define rational powers $x^q$ for $x > 0$ and $q = k/n \in \mathbb{Q}$, prove that $x^q$ is well-defined independently of the fraction representation of $q$, and establish the laws of rational exponents.

**Cantor's Nested Interval Property**
- Define closed, open, and half-open bounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$ and the length of a bounded interval.
- State and prove Cantor's Nested Interval Property for a nested sequence of closed bounded intervals $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ in $\mathbb{R}$, characterizing the intersection $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty [a_n, b_n]$ both in general and under the additional hypothesis $\inf \{b_n - a_n : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0$.
- Explain with examples why each hypothesis in Cantor's Nested Interval Property (closedness, boundedness, and completeness of the ambient ordered field) is essential.

### The Extended Real Number System ($\overline{\mathbb{R}}$)

**Definition and Order Structure of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$**
- Define the extended real number system $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and its total order relation $\le$.
- State and prove the Universal Existence of Supremum and Infimum in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ for arbitrary subsets $E \subseteq \overline{\mathbb{R}}$, including unbounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ and the empty set $\emptyset$.
- Explain why the values of $\sup \emptyset$ and $\inf \emptyset$ in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ follow from vacuous truth and why they are uniquely forced by subset monotonicity.

**Arithmetic Conventions and Indeterminate Forms in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$**
- State the standard arithmetic conventions for negation, addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$.
- Identify the indeterminate forms in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and explain both algebraically and analytically why they must remain strictly undefined in analysis.

### 0.6 Exercises

**Problem 0.6.1: Supremum and Infimum of Elementwise Products and Reciprocals of Sets**
- Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, and define their elementwise product by
  $$
  A \cdot B := \{ab : a \in A, \, b \in B\}.
  $$
  1. Suppose first that $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ consist entirely of strictly positive real numbers. Prove that $A \cdot B$ is bounded and that
     $$
     \sup(A \cdot B) = (\sup A)(\sup B) \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A \cdot B) = (\inf A)(\inf B).
     $$
  2. Suppose $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ is a non-empty bounded set of positive real numbers, and define $A^{-1} := \{1/a : a \in A\}$. Prove that $A^{-1}$ is bounded above if and only if $\inf A > 0$, in which case
     $$
     \sup(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\inf A} \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\sup A}.
     $$
  3. Now let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be arbitrary non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ (which may contain negative numbers, zero, and positive numbers). Let $\alpha_1 := \inf A$, $\alpha_2 := \sup A$, $\beta_1 := \inf B$, and $\beta_2 := \sup B$. Prove that
     $$
     \sup(A \cdot B) = \max \{\alpha_1 \beta_1, \, \alpha_1 \beta_2, \, \alpha_2 \beta_1, \, \alpha_2 \beta_2\} \quad \text{and} \quad \inf(A \cdot B) = \min \{\alpha_1 \beta_1, \, \alpha_1 \beta_2, \, \alpha_2 \beta_1, \, \alpha_2 \beta_2\}.
     $$

**Problem 0.6.2: Lipschitz Continuity of the Supremum and Infimum Functionals**
- Let $D$ be a non-empty set and let $f, g: D \to \mathbb{R}$ be bounded real-valued functions on $D$. Prove that $|f - g|: D \to \mathbb{R}$ is bounded on $D$, and establish the inequalities
  $$
  \left|\sup_{x \in D} f(x) - \sup_{x \in D} g(x)\right| \le \sup_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|
  $$
  and
  $$
  \left|\inf_{x \in D} f(x) - \inf_{x \in D} g(x)\right| \le \sup_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|.
  $$
  Furthermore, give an example showing that $\sup_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|$ on the right-hand side cannot be replaced by $\inf_{x \in D} |f(x) - g(x)|$.

**Problem 0.6.3: The Minimax (Saddle-Point) Inequality for Functions of Two Variables**
- Let $X$ and $Y$ be non-empty sets, and let $f: X \times Y \to \mathbb{R}$ be a bounded function (meaning there exists $M \in \mathbb{R}$ such that $|f(x, y)| \le M$ for all $(x, y) \in X \times Y$).
  1. Prove that for each fixed $x_0 \in X$, the quantity $g(x_0) := \inf_{y \in Y} f(x_0, y)$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$; for each fixed $y_0 \in Y$, the quantity $h(y_0) := \sup_{x \in X} f(x, y_0)$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$; and the functions $g: X \to \mathbb{R}$ and $h: Y \to \mathbb{R}$ are bounded.
  2. Prove the **Minimax Inequality**:
     $$
     \sup_{x \in X} \inf_{y \in Y} f(x, y) \le \inf_{y \in Y} \sup_{x \in X} f(x, y).
     $$
  3. Construct an explicit bounded function $f: \{0, 1\} \times \{0, 1\} \to \mathbb{R}$, and also an explicit bounded function $F: [0, 1] \times [0, 1] \to \mathbb{R}$, for which the strict inequality $\sup_x \inf_y f(x, y) < \inf_y \sup_x f(x, y)$ holds.

**Problem 0.6.4: Necessity of Hypotheses in Cantor's Nested Interval Property**
- Construct explicit nested sequences of intervals $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \cdots$ (with $I_n \neq \emptyset$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$) and prove rigorously that $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty I_n = \emptyset$ in each of the following three settings:
  1. **Open Bounded Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$:** $I_n = (a_n, b_n) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ are open bounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$.
  2. **Closed Unbounded Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$:** $I_n = [a_n, +\infty) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x \ge a_n\} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ are closed unbounded intervals in $\mathbb{R}$.
  3. **Closed Bounded Intervals in $\mathbb{Q}$:** $I_n = [a_n, b_n]_{\mathbb{Q}} := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : a_n \le q \le b_n\} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}$ are closed bounded intervals in the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ with rational endpoints $a_n, b_n \in \mathbb{Q}$.

**Problem 0.6.5: Construction of Real Exponents $a^x$ via Supremum**
- Let $a \in \mathbb{R}$ with $a > 1$. In this problem, you will extend the definition of rational powers $a^q$ ($q \in \mathbb{Q}$) to arbitrary real exponents $x \in \mathbb{R}$ using the Least Upper Bound Property, without invoking logarithms or continuity.
  1. **Strict Monotonicity and Bernoulli Estimate on $\mathbb{Q}$:** Prove that for all rational numbers $p, q \in \mathbb{Q}$, $p < q \iff a^p < a^q$. Furthermore, using Bernoulli's inequality $(1 + \delta)^n \ge 1 + n\delta$, prove that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$,
     $$
     1 < a^{1/n} \le 1 + \frac{a - 1}{n},
     $$
     and deduce that $\inf \{a^{1/n} - 1 : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\} = 0$.
  2. **Definition and Consistency of $a^x$:** For each $x \in \mathbb{R}$, define the subset $E_x := \{a^q : q \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ and } q \le x\}$. Prove that $E_x$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, so that
     $$
     a^x := \sup \{a^q : q \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ and } q \le x\}
     $$
     is well-defined in $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$. Prove also that if $x \in \mathbb{Q}$, this supremum coincides with the rational power $a^x$ already defined.
  3. **Strict Monotonicity on $\mathbb{R}$:** Prove that for any $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, we have $a^x < a^y$.
  4. **Additivity Law $a^{x+y} = a^x a^y$:** Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$. Using the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and Part (1), prove that $a^x = \inf \{a^r : r \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ and } r > x\}$, and prove that
     $$
     a^{x+y} = a^x a^y \quad \text{for all } x, y \in \mathbb{R}.
     $$

**Problem 0.6.6: Kronecker's Approximation Theorem (1D Density of $\mathbb{Z} + \alpha \mathbb{Z}$)**
- Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ be an irrational real number, and define the subset of $\mathbb{R}$ generated additively by $1$ and $\alpha$:
  $$
  G_\alpha := \mathbb{Z} + \alpha\mathbb{Z} = \{m + n\alpha : m, n \in \mathbb{Z}\}.
  $$
  1. Prove that for distinct integers $n_1, n_2 \in \mathbb{Z}$, their fractional parts $\{n_1 \alpha\} = n_1 \alpha - \lfloor n_1 \alpha \rfloor$ and $\{n_2 \alpha\} = n_2 \alpha - \lfloor n_2 \alpha \rfloor$ in $[0, 1)$ are distinct and strictly positive whenever $n_i \neq 0$.
  2. Let $N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ be any positive integer. By partitioning the half-open unit interval $[0, 1)$ into $N$ subintervals $\left[\frac{j-1}{N}, \frac{j}{N}\right)$ for $j = 1, 2, \dots, N$ and applying the **Pigeonhole Principle** to the $N + 1$ points $\{0\alpha\}, \{1\alpha\}, \{2\alpha\}, \dots, \{N\alpha\} \in [0, 1)$, prove that there exist integers $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $1 \le |n| \le N$ such that
     $$
     0 < m + n\alpha < \frac{1}{N}.
     $$
  3. Deduce that for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists an element $g \in G_\alpha$ with $0 < g < \varepsilon$, and prove that $G_\alpha = \{m + n\alpha : m, n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ is **dense in $\mathbb{R}$** (meaning that for every $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exist integers $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $x < m + n\alpha < y$).

**Problem 0.6.7: Complete Classification of Additive Subgroups of $(\mathbb{R}, +)$**
- Let $G \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be an **additive subgroup** of $(\mathbb{R}, +)$, meaning that $G \neq \emptyset$ and for all $x, y \in G$, we have $x - y \in G$ (which implies $0 \in G$, $-x \in G$, and $x + y \in G$). Assume $G \neq \{0\}$, define the set of positive elements of $G$ by $G^+ := G \cap \mathbb{R}_{>0}$, and show that $G^+ \neq \emptyset$ so that
  $$
  c := \inf G^+ \ge 0
  $$
  exists in $\mathbb{R}$. Prove the following dichotomy classifying all non-trivial additive subgroups of $\mathbb{R}$:
  1. **Discrete Cyclic Case ($c > 0$):** If $c > 0$, prove that $c \in G^+$ (i.e., $c = \min G^+$) and that
     $$
     G = c\mathbb{Z} := \{nc : n \in \mathbb{Z}\}.
     $$
  2. **Dense Case ($c = 0$):** If $c = 0$, prove that $G$ is **dense in $\mathbb{R}$**.
  3. **Application to Kronecker's Theorem:** Use this classification to give an alternative proof that for any $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$, the subgroup $G_\alpha := \{m + n\alpha : m, n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $\alpha \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$.

**Problem 0.6.8: Equivalence of the Nested Interval Property + Archimedean Property to the Least Upper Bound Property**
